APEP Answer Keys and Explanations

Numbered to match the practice tests in your printed study guide.

Practice Test 1 Answer Key

Case CHB1

Daniel Okafor, 46, a primary school teacher, is 3 months after orthotopic heart transplantation for dilated cardiomyopathy and attends the outpatient cardiac rehabilitation gym twice weekly. Medications: tacrolimus, mycophenolate and prednisolone. A recent bone density scan showed osteopenia. Today: resting HR 96 bpm (higher than usual because the transplanted heart lacks vagal tone), BP 134/82 mmHg, SpO2 98 percent on room air, temperature 36.8 degrees Celsius. Quadriceps strength is 4/5 bilaterally and he completes 12 sit-to-stands in 30 seconds. The sternal wound has healed and his surgeon has cleared resistance training. He has attended 10 sessions with you, is cheerful, and says you are the reason he feels well again. You are employed by the hospital outpatient service.

  1. The correct answer is A (Use a longer warm-up and cool-down, and guide intensity by perceived exertion, not heart rate): A denervated transplanted heart responds mainly to circulating catecholamines, so heart rate rises late, peaks lower and recovers slowly. A longer warm-up and cool-down lets the circulation adjust, and perceived exertion is a more dependable guide than heart rate. Slow recovery is expected here and is not itself a sign of rejection.
  2. The correct answer is C (Progressive lower limb resistance with weight-bearing work, avoiding high impact and forced spinal flexion): Progressive resistance training counters glucocorticoid myopathy and weight-bearing work supports bone, so both suit him when loads are controlled and impact and loaded spinal flexion are avoided with osteopenia. Aerobic work alone leaves the muscle loss unaddressed, high-impact plyometrics are poorly suited to reduced bone density, and a transplanted heart can raise cardiac output through stroke volume and catecholamines.
  3. The correct answer is A (Cancel exercise, use infection control measures, and advise him to ring the transplant team): Fever and respiratory symptoms in someone on three immunosuppressants may mean an infection that can progress quickly or an early sign of rejection, so exercise stops and the transplant team needs to hear the same day. Infection control protects other participants. Light exercise or rechecking temperature misses the urgency, and a home program does not address the need for medical review.
  4. The correct answer is A (Decline kindly, explain the limits of your role with the service, and tell your supervisor): Private paid sessions would blur the therapeutic relationship, create a conflict with your employer and leave his care outside the service's clinical governance and indemnity. Declining kindly with an explanation and informing your supervisor keeps him within the transplant team's oversight. Agreeing with team approval or recording sessions does not remove the boundary problem, and withdrawing warmth would harm his care.
Case CHB2

Jarrah Williams, 44, Wiradjuri man, fencing contractor in a remote shire. Five weeks ago he strained his lower back lifting a post rammer. His WorkCover claim is accepted. Pain is in the right lower back, 4 out of 10 on most days, worse after bending and long drives. No leg symptoms. Sleeps poorly. He believes his back is damaged and worries that work will make it worse; he has stayed home for four weeks and scored high on a fear avoidance questionnaire. The GP certificate allows suitable duties. His employer, a small farm, can offer supervised light duties. He smokes about 15 cigarettes daily and does little activity outside work. Examination: lumbar flexion about half range, extension pain free, neurology normal, straight leg raise negative bilaterally. Seen at a fortnightly outreach clinic, 90 minutes from the nearest hospital.

  1. The correct answer is C (Explain that his back is sound and activity aids recovery, then agree on simple graded steps): With no red flags and normal neurology, reassurance and activity are the evidence-informed first steps, and they directly address his fear of harm. Waiting for zero pain, bracing or protecting the segment all reinforce the belief that his back is fragile and prolong disability.
  2. The correct answer is C (Increase by planned, time-based steps that he agrees to, rather than by how his pain feels): Time-contingent progression sets the next step in advance, so pain does not govern activity, which builds confidence and reduces avoidance. Pain-contingent rules, such as waiting for zero pain or stopping at any rise, keep him tied to symptoms. Starting at maximum capacity risks a flare and loss of trust.
  3. The correct answer is D (Reassure him and temporarily reduce heavy digging while keeping him on modified tasks): Flares with normal neurology and no red flags are common during return to work and respond to temporary load reduction with reassurance, keeping him in the workplace. Full withdrawal feeds avoidance, pressing on at full load risks a longer setback, and imaging is not indicated without red flags.
  4. The correct answer is D (Agree one or two goals he chooses, give a written plan, and arrange a phone check-in): Self-management support works best with a few goals the person chooses, a written plan and some follow-up, which suits a remote setting. Overloading him with simultaneous changes or generic pamphlets rarely sustains change, and ignoring health behaviours misses an opportunity within the health promotion role of physiotherapy.
Case CHB3

Ibrahim Haddad is a 71-year-old retired taxi driver, day 3 after a left transtibial amputation for chronic limb-threatening ischaemia. He has type 2 diabetes, ischaemic heart disease and stage 3 chronic kidney disease, and stopped smoking last year. He is on the vascular ward. Observations: HR 88, BP 142/84 mmHg, RR 16, SpO2 95% on room air, temperature 37.2 degrees. The residual limb dressing is dry and intact with minor serous strikethrough. Pain is 5 of 10 with phantom limb sensations. The right foot has dry skin and a reddened heel. He can sit unsupported at the edge of the bed but is anxious about falling. The surgical team's orders allow mobilising with a frame, weight bearing through the right leg only. He wants to return home to his wife, who has arthritis.

  1. The correct answer is C (No pillow under the knee, keep the limb supported extended when sitting, and lie flat regularly): Contractures develop when the limb rests bent, so the knee is kept extended with support in sitting, pillows under the knee are avoided, and regular time lying flat or prone is included. A hanging limb encourages swelling and a flexed position encourages contracture. Flexing for comfort protects the wound briefly but stores up problems for later prosthetic walking.
  2. The correct answer is C (This is phantom limb pain, common after amputation, linked to nervous system changes, and treatable): Phantom sensations and pain are very common after amputation and relate to changes in the nervous system. Explaining this reassures the patient and opens treatment options such as mirror therapy and desensitisation. Neuroma or ischaemia in the remaining leg can cause pain in real tissue but do not account for pain felt in the missing foot, and calling it purely psychological dismisses a genuine condition.
  3. The correct answer is B (Sit him back down promptly, monitor observations until recovered, and retry standing in stages later): A fall in blood pressure with light-headedness on first standing is common after surgery but needs a safe response: sitting him down, checking observations until they recover, and trying again in stages. Continuing as though the change is harmless risks a fall. Walking while symptomatic is unsafe, and standing still to breathe delays dealing with the drop in pressure.
  4. The correct answer is B (Document findings, withhold residual limb exercises, and escalate promptly to the surgical team): Spreading redness, warmth, odour, increased discharge and a raised temperature suggest wound infection, which needs surgical and nursing assessment rather than physiotherapy management. Documenting, withholding limb loading and escalating promptly is within scope and safe. Redressing the wound or applying a shrinker over a possibly infected site goes beyond physiotherapy scope and may trap infection, and continuing gently delays care.
Case CHB4

Chidi Mutasa, 34, has spastic diplegic cerebral palsy (GMFCS level III) and walks with forearm crutches. He lives in supported accommodation, works part time in a library and has a mild intellectual disability. He attends outpatient physiotherapy with his support worker after a GP referral for increasing calf tightness and shoes that no longer fit well. Findings: passive ankle dorsiflexion with the knee extended is neutral and with the knee flexed is 8 degrees; modified Ashworth score 2 in both plantarflexors; brief clonus on fast stretch; toe walking with a crouch tendency; skin intact. He uses night splints inconsistently. The physiotherapist suggests a trial of serial casting, a review for an ankle foot orthosis and a home stretching programme. His support worker says Chidi will do whatever the clinic suggests, but Chidi says he does not want a cast.

  1. The correct answer is D (Gastrocnemius shortness is the main limit, so stretch that muscle across both knee and ankle): Dorsiflexion that improves when the knee is flexed shows that the gastrocnemius, which crosses the knee, is limiting range, because flexing the knee slackens it. A soleus or joint limit would persist in both positions. Spasticity may contribute but does not explain the position dependent difference.
  2. The correct answer is C (Slow sustained stretches in supported standing, repeated through the day within comfortable range): Slow sustained stretch in a supported position avoids provoking the stretch reflex in spastic muscle and can be done safely at home. Rapid or forceful stretching tends to trigger clonus and increased tone, and strengthening alone does not maintain muscle length. A support worker can prompt practice but should not apply force.
  3. The correct answer is B (Presume capacity, explain risks and alternatives accessibly, and respect his decision if he understands): Adults are presumed to have capacity, and capacity is specific to each decision and can be supported with plain language, pictures and time. Chidi is expressing a clear view, so his decision stands unless he cannot understand or weigh the information. A support worker cannot consent on his behalf, and a formal assessment is not needed without genuine doubt.
  4. The correct answer is D (Stop stretching and arrange urgent medical assessment for possible deep vein thrombosis today): A newly swollen, warm, tender calf on one side without trauma raises concern about deep vein thrombosis, so stretching is stopped and urgent medical review is sought the same day. Clinical tests such as calf squeezing are unreliable and delay care, and massage risks dislodging a clot. Ice and strain management would miss a potentially life threatening cause.
Case CHB5

Marco Rinaldi, 31, plasterer, fell from a 1.5 metre ladder at work three hours ago and struck his head on concrete. A workmate reports a brief loss of consciousness (under one minute). CT head was reported as normal and the ED doctor has cleared his cervical spine clinically. He now has a frontal headache 5/10, nausea, light sensitivity and feels 'foggy'. GCS 15, orientated, recalls 2 of 5 words at five minutes, tandem walk shows two sidesteps, eye movements full without nystagmus. HR 72, BP 126/78, RR 14, SpO2 98 percent. No anticoagulants. His partner will drive him home. The ED doctor has asked the physiotherapist to assess balance and give discharge advice. During the assessment he vomits once, and the physiotherapist must hand over to the nurse in charge.

  1. The correct answer is B (Concussion with temporary functional symptoms that usually settle in days to weeks with graded activity): A brief loss of consciousness, normal CT, GCS 15 and transient headache, nausea, cognitive and balance symptoms fit concussion, which usually recovers over days to weeks with a gradual return to activity. Prolonged strict rest is outdated and can slow recovery. His cervical spine has been cleared, so immobilisation is not needed, and persistent symptoms are the exception rather than the expectation.
  2. The correct answer is A (Pause testing, tell the nurse and doctor about the vomit, and continue only once they agree): Vomiting after a head injury is a recognised warning feature, so the treating medical team should know about it and decide whether he needs further observation before more testing. Continuing or delaying the report risks missing deterioration even though he looks well now. Discharging him straight away removes the opportunity for medical review.
  3. The correct answer is D (Identify the patient, state situation, background and findings, then voice concern and request review): A structured handover such as ISBAR identifies the patient, states the situation and background, gives the relevant findings, voices a specific concern and asks for a response. Leading with the vomiting alone gets attention but leaves out who the patient is, the background and what is wanted. Writing findings in the notes is not a verbal escalation that prompts action now. Stopping before the concern and the request leaves the nurse unsure what to do next.
  4. The correct answer is D (Resume light symptom-free activity, progress gradually, and defer ladder work until medically cleared): Early light activity kept below the level that provokes symptoms supports recovery, and a stepwise return is guided by symptoms. Working at heights with balance and cognitive deficits risks a second injury, so ladder work needs clearance first. Strict rest until symptom free is outdated, and pushing through a returning headache or resuming normal duties ignores his current symptoms.
Case CHB6

Giuseppe Moretti, 74, a retired baker, attends a community health centre with his daughter, Maria. Over eight months he has developed cramping pain in the right calf after walking about 150 metres on level ground, easing within a few minutes of standing still. He has type 2 diabetes, hypertension and a 40 year smoking history, now ceased. Right ankle brachial index is 0.65 and pedal pulses are weak. Skin is dry and hairless on the lower leg with no ulcers. Lumbar flexion does not change his symptoms. He has mild memory difficulties noted by his GP, lives alone, manages his own medications with a dosette box and walks without an aid. Maria wants him enrolled in a supervised exercise programme three times a week. Giuseppe says he is happy to walk on his own and does not want to attend the centre.

  1. The correct answer is B (Relief within minutes of standing still, with pain returning at a consistent walking distance): Arterial claudication is reproducible at a consistent distance and settles quickly with rest in any posture, because the issue is muscle blood supply. Neurogenic claudication typically needs spinal flexion or sitting for relief and is often eased by uphill walking. The other patterns describe radicular or positional spinal symptoms.
  2. The correct answer is C (Explain options plainly, check his understanding and reasoning, and respect his decision if he has capacity): Capacity is decision specific and presumed, so a diagnosis of mild memory difficulty does not remove the right to decide. The physiotherapist supports understanding, tests the reasoning and then respects the outcome. A daughter has no automatic power to consent unless formally appointed, and simply discharging him ignores the chance to offer a home programme he would accept.
  3. The correct answer is C (Walk until the calf pain is moderate, rest until it settles, then repeat several times): Interval walking that reaches moderate claudication pain, followed by rest until it settles, is the exercise stimulus that improves walking capacity in peripheral arterial disease. Staying below the pain threshold or stopping at the first twinge gives too little stimulus. Pushing through pain without rest is poorly tolerated and hard to sustain, especially in a man with diabetes.
  4. The correct answer is B (Pause the walking programme and arrange an urgent medical review for possible critical limb ischaemia): Night rest pain, a cool pale foot and a dark area on a toe in a man with a low ankle brachial index point to possible critical limb ischaemia, which threatens the limb and needs urgent medical and vascular assessment. Podiatry can care for the toe but cannot treat the poor blood supply, and walking adds demand to tissue that is already starved. Letting the foot hang may ease pain but a visit next week is far too slow. Reducing the distance still keeps loading an ischaemic foot.
Case CHB7

Mr Wayne Bonson, 61, an Aboriginal man from a remote community about 600 km away, is in week three after a left middle cerebral artery stroke. He has right hemiparesis: hip flexion 3/5, ankle dorsiflexion 2/5, plantarflexion 4/5, and mild calf tone (modified Ashworth 1). He walks 10 m with a hemi-walker and close supervision, with poor right foot clearance and knee hyperextension in stance. Berg Balance Scale 31/56. He transfers independently, and his blood pressure and heart rate are stable. He speaks quietly, avoids prolonged eye contact, and says his main goal is to get home for family obligations. His sister and nephew visit daily. The hospital Aboriginal Liaison Officer is available. He has declined the group gym class twice.

  1. The correct answer is C (Repeated task-specific walking practice, trialling an ankle-foot orthosis and cueing knee control in stance): Foot drop and knee hyperextension are best addressed with repeated, task-specific walking practice supported by an ankle-foot orthosis trial and cueing, since walking practice is the main driver of gait recovery. Stretching alone does not treat weak dorsiflexors, and open-chain quadriceps work does not teach stance control. Maximal speed treadmill work risks poor movement patterns and falls given his balance score.
  2. The correct answer is B (Offer private sessions, invite family or the Aboriginal Liaison Officer to join, and ask his preferences): Culturally safe practice means asking about and responding to his preferences, offering privacy, and involving family and the Aboriginal Liaison Officer, rather than reading quietness or eye contact through one's own norms. Pressing for group attendance, relying on handouts alone, or presuming a language barrier without asking each place the clinician's assumptions ahead of the patient's needs.
  3. The correct answer is B (Plan with him, family and community health staff, including outdoor terrain practice and a carer-supported programme): Practising walking on the terrain he will meet and involving his family and community health staff builds a realistic bridge to home and respects his stated priority. Keeping him in hospital until he is independent on every surface delays his goal, weekly outpatient attendance is not feasible given the distance, and long-term relocation conflicts with his wish to be with family and community.
  4. The correct answer is C (He is at increased risk of falls and needs supervision and an aid for walking): The Berg score of 31 out of 56 sits in the range linked with increased fall risk and a need for supervision and a walking aid, which matches his poor foot clearance and knee hyperextension. Walking independently with a frame would ignore the supervision he needs. Limiting him to therapy sessions with two staff is too restrictive and would cost him practice and function on the ward. The score describes current balance and cannot on its own set a discharge destination.
Case CHB8

Mrs Margaret O'Connor, 76, a retired teacher, is on day 2 after a left middle cerebral artery ischaemic stroke treated with thrombolysis 36 hours ago. She lived with her husband and was independent before the stroke. She has right hemiparesis (arm 2/5, leg 3/5), mild expressive aphasia, and poor sitting balance. History: atrial fibrillation and hypertension. BP 148/82, HR 82 irregular, SpO2 96% on room air, afebrile. She has an indwelling urinary catheter and a peripheral IV line, and the nurses say she is drowsy at times. The medical team has cleared her to start mobilising out of bed. The ward is busy and the nurse manager has reminded all staff to record any incidents.

  1. The correct answer is B (Mobilise early using frequent, short sessions of lower intensity, with monitoring of her responses): Current stroke guidance supports early mobilisation once she is medically stable, but a large trial found that very early, high-dose out of bed activity led to worse outcomes. Frequent short sessions of lower intensity with monitoring give the benefit of early activity while limiting that risk. Delaying standing for days cuts the benefit of early rehabilitation and adds the risks of bed rest. One long session suits her poorly because fatigue and drowsiness limit what she can manage at once.
  2. The correct answer is A (Lay her flat promptly, recheck observations and neurological status, and inform the medical team): A fall in blood pressure with new pallor and worsened speech may signal reduced cerebral perfusion or neurological deterioration, so she should be laid flat and the team told. Continuing or standing her risks further compromise. Recovering colour does not exclude a neurological change that needs medical review.
  3. The correct answer is C (Keep her supported on the floor, assess for injury first, then lift her with equipment): After a fall, the first step is a safe assessment for injury before any movement, and recent thrombolysis means she may bleed or bruise more easily, so hidden injury matters. Lifting manually or asking her to stand could worsen an unrecognised injury. The report follows once she is safe and the team has been informed.
  4. The correct answer is C (Enter the fall in the incident system promptly with factual details, and inform the patient): Incident systems capture falls and near misses whether or not harm occurred, so that causes can be reviewed and risks reduced. Every clinician who witnesses an event has a duty to report it, and the patient should be told openly. Delaying or delegating loses detail and ownership.
Case CHB9

Jacob Haddad, 8, attends outpatient physiotherapy with his mother, Rana. Four months ago a paediatric rheumatologist diagnosed oligoarticular juvenile idiopathic arthritis of the right knee. He takes his prescribed medicines and had an intra-articular injection six weeks ago. He has regular eye checks. Today: mild right knee effusion with minimal warmth, extension lacks 8 degrees, flexion 115 degrees, visible quadriceps wasting, and a mild antalgic gait with the knee held flexed. Morning stiffness lasts about 40 minutes and pain is 3/10 after school sport. He plays in the school football team. His class teacher emailed you asking for his diagnosis, prognosis and activity limits, and Rana says it is fine to tell the school anything. His father, who lives separately, also phoned asking for a progress summary.

  1. The correct answer is B (Effusion makes a flexed position the most comfortable, and pain-inhibited quadriceps then weaken from disuse): An effusion raises pressure inside the joint, and the knee rests in the flexed position where that pressure is lowest. Pain and swelling also inhibit quadriceps activation, so the muscle weakens and wastes, which produces an extension lag. Hamstring tightness is a possible later contributor but does not explain the wasting. Structural joint damage is unlikely four months into treated disease with mild signs. Leg length overgrowth can occur in this condition, but it does not account for the extension lag and quadriceps wasting seen with an active effusion.
  2. The correct answer is A (Daily range of motion and pain-free quadriceps strengthening, with graded return to sport guided by symptoms): With controlled disease, regular movement and pain-free strengthening restore extension and quadriceps strength while allowing a graded return to activity. Prolonged rest worsens wasting and contracture risk. Heavy loading and forceful overpressure stress a joint that still has an effusion and can provoke a flare.
  3. The correct answer is C (Get written consent from Rana, share only the activity guidance the school needs, and document it): Health information about a child is shared with a third party only with valid consent from a parent or guardian, ideally written and specific, and only as much as is needed for the purpose, here safe participation. A broad verbal remark does not cover diagnosis and prognosis for all staff. Refusing to share anything would deny the school guidance that helps Jacob stay active, and a discussion before consent is a privacy breach.
  4. The correct answer is B (Withhold loaded exercise, record the findings, and contact the rheumatology team today about a possible flare): A hot, more swollen and painful knee with a sudden fall in range and no injury suggests a flare of active synovitis. Loaded exercise should stop and the treating rheumatology team should be told today because treatment may need to change. Ice and waiting for the next session look after symptoms but delay specialist input. Halving the strengthening still loads an inflamed joint. A GP review within the week is too slow and goes to the wrong team for adjusting his arthritis treatment.
Case CHB10

Mele Tupou, 32, seven weeks postpartum after an uncomplicated vaginal birth of her third child. Pelvic girdle pain began at 28 weeks and has not settled. She reports deep pain over the pubic bone and right buttock, worse rolling in bed, climbing stairs, standing on one leg and lifting her toddler. Pain 6 out of 10 at worst. She is also wary of leaking urine when she coughs. No bowel changes or leg numbness. No relevant medical history. Seen at the community health centre. Examination: neurology normal. Palpation is tender over the pubic symphysis and right posterior pelvis. Active straight leg raise on the right is painful and feels heavy, and the pain lessens when the physiotherapist gently compresses her pelvis. Trendelenburg sign mild on the right. Hip range is full and pain free.

  1. The correct answer is A (Impaired load transfer through the pelvis, so support and stabilising exercise are likely to help): Pain that eases with manual compression suggests the pelvic joints need extra force closure, pointing to impaired load transfer. Support and strengthening are therefore sensible first steps. Her full, pain free hip range and normal neurology make hip joint and nerve root sources unlikely.
  2. The correct answer is B (Education, a pelvic belt trial, and graded gluteal and deep abdominal exercise with pacing): Education, support and stabilising exercise form the core of management for pelvic girdle pain and fit a home based plan with pacing. Manipulating the sacroiliac joint targets mobility, whereas her problem is control during load. Prolonged rest weakens the muscles she needs and delays return to lifting her children.
  3. The correct answer is A (Withhold exercise and arrange same-day medical assessment for a possible deep vein thrombosis): A tight, warm, swollen and tender one-sided calf seven weeks after birth fits deep vein thrombosis, which can embolise to the lungs. The safest step is to stop exercise and arrange medical assessment the same day. Stopping massage and elevating the leg protects her somewhat but leaves the diagnosis to wait for the next visit. A stocking and a two day wait delay diagnosis. A scan booked next week is also too slow for a suspected clot.
  4. The correct answer is C (Do the time-critical equipment assessment first, then offer Mele a brief telehealth review): Urgency and consequence guide prioritisation: a discharge tomorrow without safe equipment risks harm and a failed discharge, while Mele is stable and can be reviewed briefly by telehealth or rebooked with her agreement. Notes and the GP call matter but can be scheduled around the time-critical task.
Case CHB11

Hussein Aziz, 71, retired taxi driver, lives with his wife who has limited mobility. Admitted 4 days ago with increasing breathlessness; large right pleural effusion due to congestive heart failure. Yesterday an ultrasound-guided thoracentesis removed 1.2 L and no drain remains. History: heart failure, type 2 diabetes, ex-smoker. Today: RR 22, SpO2 94 percent on room air, HR 84, BP 118/72 mmHg. Examination: reduced air entry and stony dullness at the right base, trachea central, reduced right basal expansion, mild ankle oedema. He walks 30 metres with breathlessness rated 4 on the Borg scale. Chest X-ray shows a small residual effusion. He is independent in transfers. Discharge is planned in 2 days. He will need help with showering and shopping and a supervised exercise programme. He holds a pension card and has no private health insurance.

  1. The correct answer is D (Positioning in upright sitting, thoracic expansion exercises and graded walking monitored with SpO2 and Borg): With a stable patient and a small residual effusion, expansion exercises, upright positioning and monitored graded walking improve ventilation and exercise tolerance. Percussion cannot mobilise fluid in the pleural space. Prolonged bed rest worsens deconditioning, and unmonitored brisk walking is unsafe in heart failure.
  2. The correct answer is A (Stony dull percussion, reduced air entry and diminished vocal resonance over the right base): Fluid in the pleural space dampens sound transmission, giving stony dullness, quiet breath sounds and reduced vocal resonance at the base. Hyperresonance and a deviated trachea suggest air under tension rather than fluid. Dullness with reduced air entry but increased vocal resonance fits collapse with a patent airway, not fluid. Bronchial breathing with crackles and increased resonance points to consolidation.
  3. The correct answer is A (Stop walking, sit him upright and request urgent medical review for a possible complication): Cough, tightness and falling saturation soon after fluid removal raise concern for complications such as re-expansion pulmonary oedema or pneumothorax, which need medical assessment. Exercise should stop immediately. Waiting to see whether saturation recovers delays diagnosis, and airway clearance does not treat these causes.
  4. The correct answer is D (Referral to My Aged Care for assessment of eligibility for home support and community programs): People aged 65 and over who need help at home are assessed for subsidised services through My Aged Care. The NDIS is generally not available to new applicants over 65, and a compensable scheme needs a work-related injury or illness. A GP plan supports a limited number of allied health visits but does not fund personal care.
Case CHB12

Maria Rossi, 72, lives alone and receives home support through My Aged Care. She has had low back pain for two years that now spreads to both thighs. She describes it as constant, 7/10 on average, and reports tender skin to light touch over her back and thighs, poor sleep and fatigue. She stopped gardening and now walks only to the letterbox. She believes her scan report, which described age-typical degenerative changes ('wear and tear'), means movement will damage her spine. There are no red flags: no weight loss, fever, night-only pain, bladder or bowel change, or neurological deficit. A screening questionnaire for central sensitisation scored high. Medications: paracetamol, an antihypertensive. Walking tolerance is five minutes before she stops because of pain. The community team has an allied health assistant who could support home sessions.

  1. The correct answer is A (Heightened central nervous system processing of pain, amplifying input from tissues that show little ongoing damage): Widespread pain, tenderness to light touch, poor sleep and a high screening score with age-typical imaging fit sensitised central pain processing. Degenerative change is common in people without pain, so continuing local tissue input does not explain her picture. Small fibre neuropathy is possible but nothing in her history or examination supports it. Fear of movement and deconditioning contribute to her disability, but they do not explain spread of pain or sensitivity to light touch.
  2. The correct answer is B (Pain education reframing her scan, plus time-based graded walking and strengthening from a tolerable baseline): Persistent pain with fear avoidance responds best to explanation that lowers threat combined with graded, time-based activity that is not driven by pain level. Passive care builds dependence and avoids the real problem. Alignment cues reinforce fear of movement, and pain-contingent pacing keeps activity tied to symptoms and tends to maintain the cycle.
  3. The correct answer is B (Give written, patient-specific instructions, confirm competency, set review points and keep responsibility for reassessment and progression): Delegation to an assistant requires clear, individual instructions, confirmed competence and regular review, while the physiotherapist remains accountable for assessment, clinical reasoning and progression. A qualification does not transfer professional judgement. Adjusting treatment or leaving supervision to others removes the physiotherapist's oversight.
  4. The correct answer is D (Explain flares are common, reduce volume briefly, rebuild gradually and agree a written flare plan): Without red flags, a flare is an expected part of persistent pain and is managed with reassurance, a temporary reduction in load and a plan she can follow herself. Long rest deconditions her and strengthens fear. Imaging is not indicated without new signs, and adding passive treatment shifts the focus away from self-management.
Case CHB13

Tomasi Faleolo, 27, courier, eight weeks after a motorbike crash with a right clavicle fracture (plated) and a traction injury to the right brachial plexus. Inpatient rehabilitation. The surgeon is considering nerve transfer surgery and nerve conduction studies are pending. Right arm examination: shoulder abduction 0/5, external rotation 0/5, elbow flexion 1/5, wrist extension 2/5, triceps 3/5, finger flexion and grip 4/5. Sensation reduced over the lateral upper arm and lateral forearm; Tinel's sign positive at the supraclavicular fossa. A palpable gap below the acromion when the arm hangs. Passive shoulder flexion 90 degrees and external rotation 20 degrees, both limited by end-range pain. Burning neuropathic pain 6 out of 10, worse at night. He is keen to return to the gym. Today the physiotherapist also has four other patients on the ward.

  1. The correct answer is C (Mobilise the postoperative patient, then do the discharge assessment, then see Tomasi, then triage the referral): Early mobilisation after hip fracture surgery is time-sensitive and its benefits fall away with delay, so it takes priority. The discharge equipment assessment is tied to a deadline tomorrow, so it comes next. Tomasi's rehabilitation is important but can be moved within the day, and a routine new referral can be triaged last.
  2. The correct answer is D (An upper trunk injury involving the C5 and C6 nerve roots, with the hand largely spared): Loss of shoulder abduction, external rotation and elbow flexion with lateral arm sensory loss maps to the C5 and C6 roots, which form the upper trunk. A lower trunk or medial cord lesion would weaken the hand and affect the medial arm, which is not seen here, while a posterior cord lesion would spare the biceps.
  3. The correct answer is A (Support the arm when upright and move the shoulder passively within pain-free range): A paralysed shoulder has no active muscle support, so the joint needs protection from traction while gentle, pain-free passive movement preserves range for later recovery. Pulleys and forceful stretching load the unprotected capsule and risk further injury or pain.
  4. The correct answer is A (Low-load active work of weak muscles in short bouts, avoiding substitution by stronger muscles): Weak, partly denervated muscle tires easily and overload can damage it, so short low-load bouts that target the muscle itself are appropriate. Strong muscles will take over if allowed, producing trick movements, and heavy loading or maximal stimulation risks overwork without proven benefit.
Case CHB14

Bill Kerrigan, 74, retired farmer, ex-smoker, has COPD and lives with his wife in a regional town. His last exacerbation was three months ago, treated by his GP with oral steroids and antibiotics. He uses a long acting inhaled bronchodilator daily. Referred to a community pulmonary rehabilitation programme. Baseline assessment: 6 minute walk distance 280 m, stopping once; SpO2 94% at rest on room air, falling to 87% at the end of the walk and recovering to 93% within two minutes; modified Borg dyspnoea 6/10 at the end. HR 88 at rest, BP 135/80, RR 18. Chest: reduced air entry, prolonged expiration, no crackles. No ankle swelling. Bill says his puffer does nothing and he wants to stop it.

  1. The correct answer is B (Moderate to somewhat severe breathlessness, about 3 to 4 on the modified Borg scale): Pulmonary rehabilitation commonly sets the training target at moderate to somewhat severe breathlessness, enough to drive adaptation while staying tolerable and safe. Severe breathlessness reduces adherence and raises the risk of marked desaturation in a man who already fell to 87 percent. Mild breathlessness protects saturation but gives too little stimulus to improve his fitness. Training at his baseline walk peak is too hard to sustain across a session and does not allow for progression.
  2. The correct answer is D (Pause to recover, then seek medical review of exertional oxygen need before progressing): Marked exertional desaturation needs a pause and recovery, and the need for supplemental oxygen is a medical decision based on assessment. Continuing to exercise while desaturated is unsafe even if the change is common in COPD. Physiotherapists do not choose oxygen flow rates independently, and shortening rests while saturation is low adds risk.
  3. The correct answer is B (Withhold exercise today and arrange same day GP review for a suspected exacerbation): Increased dyspnoea, purulent sputum, tachycardia, tachypnoea and a low resting saturation indicate an acute exacerbation, so exercise and testing should stop and he needs prompt medical assessment. Exercising or testing would add stress to an unwell patient and the result would not reflect his baseline. Starting antibiotics is a medical decision, not a physiotherapy one.
  4. The correct answer is A (Coach his inhaler technique and ask him to raise stopping it with his GP): A quick, shallow breath is a common reason an inhaler seems to do nothing, and coaching correct technique is within physiotherapy practice. Deciding whether to stop a prescribed medicine belongs to his GP, so he should be encouraged to raise it there. Agreeing to a pause makes a medication decision, even though the coaching is sound. Halving the dose is a change to treatment outside the physiotherapist's role. Suggesting exercise replaces the inhaler is a misconception that risks deterioration.
Case CHB15

Dorothy Hargreaves, 82, fell at home three days ago landing on her right side and was admitted with a right proximal humerus fracture (two-part surgical neck, minimally displaced). The orthopaedic team is managing it non-operatively in a sling, with pendulum exercises and elbow, wrist and hand movement allowed, and no other active or passive shoulder movement until the two-week review. History: osteoporosis, hypertension, mild cognitive impairment. She lives alone in a single-storey house and her daughter lives nearby. Today: pain 4/10 at rest, bruising tracking down the upper arm, radial pulse present, sensation intact, hand warm and pink with good grip. She walks 20 metres with a frame and supervision. Timed Up and Go 22 seconds. The ward is reviewing how it measures outcomes for fall-related admissions.

  1. The correct answer is A (Gentle pendulum swings, plus active elbow, wrist and hand exercises taken out of the sling): Early distal mobilisation limits swelling and stiffness in the elbow, wrist and hand, and pendulum movement uses gravity without muscle pull on the healing surgical neck. Active-assisted elevation, rotator cuff loading and passive stretching all stress the fracture before the orthopaedic team has cleared them, risking displacement. Progression follows the review plan.
  2. The correct answer is B (Withhold exercise, support the arm and urgently inform the medical team of possible vascular compromise): A cool, pale hand with a weaker pulse, new pins and needles and increasing swelling suggests vascular compromise after a proximal humerus fracture, which can threaten the limb within hours. Exercise stops, the arm is supported and the medical team is told urgently. Elevation and a short wait use time the limb may not have. Loosening the sling is sensible but raising it at the ward round delays the response. Repeating observations first adds a delay before anyone with authority to act is told.
  3. The correct answer is D (Mobility is impaired and falls risk is raised, so a falls assessment should precede discharge): The Timed Up and Go is valid with the person's usual walking aid, and a slow time points to impaired mobility and raised falls risk. Because Mrs Hargreaves fell at home and will return to living alone, a structured falls risk assessment and balance review should inform discharge. Attributing the time to the arm alone ignores her fall history.
  4. The correct answer is C (Proven validity in older inpatients, quick administration and consistent use by all clinicians): Quality improvement data are useful when the measure is valid for the population being audited, quick enough to complete in a busy ward and used the same way by every clinician. Individualised scoring suits patient care but makes results hard to compare across patients and over time. A long battery gives richness but is rarely completed for every patient. Reliability in young healthy adults does not show that the measure works for older inpatients.

Practice Test 2 Answer Key

Case CHB16

Mrs Lan Pham, 72, retired seamstress, is day 5 after a right total hip replacement (posterolateral approach) for osteoarthritis and has been transferred to subacute rehabilitation. History: type 2 diabetes and hypertension. She lives alone in a single storey house in a regional town, and her daughter lives 3 hours away. Observations: BP 128/76, HR 74, afebrile. The wound is clean and dry, with mild thigh swelling and a soft, non tender calf. Right hip flexion is active assisted to 70 degrees, abduction 3/5, knee extension 4/5. She walks 20 metres with a frame, weight bearing as tolerated, and needs prompting about her hip precautions. Pain is 3/10. Discharge is planned in 5 days. She has hospital cover only (no extras), has used her five Medicare allied health items this year under a diabetes plan, and asks how she will pay for physiotherapy at home.

  1. The correct answer is A (Sit on a raised chair, use a sock aid, and dress the operated leg first): After a posterior approach, hip flexion past about 90 degrees, adduction past the midline and internal rotation put the joint at risk of dislocation, so equipment that avoids reaching is used. Dressing the operated leg first needs less hip movement while the other leg is still free to support. Crossing the legs or leaning forward breaks the precautions.
  2. The correct answer is A (Keep her still, avoid moving the limb, and urgently alert the medical team on duty): A pop, a shortened and internally rotated limb and inability to weight bear after a posterolateral replacement strongly suggest prosthetic dislocation. She should be kept still and the medical team alerted urgently for assessment and imaging. Straightening the leg moves an unstable joint and informing staff only at the end of the session delays care. Ice and reassessment lose time. Attempting to relocate the hip is outside the physiotherapist's role and risks nerve or vessel injury.
  3. The correct answer is C (Standing hip abduction with a resistance band, trunk kept upright, and not crossing the midline): Standing abduction against a band loads the abductors in a closed, functional position that does not require flexion past ninety degrees, adduction past neutral or internal rotation. The other options each push the hip into a precaution-breaking position, even though they target hip muscles.
  4. The correct answer is D (Arrange a My Aged Care assessment to access subsidised home based allied health services): People over 65 access government-subsidised in-home support through a My Aged Care assessment. New NDIS access generally requires entry before 65 and a permanent disability, and a surgical recovery does not qualify. Medicare items are capped each year, and hospital-only cover does not pay for outpatient physiotherapy.
Case CHB17

Folake Adeyemi, 38, primary school teacher, has relapsing remitting multiple sclerosis diagnosed five years ago. Three weeks ago she had a relapse with right leg weakness and unsteadiness, treated by her neurologist with intravenous corticosteroids. She now reports fatigue that builds through the day, and her legs feel heavier in hot weather. Findings: right hip flexion 4/5 and ankle dorsiflexion 4-/5, reduced joint position sense at the right great toe, unsteady Romberg with eyes closed, Berg Balance Scale 41/56, Timed Up and Go 14 seconds. One near fall on stairs. No spasticity. She wants to return to a gym class. The clinic manager asks physiotherapists to adopt a standard set of outcome measures for a quality improvement project.

  1. The correct answer is A (Sensory ataxia, so train balance with vision available, progressing somatosensory challenges gradually): Marked deterioration with eyes closed plus impaired joint position sense and no cerebellar signs indicates a sensory ataxia from dorsal column involvement. Training with vision available keeps her safe while gradually challenging somatosensory input. Cerebellar or vestibular explanations lack supporting signs, and weakness alone does not account for the eyes-closed pattern.
  2. The correct answer is B (Begin with short moderate sessions earlier in the day, in a cool space, with rest breaks): Heavy legs that settle within an hour after exertion reflect temporary heat and fatigue effects, not new damage, so exercise remains beneficial when paced and cooled. Starting short and earlier in the day avoids the hot afternoon studio. Avoiding exercise is unnecessary, while intense or push-through exercise in the heat will worsen symptoms and fatigue.
  3. The correct answer is B (Re-test with the same measures, same conditions and time of day, and document the results consistently): Valid monitoring needs the same standardised measures repeated under similar conditions, and her fatigue builds through the day so time of day matters. Retesting at whatever time suits the clinic adds variation that can look like change. Testing only when she is rested gives results that do not reflect how she functions on a typical day. Changing the measure at each review breaks comparison with earlier scores, however sensitive the new tool may be.
  4. The correct answer is D (Raised body temperature temporarily slows conduction in damaged nerves, and reverses as she cools): Heat sensitivity in multiple sclerosis is a temporary conduction block in previously damaged nerves, so symptoms return to baseline once her temperature falls and no new lesion forms. A relapse involves new inflammation and lasts days to weeks. Blood pressure changes and infection can worsen symptoms but do not explain the typical hot shower pattern here.
Case CHB18

Tyson Walker, 22, apprentice electrician, Aboriginal man from regional New South Wales, fell from scaffolding ten days ago. Complete cervical tetraplegia (AIS A) after cervical fusion, wearing a collar, on the acute spinal unit and off the ventilator for four days. SpO2 96% on room air, RR 20, weak cough, audible secretions, reduced air entry at the left base. Lying BP 96/58, HR 58; indwelling urinary catheter. Elbow flexion 5/5, wrist extension 2/5, triceps 0/5, no movement and no sensation below C5. Shoulder pain 4/10. He is low in mood. Yesterday he declined chest physiotherapy. This morning he is drowsy after opioid analgesia, and his mother and the nurse want you to treat him now.

  1. The correct answer is B (C5: the elbow flexors are graded 3 or above and wrist extensors are below 3): The motor level is the lowest key muscle graded at least 3/5 provided the level above is normal. Elbow flexors (C5) meet this, while wrist extensors (C6) are graded 2/5, so the level is C5. Partial movement below 3/5 does not set the level, and the first abnormal muscle is not named as the level.
  2. The correct answer is B (Manually assisted cough, applying abdominal compression timed with expiration after a deep breath): With C5 tetraplegia the abdominal and intercostal muscles that produce a forceful cough are paralysed, so the therapist supplies the expiratory force with a timed abdominal compression. Techniques that depend on his own forced expiration, such as huffing or coughing hard, will be ineffective. Percussion alone moves secretions little when he cannot cough them out.
  3. The correct answer is D (Keep him upright, loosen binders and clothing, check the catheter, and call for help): This picture of severe hypertension, slow heart rate and flushing above the lesion is autonomic dysreflexia, a medical emergency in injuries at or above T6. Staying upright uses gravity to lower the pressure, and removing the likely trigger such as a tight garment or blocked catheter must happen while help is called. Lying him flat would raise the pressure further.
  4. The correct answer is C (Wait until he is alert, explain the options, check understanding, and obtain his own consent): Consent must be informed and given by a person with capacity, and drowsiness from opioids can temporarily impair that, so a casual do whatever is not valid consent. He is stable, so the discussion can wait until he is alert, and an adult with capacity decides for himself rather than his relatives. Treating his earlier refusal as fixed or handing the decision to the team also removes his voice.
Case CHB19

Bruno Rossi, 74, retired builder, five weeks after a right middle cerebral artery ischaemic stroke. You see him at home through a community rehabilitation programme. He lives with his wife Maria in a two-storey house with the bedroom upstairs. History: atrial fibrillation and hypertension; he takes an anticoagulant. Today: left ankle dorsiflexion 3/5, hip flexion 4/5, knee extension 4/5, mild left inattention. He walks with a single-point stick, with toe catch in left swing and knee hyperextension in left stance. Timed Up and Go 24 seconds, Berg Balance Scale 38/56. Resting HR 76 irregular, BP 135/80, SpO2 97 percent. Last week he had a near fall in the hallway when his stick slid on the floorboards; you recorded it only in the progress note. Goal: sleep upstairs again and walk to the letterbox.

  1. The correct answer is D (Task-specific walking practice with an ankle foot orthosis trial and stance knee control cues): Repeated, goal-directed walking practice is the best-supported way to improve gait after stroke, and an orthosis can improve foot clearance while strength recovers. Delaying walking until the ankle reaches full strength or bracing the knee rigidly removes the practice that drives recovery and does not address the stance control problem.
  2. The correct answer is B (Sleep downstairs for now, practise stairs with Maria guarding after training, reassess weekly): Fatigue, left inattention and an anticoagulant make an unsupervised fall on stairs high risk, so the safest step is a temporary downstairs arrangement with supervised, graded practice and a trained carer. Stopping stair work entirely ignores his goal, and solo practice or early return upstairs relies on him judging his own limits.
  3. The correct answer is D (Call 000 for urgent assessment, because a settled episode can come before another stroke): Transient focal symptoms in a person with atrial fibrillation and a recent stroke are a warning for recurrent stroke, so same-day emergency assessment is needed even though he looks back to baseline. Waiting for symptoms to return or booking a routine appointment delays treatment that is time critical.
  4. The correct answer is D (Submit an incident report through the employer's system and record the event factually): A fall during a session is a reportable incident whether or not injury is evident, and the anticoagulant raises the chance of delayed harm. Reporting through the employer's system, together with factual notes, supports learning and safety. Waiting for an injury or leaving the report to the family does not meet that duty.
Case CHB20

Darren Nowak, 52, electrician, accepted WorkCover claim. Fell from a ladder five weeks ago onto his outstretched right arm. Night pain 6/10, worse with overhead work. History: hypertension, otherwise well. Examination: active elevation 70 degrees with shoulder hitching, passive elevation 150 degrees. Supraspinatus strength 2/5 with pain, positive external rotation lag sign and positive drop arm sign. Neck movement and upper limb sensation normal. GP-ordered ultrasound: full thickness supraspinatus tear, 2 cm retraction. The insurer case manager has approved six sessions. Darren works at heights and must return to full duties within three months or face redeployment. He is anxious about surgery. His GP asks what evidence supports exercise instead of surgery, and the clinic manager wants a published shoulder guideline followed.

  1. The correct answer is C (Structural cuff failure with loss of active elevation, not a stiff or neurologically driven shoulder): Active elevation with hitching and lag signs, despite near full passive range and normal neurology, point to loss of cuff continuity and active control. A stiff shoulder would limit passive range, and a root lesion would usually change neck movement or sensation. Instability does not explain a positive drop arm sign.
  2. The correct answer is A (Education, activity modification and a graded home programme of scapular and cuff loading within symptoms): A full thickness tear with preserved passive range can be managed first with education, load modification and graded exercise, which also fits the six approved sessions. Passive stretching of a joint that already moves well adds little. Repeat imaging and an injection add little at this stage, and deferring all exercise wastes time, although surgical referral may become appropriate if he does not improve. Electrotherapy and rest alone do not build the strength he needs for ladder work.
  3. The correct answer is D (Recommend an orthopaedic opinion via the GP, explaining that function is below job demands despite adherence): Persisting lag signs and a job that needs overhead work mean function is not meeting demands despite adherence. A surgical opinion arranged through the GP is warranted while a traumatic tear may still be repairable. More of the same programme or heavier loading delays that decision, and discharge ignores the unmet work goal.
  4. The correct answer is A (Appraise its quality and relevance, then combine it with Darren's findings, preferences and recent evidence): Guidelines inform but do not replace judgement: their quality, age and fit to this patient must be appraised and combined with recent evidence and Darren's preferences. Applying them blindly ignores the mismatched population, while discarding them wastes useful synthesis. The insurer's role is to fund care, not to decide treatment.
Case CHB21

Colin Murray, 58, a panel beater with hypertension, attends the emergency department with 4 days of brief episodes of spinning vertigo when he rolls over in bed or looks up, each lasting under a minute with nausea. No hearing loss, tinnitus, headache, neck pain, double vision, speech change or limb weakness. No recent head or neck trauma. BP 134/82 mmHg, HR 70, afebrile. The ED doctor excluded central causes and requested physiotherapy assessment. Neurological screen is normal and gait is steady. The Dix-Hallpike test to the right produces upbeating torsional nystagmus after a short delay, lasting about 20 seconds, with intense vertigo that settles on repeat testing. The left side is negative. The assessment is done on a shared ED trolley. When Colin was brought upright, the trolley moved because its brakes were off, and he slid toward the edge before the physiotherapist steadied him. He was not injured.

  1. The correct answer is C (Right posterior canal BPPV, treated with the Epley canalith repositioning manoeuvre): Upbeating torsional nystagmus with a latency, brief duration and fatigue on Dix-Hallpike to the right is the typical picture of right posterior canal BPPV, treated by the Epley manoeuvre to the same side. Horizontal canal BPPV produces horizontal nystagmus on a roll test, and a normal neurological screen with fatiguing findings does not suggest a central cause.
  2. The correct answer is A (Report the near miss in the incident system and ask for the trolley to be checked): Near misses are reported because they reveal system hazards, here a worn or unapplied brake, before someone is harmed, and reporting through the organisation's incident system allows review and equipment repair. Notes alone do not trigger investigation, verbal mentions leave no record, and waiting for harm defeats the purpose of reporting.
  3. The correct answer is A (Choose a variant that avoids neck extension, such as the Semont manoeuvre, with assistance): A canalith repositioning manoeuvre can be modified when neck extension is limited or painful. The Semont variant uses side lying and avoids the neck extension of the Hallpike position, and assistance helps protect safety. Forcing the standard position risks neck injury and distress, while delaying treatment or relying on habituation alone prolongs symptoms in a condition that usually responds well to repositioning.
  4. The correct answer is C (Resume usual activity with no postural restrictions, and return for reassessment if vertigo recurs): Current evidence does not support routine postural restrictions or collars after canalith repositioning, so normal activity is encouraged along with advice that BPPV can recur and that reassessment is available. Sleeping semi upright and wearing collars were older habits that add inconvenience without clear benefit. Specialist clearance is not needed after successful treatment, and safety advice about driving applies only while symptoms persist.
Case CHB22

Aarav Sharma, 5 months, born at 34 weeks, is in the paediatric intensive care unit with RSV bronchiolitis. He was extubated yesterday after three days of ventilation and is now on high flow nasal cannula. Chest X-ray this morning shows right upper lobe collapse. Observations: RR 58, HR 164, SpO2 93 percent on FiO2 0.4, temperature 37.2 degrees Celsius, moderate subcostal recession, minimal pain behaviours. Auscultation: reduced air entry over the right upper zone with transmitted upper airway sounds. He is fed by nasogastric tube and the last feed finished 30 minutes ago. His parents are at the bedside. A nurse is assigned to him and the medical team has requested physiotherapy for the collapse.

  1. The correct answer is C (Left side lying, right side uppermost, supported with rolls and the head slightly elevated): The affected lung is placed uppermost so that ventilation to it is favoured, and slight head elevation reduces reflux risk in an infant after a feed. Putting the collapsed lobe dependent reduces its expansion. Head down tilt is avoided in infants because of reflux and increased work of breathing.
  2. The correct answer is B (Ask for the stomach to be aspirated, then treat with positioning and gentle manual techniques): A recent feed raises the risk of reflux, vomiting and aspiration during handling, so the stomach is emptied first and the treatment uses low risk techniques. Treating straight after a feed in head down tilt is hazardous. A fixed long delay is unnecessary and leaves a collapse untreated.
  3. The correct answer is B (Stop treatment, settle him in a supported position, reassess airway and breathing, and call for help): Marked desaturation with tachycardia, pallor and increasing recession signals deterioration, so treatment stops and he is made comfortable while airway and breathing are checked and the team is called. Adjusting oxygen settings is outside the physiotherapist's role without medical direction. Continuing or suctioning blindly risks worsening distress.
  4. The correct answer is C (Ensure his safety, inform the nurse, be open with the parents, and complete an incident report): After securing the infant, the event is communicated to the responsible nurse, disclosed honestly to the family, and entered in the incident system, which is the duty of any staff member who was involved or witnessed it. Tube replacement is nursing work outside physiotherapy scope. A chart note alone does not allow the organisation to learn from the incident.
Case CHB23

Tanya Walker, 42, Aboriginal woman and part time community worker, is seen by telehealth from her local Aboriginal Community Controlled Health Service, 300 km from the nearest hospital. An Aboriginal health worker is in the room with her. Ten weeks ago she had COVID-19, managed at home without hospital admission. Since then she reports breathlessness on stairs, daily fatigue, poor concentration and disturbed sleep. Past history: mild asthma, reliever inhaler only. Resting observations taken by the health worker: SpO2 97 percent, HR 82, RR 16. She has not been back to work. She says she feels uneasy talking to health professionals she has not met. Referral from her GP asks for assessment and an exercise plan.

  1. The correct answer is D (Welcome her sister, introduce yourself, explain the purpose and let her set the pace): Culturally safe practice begins with relationship: introduce yourself and your role, welcome family or support people, explain why you are asking and let the patient guide the pace and who is present. Welcoming her sister is right, but going straight into set questions skips the explanation and the control she needs. Sending her sister out removes support she has chosen. Asking consent and keeping things brief is courteous, but it does not answer her request or build trust.
  2. The correct answer is A (Reduced exertional capacity with a modest saturation fall above 90 percent, suitable for monitored, paced activity): A modest exertional fall in saturation that stays above 90 percent, with a heart rate rise and leg fatigue, indicates reduced exercise capacity rather than dangerous hypoxaemia, and it supports monitored, paced activity. Urgent referral is not warranted. Calling the capacity normal ignores her symptoms and the fall, and attributing everything to deconditioning ignores her fatigue pattern.
  3. The correct answer is C (Start with short paced bouts below her symptom threshold, increasing only if no delayed worsening follows): When symptoms worsen a day or two after activity, exercise should be paced below the symptom threshold and progressed only if no delayed flare follows. Push-through targets and high intensity intervals risk repeated setbacks. Stopping all activity until she recovers causes further deconditioning and does not match current post-COVID rehabilitation principles.
  4. The correct answer is D (Stop the session, have the health worker check observations and arrange urgent medical review): Sudden pleuritic chest pain with a racing heart and lightheadedness weeks after COVID 19 may indicate pulmonary embolism or another serious cause, so the session stops and urgent medical assessment is arranged through the on site health worker. Breathing exercises first use up time that matters when the hospital is 300 kilometres away. The GP review within the week is far too slow for this presentation. Normal observations do not exclude a serious cause, so continuing the session is unsafe.
Case CHB24

Margaret Thompson, 81, widowed, lives alone in a two-storey home in outer suburban Adelaide. Two falls in six months, no fractures. Referred by her GP to a community physiotherapy service. History: osteoarthritis of both knees, hypertension, mild osteoporosis. Takes an antihypertensive and a calcium supplement. Uses no walking aid. She fears falling and has cut back her outings. Assessment: seated BP 128/76, standing BP after one minute 124/74, no dizziness. Timed Up and Go 16 seconds. 30 second sit to stand: 7 repetitions without using her arms. Berg Balance Scale 44/56. Knee extension strength 4/5 bilaterally, knee pain 3/10 on stairs. Single leg stance not held beyond 2 seconds. Home hazards include loose rugs and a poorly lit staircase with one handrail. The service is reviewing its falls program and asks clinicians to record standardised outcome measures at intake and discharge.

  1. The correct answer is C (Progressive balance and functional strength training, challenging stability gradually, at sufficient weekly dose): Exercise that progressively challenges balance and includes strengthening, at an adequate dose, has the strongest evidence for reducing falls in community-dwelling older adults. Walking alone does not train balance or strength enough to change her risk. Seated or heat-based programs would not load her stability systems, and mild knee pain on stairs is not a reason to delay.
  2. The correct answer is D (Pause standing exercise, record the readings and contact her GP promptly for review): A symptomatic postural drop with near fainting is a medical finding that needs prompt review of causes such as medication, so exercise is paused and the GP informed. Counter manoeuvres and slower transitions can be added later but do not address the cause or the immediate risk. Continuing the session, even with supervision, exposes her to a fall.
  3. The correct answer is D (Advise removing rugs and brighter lighting now, and refer to occupational therapy for home assessment): Rugs and poor lighting are low-cost hazards that can be fixed immediately, while structural changes such as rails need a trained home assessment, so both actions run together as part of a multifactorial falls approach. A checklist alone leaves her unsupported and delays action. Treating the home as someone else's job, or waiting for funding, leaves avoidable hazards in place.
  4. The correct answer is C (Repeat identical measures in the same conditions and pool de-identified change scores across clients): Valid change scores need the same measure repeated under the same conditions, and pooling de-identified results across clients is how a service audits its outcomes. Comparing one person with norms does not evaluate a program. Reporting only the best result biases the findings, and satisfaction does not measure falls risk.
Case CHB25

Hamid Rahimi, 29, accountant. Referred by his GP for chronic low back pain that began gradually 14 months ago without injury. Pain is deep in the lower back and alternates between buttocks, worst at night, waking him around 4 am. Morning stiffness lasts about 90 minutes. Pain and stiffness ease after a walk or gym session and return when he sits for long periods or rests. He takes naproxen from his GP with partial relief. The GP letter notes HLA-B27 positive and an MRI report of bilateral sacroiliac joint inflammation. A rheumatology appointment is booked in three weeks. Examination: neurology normal, straight leg raise negative. Modified Schober increase 2 cm, chest expansion 2 cm, occiput to wall distance 5 cm, cervical rotation reduced bilaterally. BASDAI score 6 out of 10. Seen in a private practice setting.

  1. The correct answer is A (Stiffness and pain that settle with activity and return when he sits or rests): Inflammatory back pain improves with movement and worsens with rest, and it often brings prolonged morning stiffness and night waking. Mechanical pain behaves the opposite way, easing with rest and worsening with load. Pain that eases when leaning forward suggests a neurogenic cause and does not fit Hamid's history.
  2. The correct answer is A (Daily mobility and extension exercises with breathing work, progressed as symptoms allow): Regular movement and extension work improves stiffness and posture and is a mainstay for suspected axial spondyloarthritis, so waiting for a confirmed diagnosis only prolongs stiffness. Manipulating stiff segments carries risk and does not address the inflammatory process. Flexion biased positions encourage the stooped posture this condition tends to produce.
  3. The correct answer is D (Keep his neck supported and still, and arrange urgent emergency department imaging for possible fracture): A stiff, fused spine behaves like a long bone, so a heavy fall with new severe neck pain can mean an unstable fracture even with intact neurology and normal early X-rays can miss it. The neck is kept supported and still and he is sent for urgent emergency department imaging. A collar with a GP visit tomorrow and a routine X-ray this week both leave a possible unstable fracture unprotected and under-investigated. Rest with heat and a GP visit this week is slower still.
  4. The correct answer is B (Baseline measures, symptom pattern, exercise response, and a clear question for the rheumatologist): A concise handover with objective baselines such as mobility measures and the BASDAI, the pattern of symptoms, response to exercise and a specific question helps the specialist act quickly. Diagnosing or advising on medicines lies outside physiotherapy scope. Sending everything or too little both weaken communication.
Case CHB26

Ms Siosaia Taufa, 27, a plumber, fell from a ladder two days ago and has fractures of right ribs 5 to 8 with a traumatic pneumothorax. An intercostal chest drain with an underwater seal was inserted yesterday and suction was ceased this morning. The drain fluid swings with breathing and bubbles only when she coughs. Pain is 7 out of 10 on deep breathing despite analgesia. RR 20, HR 88, SpO2 95% on room air. Air entry is slightly reduced on the right. This morning's chest X-ray shows the lung mostly expanded with a small apical pneumothorax. She is anxious but cooperative. During treatment she says she enjoys talking with you, asks for your personal mobile number and offers to pay you privately for sessions after discharge.

  1. The correct answer is D (The drain is patent, and a small leak on coughing is expected while the lung re-expands): Fluid swinging with breathing shows the tube is open and connected to the pleural space, and brief bubbling only with a cough fits a small residual air leak while the lung re-expands. Stripping the tube is unnecessary and can harm. Continuous bubbling would suggest a persistent leak, and the imaging still shows a small pneumothorax, so removal is a medical decision not yet due.
  2. The correct answer is D (Carry the bottle upright below chest level, with the tubing unclamped and free of kinks): The bottle must stay upright and below the level of the chest so air and fluid drain one way, and the tubing must remain open so a leak cannot build pressure in the pleural space. Clamping can cause a tension pneumothorax if air is still leaking, and disconnecting breaks the sealed system and risks contamination.
  3. The correct answer is B (Sit her down, reconnect the tubing promptly, observe her breathing and call the nurse): Restoring the closed drainage system quickly, while the end is clean and intact, limits air entering or building up, and sitting her down with observation and a call for help covers her breathlessness. Clamping can trap air from a leaking lung. Leaving the system open on a gauze, or walking back first, prolongs the open connection.
  4. The correct answer is D (Thank her, decline to share personal details, and arrange outpatient care through the hospital): Professional boundaries protect a patient who is still in your care, so personal contact details are not shared and her ongoing care is directed to the hospital referral pathway. Private arrangements while she is an inpatient create a conflict between her care and your interests. Declining without explanation or recording the event removes the transparency a boundary issue needs.
Case CHB27

Mrs Lan Nguyen, 74, retired seamstress, speaks Vietnamese and very little English. She is on day 3 after a right basal ganglia intracerebral haemorrhage on an acute stroke ward. Medical orders: head of bed elevated to 30 degrees, regular neurological observations, and no activity that provokes straining until cleared. History: hypertension, type 2 diabetes. Findings: left upper limb strength 2/5, left lower limb 3/5, left facial droop, and reduced attention to the left on cancellation tasks. HR 78, BP 138/82, RR 16, SpO2 96 percent on room air, pain 2/10. Her son offers to interpret and says she is tired and 'does not want to be moved'. The ward has telephone interpreter access, but no interpreter has been booked this admission. The physiotherapist plans a first session.

  1. The correct answer is B (Book a professional telephone or in-person interpreter and use the son only for emotional support): A trained interpreter gives accurate, impartial translation and protects confidentiality, which a relative who holds his own worries cannot reliably do. Valid consent and trustworthy neurological observations both depend on that accuracy. Nodding or picture boards alone do not establish informed consent, and delaying care until she learns English denies her access to rehabilitation.
  2. The correct answer is C (Use slide sheets with two staff, giving a clear cue to breathe out during the move): Slide sheets lower friction and staff effort, and a cue to exhale prevents a breath-hold that would raise intracranial pressure. The head of bed stays elevated to support venous drainage. Bridging demands effortful pushing and holding breath, and flattening the bed even briefly raises pressure, so both fail here.
  3. The correct answer is A (Stop the session, keep her head elevated and call the medical emergency team immediately): Falling consciousness, a dilating pupil, rising blood pressure and a slowing heart rate suggest rising intracranial pressure and possible herniation, which needs an immediate emergency call. Keeping her head elevated supports venous drainage while help arrives. Lying her flat raises pressure. Asking the nurse in charge to review her soon is too slow and may not bring the team that can act. Repeating observations for ten minutes before calling wastes time she does not have.
  4. The correct answer is D (Left hemispatial neglect, so cue her to scan left and place key items within her view): Full visual fields with failure to attend to the left side indicates hemispatial neglect, common after right hemisphere stroke, so staff should cue scanning and keep important items visible. Hemianopia would be a field loss that patients usually learn to compensate for by looking toward it. Sensory loss and aphasia do not explain ignoring half of the tray when fields and sensation are otherwise intact.
Case CHB28

Tevita Fifita, 16, a Tongan Australian schoolboy, is on day 9 after bilateral sequential lung transplant for cystic fibrosis, through a clamshell incision. He is on the transplant ward receiving 2 L/min oxygen by nasal prongs. Yesterday he was afebrile with SpO2 96 percent, RR 18 and HR 88. Today before treatment: SpO2 95 percent on 2 L/min, RR 20, HR 96, temperature 37.4. Immunosuppression includes tacrolimus, mycophenolate and prednisolone. Incision pain is 4 out of 10 and the intercostal catheters have been removed. He walks 100 m with a four wheeled frame and one rest. His cough is weak and non-productive. His mother is staying with him. Tevita enjoys your playlists and has several times asked for your personal mobile number and social media account so he can send you videos of his exercise.

  1. The correct answer is C (Active cycle breathing with huffs, supporting the incision with a folded towel when coughing): Huffing within an active cycle of breathing clears secretions with less pain and less pressure on a fresh transverse sternotomy, and manual support of the wound makes coughing tolerable. Forceful repeated coughing stresses the repair, and percussion over a healing incision is inappropriate. Suction is reserved for patients who cannot clear secretions with these methods.
  2. The correct answer is C (Decline kindly, offer hospital approved channels for videos, and inform your supervisor of the request): A young patient with a long term illness can form a strong attachment, and personal contact outside approved channels blurs the therapeutic boundary even when a parent agrees. A kind explanation, a safe alternative and a supervisor conversation protect both parties and keep the relationship supportive. Avoiding the question leaves him confused and the issue unmanaged. A work number or clinic page used outside hospital approved channels, with no supervisor aware, still breaches the boundary. Accepting contact only while he is an inpatient sets up a sudden loss of the relationship after discharge.
  3. The correct answer is D (Limit heavy arm loading and weight bearing until the surgical team confirms bony union): Bone through the sternum takes weeks to unite, and heavy pushing, pulling or weight bearing through the arms stresses the repair, so loading is limited until the surgeon confirms healing. Pain settling or skin healing are not reliable markers of bone union. Light range of motion and walking can be encouraged early.
  4. The correct answer is D (Stop treatment, sit him up with oxygen as prescribed, and urgently notify the transplant team): Fever, new hypoxaemia and tachypnoea in an immunosuppressed transplant recipient could mean infection or acute rejection, both needing urgent medical assessment. The physiotherapist should stop and escalate rather than treat the chest or adjust oxygen alone. Waiting for handover or delaying review risks rapid deterioration.
Case CHB29

Sione Faleolo, 9, has oligoarticular juvenile idiopathic arthritis affecting his right knee and left ankle, diagnosed 14 months ago. He is antinuclear antibody positive and is managed by a paediatric rheumatologist at the children's hospital, with medication reviewed every three months. You are the visiting physiotherapist at his primary school. Findings today: mild right knee effusion, 10 degrees loss of full extension, quadriceps wasting, a mild limp, and 30 minutes of morning stiffness that eases with movement. Pain is 3 out of 10. He is keen on football. His teacher, Mr Kelly, wants a school plan and the principal asks that it be based on evidence. Sione's mother brings a news article about a small study of a dietary supplement that reported reduced joint swelling in children with arthritis, and asks whether Sione should stop his medication and try it.

  1.
