The correct answer is C (Chronic anterior uveitis, which is often painless and needs regular ophthalmology screening): Chronic anterior uveitis is strongly linked to oligoarticular juvenile idiopathic arthritis, especially with positive antinuclear antibodies, and is often silent until vision is damaged, so scheduled eye screening is essential. The other problems are real but belong mainly to systemic disease or other presentations, so they are not the best match for his subtype.
  2. The correct answer is B (Daily gentle extension stretches and quadriceps strengthening, with paced participation in low impact sport): With mild inflammation and low pain, gentle range work and quadriceps strengthening restore function, and low impact participation keeps him active and included. Complete exclusion from activity increases deconditioning and muscle wasting. Heavy loading and forceful overpressure stress an effused joint and risk a flare.
  3. The correct answer is A (Send him to the emergency department today to exclude septic arthritis before assuming a flare): A hot joint with fever, sudden severe pain and refusal to bear weight is septic arthritis until proven otherwise, a time critical emergency that can destroy the joint. His arthritis history makes a flare plausible, which is why it is tempting, but infection must be excluded first. Delaying for a GP or rheumatology visit, or beginning exercise, would risk permanent damage.
  4. The correct answer is D (Explain the study's limits against guideline evidence and suggest raising it with the rheumatologist): A small study with no comparison group cannot show the supplement works, so it ranks low beside systematic reviews and clinical guidelines. The family should be told this respectfully and encouraged to take the question to the rheumatologist, since stopping disease-modifying medication risks joint damage and eye disease. Endorsing an unproven supplement and informing the specialist later skips the appraisal the mother asked for. Supporting a medication change and monitoring at school puts a decision that belongs to the rheumatologist onto the physiotherapist. A pharmacist referral recognises scope, but it leaves the evidence unexplained and bypasses the treating specialist.
Case CHB30

Jordan Pappas, 27, is a barista admitted to the acute respiratory ward with a pulmonary exacerbation of cystic fibrosis (homozygous F508del, pancreatic insufficient, CF-related diabetes on insulin). He is chronically colonised with Pseudomonas aeruginosa and has a PICC for intravenous antibiotics. He reports three days of increased cough, thick green sputum and tiredness. Observations: RR 22, HR 98, SpO2 94 percent on room air, temperature 37.6 degrees C, BMI 19. Auscultation: coarse crackles in both lower zones with scattered expiratory wheeze. FEV1 is 55 percent predicted (usual baseline 68 percent). He normally runs three times a week and wants to keep exercising in hospital. He is in a single room with contact precautions. His mother, who lives interstate, has phoned the ward asking for his test results and treatment plan. Jordan has not told staff whether she may receive information.

  1. The correct answer is B (Active cycle of breathing technique with positive expiratory pressure as needed, taught for independent use): Active cycle of breathing technique, with positive expiratory pressure if it helps, is an evidence-supported independent method that suits adults with cystic fibrosis and lets him treat himself between visits. Head-down tilt percussion is outdated and depends on the therapist, while repeated huffing without breath control can provoke bronchospasm and fatigue. Insufflation-exsufflation targets a weak cough, which he does not have.
  2. The correct answer is A (Supervised moderate aerobic and resistance exercise, monitoring SpO2, symptoms and blood glucose, with fast-acting carbohydrate available): Exercise is a core part of cystic fibrosis care and is useful during an exacerbation when supervised and adjusted to symptoms. Insulin-treated diabetes means glucose can fall during exertion, so monitoring and carbohydrate are needed. Restricting him to bed work or low intensity walking removes a beneficial component, and unsupervised high intensity intervals add risk of desaturation and hypoglycaemia.
  3. The correct answer is B (Stop and seat him, coach recovery breathing, recheck SpO2, and report the desaturation to the team): A fall to 88 percent from 94 with a Borg score of 6 means he should stop, sit and recover with controlled breathing. The team then needs to know, because exertional desaturation may need oxygen prescribed for exercise. Continuing risks further desaturation, and setting an oxygen flow is a medical or nursing decision under local protocol. Stopping exercise for the whole admission is excessive for an episode that settles with rest.
  4. The correct answer is B (Decline to share details yet, ask Jordan about consent, and document the call and his decision): An adult with capacity decides who receives his health information, and being next of kin gives no automatic right to it under Australian privacy requirements and Physiotherapy Board expectations. Asking Jordan first respects his autonomy, and documenting the call and his decision keeps the record accurate. Releasing partial details or sending her to another clinician still treats the request as acceptable without his consent.

Practice Test 3 Answer Key

Case CHB31

Mei-Ling Chen, 72, retired seamstress, is on day 2 after coronary artery bypass grafting (three grafts) through a median sternotomy, with saphenous vein harvested from the left leg. History: type 2 diabetes, hypertension, ex-smoker. Chest drains were removed this morning. Observations: RR 22, HR 88 sinus, BP 132/70 mmHg, SpO2 93 percent on 2 L/min nasal prongs, temperature 37.4 degrees Celsius. Auscultation: reduced air entry at both bases, no other added sounds. Her cough is weak and she suppresses it because of sternal pain, rated 6/10 with coughing. Pain relief was last given two hours ago. She is alert and sitting in a chair with her daughter present; English is her second language. You have six patients on your list this morning and about 45 minutes before ward rounds at 11:00.

  1. The correct answer is D (Ask nursing about timing of analgesia, then teach deep breathing and a pillow-supported huff and cough): Atelectasis at the bases is likely, and a supported huff and cough works best when analgesia is timed so pain does not suppress it. Deep breathing with sternal support reopens basal lung units. Percussion over a fresh sternotomy and leg wound adds discomfort without addressing the cause, and discouraging coughing leaves secretions retained.
  2. The correct answer is B (Hug a pillow to the chest, lean forward, and rise using the legs, not the arms): Pushing or pulling through the arms loads the sternum through the pectoral muscles and can stress the healing bone. Hugging a pillow splints the chest while the legs do the lifting, which is the usual teaching in the early weeks. Pushing on armrests or thighs still puts the load through the arms and shoulder girdle, and a pillow alone does not offset that. Being pulled up by the hands drags the arms forward and strains the sternum, even with the chest splinted.
  3. The correct answer is A (Stop upper limb and sternal loading, support the chest, and report promptly to the surgical team): A palpable click with new sternal pain suggests sternal instability, which needs prompt surgical assessment because dehiscence can follow. Stopping loading, splinting the chest and reporting promptly is the safe response. A tight binder or continued mobilisation can worsen movement at the bone ends, and a dry wound with a normal temperature does not exclude instability.
  4. The correct answer is C (See the deteriorating patient first, then Mrs Chen, and defer the discharge walking assessment): A patient with new increased work of breathing and falling saturation is at risk of deterioration and needs prompt assessment and escalation, so goes first. Mrs Chen has a real need but is stable on oxygen, and the discharge assessment can wait without harm. Assessing an unwell patient is not a task suited to delegation to an assistant, who works within a predictable delegated role.
Case CHB32

Ahmed Hassan, 28, retail assistant, has had asthma since childhood and wants to join a social running group. He reports cough, chest tightness and wheeze starting about 5 to 10 minutes after finishing runs, worse in cold, dry morning air and settling in about 30 minutes. He takes a daily preventer inhaler and a salbutamol inhaler as needed, which he uses without a spacer. Resting spirometry today is normal. Resting HR 74, RR 14, SpO2 98% on room air, chest clear. He has a mild intellectual disability and attends with his mother, who answers most questions and says she will sign the consent form for the planned supervised graded exercise session. Ahmed nods, can describe in his own words what the session involves, and says he wants to do it.

  1. The correct answer is A (Airway narrowing peaking shortly after exercise stops, worsened by breathing cold, dry air): Exercise induced bronchoconstriction typically develops after exercise ends, as airway cooling and drying trigger narrowing, and it responds to a reliever. Cold, dry air makes it worse. The pattern is not an early-run problem that clears with continued effort, nor a delayed reaction hours later, and normal resting spirometry does not exclude it.
  2. The correct answer is B (Use a graded warm up with brief hard efforts and his planned pre-exercise reliever): A graded warm up with short intense bursts can produce a refractory period that lessens bronchoconstriction, and a pre-exercise reliever as prescribed adds protection. A long gentle jog lacks the brief hard efforts that give this effect, and waiting for symptoms repeats his current problem. A scarf warms and humidifies the air and helps, but using the reliever only afterwards leaves him unprotected during the run. The preventer works on a daily schedule and does not replace a warm up or the planned reliever before exercise.
  3. The correct answer is D (Sit him upright and help him take four reliever puffs through a spacer): Standard asthma first aid is to stop activity, sit the person upright and give reliever puffs one at a time through a spacer, then reassess and escalate if there is no improvement. Lying flat and delaying medication allows worsening, and restarting exercise during symptoms is unsafe. Withholding the reliever while awaiting paramedics removes the most effective early treatment.
  4. The correct answer is C (Presume capacity, explain risks in plain language to Ahmed, and obtain his own consent): Adults are presumed to have capacity, and a disability alone does not remove it. The physiotherapist must give information he can understand, including the risk of provoked wheeze and how to stop, and seek his own consent, with his mother supporting if he wishes. A relative cannot consent for a capable adult, and a formal assessment is needed only if real doubt about his decision-making arises.
Case CHB33

Thanh Nguyen, 71, a retired factory worker, attends community rehabilitation with six weeks of left neck pain spreading to the lateral arm and thumb, with tingling in the thumb and index finger. Symptoms are worse looking up and turning left, and sleep is disturbed. He has hypertension, treated by his GP. He speaks very little English and the centre has booked a telephone Vietnamese interpreter. Examination: cervical extension and left rotation are limited and reproduce arm pain, Spurling's test is positive on the left, manual distraction relieves the arm pain, and the upper limb tension test is positive. Left biceps and wrist extensor strength is 4/5, biceps reflex is reduced, and light touch is reduced over the left thumb and index finger. Gait is normal, with no hand clumsiness. Neck Disability Index is 46 percent. His son attends and offers to translate. Thanh wants to avoid surgery if possible.

  1. The correct answer is B (The C6 root, supplying biceps, wrist extensors and the thumb and index finger): C6 supplies the biceps and brachioradialis reflex area, wrist extension and sensation over the thumb and index finger, which matches Thanh's findings. C5 mainly affects shoulder abduction and lateral upper arm sensation. C7 and C8 would show triceps weakness or finger flexor and ulnar side sensory changes.
  2. The correct answer is D (Education, positions of relief, gentle mobility, and deep neck flexor and scapular exercises within tolerance): Initial care for cervical radiculopathy without myelopathy is education, relieving positions, gentle mobility and progressive deep neck flexor and scapular exercise within symptom tolerance. Traction can ease arm pain in the short term, but relying on a passive treatment does not build the active self-management he needs. His deficits are mild and he has no cord signs, so surgical assessment is a later step after conservative care has been tried. A collar and rest reduce provocation briefly, but they do not restore function and can weaken the neck muscles.
  3. The correct answer is C (Stop cervical loading and arrange urgent medical referral for suspected cervical myelopathy with spinal cord involvement): Hand clumsiness, gait disturbance, hyperreflexia and a positive Hoffmann's sign indicate upper motor neuron involvement, which suggests cervical myelopathy and needs urgent medical assessment. Unchanged arm pain does not change that decision. Recognising the problem but referring within the week, or after a repeat examination, delays treatment of a condition that can cause permanent cord damage. The signs are objective and bilateral, so an interpreter does not make a repeat test necessary. Discussing with a senior colleague is sensible but does not replace an urgent medical referral.
  4. The correct answer is D (Use the professional interpreter for assessment and consent, and invite the son to stay as support): Accredited interpreters give accurate, impartial translation and protect confidentiality, which family members cannot always do, so they should be used for assessment, consent and teaching. The son can still attend as support. Using him for instructions risks errors in exercise safety advice, and sheets with gestures cannot confirm understanding.
Case CHB34

Harold Pemberton, 81, retired bus driver, is on day 4 of an admission for urosepsis and is now afebrile on oral antibiotics. History: hypertension, type 2 diabetes, benign prostatic hyperplasia. Medications include ramipril, amlodipine and tamsulosin. Oral intake has been poor and he is on a fluid chart. Yesterday he felt faint when standing at the bedside with the nurse. The medical order reads mobilise as tolerated. Today: lying BP 138/78 mmHg, HR 84; after three minutes standing BP 104/66, HR 88, light-headed and pale. SpO2 96 percent on room air, RR 16. Haemoglobin 105 g/L. Hip flexion and knee extension 4 out of 5 bilaterally. Barthel Index 60 (95 before admission). He lives with his wife in a single storey home. A junior colleague says bed rest is safer for older inpatients, quoting a textbook, while you have found a recent systematic review of early mobilisation in older hospitalised adults.

  1. The correct answer is C (Symptomatic orthostatic hypotension, so mobilise in monitored stages and inform the medical team of contributors): A fall in systolic pressure with symptoms on standing, plus dehydration and vasoactive medications, fits symptomatic orthostatic hypotension. Safe practice is staged upright positioning with repeated observations and communication with the team about fluids and medications. Treating it as normal ageing ignores a documented near faint and a fall risk.
  2. The correct answer is C (Guide him to sit in the chair, then recline with legs raised and recheck observations): Pre syncope calls for getting the patient to a safe supported position quickly, and a nearby chair allows a controlled sit followed by recline and leg elevation to restore cerebral perfusion. Keeping him upright prolongs the reduced perfusion and raises fall risk. A floor lowering is reserved for when no safe seat is available.
  3. The correct answer is D (Staged sitting with ankle pumps, then standing and a short walk, taking observations at each stage): His improved readings support progression, but residual unsteadiness and three days of inactivity mean the staged approach with ankle pumps and repeated observations is still safest. Waiting for every contributor to resolve would deepen deconditioning, and skipping the stages relies on symptoms to detect a drop.
  4. The correct answer is C (Appraise its quality and relevance to him, then combine it with clinical findings and his preferences): Evidence based practice integrates appraised research with clinical expertise and the patient's values. A review still needs checking for quality and fit to this man, whose orthostatic findings shape how it is applied. Hierarchy alone does not decide applicability, and an old textbook or seniority is not a substitute for appraisal.
Case CHB35

Darren Kowalski, 58, was admitted two days ago with a productive cough, fever and right lower lobe consolidation. A respiratory viral swab is positive for influenza A, so droplet precautions are in place and the door sign is displayed. He is a retired electrician with type 2 diabetes. The ward physiotherapist has been asked to review his sputum retention.

Observations: T 37.9, HR 98, RR 22, SpO2 94 percent on 2 L oxygen via nasal prongs. Coarse crackles at the right base, cough weak and wet with thick yellow sputum he cannot clear. He is alert and mobilises to the chair with supervision.

Your list also includes Mrs Lau, 69, day 3 after hip replacement, who is not in isolation and needs her first mobilisation with a frame. Her nurse says she is comfortable and stable.

  1. The correct answer is D (See Mrs Lau first, then Mr Kowalski, performing hand hygiene and changing PPE according to precautions): Seeing patients who are not isolated before those on transmission based precautions is standard practice when clinical urgency does not dictate otherwise. Hand hygiene and PPE changes between patients are still required, and the isolated patient is still treated, not avoided. Delegating his airway clearance to an assistant would not suit a patient needing skilled assessment, and shared equipment spreads infection.
  2. The correct answer is D (Hand hygiene follows PPE removal, and wearing gloves does not replace it before the next patient): Gloves reduce but do not remove contamination, so hand hygiene is performed after glove and PPE removal and before the next patient contact. Alcohol based hand rub is the preferred method when hands are not visibly soiled, including after contact with a patient with influenza. Relying on gloves, on delayed hygiene or on cleaning equipment alone leaves the physiotherapist's hands as a route of transmission.
  3. The correct answer is C (Fit-checked P2 respirator, eye protection, fluid resistant gown and gloves, with the door kept closed): Procedures that generate aerosols require airborne level protection in addition to the usual droplet measures, which means a fit-checked P2 respirator, eye protection, gown and gloves, ideally in a closed single room. A surgical mask does not filter fine aerosols adequately. Gown and eye protection cannot be dropped, because splash and spray from coughing are expected during this treatment.
  4. The correct answer is C (Give a structured ISBAR handover of the change, his precautions and a request for medical review): A worsening oxygen requirement and respiratory rate suggest deterioration, so the nurse in charge needs a clear, structured handover and a request for medical review before he leaves the ward. ISBAR makes the concern and the request explicit and reduces the chance of lost information. Passing the message through a clerk or porter, or normalising the change, delays recognition of a deteriorating patient.
Case CHB36

Mr Ravi Rao, 31, a professional orchestral violinist, is referred by his GP to a hospital outpatient physiotherapy clinic. He has had 4 months of intermittent tingling along the ulnar side of the left forearm and hand, with aching in the left neck, shoulder and upper arm after long rehearsals. Symptoms build after about 90 minutes of playing, with the left arm elevated and head turned left, and ease within an hour of rest. No weakness, no dropping of objects, no trauma. He plays about five hours daily. Examination: forward head posture, tight scalenes and pectoralis minor, left first rib elevated on palpation. Upper limb neurological examination is normal. Spurling's test negative. The ulnar nerve bias upper limb tension test reproduces tingling, which eases with neck side flexion towards the arm. Tinel's sign at the elbow and the elbow flexion test are negative. Roos test reproduces forearm tingling at 50 seconds, while radial pulses stay strong and hand colour is normal. Blood pressure is equal in both arms. The clinic runs with a long waiting list and one allied health assistant.

  1. The correct answer is B (Neurogenic thoracic outlet involvement with mechanosensitive nerve tissue, as symptoms respond to positioning): Symptoms that change with positioning along the neural pathway, with a normal vascular response and negative elbow and cervical root tests, point to neurogenic thoracic outlet involvement with a mechanosensitive lower trunk or ulnar nerve component. Cubital tunnel and C8 radiculopathy are each excluded by their own negative provocation tests. Arterial involvement would show pulse loss or pallor.
  2. The correct answer is C (Education, activity pacing with scheduled playing breaks, gentle nerve gliding and scapular strengthening): Conservative care is first line: load management that keeps him playing within tolerance, sliding rather than tensioning neural mobilisation, and postural and scapular strengthening. Aggressive end range tensioning in an irritable nerve tends to flare symptoms. Total rest and passive stretching deconditions him, and surgery is only considered after conservative care fails.
  3. The correct answer is C (Stop treatment and arrange urgent same-day medical assessment for possible venous thrombosis): Sudden arm swelling, heaviness and discolouration after repetitive overhead use suggests venous obstruction such as effort thrombosis of the subclavian vein, which needs same-day medical imaging and treatment. This is a vascular red flag that overrides the neurogenic plan. Elevation and stopping play are reasonable, but a GP visit later in the week delays diagnosis. First rib mobilisation and scalene stretching continue the old plan and could worsen a clot. A compression sleeve and a review next appointment also postpone the urgent assessment.
  4. The correct answer is B (Call the postoperative patient now and arrange emergency assessment if symptoms are confirmed): New calf swelling with breathlessness after knee surgery may signal deep vein thrombosis or pulmonary embolism, so patient safety outranks administrative deadlines and stable reviews. Assessing urgency of the symptoms is a clinical task that sits with the physiotherapist and cannot be delegated to an assistant. The report and waiting list can follow once the risk is managed.
Case CHB37

Giuseppe Rossi, 67, a retired electrician, has had right lateral elbow pain for five months, worse when gardening and lifting a full kettle. Medical history: type 2 diabetes managed with diet and tablets, mild osteoarthritis of the hands. Examination: tender at the lateral epicondyle, pain with resisted wrist extension and with the third finger extension test, painful grip with reduced pain-free grip strength compared with the left, elbow range full, neurological examination and cervical screen clear, no swelling. Pain is 4 out of 10 at rest and 7 out of 10 when gripping. You see him at a community health centre. He has brought a magazine article about a new elbow taping device and wants to know if it works.

  1. The correct answer is D (Lateral epicondylalgia, a load-related tendon problem of the common wrist extensor origin): Localised tenderness at the epicondyle with pain on resisted wrist extension and gripping is the typical picture of lateral epicondylalgia. A clear cervical and neurological screen makes radiculopathy or nerve entrapment less likely, and full range without giving way argues against ligament instability.
  2. The correct answer is B (Education on load management with a progressive loading programme for the wrist extensors and grip): Chronic tendon pain responds best to explanation, activity modification and progressive loading that suits his gardening needs. Prolonged splinting and passive modalities have limited long-term value. Corticosteroid injection may ease pain briefly but is linked with worse outcomes later, so it is not first-line.
  3. The correct answer is B (Explain that with no comparison group or longer follow-up, the result cannot show the device works): A small uncontrolled study with brief follow-up cannot separate the effect of the device from natural recovery or placebo response, so it gives weak evidence. The physiotherapist should say this plainly and explain how to weigh the evidence. Low risk does not make an unproven device worthwhile, and recommending purchase because people improved overlooks that most would improve anyway. Telling him to avoid taping goes beyond the evidence, because a weak study does not show the device is ineffective, and it leaves out what the guidelines recommend.
  4. The correct answer is A (Reduce the load or repetitions slightly, and monitor next-day pain before progressing again): Mild pain during loading is acceptable, but a clear rise in next-day pain suggests the dose was too high. Lowering the load a little and re-checking keeps him loading while respecting tendon tolerance. Stopping entirely or repeating the same dose is less effective, and one flare does not mean loading is unsuitable.
Case CHB38

Tavita Faleolo, 7, is on a paediatric hospital ward day 8 after resection of a posterior fossa tumour (medulloblastoma); radiotherapy is planned in a few weeks. Neurosurgical wound clean and dry. He is on oral steroids being weaned and his appetite is low. Examination: truncal ataxia in sitting with wide-based arm support, intention tremor and dysmetria on finger to nose, mildly slurred speech, gaze-evoked nystagmus. Strength 4/5 throughout, sensation normal. He needs two people to walk short distances with a wide-based, staggering gait. Resting HR 88, BP 100/62, afebrile. His mother stays at the bedside and he is keen to play. Tonight the evening nurse will take over from the day team, and the occupational therapist is also seeing Tavita tomorrow.

  1. The correct answer is B (Cerebellar dysfunction at the surgical site, affecting coordination and trunk control rather than strength): Truncal ataxia, intention tremor, dysmetria and nystagmus are typical cerebellar signs, and the cerebellum lies in the posterior fossa. Strength is only mildly reduced and sensation is normal, so the picture is mainly one of incoordination. Brainstem injury would be expected to add cranial nerve palsies or long tract signs. Steroid effects may explain mild weakness but not nystagmus or dysmetria. Hydrocephalus is a real postoperative risk, but he has no headache, vomiting or drowsiness, and his signs point to the cerebellum.
  2. The correct answer is D (Play-based tasks that build trunk control in sitting and standing, closely supervised and graded): Cerebellar ataxia improves with repeated, graded, task-specific practice that develops proximal stability, and play keeps a child engaged. Heavy weights and unsupported fast movements overload his control and risk falls, and passive positioning alone removes the practice needed for recovery.
  3. The correct answer is D (Stop the session, keep him safe and alert the nurse and medical team for urgent review): New headache, vomiting, drowsiness and a falling pulse after posterior fossa surgery can indicate raised intracranial pressure from hydrocephalus, which is an emergency. The physiotherapist stops activity and escalates immediately, because delay risks serious harm. Stopping the session is correct, but waiting for routine observations or for his mother to report again leaves a deteriorating child without review. Checking observations yourself first sounds careful, yet the slow pulse and drowsiness are already enough to call for help.
  4. The correct answer is C (Give a structured verbal handover of his needs, precautions and plan, and record it): A structured verbal handover, such as one following the ISBAR format, combined with clear written notes lets the incoming nurse act on his needs, precautions and plan. Relying on notes alone or on a parent leaves room for misunderstanding, and altering the assistance level for convenience puts him at risk of a fall.
Case CHB39

Margaret O'Brien, 71, a retired teacher, is on day 3 after three-vessel CABG through a median sternotomy with left leg saphenous vein harvest. History: type 2 diabetes, BMI 34, ex-smoker with mild COPD. Today: sternal pain 5 out of 10 on coughing, weak ineffective cough with white sputum, RR 22, HR 90 sinus, BP 130/78 mmHg, SpO2 93 percent on 2 L/min via nasal prongs, temperature 37.4. Auscultation: reduced air entry with fine crackles at the right base. Sternum stable with no click. Left calf oedema with a clean wound. She sits out of bed with assistance and has not yet walked. At 1 pm your list also holds Mr Chen, day 1 after aortic valve replacement, whom the nurse says has new wheeze and SpO2 falling to 88 percent; Mr Adams, day 5, walking independently and awaiting discharge advice; and an allied health assistant is available for 45 minutes.

  1. The correct answer is D (See Mr Chen first for falling oxygenation, then Mrs O'Brien, and Mr Adams's discharge advice last): Priority goes to the patient with acute physiological deterioration, here the falling SpO2 with new wheeze, followed by the patient with an active chest problem that is treatable. Discharge advice for a stable, independent patient can safely wait. Leaving a deteriorating patient to be monitored by others while treating a stable one inverts urgency.
  2. The correct answer is B (Supported huffing and coughing while hugging a firm pillow tightly against her sternum): Splinting the sternum with a pillow reduces pain and stress on the wound while huffing and coughing clear secretions. Anterior percussion or compression over the sternum loads the healing sternotomy, and raising both arms overhead stretches the sternum. Active, self-supported techniques are preferred at this stage.
  3. The correct answer is C (Stand using her legs with elbows close to her body, and carry only a light handbag): Current practice limits heavy pushing, pulling and carrying that load the healing sternum, so patients stand using leg strength with arms close to the body and keep loads light while staying in a pain-free range. Pushing through the arms and pulling on a rail both strain the sternum. Leaning on a frame still loads the arms and total arm immobility is neither needed nor helpful.
  4. The correct answer is B (Delegate Mr Adams's supervised walking from his written plan, and treat Mrs O'Brien personally): Delegation suits a stable patient with a clear written plan, such as Mr Adams's supervised walking, while the physiotherapist keeps responsibility for assessing and treating a patient with an evolving chest problem. Assessment and discharge education involve clinical reasoning that cannot be delegated. Using the assistant only for equipment wastes available help.
Case CHB40

Margaret Hollis, 71, retired bookkeeper, referred by her GP with four months of tingling in the right thumb, index and middle fingers. Symptoms wake her at night and she drops cups. She still types for two hours daily doing the books for a community club and sleeps with her wrists curled under her pillow. History: type 2 diabetes (well controlled), hypertension. No neck pain. Examination: Phalen's test reproduces tingling within 30 seconds; Tinel's sign at the wrist positive; thenar bulk normal; abductor pollicis brevis 4/5; reduced light touch in thumb, index and middle fingers; normal sensation over the thenar eminence; cervical movements full and Spurling's test negative. Night pain 5/10. Setting: busy public outpatient clinic running behind, six patients waiting.

  1. The correct answer is C (Compression at the wrist, because the palmar cutaneous branch leaves the nerve before the tunnel): The palmar cutaneous branch of the median nerve leaves above the wrist and passes over the flexor retinaculum, so thenar skin is spared when the nerve is squeezed inside the carpal tunnel. A lesion higher in the arm, such as at pronator teres, would usually also alter thenar sensation. A C6 root problem would typically change with neck movement, and her Spurling's test is negative.
  2. The correct answer is D (A neutral wrist night splint with positioning advice, reviewed in a few weeks): Mild to moderate carpal tunnel syndrome with night symptoms usually responds to a neutral wrist splint worn in bed, which keeps pressure inside the tunnel lowest, together with positioning advice. A splint held in extension raises tunnel pressure and can worsen symptoms. Strengthening does not decompress the nerve, and surgery is kept for severe, progressive or unresponsive cases.
  3. The correct answer is B (Refer promptly to her GP for a surgical opinion, sending a summary of findings): Constant numbness, visible wasting and falling strength despite splinting indicate progressive nerve compromise, where delay risks lasting loss of hand function. Prompt referral through her GP for a surgical opinion is the expected response. Injection is a recognised conservative approach, but it is not enough once wasting and weakness have appeared. More splinting and gliding exercises prolong conservative care that has already failed. Nerve conduction studies can help, yet holding decisions until results return postpones the decompression she needs.
  4. The correct answer is A (Assess the post-surgical patient now and arrange medical review, then write the letter): New fever and wound redness after surgery may signal early infection, which is more time critical than a letter or a routine review. Assessing that patient and arranging medical review comes first, and the letter can follow within the day. Her progressing deficit matters but is not as urgent, and an assistant should not perform this clinical assessment.
Case CHB41

Mrs Eleni Papadopoulos, 81, was admitted 30 hours ago with a left middle cerebral artery ischaemic stroke. She has known atrial fibrillation. Findings: right arm strength 2/5, right leg 3/5, sits at the bed edge with moderate assistance, no obvious right shoulder subluxation. She has expressive aphasia: understands short, simple phrases, says only a few words, and becomes frustrated. Observations: HR 82 irregular, BP 148/84, SpO2 96 percent, GCS 14. The medical team states she is stable, her blood pressure is within their set range, and she may mobilise. Her daughter Sofia is present and wants to help. Later a nurse suggests filming Mrs Papadopoulos walking on the physiotherapist's own mobile phone, to show the ward team and to email to Sofia.

  1. The correct answer is D (Begin short, frequent out of bed sessions with monitoring, building gradually as tolerated): Current stroke guidance supports early mobilisation in short, frequent, lower dose sessions with monitoring, rather than very early high dose activity or prolonged bed rest. Waiting for command following would delay useful active practice. Gradual progression balances benefit against the risk of neurological or blood pressure change.
  2. The correct answer is D (Use short sentences with gestures and demonstration, ask yes or no questions, allow extra time): With expressive aphasia and good comprehension of simple language, brief sentences with gesture, demonstration and closed questions give her a way to respond. Raising the volume does not help comprehension, and speaking only to a relative removes her from decisions. Longer written instructions may be beyond her language ability after a left hemisphere stroke.
  3. The correct answer is A (Support the arm on a pillow or table when seated, and avoid pulling on it): The flaccid shoulder is vulnerable to traction injury, so it is supported in sitting and never used as a handle in transfers. Pulleys and forced passive elevation overload a poorly stabilised joint, and a hanging arm encourages subluxation and pain.
  4. The correct answer is C (Decline the personal phone, and seek her consent for any recording under hospital policy): Recording a patient on a personal device breaches privacy and records management standards, and a relative's agreement does not replace the patient's own consent where she can show a preference. Any recording must follow hospital policy on approved devices, consent and storage. Emailing identifying clinical detail outside approved channels carries similar privacy risks.
Case CHB42

Daniel Ferraro, 29, is a recreational skier who twisted his left knee on a ski trip at Perisher two weeks ago, felt a pop and could not continue. An MRI report from a radiology clinic describes a complete ACL rupture with intact menisci and collaterals. Reconstruction is booked in six weeks with an orthopaedic surgeon. He works as a surveyor and flew home five days after the injury. Examination: trace effusion, active knee extension lacks 8 degrees compared with the right, flexion to 120 degrees, small quadriceps lag on straight leg raise, quadriceps strength 4 out of 5, Lachman test positive with a soft end feel, mild limp with full weight-bearing. He takes no medications. He mentions his left calf has felt tight since the flight but thinks it is just stiffness from the knee.

  1. The correct answer is B (The anterior cruciate ligament, the main restraint to forward tibial translation in the nearly extended knee): The Lachman test uses low flexion to isolate the ACL, and a soft end feel with excess anterior translation points to rupture. The posterior cruciate and medial collateral ligaments are tested with different directions of force. The meniscus can limit translation only secondarily and does not produce a soft end feel on its own.
  2. The correct answer is B (Restore full knee extension, settle the effusion and regain quadriceps control to protect early post-operative recovery): Entering reconstruction with a quiet knee, full extension and good quadriceps activation is associated with a smoother post-operative course. Heavy open chain loading and cutting drills stress an unstable knee and risk further injury. Deferring range of motion leaves the extension deficit that is the main modifiable problem.
  3. The correct answer is D (Add controlled neuromuscular and quadriceps exercises, advise avoiding pivoting and uneven ground, and monitor swelling): Giving way is expected with an ACL deficient knee, and he is improving without a reactive effusion. Controlled neuromuscular training with activity advice protects the knee and maintains progress. Stopping exercise causes loss of strength, and pivoting drills or an urgent date change are not justified without new injury or locking.
  4. The correct answer is C (Withhold exercise, avoid calf massage and arrange same-day medical assessment to exclude deep vein thrombosis): Recent injury, reduced mobility, a long flight and a swollen, tender, warm calf make deep vein thrombosis a serious possibility that needs same-day medical assessment. Physiotherapists do not diagnose or exclude it with bedside tests, and manipulating the calf could dislodge a clot. Normal observations do not rule it out.
Case CHB43

Bao Tran, 81, resident in a residential aged care home, three weeks after a left hemisphere stroke. Right-sided weakness; sits unsupported for 20 minutes; transfers with one assist. The speech pathologist recommends mildly thick fluids and a minced and moist diet with supervised meals. Staff report coughing at several meals and a wet-sounding voice. Yesterday: temperature 36.9, respiratory rate 18, SpO2 96 percent on room air. Today: temperature 37.8, respiratory rate 24, SpO2 92 percent on room air, new coarse crackles at the right lung base, weak cough, alert and following commands. The GP has been notified. The home manager wants to reduce chest infections across all residents. The physiotherapist visits twice a week and is asked to help plan how improvements will be judged.

  1. The correct answer is A (Aspiration of oral intake into the right lower lobe causing early aspiration pneumonia): Meal-time coughing, a wet voice and new focal crackles over a dependent lung zone with fever and falling saturation fit aspiration into the right lower lobe. Secretion retention and lobar collapse are common after stroke, but neither explains the link with swallowing that the staff observed. Pulmonary embolism is a real risk after immobility, yet it would not usually cause coarse crackles or a wet voice at meals.
  2. The correct answer is A (Sitting fully upright with a neutral head position, staying upright for a period after the meal): An upright, neutral head posture helps protect the airway during swallowing, and remaining upright afterwards reduces reflux and aspiration of residue. Reclined or flat positions make aspiration more likely, even when they seem restful.
  3. The correct answer is C (Upright sitting, deep breathing, supported huffing and early mobilisation, and avoiding head-down positions): The aim is to improve lung volume and clear secretions without raising the risk of reflux or further aspiration. Head-down drainage soon after a meal in an older person with swallowing difficulty is hazardous, and routine suction is invasive when he can still cough. Rest alone loses the benefit of mobilising.
  4. The correct answer is A (Track chest infection and aspiration event rates before and after, plus a validated function measure): A useful evaluation measures what happens to residents, here infections and aspiration events compared with a baseline, alongside function. Comparing with another home is confounded by differences in residents and care, and does not show change in this home. Prescriptions in a single month are a weak proxy, since they depend on GP decisions and on seasonal illness. A checklist audit shows whether staff followed the programme, not whether residents were helped.
Case CHB44

Mr Giuseppe Romano, 71, retired baker, is on day 4 after a left middle cerebral artery ischaemic stroke on an acute stroke unit. He has right face and arm weakness (arm 2/5, leg 3/5), mild expressive aphasia, and is alert and cooperative. History: hypertension and type 2 diabetes. Nursing notes: coughing with thin fluids at breakfast and a wet, gurgly voice after sips of water. He is nil by mouth and the speech pathology assessment is booked for tomorrow. Observations: HR 78, BP 148/86, RR 18, SpO2 96 percent on room air, afebrile, chest clear on auscultation. He sits out of bed for 30 minutes but slumps to the right with his head forward. His wife Maria visits daily, wants to help and hopes to bring in his favourite foods. Goals: sit upright for meals, keep his chest clear and return home with Maria's support.

  1. The correct answer is C (A wet, gurgly voice quality after he swallowed small sips of water): A wet or gurgly voice after swallowing suggests residue or fluid sitting at the laryngeal inlet, which is a recognised sign of possible aspiration. Facial weakness with food pooling in the cheek and slow chewing show oral stage difficulty and raise risk, but they do not show that fluid has reached the airway. Slumped posture also raises risk without showing airway entry. Only the voice change after swallowing points to material at the larynx.
  2. The correct answer is B (Upright in a chair, trunk supported at midline, with chin slightly tucked): A symmetrical, upright and supported trunk with a neutral to slightly tucked chin protects the airway and gives better laryngeal control. Reclining or tilting the head back increases the chance of food or fluid reaching the airway. Side-lying is not a mealtime position and makes swallowing harder.
  3. The correct answer is C (Pause mobilisation and urgently escalate to the medical team about suspected aspiration pneumonia): New fever, tachypnoea, falling saturation and focal crackles after likely aspiration suggest pneumonia, which needs medical assessment before treatment. Starting chest techniques first delays diagnosis and escalation. Thickened fluids are a speech pathology and medical decision and would not address the deterioration.
  4. The correct answer is C (Explain why oral intake waits for assessment and teach her safe positioning and mouth care): Involving the carer with a clear reason and practical skills builds confidence and carries safe practice into discharge. Tastes before assessment expose him to aspiration risk, even when well meant. Excluding her or only handing over a booklet misses the chance for supported education.
Case CHB45

Ahmed Haddad, 8, is on day 12 of a children's hospital admission for pneumococcal meningitis. Intensive care stay of four days, seizure on day three, now seizure free on anticonvulsant medication. Afebrile, HR 98 at rest, SpO2 98% on air, BP normal. Walks 50 metres with supervision on a wide base, unable to tandem walk, and sways when standing with eyes closed or on foam. Left head impulse test is positive. Hearing screen failed on the left; audiology is booked. Left ankle dorsiflexion 4/5, tone normal. Tires quickly and has mild headaches after activity. Mother is at the bedside; the ward nurse and neurology team each ask for updates, and discharge to the community rehabilitation team is planned in three days.

  1. The correct answer is B (Left inner ear (labyrinthine) damage affecting both the hearing and balance pathways): Meningitis can damage the labyrinth, and one damaged inner ear explains a positive head impulse test and hearing loss on the same side, as well as worse balance when vision and surface cues are removed. Cerebellar damage does not produce a positive head impulse test and would not account for the hearing loss. Middle ear fluid reduces hearing but does not cause a vestibulo-ocular reflex deficit. Drug toxicity can affect the inner ear, but it usually acts on both sides, so it fits a one sided finding poorly.
  2. The correct answer is B (Stop activity, seat him to rest, recheck symptoms and observations, and inform the nurse): A worsening headache with pallor and a marked heart rate rise after meningitis means he may be exceeding tolerance or developing a complication, so activity stops and the team is told. Mild headaches after activity were expected, but a worsening pattern is different. Continuing, or only documenting the event, delays the review.
  3. The correct answer is D (Graded gaze stabilisation and balance tasks, removing visual and surface cues as he improves): Vestibular loss is managed with progressive gaze stabilisation and balance work that gradually challenges vision and surface, to promote compensation. Staying on firm ground with eyes open removes the challenge that drives recovery. Dix-Hallpike positioning treats canal debris, not vestibular loss, and strength is a minor problem here.
  4. The correct answer is B (A structured summary of function, precautions, hearing and balance findings, goals, and actions needed): A structured handover gives the receiving team the current status, safety precautions, baseline findings and what remains to be done in a form they can act on. A long log buries key information, and a vague note omits what they need. An exercise sheet alone leaves the physiotherapy-specific findings and precautions out.

Practice Test 4 Answer Key

Case CHB46

Darren Walker, 52, a Yorta Yorta man and truck driver, is on day 4 in intensive care with severe ARDS after influenza pneumonia. He is intubated and ventilated, sedated to a RASS of minus 3, with neuromuscular blockade ceased yesterday. Settings: FiO2 0.6, PEEP 12 cmH2O, SpO2 91 percent, PaO2/FiO2 ratio 140. Chest X-ray shows bilateral infiltrates. HR 96, MAP 72 mmHg on low-dose noradrenaline, afebrile. Secretions are moderate, thick and white. Prone positioning for 16 hours a day began two days ago; he is currently supine for a scheduled turn. A small pressure area is present on the left cheek. His wife Lorraine and extended family visit daily and have asked to stay at the bedside. The hospital Aboriginal Liaison Officer has offered to support them. Physiotherapy has been asked to help with chest clearance and positioning.

  1. The correct answer is C (Dorsal lung units recruit and ventilation matches perfusion more evenly across the lung): Prone positioning recruits collapsed dorsal lung and distributes ventilation and perfusion more evenly, so shunt falls and oxygenation improves. Gravity drainage of secretions can happen but is not the main reason for the gas exchange benefit in ARDS. Changes in diaphragm movement and cardiac output are minor contributors at most.
  2. The correct answer is B (Keep the circuit closed and suction in-line only if signs of retained secretions develop): In severe ARDS on high PEEP, circuit disconnection and routine airway interventions risk derecruitment and desaturation, so treatment is targeted to clinical signs of retention and uses closed in-line suction. Manual hyperinflation and percussion carry the same risks and add little when he is not audibly retained. Routine saline instillation and scheduled deep suction irritate the airway without a clear indication.
  3. The correct answer is C (Offload the cheek by changing head position and padding, then report and document the skin change): Prone sessions are protective in severe ARDS, so the aim is to keep proning while relieving pressure by changing head position, padding and offloading, and to report the skin injury so the team can manage it. Ending proning only because oxygenation looks stable gives up the benefit and risks relapse. Leaving the head fixed or applying a dressing without review ignores the pressure damage and the nursing and wound care roles.
  4. The correct answer is D (Welcome family involvement, explain the safety limits, and invite the Aboriginal Liaison Officer to support communication): Culturally safe care recognises that kinship and family are central to wellbeing and decision-making for many Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander people, so the physiotherapist works with family within safety limits and uses the liaison officer. Welcoming the family and explaining plainly is good practice, but passing the limits and cultural support to others misses the chance to explain safety and involve the liaison officer. Updates to one person only does not recognise wider kinship obligations. A meeting tomorrow postpones the plain explanation Lorraine asked for today.
Case CHB47

Rosa Bellini, 72, a retired seamstress, attends a community health centre with a three month history of central low back pain that began after gardening. Pain is worse with sustained standing and eased by walking and changing position, averaging 4/10. No leg symptoms, bladder or bowel change, weight loss, night pain or trauma. Medical history: hypertension and mild osteoporosis, both managed by her GP. Examination: lumbar extension reduced by about half, flexion near full, no neurological deficit, straight leg raise negative bilaterally, tenderness over the L4 to L5 paraspinals but none over the spinous processes, gait normal. Patient Specific Functional Scale: standing 4/10, gardening 3/10. Rosa lives alone and is keen to garden again. The centre's allied health assistant, Priya, runs the Tuesday exercise class and is available to help with Rosa's gym sessions.

  1. The correct answer is A (Pain eased by walking, tenderness over paraspinals rather than spinous processes, and no history of trauma): Relief with movement, paraspinal rather than bony midline tenderness and no trauma together make an acute osteoporotic fracture less likely and fit a mechanical pattern. A movement pattern like reduced extension can occur with several causes and does not separate a mechanical problem from a fracture. A negative straight leg raise tests neural tension only, and normal gait does not exclude a fracture. A modest pain score and onset after gardening are also common to both.
  2. The correct answer is B (Advice to stay active, graded gardening exposure, and trunk and hip strengthening without loaded flexion): Staying active, graded exposure to the valued task and strengthening are the mainstay for mechanical back pain, and loaded or repeated end range flexion is avoided with osteoporosis. Thrust manipulation carries more risk with reduced bone density. Rest and supports without exercise do not restore her function.
  3. The correct answer is B (Cease exercise, avoid loading the spine, and arrange urgent medical review for suspected vertebral fracture): Sudden severe pain after minor loading, midline bony tenderness and pain on coughing in a person with osteoporosis suggest a vertebral compression fracture. Loading should stop and she needs prompt medical review and imaging. Stopping exercise is right, but treating spasm with heat and a routine review is too slow. Bracing with imaging next week also delays diagnosis. Mobilising a possibly unstable fracture risks further collapse, so it should not be tried first.
  4. The correct answer is D (Give Priya clear written instructions and precautions, confirm competence, and retain responsibility with scheduled reviews): A physiotherapist may delegate supervision of a defined programme, but must give clear instructions, check the assistant's competence, review the client and remain accountable. Progression and adjustment of load need clinical reasoning and stay with the physiotherapist. Comorbidities alone do not prevent delegation when the task is suitable and supervised.
Case CHB48

Noah Okafor, 3 weeks old, born at 38 weeks by breech presentation, is seen in the hospital outpatient clinic with his parents. Left hip: Ortolani positive, limited abduction and asymmetric thigh skin folds, and ultrasound confirms a dislocated left hip. Right foot: rigid equinus, varus and adduction of the forefoot with a deep medial crease that does not correct passively; the diagnosis is idiopathic talipes equinovarus. He is otherwise well and feeding normally. Neurological and spinal examination are normal. His mother, Ngozi, has found an online article describing a small uncontrolled study in which stretching alone corrected clubfoot, and asks whether the team can skip casting. She is calm and wants to understand the reasoning behind the plan.

  1. The correct answer is A (Ortolani test: the clunk marks a dislocated femoral head being reduced back into the acetabulum): The Ortolani manoeuvre abducts and lifts the femoral head forward, producing a clunk as a dislocated head reduces into the socket. The Barlow test instead adducts with posterior pressure to provoke dislocation of a located but unstable hip, so the two are not interchangeable.
  2. The correct answer is C (Change the nappy with the harness on, keeping hips flexed and avoiding straightening the legs): The harness maintains the flexed, abducted position that keeps the femoral head centred in the socket, so it should stay on and care should avoid extending or adducting the hips. Removing it, lifting by the ankles or loosening the straps lets the hip straighten and risks redislocation.
  3. The correct answer is A (Refer for a percutaneous Achilles tenotomy, followed by a final cast holding the foot abducted): In the Ponseti method, residual equinus after the other components are corrected is treated by a percutaneous Achilles tenotomy and a final cast, then bracing. Forcing dorsiflexion risks a rocker-bottom foot, and bracing or stretching alone leaves the equinus uncorrected.
  4. The correct answer is D (Thank her, explain that uncontrolled studies are prone to bias, and share the Ponseti evidence): A small uncontrolled study cannot show that an intervention caused the outcome and is prone to selection and reporting bias, whereas the Ponseti method has strong supporting evidence and guideline support. Respectful, transparent explanation supports shared decision making. Dismissing the parent or judging only a headline rate does not appraise the study properly.
Case CHB49

Margaret Nowak, 76, retired seamstress, lives with her husband in a single-storey home. Parkinson disease for eight years; levodopa-carbidopa at 7am, 11am, 3pm and 7pm. Her GP referred her after two falls this month, both when turning in the kitchen. Home visit at 1pm, two hours after her 11am dose. Reports slow, stiff movement and more freezing in the hour before each dose. Lying BP 130/80, standing BP 108/66 at one minute with light-headedness, HR 72. Shuffling gait with festination on turning, freezing in doorways, stooped posture, right hand rest tremor. Timed Up and Go 18 seconds today in her lounge room. Cognition intact. Her goal is to cook and hang washing without falling. Your service is auditing falls outcomes using the Timed Up and Go and a falls diary.

  1. The correct answer is A (Use an external cue, such as a floor marker or rhythmic beat, to trigger stepping): External visual or auditory cues can bypass the impaired automatic control of stepping and are the best supported way to break a freeze. Mental checklists while carrying items add a second task and tend to worsen freezing. Rushing off after a pause builds the festinating pattern that causes falls.
  2. The correct answer is A (Seat her, recheck pressures, teach slow position changes, and report the drop to her GP): A symptomatic drop on standing is a falls risk that needs safe positioning, repeat measurement, practical strategies and medical review because medication may contribute. Continuing to stand her while light-headed is unsafe. Fitting stockings without medical input skips the review, and stopping all balance work removes the exercise that reduces falls.
  3. The correct answer is C (In her on period, adding a short supervised trial of the cues in her off period): Motor learning is most effective when she moves well, so the main practice belongs in her on period, with a brief supervised rehearsal of the same cues when she is stiffer so they work in real life. Practising mainly when she is off is tiring, risky and gives poor-quality repetitions. Fatigue late in the day also degrades learning.
  4. The correct answer is C (Repeat it at the same time after her dose, with the same chair and route): Outcome measures only show real change when the conditions are standardised, and in Parkinson disease the timing relative to medication, the chair, the distance and the instructions all change performance. Switching measures or changing the setting makes results incomparable. Altering the test method, such as adding rests, removes the standard against which the baseline was set.
Case CHB50

Marcus Tuilagi, 38, a plasterer, fell from a ladder two days ago and underwent plate and screw fixation yesterday for a left lateral tibial plateau fracture. The surgeon's orders are non-weight bearing on the left leg for six weeks, a hinged knee brace locked in extension when standing or walking, and knee flexion 0 to 90 degrees allowed in bed from tomorrow. Pain is 6/10 on movement, the dressing is dry, the calf is soft and non-tender, and sensation and foot movement are normal. He lives in a two-storey home with seven entry steps and a rail, with his wife and two young children. He has good upper limb strength and walks on the flat with crutches, hopping safely on the right leg. He is keen to get back to work as soon as he can.

  1. The correct answer is A (Step up with the right leg first, then bring the crutches and operated leg together): The rule is up with the good leg and down with the operated leg: the right leg lifts the body, then the crutches and non-weight-bearing leg follow, keeping load off the fixation. Moving the operated leg first or hopping beside the crutches leaves him unstable and risks a fall that could load the fracture. Stairs should be practised with supervision before discharge.
  2. The correct answer is B (Stop mobilising and urgently refer for medical assessment of possible deep vein thrombosis): A swollen, warm, tender unilateral calf in an immobile post-fracture patient is a high-risk presentation for deep vein thrombosis and needs medical assessment before further mobilisation or any calf manipulation. Homans sign is unreliable and a negative result does not exclude a clot. Compression and massage could dislodge a thrombus or delay diagnosis.
  3. The correct answer is A (Explain that pain does not show bony healing and contact the surgeon to discuss his goals): Pain relief reflects settling inflammation, not bony union, and early loading of an articular fracture can lead to loss of reduction or fixation failure. The physiotherapist works within the surgeon's orders, explains the reasoning, and contacts the surgeon because his work goals may influence the plan. Allowing progression on personal judgement, or endorsing a return to ladder work, would breach the orders.
  4. The correct answer is C (Decline the voucher politely, give the service contact details, and explain how to reach the team): Gifts of significant value and use of personal contact channels blur the professional relationship and can create conflicts of interest, so the physiotherapist declines kindly and keeps communication within service channels. Continuity of care is protected by giving him the clinic's contact details and handing over to the community team. Accepting the gift or sharing a personal number compromises boundaries.
Case CHB51

Tomasi Fifita, 9, has cystic fibrosis and is on day 3 of intravenous antibiotics for a pulmonary exacerbation on the paediatric ward. He has increased cough, thick yellow sputum and a lower FEV1 than at his last clinic visit. Today: HR 108, RR 24, SpO2 96 percent on room air, coarse crackles at both bases, no wheeze. A peripheral IV cannula is in his right hand. His chest physiotherapy is an oscillating PEP device and huffing once daily, which he tolerates well. He usually plays soccer and cycles, and is bored in his room. His parents are present. School staff have been calling the ward for updates, and his parents have not yet discussed what the school should be told.

  1. The correct answer is D (Increase to two or three sessions daily with his device and huffing, timed with prescribed nebulisers): During an exacerbation airway clearance is usually intensified, and using the technique he already tolerates more often, timed with prescribed nebulised therapy, is practical and effective. Manual percussion has not been shown to be superior and is less independent. A single session does not match the increased secretion load, and pausing clearance during an infection is the opposite of standard care.
  2. The correct answer is A (Secure the cannula hand, monitor saturation and symptoms, and stop if he desaturates or becomes distressed): Exercise during an exacerbation is encouraged when it is monitored, so the key steps are protecting the line, watching saturation and symptoms, and having clear stopping criteria. Removing a cannula is outside physiotherapy scope and would force a repeat insertion. Banning leg exercise has no basis and removes the main benefit, and chasing a heart rate target ignores the monitoring that makes the session safe.
  3. The correct answer is D (Exercise helps sputum clearance and maintains aerobic fitness, which is associated with better long term health): Regular exercise supports mucus clearance, maintains aerobic fitness and is linked with better outcomes in cystic fibrosis. It complements, but does not replace, prescribed airway clearance. Its effects go well beyond appetite, and carefully monitored activity during an exacerbation is generally encouraged, not delayed.
  4. The correct answer is A (Withhold details without parental consent, and offer to pass the school's request on to his parents): Health information about a child is private and is released to a third party only with the parents' consent or under a clear legal exception. The respectful response is to decline and let the family decide what the school is told. A school's interest does not create a right to the information. Even general advice reveals that he has an infectious condition, and verifying identity does not replace consent.
Case CHB52

Mr Marco Marino, 84, lives in a residential aged care home in Melbourne. He has Parkinson's disease (Hoehn and Yahr stage 3) and moderate cognitive impairment, with fluctuating alertness. Eight days ago he fell without injury while walking to the dining room. Findings: walks 10 metres with a four wheeled frame and supervision, freezing at doorways, Timed Up and Go 28 seconds. Blood pressure 128/78 lying and 104/62 on standing, with light-headedness. Sit to stand needs moderate assistance. The care team (nurse, occupational therapist, speech pathologist, GP) are meeting to set goals. Staff want him walking independently to meals. His wife wants him to return to their home. Mr Marino says only that he wants to see his tomatoes. This morning he told the physiotherapist he does not feel like exercising.

  1. The correct answer is B (The blood pressure fall with light-headedness, needing staged position changes and notifying nursing staff): A postural drop with symptoms is an immediate safety issue, so changes of position must be gradual and the nurse and GP told. Freezing and slow gait speed are real treatment targets but can be worked on once standing is safe. A lifter for all transfers would remove the practice he needs.
  2. The correct answer is A (Offer an external visual cue, such as a floor line to step over): External cues, visual or rhythmic, bypass the impaired automatic stepping pathway and are a core strategy for freezing in Parkinson's disease. Trying harder and attending to the step tends to worsen freezing, and pulling him risks a forward fall. Dual tasking competes for attention and usually increases freezing.
  3. The correct answer is D (Document his own valued goal as the focus, then agree measurable steps with the team): Goal setting should be centred on what matters to the person, even when cognition is impaired, and then translated into steps the team and family can support. Staff or family goals can inform but should not replace his own. Delaying goals until retesting leaves him without direction and is not required.
  4. The correct answer is D (Capacity is decision specific, so check his understanding of this choice and respect a capable refusal): Capacity is assessed for a particular decision at a particular time and cannot be presumed absent from a diagnosis. If he understands the decision and its consequences, his refusal stands, and alternatives can be offered. Admission consent from a relative does not override a capable person's refusal on the day.
Case CHB53

Mia Nguyen, 14, plays representative netball. Four hours ago she landed from a rebound and inverted her right ankle, then walked off court with a limp. She attended after school with her coach. Her mother is at work and has texted that she is happy for 'whatever is needed'. Mia has no medical history and takes no medication. Examination: swelling and bruising over the anterolateral ankle, tenderness over the anterior talofibular ligament, no tenderness over the bone at the malleoli, navicular or base of the fifth metatarsal, and she took four steps in the clinic with a limp. Pain on weight-bearing is 5 out of 10. Neurovascular status is normal. Mia asks whether you can tape her ankle so she can play on Saturday.

  1. The correct answer is B (Tenderness over the bone at the posterior edge or tip of the lateral malleolus): The Ottawa ankle rules screen for fracture using bony tenderness at specific sites and the ability to take four steps. Bone tenderness at the posterior edge or tip of the malleolus triggers imaging. Soft tissue swelling, ligament stress tests and sinus tarsi tenderness are common in sprains and are not part of the rule.
  2. The correct answer is C (Support with a brace, start early pain-limited weight-bearing and movement, then progress loading as symptoms allow): Moderate lateral ankle sprains recover best with brief protection, early guided loading and movement, and graded progression. Prolonged immobilisation or rest delays recovery and weakens the ankle. Aggressive massage and strengthening on the first day irritate healing ligament fibres and are not supported.
  3. The correct answer is B (Arrange a prompt radiograph through her GP and protect the foot until a fracture is excluded): Bone tenderness at the fifth metatarsal base together with an inability to take four steps meets the Ottawa foot rule for imaging. Worsening function after early rehabilitation also raises concern for a missed fracture. Ultrasound assesses ligaments, not bone, and walking with tape risks displacing a fracture. Crutches protect the foot, but waiting a week delays a diagnosis that is needed now. Treating the change as a worse sprain overlooks the new bony tenderness.
  4. The correct answer is D (Mia's own informed consent given her demonstrated understanding, recorded in the notes alongside her mother's message): A young person who understands the nature, benefits and risks of a low risk intervention can give valid consent, and the physiotherapist assesses and records that understanding. Involving her mother is good practice and her message is documented. A coach has no authority to consent, and age alone does not remove Mia's capacity for this treatment.
Case CHB54

Priya Raman, 41, a primary school teacher, has secondary progressive multiple sclerosis diagnosed 9 years ago and attends a rehabilitation day programme two days a week. She uses a walking stick indoors and a wheelchair for distance. Findings: bilateral lower limb spasticity (Modified Ashworth 2 at hip adductors and plantarflexors), hip flexors 4-/5, ankle dorsiflexion 3/5, reduced sensation in both feet, Berg Balance Scale 38/56. Fatigue is worst in the afternoon, and warm weather makes her symptoms worse. She has urinary urgency and two recent falls hurrying to the toilet. Oral baclofen was started by her neurologist. Priya says the stiffness is worse and asks you to tell her to take extra tablets before therapy. She reports no other new symptoms. Her goals are to return to teaching two days a week and to stop falling. The team includes occupational therapy, a continence nurse, social work and her neurologist's clinic. HR 74, BP 124/78, temperature 36.8 degrees C.

  1. The correct answer is A (Decline to advise on dosing, record her report, and contact her neurologist about the stiffness): Adjusting medicine doses is outside the scope of a physiotherapist, and advice about extra tablets could cause harm. The appropriate action is to record her report and communicate with the prescriber. Handing the question to another profession or leaving it to a future appointment leaves worsening spasticity unaddressed.
  2. The correct answer is C (Train balance and transfers to the toilet, and agree a timed toileting routine with the team): The falls happen on urgent toilet trips, so training balance and toilet transfers targets the actual problem, and a timed routine agreed with the continence nurse reduces urgency episodes. Restricting fluids is outside scope and risks dehydration and worse bladder symptoms. Leaving toileting to others, or restricting walking, ignores the shared problem and her goal to stay mobile.
  3. The correct answer is A (Warmth temporarily slows conduction in demyelinated nerves, so cool the room and exercise earlier): In multiple sclerosis a rise in body temperature temporarily impairs conduction in demyelinated axons, producing transient symptoms that settle with cooling. Cooling the environment and exercising earlier in the day are standard strategies. Treating the pattern as a relapse, deconditioning or dehydration would lead to wrong decisions about her activity.
  4. The correct answer is B (Agree shared, measurable goals based on Priya's priorities, naming each member's contribution): Shared goals that start from the patient's priorities, are measurable, and make each profession's role clear support coordinated rehabilitation. A single profession's goal, or separate goals in separate notes, fragments care. Goals chosen by the team and then explained to her do not reflect what matters most to Priya.
Case CHB55

Mr Walter Johnson is a 68 year old Aboriginal man from a remote Northern Territory community, admitted to intensive care five days ago with severe pneumonia that progressed to acute respiratory distress syndrome. He is intubated and ventilated with PEEP 12 cmH2O and FiO2 0.6, and his PaO2/FiO2 ratio was 140 before he was proned for 16 hours on each of two days. He was returned supine this morning. He is sedated (RASS minus 4) on low-dose noradrenaline, MAP 70 mmHg, and has type 2 diabetes. His sister and nephew have travelled 800 km and wait in the family room, supported by the hospital's Aboriginal Health Worker. Physiotherapy has been asked to assess his chest and begin passive limb range of movement.

  1. The correct answer is D (It recruits dorsal lung regions and evens out ventilation, improving ventilation and perfusion matching): Proning reduces compression of the dorsal lung by the heart and abdominal contents and makes ventilation more uniform, so previously collapsed dorsal units are recruited and ventilation is better matched to perfusion. Secretion drainage may occur but is not the main mechanism. Cardiac output does not rise meaningfully, and tube position does not explain the oxygenation gain.
  2. The correct answer is B (Ask family wishes with the Aboriginal Health Worker, explain the care, and allow presence if safe): Culturally safe practice means working with the family and the Aboriginal Health Worker, asking about their wishes and explaining care in plain language. Family presence during care is often important to Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander people, so it is accommodated where clinically safe. Restricting visits, or setting up a single spokesperson without asking the family, imposes hospital routines on them rather than involving them in his care.
  3. The correct answer is D (Use closed-circuit suction and positioning, avoiding disconnection that risks loss of lung volume): In severe ARDS, breaking the circuit or using manual hyperinflation loses PEEP and lung volume, causing rapid derecruitment and desaturation. A closed suction system keeps the circuit intact while clearing proximal secretions. Percussion and vibration add little and risk further desaturation, and changing ventilator settings is a medical decision rather than a physiotherapy one.
  4. The correct answer is B (Stop the movements, keep him stable, and alert the bedside nurse and intensivist): A fall in MAP and SpO2 that does not recover after pausing is an adverse response to treatment, so activity stops and the medical and nursing staff are told at once. Waiting another five minutes delays recognition of deterioration in an unstable patient. Raising the head of the bed can lower blood pressure further and is a change that should be agreed with the team. Ventilator settings are managed by the intensive care team, and documenting before telling them wastes critical time.
Case CHB56

Hamza Qureshi, 10, has Duchenne muscular dystrophy and attends the hospital outpatient respiratory clinic with his mother. He walks short distances and uses a wheelchair at school. For two days he has had a wet-sounding cough he cannot clear, with a mild fever. Today: RR 26, HR 108, SpO2 95% on room air, coarse crackles at both bases, forced vital capacity 45% of predicted and peak cough flow 120 L/min. Notes show his respiratory muscle strength has declined over the past year. He has a mechanical insufflation-exsufflation (cough assist) device at home that his mother uses inconsistently. His school nurse has emailed asking for his diagnosis, cough assist settings and recent results so the teacher aide can help. His mother said by phone she is happy for school to have whatever helps.

  1. The correct answer is D (Weak inspiratory and expiratory muscles limit cough volume and force, so he needs assisted cough techniques): In Duchenne muscular dystrophy the cough fails because muscle weakness reduces the inspired volume and the expiratory force, and the low peak cough flow confirms this. That points to volume recruitment, manually assisted cough or mechanical insufflation-exsufflation. Percussion or bronchodilators do not address the underlying weakness, and fibrosis is not the usual mechanism.
  2. The correct answer is C (Start with low pressures, practise with the mask first, and increase gradually as tolerated): Familiarisation at low pressures builds trust and a good mask seal, and pressures are then titrated up to the level that gives an effective cough. Starting high frightens a child and can cause discomfort or poor cooperation. Inflation before exsufflation is needed to supply the volume behind the cough, and long continuous runs tire a weak child; short sets with rests are used.
  3. The correct answer is D (Confirm specific parental consent, share only what the cough plan needs, and document what was shared): A parent can consent for a child, but consent should cover the particular information and recipient, and disclosure should be limited to what is needed for his care at school. The decision and the content shared belong in the record. Sending everything exceeds that need, refusing outright blocks safe care at school, and a vague phone agreement leaves no clear scope or record.
  4. The correct answer is B (Arrange urgent assessment at the emergency department, since cough assist alone is unlikely to be enough): Drowsiness, faster laboured breathing and a saturation of 90% in a boy with weak respiratory muscles point to developing respiratory failure, which needs urgent medical assessment now. Further cough assist cannot treat fatigue or hypoventilation, and oxygen alone from home can mask rising carbon dioxide. Waiting until morning for advice delays the care he needs.
Case CHB57

Mrs Dorothy Kelly, 79, a retired seamstress, has idiopathic pulmonary fibrosis and has been in a hospital palliative care unit for 5 days. Goals of care, agreed with her and her family: comfort and being able to sit with her family. She is on 4 L/min oxygen by nasal prongs. At rest: RR 26, HR 96, SpO2 89 percent, Borg dyspnoea 4 of 10. Chest: bibasal fine end-inspiratory crackles, finger clubbing, dry cough. Her palliative physician has prescribed an opioid and an anxiolytic as needed. With any activity she becomes very breathless and anxious, dyspnoea rising to 7 of 10, and she fears dying breathless.
