Her grandson's birthday party is on Saturday and she wants to join the family in the day room, about 20 metres from her bed. A nurse and an allied health assistant are on the ward.

  1. The correct answer is A (A handheld fan directed at the face, with calm paced breathing and supported upright posture): Airflow across the face stimulates trigeminal receptors and eases the sensation of breathlessness, and it pairs well with paced breathing and a supported upright posture. Breathing exercises lying flat can increase effort. Inspiratory muscle training and interval training aim at longer-term capacity, which does not suit her goals of comfort at the end of life.
  2. The correct answer is C (Report it in the incident system, request a medical review and tell her family openly): Any fall or near fall is a reportable incident even without injury, because reports let the organisation find hazards such as trailing oxygen tubing. She also needs medical review after a fall, and open disclosure to her and her family. Notes alone or delayed reporting lose timely learning and delay review.
  3. The correct answer is B (High side-lying or forward leaning sitting with arms supported on a table and pillows): Forward leaning or high side-lying with supported arms fixes the shoulder girdle so accessory muscles can assist breathing, which eases breathlessness. Lying flat or prone increases effort and discomfort for a breathless palliative patient. Long sitting without arm support does not offload the shoulders and keeps the poor posture she already has.
  4. The correct answer is A (Her comfort and goals, with the fan and pacing, and report the reading to the doctor): In palliative care the goals of care guide treatment, and a low saturation in a calm, comfortable patient is not by itself a reason to stop. Symptom relief and her chosen activity come first, and the doctor should know the reading. Changing oxygen beyond the prescription is not for the physiotherapist to decide, and shortening the visit ignores her goal.
Case CHB58

Kavya Raman, 13, has bilateral spastic cerebral palsy (diplegia) and attends a mainstream school, walking with a posterior walker. She is on day 2 after bilateral hamstring lengthening and gastrocnemius recession on a paediatric ward. She takes oral baclofen prescribed by her paediatric rehabilitation physician. The surgeon's protocol: knee extension positioning, weight bearing as tolerated, and no passive stretching beyond comfortable end range for two weeks. Observations: HR 96, BP 108/66, temperature 37.2 degrees Celsius, pain 4 to 5 out of 10 with movement on regular analgesia. Popliteal angle: right 55 degrees, left 50 degrees. Ankle dorsiflexion with the knee extended: 0 degrees on both sides. Modified Ashworth Scale: hamstrings 1+, plantarflexors 2. Hip adductor tone is increased. Spasms disturb her sleep. Kavya wants to return to school sport. Her mother stays overnight and asks many questions.

  1. The correct answer is C (Walks with a hand-held device indoors but typically needs wheeled mobility for longer community distances): GMFCS level III describes walking with a hand-held mobility device in most indoor settings and using wheeled mobility for longer distances outdoors or in the community. Walking without a device with trouble on stairs and uneven ground describes level II. Powered mobility with assisted short walking is level IV, and unrestricted walking with only speed and balance limits is level I.
  2. The correct answer is B (Support the knees in comfortable extension for planned periods, alternating with side lying or prone): After hamstring lengthening, the protocol calls for knee extension positioning within comfort, mixed with other postures so she is not held in one position. Resting in flexion invites the contracture to return, and sitting for long periods keeps the hamstrings shortened. Rigid all-day splinting exceeds the protocol's comfortable end range and risks pain and skin problems.
  3. The correct answer is A (Record the spasm pattern and tone findings, then ask the medical team to review her medication): Adjusting medication is a prescriber's decision, but a physiotherapist can supply objective evidence of tone and spasm patterns and escalate to the treating team now while the problem is active. Advising the nurse to give a larger dose is prescribing beyond scope, and deferring to discharge leaves her sleeping poorly. A stretching routine conflicts with the surgeon's protocol of comfortable range only.
  4. The correct answer is D (Stop activity and urgently notify the surgical team to assess for a post-operative complication): Severe pain out of proportion that is unrelieved by analgesia, with a tense swollen calf after surgery, is a red flag for compartment syndrome or a deep clot, and normal colour and warmth do not exclude it. The safest first step is to stop loading the limb and escalate urgently. Waiting to reassess or continuing mobilisation and stretching could worsen the injury.
Case CHB59

Sunita Reddy, 52, administration officer, lives on a rural property in north-west Queensland, three hours from the nearest physiotherapy clinic. Two years of gradual right knee pain, diagnosed as osteoarthritis by her GP after x-ray. Pain 5/10 with stairs and prolonged standing, morning stiffness under 30 minutes, no locking or giving way. BMI 36. Medical history: treated hypertension (well controlled), no known heart disease. Not diabetic. Knee: small effusion, flexion 125 degrees, extension full, crepitus, tenderness along the medial joint line. Walks little, sits most of the day. Wants to start exercise at home. A video consultation is booked; she has a sturdy chair and a phone stand. The pain began gradually and is not work related.

  1. The correct answer is C (Education, progressive exercise and weight loss support, with analgesia as an adjunct): Current Australian guidance for knee osteoarthritis places education, exercise and weight management at the centre of care, with medicines used to support those measures. Rest and repeated injections do not change function over time. Arthroscopic lavage is not recommended for degenerative knee pain, and high-impact work is unsuitable for a painful, loaded joint.
  2. The correct answer is D (A 30 second chair stand count with the chair secured against a wall): A chair stand count needs only a stable chair, can be observed and counted on video, and suits her level of function. Ligament stress tests need hands-on skill and cannot be self-performed meaningfully. Maximal testing is unsafe without supervision, and a walk test requires a standardised course length that a home hallway does not provide.
  3. The correct answer is D (Stop exercise, seat her and keep the call open, arranging an ambulance if symptoms persist): New chest tightness with light-headedness during exertion needs exercise to stop immediately, with her seated and monitored while the physiotherapist stays on the line, and emergency help called if it does not settle. Continuing at lower intensity ignores a possible cardiac cause. Ending the call leaves her alone and unmonitored, and driving while symptomatic is unsafe.
  4. The correct answer is C (Asking her GP about a chronic disease management plan for rebated allied health visits): A GP chronic disease management plan can open Medicare rebates for a limited number of allied health visits each year, which fits her situation. My Aged Care is aimed at older people, and she is not in that group. The NDIS assesses permanent and significant disability rather than a diagnosis, and her pain is not work related, so a compensation claim does not apply.
Case CHB60

Thi Lan Pham, 82, lives in a residential aged care facility, three months after a right hemisphere stroke with left hemiplegia. She is transferred by two staff using a sling hoist. Examination: left upper limb has low tone and 2/5 shoulder strength; a one fingerbreadth gap is palpable below the acromion when the arm hangs. Pain 6/10 when staff lift her under the left armpit or pull the arm to dress her, and 2/10 at rest. No hand swelling, normal skin colour and temperature. She is cognitively intact and speaks Vietnamese and some English. Sitting balance is good in a supported chair. Her daughter wants less pain during care. The facility has an allied health assistant (AHA) who works with you two days a week, and the manager asks whether the AHA can run her programme.

  1. The correct answer is D (Traction on the soft tissue around an inferiorly subluxed glenohumeral joint with poor muscular support): A low tone arm with weak stabilisers allows the humeral head to drop, and lifting or pulling on the limb loads the capsule and soft tissue. Capsulitis and spasticity typically involve stiffness or raised tone, and impingement needs overhead loading, none of which this presentation shows.
  2. The correct answer is D (Train the AHA on supported positioning, check competence, then delegate it with scheduled review): Delegating a defined, low risk task after training the assistant, confirming competence and arranging supervision keeps accountability with the physiotherapist. Assessment, clinical decisions about progression and joint mobilisation are not appropriate to delegate, and written instructions without a competence check or review are inadequate.
  3. The correct answer is A (Rest the forearm on a lap tray or pillows with the shoulder in neutral): A supported forearm takes the weight of the limb off the capsule and keeps the joint aligned. A hanging arm drags the humeral head down, and a continuous sling or an abducted stretch posture adds risks without supporting recovery.
  4. The correct answer is C (Stop passive movement and arrange prompt medical review for possible complex regional pain syndrome): Swelling, warmth, skin change and pain from light touch after stroke suggest complex regional pain syndrome, which needs early medical assessment. Forceful passive movement or pulleys can aggravate it, and a firm bandage over a very sensitive hand is not appropriate without medical advice.

Practice Test 5 Answer Key

Case CHB61

Meera Chopra, 41, an accountant, presents to the emergency department with right facial weakness that began overnight. She woke with a drooping right face, difficulty closing the right eye and a dry, altered taste on the right of her tongue. She had a cold sore last week. She denies limb weakness, speech change, headache, ear pain or injury. BP 128/80 mmHg, HR 76, afebrile. The ED doctor diagnosed Bell palsy, prescribed eye lubricant and requested physiotherapy advice. Findings: unable to wrinkle the right forehead, right eye closes incompletely, flattened right nasolabial fold, mouth pulls to the left on smiling, limb strength and speech normal. Meera has asked that her diagnosis stay confidential because several people she knows work at this hospital.

  1. The correct answer is C (Weakness of the right forehead movements, because a stroke usually spares the upper face): The forehead muscles receive input from both cerebral hemispheres, so a stroke typically spares forehead movement while weakening the lower face. A lesion of the facial nerve itself, as in Bell palsy, paralyses the whole half of the face including the forehead. Mouth droop and eye dryness occur with either cause and do not separate them.
  2. The correct answer is B (Apply the drops regularly, wear glasses outdoors, and gently tape the lid shut overnight): An eye that cannot close fully loses its tear film and is at risk of corneal injury, so regular lubrication, protection from wind and dust and lid taping at night are standard advice that supports the medical plan. Waiting for pain delays protection, a firm pad can abrade the cornea, and exercises do not replace lubrication while closure is incomplete.
  3. The correct answer is C (Politely decline to share anything, not even confirming she is a patient here): Health information is confidential and may be disclosed only with the patient's consent or where the law requires or permits it, which does not include concern from a colleague who is not involved in her care. Even confirming that she is a patient discloses information. Asking in public exposes her privacy, and general updates still breach confidentiality.
  4. The correct answer is C (Short sessions of gentle, symmetrical movements in front of a mirror, avoiding forceful grimacing): Early rehabilitation favours gentle, controlled and symmetrical movement with mirror feedback in short sessions, because forceful or exaggerated effort can encourage unwanted co-contraction and abnormal movements as the nerve recovers. Routine early electrical stimulation lacks good support, and firm massage or stretching of paralysed tissue adds no benefit and may irritate it.
Case CHB62

Daniel Papadakis, 31, an electrician, attends outpatient physiotherapy six weeks after a fall from a ladder that caused a closed right humeral shaft fracture, managed in a functional brace. A right radial nerve palsy has been present since the injury. Findings: unable to extend the wrist, thumb or fingers at the metacarpophalangeal joints, so the wrist rests flexed. Triceps 5/5, elbow flexion 5/5. Sensation is reduced over the dorsum of the first web space. Tinel's sign is positive at the lateral arm. X-ray shows early callus. Finger joints are beginning to feel stiff and he avoids using the hand because it feels useless. He has a compensable WorkCover claim. The orthopaedic surgeon reviews him in two weeks, the GP has asked for an update, and the insurer has requested a progress report.

  1. The correct answer is A (The radial nerve at the spiral groove, distal to the branches supplying triceps): A humeral shaft fracture injures the radial nerve in the spiral groove, which spares triceps but affects wrist and finger extensors and the first web space sensation. A posterior interosseous lesion spares sensation, and higher lesions would involve triceps.
  2. The correct answer is A (Provide a wrist and finger extension splint and teach regular passive range of movement): While the extensors are paralysed the aims are to keep joints supple, prevent overstretch of the weak muscles and maintain function with a splint. Resisted work cannot act on paralysed muscle, and prolonged immobilisation or neglect of the hand invites stiffness and contracture.
  3. The correct answer is D (It suggests axonal regeneration, so continue the splint, movement and regular review): An advancing Tinel's sign and a flicker in the most proximal muscle show the nerve is regenerating, so the wrist and fingers still need splinting and regular passive movement while distal muscles recover over months. Removing support and loading weak muscles early risks overstretch. Resting the arm invites stiffness, and the findings do not justify surgical exploration.
  4. The correct answer is B (Send a concise written summary of findings, splinting and progress to the surgeon and GP): Clear written handover with objective findings and the plan supports continuity of care between treating clinicians who have his consent. Verbal relay through the patient or a receptionist loses detail, and the insurer is not a member of the treating team.
Case CHB63

Mr David Costa is a 59 year old accountant attending his first community cardiac rehabilitation session three weeks after an anterior STEMI treated with primary PCI and a drug-eluting stent to the LAD. Echo shows LVEF 45 percent. Medications: aspirin, ticagrelor, atorvastatin, ramipril and metoprolol. He smokes 20 cigarettes per day, has a BMI of 31 and a family history of heart disease. Resting HR 62, BP 124/76 mmHg, SpO2 98 percent, no symptoms at rest. Baseline 6 minute walk test: 420 metres, Borg RPE 13, no chest discomfort. He wants to return to golf and full time work. The cardiac nurse coordinates the program, and the exercise physiologist and cardiologist are not on site today. The physiotherapist leads the exercise session.

  1. The correct answer is C (Borg rating of perceived exertion, interpreted with symptoms and blood pressure responses during activity): Beta-blockers blunt the heart rate response, so heart rate targets are unreliable even when adjusted. The Borg rating of perceived exertion, read together with symptoms and blood pressure, reflects true effort. The talk test is a useful adjunct but does not replace other measures, and a smartwatch rate would mislead for the same reason.
  2. The correct answer is D (Light loads with higher repetitions for major muscle groups, avoiding breath-holding and monitoring RPE): Low-load, higher-repetition work for major muscle groups is a standard part of cardiac rehabilitation once a person is stable, and breath-holding is avoided to limit blood pressure surges. Maximal loads are not suited to this stage. Sustained isometric holds cause a marked pressure response, and delaying resistance work for months is unnecessary when no sternotomy is present.
  3. The correct answer is A (Stop exercise, sit him down, check his observations, and activate the emergency response now): Tightness he recognises as like his infarct, with pallor and a rising heart rate, may be an acute coronary event, so exercise stops and the emergency response is activated straight away. Watching for the symptom to persist or having the nurse assess first delays definitive care, because the cardiologist is not on site. Waiting to see how he responds to rest is not safe in a patient three weeks after a STEMI.
  4. The correct answer is A (Use a structured format such as ISBAR covering symptoms, observations, timing, medications and exercise): A structured ISBAR handover gives the receiving nurse the situation, background, assessment and request in a predictable order, including his medications, the timing of onset and the activity at the time. A chronological account buries the key concern. Deferring medication details or delegating the call leaves the emergency team without information they need as he arrives.
Case CHB64

Eleni Georgiou, 72, a retired seamstress, attends outpatient pulmonary rehabilitation 8 weeks after a right upper lobectomy for non-small cell lung cancer. Chemotherapy is planned. She has lost about 12 percent of her body weight in 3 months, her BMI is 18.5, her appetite is poor and she feels fatigued. History: ex-smoker, mild COPD, hypertension. At rest SpO2 is 95 percent, HR 82, BP 126/74. Six minute walk distance is 280 metres, with SpO2 falling to 90 percent, Borg dyspnoea 5 of 10 and leg fatigue 6 of 10. Grip strength is low and five sit-to-stands take 18 seconds. The wound has healed and right shoulder flexion is 120 degrees. The service plans for an allied health assistant to supervise her group exercise sessions twice a week.

  1. The correct answer is D (Cancer-related muscle loss and deconditioning are major contributors, so strength training should accompany aerobic training): Leg fatigue exceeding dyspnoea, weight loss and slow sit-to-stand point to skeletal muscle loss and deconditioning rather than ventilatory limitation alone, which resistance training helps to address. Lung volume loss after lobectomy is real but does not explain these muscle findings, and no anaemia has been shown.
  2. The correct answer is C (Moderate aerobic and progressive resistance exercise, with a dietitian referral to support her energy intake): Exercise for people with cancer cachexia is most effective as a combined, moderate programme alongside nutritional support, because exercise without adequate energy intake can accelerate muscle loss. Very intense or heavy loading is poorly tolerated, and withholding resistance work or nutrition input misses the main modifiable problems.
  3. The correct answer is A (Prescribe intensity using Borg ratings of dyspnoea and leg fatigue, checking SpO2 and heart rate regularly): Symptom ratings are a practical way to set intensity in older adults with lung disease, and saturation and heart rate checks add safety. Predicted heart rate targets are unreliable in this group, and mild exertional desaturation is expected in COPD and does not alone require stopping.
  4. The correct answer is C (Delegate delivery of the written programme with clear stop criteria, and review her progress regularly): The physiotherapist remains accountable, so assessment, programme design and progression stay with them, while the assistant delivers a defined programme with stop criteria and clear escalation, under regular review. Delegating clinical decisions exceeds an assistant's role, and withholding delegation altogether is unnecessary when the task is well defined.
Case CHB65

Baby Aroha Tane, 7 weeks old, was born at term and is admitted to a paediatric ward with a 3 day history of coryza, cough and poor feeding. RSV is positive. Observations: RR 62, HR 160, SpO2 93 percent in room air, afebrile. Mild subcostal recession and nasal flaring, diffuse fine crackles with expiratory wheeze, and nasal congestion with visible mucus. She is taking about half her usual bottle feeds and is unsettled. No apnoea episodes have been recorded and she has no previous lung disease. The medical team has written 'physio for chest' on the referral. Her mother is with her. The physiotherapist is supported by an allied health assistant who is experienced with mobilising older children but has no paediatric respiratory training.

  1. The correct answer is D (Routine percussion, vibration and forced techniques have not been shown to help and may add distress): In uncomplicated bronchiolitis, routine chest physiotherapy has not been shown to shorten illness or admission, and forced techniques can distress infants. Wheeze reflects airway narrowing and inflammation rather than mobilisable secretions. Head-down tilting adds risk to a breathless infant and is not supported.
  2. The correct answer is B (Gentle superficial nasal suction before feeds when the nose is blocked, avoiding deep or routine passes): Young infants breathe mainly through the nose, so clearing a blocked nose with gentle superficial suction before feeds can relieve work of breathing and help feeding. Deep or scheduled suction causes trauma and swelling, and nebulised saline with percussion is not supported. Avoiding suction altogether leaves a clear, treatable cause of distress untouched.
  3. The correct answer is D (Helping her mother with upright positioning during feeds, and reporting any change in breathing or colour): Delegation should match the assistant's trained competence: supervised positioning and parent support with clear reporting triggers is a low-risk task. Suction, assessment and clinical decisions about a small infant require paediatric respiratory skill. The physiotherapist stays accountable for assessment and the plan and cannot hand those over.
  4. The correct answer is A (Stay with her, call urgent medical review via the escalation process and support her airway): An apnoea episode followed by grunting, deep recession, a rate of 72 and a saturation of 89% signals impending respiratory failure in an infant, so the physiotherapist stays, escalates through the urgent process and supports her airway. Treating secretions first or waiting to reassess delays the response. Starting airway clearance while the team arrives adds handling to a tiring infant who needs senior assessment.
Case CHB66

Brendan Hartley, 58, station manager, attends a remote community health clinic in the Northern Territory where you work alongside a remote area nurse. The nearest hospital is about 300 km away, and the Royal Flying Doctor Service can be contacted through the clinic. He has had two days of a hot, swollen, painful right knee after a weekend of heavy drinking. History: gout and hypertension, for which he takes a diuretic. He had rigors overnight. Observations: temperature 38.4 degrees C, HR 104, BP 138/84, RR 20, SpO2 97%. The knee is red and warm with a large effusion. He flinches at any passive movement and cannot weight bear. He says it feels like his previous gout flares and asks you to 'get the knee moving' so he can return to the station.

  1. The correct answer is C (Fever and tachycardia with severe pain on even small passive movements of the joint): Systemic signs such as fever and tachycardia, with pain on the slightest passive movement, point toward joint infection, which can destroy cartilage within days. Gout history, alcohol and a diuretic make crystal arthritis likely but do not exclude infection. Redness, warmth, effusion and inability to weight bear occur in both conditions and do not discriminate.
  2. The correct answer is B (Withhold physical treatment, rest the knee, and arrange urgent medical review the same day): Hot swollen joint with fever, tachycardia and rigors is a possible septic joint until proven otherwise, which is an emergency needing aspiration and antibiotics. Exercise, mobilisation or electrophysical agents can increase pain and delay diagnosis, and deferring review for education or a week risks joint destruction and sepsis. Physical treatment can wait until the diagnosis is made.
  3. The correct answer is B (Aspiration of the joint with synovial fluid microscopy for crystals and culture for organisms): Only aspiration of joint fluid can show urate crystals and identify bacteria on culture, and the two can coexist. Serum uric acid can be normal or high during an attack so it does not discriminate, and inflammatory markers rise in both conditions. Plain radiographs are usually normal in the first days of either.
  4. The correct answer is D (Use ISBAR: identify yourself, state the suspected septic joint with observations, and request urgent review): ISBAR puts the identity, the suspected septic joint, his deteriorating observations and a clear request for urgent review in a predictable order, which matters when retrieval over 300 km may be needed. A long chronology delays the main message. Framing it as gout hides the urgency, and passing only observations on the phone leaves the doctor without your assessment.
Case CHB67

Mrs Thi Nguyen, 52, a Vietnamese-speaking cleaner, attends a remote community clinic where a doctor visits twice a week. She has had low back pain for 10 days after lifting. Over two days the pain has spread into both legs, and yesterday she did not feel the toilet paper properly and passed urine without realising. Her adult son interpreted at the front desk and says she is embarrassed. She speaks little English. Findings: plantarflexion 3/5 bilaterally, reduced pinprick sensation over the perineum and both posterior thighs, absent ankle reflexes bilaterally, straight leg raise limited by pain. HR 84, BP 128/76 mmHg, afebrile. The nearest hospital with surgical services is four hours away by road, and the Royal Flying Doctor Service can be requested through the clinic.

  1. The correct answer is A (Saddle sensory change with new bladder dysfunction and bilateral lower limb neurological signs): Saddle sensory loss with new bladder dysfunction, together with bilateral neurological deficit, is the pattern that signals compression of the cauda equina. Bilateral leg pain, reflex change and plantarflexion weakness can occur with other lumbar pathology and are not specific. Persistent back pain alone is not a red flag for this condition.
  2. The correct answer is C (Alert the clinic doctor and arrange urgent emergency transfer to a surgical hospital): Suspected cauda equina syndrome with progressive bilateral weakness, saddle sensory loss and new bladder change is a surgical emergency, and the nearest surgical hospital is four hours away, so the doctor is alerted and emergency transfer starts now. Imaging at a local centre, a next-day plan or an overnight stay each add hours before decompression. Specialist advice by telehealth does not replace getting her to surgery.
  3. The correct answer is A (Book a qualified telephone interpreter, explain why, and offer her son a supportive role): A qualified, impartial interpreter protects accuracy and confidentiality, particularly for intimate topics and when giving urgent information and obtaining consent. Family members and staff may filter or soften content, and apps are not reliable for clinical communication. Her son can still offer comfort without interpreting.
  4. The correct answer is C (Repeat and record neurological and bladder observations, and give findings to the retrieval team): Repeated neurological and bladder observations show whether the deficit is progressing and give the retrieval and surgical teams a clear trend. A single baseline cannot show change, and relying on her son or on rest alone could miss deterioration in a patient who speaks little English. Walking trials, heat or stretching are not safe options, and stopping examination removes information the surgical team will need.
Case CHB68

Tom Nguyen, 27, apprentice electrician, is an inpatient in a brain injury rehabilitation unit, 10 weeks after a motorcycle crash causing a severe traumatic brain injury with frontal contusions. Post-traumatic amnesia has resolved. Right hemiparesis has improved (right leg 4/5, arm 3+/5) with mild ataxia. He walks 50 metres with a four-wheeled walker and supervision. Cognitive screening shows reduced planning, poor judgement and low frustration tolerance, with intact orientation. He takes an antiepileptic. Staff report that he has twice stood from his wheelchair without calling for help and has made repeated personal comments to female staff. His parents visit daily and discharge home with them is planned in three weeks. He enjoys his physiotherapy sessions and tells the team he is looking forward to riding again.

  1. The correct answer is D (Use set routines, visual cues, a tidy setup and close supervision instead of reminders): With frontal executive impairment, knowing a rule does not guarantee acting on it, so safety comes from structure in the environment and supervision rather than relying on self-control. More explanation and rehearsal target knowledge he already has. Restraint is a last resort and is not justified when less restrictive measures are available.
  2. The correct answer is D (Stay calm, reduce demands, offer a short break and a choice of task, then resume later): A calm response that lowers the demand and gives him control de-escalates frustration and keeps him engaged. Insisting or confronting him tends to escalate agitation in someone with reduced self-regulation. Continuing the same task at the same difficulty leaves the trigger in place.
  3. The correct answer is B (Kindly decline, explain her role, document it and discuss it with her supervisor): The therapist keeps the boundary kindly, restates the professional relationship, records the event and discusses it with her supervisor so the team can respond consistently. His injury explains the disinhibition but does not shift her responsibility. Accepting the gift, handing him over without a plan or a firm confrontation each fail to give him a clear and consistent team response.
  4. The correct answer is C (Executive dysfunction with poor inhibition, so he knows the rules but cannot reliably apply them): Frontal injury commonly impairs planning, inhibition and self-monitoring while knowledge of rules stays intact, which matches his presentation. Neglect, depression and ataxia can occur after brain injury but do not account for acting impulsively despite recalling the rule. Plans should therefore build external structure.
Case CHB69

Raymond Walker, 46, an Aboriginal man from a remote community 900 km away, is on day 4 in intensive care with severe ARDS from pneumonia. He is intubated and ventilated (PEEP 14, FiO2 0.7), deeply sedated with a neuromuscular blocker infusion, and his PaO2/FiO2 ratio is 110. The team is using prone positioning in 16-hour cycles, and he was turned prone last night. Secretions are moderate and thick. Chest X-ray shows bilateral infiltrates. His sister Tanya and an uncle are at the bedside and want to stay. The family has said English is their second language at home, and Tanya asks if an Aboriginal liaison officer can attend. Raymond has no pressure injuries yet. You are asked to review his respiratory physiotherapy needs.

  1. The correct answer is A (It opens the back of the lungs and spreads air and blood more evenly): In ARDS the lungs are heavy, so the back areas collapse while most blood flows to them. Face down, the back of the lung can reopen and ventilation matches blood flow better, which improves oxygen levels. Secretion drainage is a minor effect, and he is paralysed so his own muscles are not doing the breathing.
  2. The correct answer is A (Closed-circuit suction with optimised humidification, avoiding circuit disconnection and keeping the prone position): In severe ARDS on high PEEP, disconnecting the circuit or bagging risks derecruitment and falling oxygenation, so secretions are cleared through a closed suction system with attention to humidification while prone positioning continues. Turning him supine on demand abandons the main oxygenation strategy, and percussion or saline instillation adds instability with little proven benefit.
  3. The correct answer is B (Call urgently for senior help while checking the tube, circuit and breath sounds): A sudden saturation drop with rising airway pressure and little secretion suggests a mechanical problem such as tube displacement or obstruction, or a pneumothorax, so the priority is immediate senior help while checking the tube, circuit and breath sounds. Raising oxygen, bagging or repeated suction delay the diagnosis and may worsen barotrauma or derecruitment.
  4. The correct answer is A (Contact the Aboriginal liaison officer, welcome the family, and ask how they prefer to be involved): Culturally safe practice means working with Raymond's family on their terms: involving an Aboriginal liaison officer, recognising family as part of his care and checking preferences without assuming roles. Naming one spokesperson, relying on leaflets or deferring the request treat the family as a problem to manage and delay support at the time they most need it.
Case CHB70

Rashid Hamdan, 46, a bus driver on reduced duties, lives alone in a regional town and attends his third telehealth session from his kitchen. His GP diagnosed fibromyalgia two years ago. He reports widespread pain (usually 6 to 7 out of 10), poor sleep, fatigue and a fear that exercise will harm him. Blood tests and imaging have been normal and his GP has no concerns about exercise. He walks about 10 minutes daily. Today he says that last night, while doing the standing heel raises you prescribed at the kitchen bench, he lost his balance and fell onto his left hip. He has a bruise, walked normally this morning, did not hit his head and has no other new pain. On video he walks steadily and squats to half range without distress. He wants to keep working and says he is tired of being told it is all in his head.

  1. The correct answer is B (Altered central pain processing amplifies signals, so pain is real without ongoing tissue damage): Current understanding is that fibromyalgia involves altered central pain processing, where the nervous system amplifies incoming signals, so the pain is real without ongoing tissue damage. This also supports telling him that movement is safe and helpful. Explanations built on overuse, weakened tissue or reduced blood flow imply ongoing structural harm, which is not supported and would deepen his fear of movement.
  2. The correct answer is B (Small planned increases from his current walking time, with flexible targets on flare days): A low, gradual and individually paced increase builds tolerance without provoking large flares, and flexible targets reduce the boom and bust cycle common in persistent pain. Waiting for pain to fall may never happen and feeds deconditioning. Vigorous intervals risk a flare that deepens his fear of movement, and delaying activity until education finishes loses the benefit of combining both.
  3. The correct answer is A (Explain these features are not typical of fibromyalgia and arrange prompt GP review first): Unexplained weight loss with fevers and night sweats are systemic red flags for infection, malignancy or other serious disease and are not part of fibromyalgia, so prompt GP review comes before progressing the program. Monitoring for a fortnight or waiting for a routine appointment delays diagnosis. Attributing the features to sleep or stress repeats his own assumption instead of testing it.
  4. The correct answer is C (Document it, lodge an incident report, and openly discuss what happened and program changes): Adverse events linked to care, including near misses and minor injuries, should be documented accurately, reported through the practice's incident system and discussed openly with the patient so that learning and program changes follow. Rashid's wish for no fuss does not remove that duty. Recording in the notes alone, or reporting only if harm escalates, defeats the purpose of risk management.
Case CHB71

Linh Pham, 46, works part-time as an aged care support worker and lives with her two teenage children. A rheumatologist diagnosed fibromyalgia two years ago. She has a GP management plan with Medicare-funded allied health visits and attends your community health centre. She reports widespread pain 6/10, fatigue, unrefreshing sleep and poor concentration. On good days she cleans the whole house for hours, then spends two days in bed. She can walk 10 minutes before stopping. Examination: widespread tenderness, full joint range, normal strength, normal neurological examination, resting heart rate 78, blood pressure 118/74. Her mood is low but she denies thoughts of self-harm, and she sees her GP monthly. The centre employs an allied health assistant, Ben, who holds a Certificate IV in Allied Health Assistance and works under your supervision. Your manager suggests Ben could run Linh's walking sessions.

  1. The correct answer is D (Activity pacing, with tasks set below her flare threshold and increased in small planned steps): Her boom and bust cycle drives flares and deconditioning, and pacing breaks it by holding activity at a sustainable baseline and building up slowly. Doing more on good days feeds the cycle, and resting until pain settles reinforces inactivity. Pushing to a fixed target regardless of symptoms ignores the flare response that fibromyalgia produces.
  2. The correct answer is D (Low-intensity walking or warm water exercise, starting below current tolerance and progressing slowly): Low-intensity aerobic exercise started below current tolerance and progressed slowly is the best-supported approach, and it limits post-exercise flares in a deconditioned person. Starting at her current limit, or alternating bursts with full rest, repeats the boom and bust pattern she already has. Moderate intensity cycling progressed quickly is too much too soon, and the assistant should not set the program.
  3. The correct answer is B (You assess and write the programme, then Ben delivers it with set limits and escalation steps): Assessment, clinical reasoning and programme design remain the physiotherapist's responsibility, while a trained assistant can deliver a defined programme with clear parameters and instructions on when to stop and report. The physiotherapist stays accountable for the delegation. Handing over design or relying only on periodic review leaves a patient with fluctuating symptoms without adequate supervision.
  4. The correct answer is C (Unintentional weight loss with new night pain that does not ease with position change or rest): Unexplained weight loss with new night pain unrelieved by position is a red flag pattern for serious pathology such as malignancy or infection and needs medical review rather than attribution to her known condition. Post-exertion fatigue, unrefreshing sleep and weather-related aching are expected features of fibromyalgia. Recognising the difference protects her from delayed diagnosis.
Case CHB72

Meera Nair, 58, a retired school administrator, attends a community pulmonary rehabilitation programme run by her local health service. Diagnosis: severe COPD with pulmonary hypertension and signs of cor pulmonale, confirmed by her respiratory physician. Medications are inhaled bronchodilators and a diuretic. Observations at rest: HR 88 bpm, BP 124/78 mmHg, RR 20, SpO2 91 percent on room air. Examination: raised jugular venous pressure, pitting oedema to mid shin and a loud second heart sound. mMRC dyspnoea grade 3. Six minute walk test: 240 m, with SpO2 falling to 84 percent, HR 104 and Borg dyspnoea 7 out of 10; she paused once for about a minute. She does not currently use home oxygen. The programme manager has asked every clinician to contribute to a service evaluation of programme outcomes this year and wants a consistent approach across staff.

  1. The correct answer is D (Use the same standardised protocol, course and instructions, and document her oxygen use and rests): A change in walking distance is only meaningful when the test conditions are repeated, including the course, instructions, encouragement and oxygen use, and when rests are recorded. A different test measures something else and cannot be compared directly. Comparing with predicted values describes impairment but does not show change from the programme.
  2. The correct answer is A (Report the desaturation to her physician and seek exercise oxygen advice before training): Marked exertional desaturation in pulmonary hypertension increases strain on the right ventricle, so the physician needs to decide whether exercise oxygen is required. Breathing techniques may ease symptoms but do not reliably restore saturation. Switching to arm work or interval work does not remove the risk and delays the medical decision.
  3. The correct answer is A (Right heart strain and failure are present, so exercise needs cautious grading and monitoring): Peripheral oedema with a raised jugular venous pressure and a loud pulmonary second sound points to a pressure loaded right ventricle that is struggling, which is cor pulmonale. This makes exercise capacity limited and calls for careful progression with symptom and saturation monitoring. Left heart failure would more typically show basal crackles and orthopnoea.
  4. The correct answer is C (Keep her seated, monitor observations until recovered, and arrange medical review before she exercises again): Near syncope on exertion in pulmonary hypertension signals that the right heart cannot raise output, so she needs observation and medical review before any further exercise. Lying flat can worsen breathlessness and venous return to a strained right ventricle. Resuming after rest, or on a saturation figure alone, treats the symptom without addressing the cause.
Case CHB73

Ms Divya Joshi, 44, office manager, presents to the emergency department with five days of worsening low back pain radiating down both legs. Pain is 8 out of 10. Since this morning she has had difficulty starting her urine stream and a sensation of incomplete emptying. She reports numbness 'like sitting on a cushion' between her legs. Observations: HR 82, BP 126/78, afebrile. Bilateral straight leg raise reproduces leg pain; ankle dorsiflexion is 4/5 on both sides; ankle reflexes are reduced bilaterally. No history of cancer or trauma, no fever. The department is busy: the physiotherapist also has a waiting list of referrals, and the triage nurse has asked for Ms Joshi's assessment to be completed quickly.

  1. The correct answer is A (Suspected cauda equina syndrome, a surgical emergency needing urgent imaging despite mild weakness): New bladder dysfunction with saddle sensory change and bilateral leg symptoms are the hallmark red flags of cauda equina syndrome. Mild weakness does not reduce urgency, because outcomes depend on decompression before bladder function is lost. Treating this as sciatica or infection risks permanent bladder, bowel and sexual dysfunction.
  2. The correct answer is C (Tell the emergency physician now using a concise summary, before any further testing): Suspected cauda equina is time critical, so the treating doctor is told directly and promptly with a clear summary. Further examination can follow or be done by the medical team. Waiting for a scan or only documenting and relying on the nurse to pass it on delays the decision to image.
  3. The correct answer is C (Withhold spinal treatment, position her comfortably, and recheck neurology and bladder regularly): With suspected cord or root compression, manual therapy or repeated spinal movements could worsen the injury and are not appropriate. Comfortable positioning and serial neurological and bladder checks allow early detection of deterioration. Heat and walking laps do not address the risk and may delay her being supervised.
  4. The correct answer is B (The woman with calf swelling and breathlessness, who needs urgent review for possible embolism): Sudden calf swelling with breathlessness suggests venous thromboembolism that may be life threatening, so she needs prompt medical assessment before any rehabilitation. Waiting time and bed flow matter, but they rank below risk to life. Ms Joshi is now with the medical team and imaging, so repeating her checks is not the best use of the next minutes.
Case CHB74

Mr Tevita Latu, 52, a Tongan-born electrician, is on day two after a spontaneous left basal ganglia intracerebral haemorrhage. He is intubated and ventilated, sedated (responds to voice), and has an external ventricular drain in situ. Head of bed is at 30 degrees. Baseline ICP is 12 mmHg, mean arterial pressure 92 mmHg and cerebral perfusion pressure 80 mmHg. The medical order is to notify the doctor if ICP stays above 20 mmHg. Chest: coarse crackles at the right base, moderate secretions, SpO2 96%. No movement of the right arm or leg to pain. Turning raises his ICP briefly to 18 mmHg. The physiotherapist is asked to treat his chest before the afternoon nursing handover, when the night nurse will take over his care.

  1. The correct answer is C (Head-up positioning, brief targeted techniques, nurse-coordinated suction, stopping if ICP stays above 20): A careful head-up approach with brief targeted techniques and nurse-coordinated suction treats the retained secretions while limiting rises in intracranial pressure, with an agreed stop point. Head-down tipping and vigorous percussion can raise ICP, and high-pressure hyperinflation increases intrathoracic pressure. Withholding treatment leaves a developing chest problem untreated when a modified approach is available.
  2. The correct answer is A (Stop treatment, keep his head up and neutral, and alert the nurse while watching the trace): A sustained ICP above the medical threshold means treatment should stop, with the head kept up and neutral so venous drainage is not impeded, and the nurse and medical team alerted. Finishing the pass or continuing treatment risks further rises. Lying him flat tends to increase ICP, and managing the drain is outside the physiotherapist's role.
  3. The correct answer is B (Give a structured verbal handover, document it in the notes, and confirm the nurse understands): Safety-critical information such as a sustained ICP rise needs a structured, timely verbal handover to the nurse now caring for him, backed by documentation, with confirmation that the message was understood. Notes alone may not be read promptly, a vague reassurance omits the event, and relaying through a later shift leaves the current team unaware.
  4. The correct answer is A (CPP is 62 mmHg, calculated as mean arterial pressure minus intracranial pressure): Cerebral perfusion pressure is the mean arterial pressure minus the intracranial pressure, which here gives 62 mmHg and shows how a rise in ICP erodes perfusion. Using systolic pressure overestimates perfusion, using diastolic pressure underestimates it, and adding ICP to the mean pressure reverses the relationship.
Case CHB75

Marco Lombardi, 74, a retired bus driver, is on day two after a cemented right total knee replacement for osteoarthritis. He may weight bear as tolerated. History: type 2 diabetes, hypertension, BMI 33. Pain is 5/10 with movement on regular analgesia, the wound is dry, swelling is mild and his calf is soft. Active knee flexion is 45 degrees and he has a 10 degree active extension lag. He walks 15 metres with a frame and stand-by assistance. He lives with his wife, who has early dementia and depends on him for meals and reminders, in a house with four entry steps with a rail and a shower over the bath. He is a public patient with a Pensioner Concession Card and has not had an aged care assessment. The ward plans discharge for day four or five.

  1. The correct answer is C (Static quadriceps contractions and assisted straight leg raises, with ice and elevation for swelling): An early extension lag after knee replacement is usually due to pain, swelling and quadriceps inhibition rather than weakness alone, so activation work with swelling control comes first. Heavy resistance and forceful stretching irritate the joint and can increase swelling and inhibition. Continuous passive motion adds little to active exercise outcomes.
  2. The correct answer is C (Home with outpatient or community rehabilitation and an assessment of his wife's support needs): He is mobilising independently with a frame, managing steps and is medically stable, so discharge home with ongoing rehabilitation suits him. His wife relies on him for meals, so her needs must be assessed and supports arranged before he leaves, rather than assumed. Inpatient rehabilitation is not warranted by a flexion target alone, and respite placement should follow assessment of what the couple want.
  3. The correct answer is A (Request an occupational therapy review for bathing equipment such as a shower chair): A high-sided bath is a risky transfer for a man with a fresh knee replacement, limited flexion and a need for both hands on the frame, so bathing equipment should be assessed and arranged before he leaves hospital, usually through occupational therapy. Swinging his legs over the edge risks a fall. Basin washing or nursing help only postpones the problem and does not support the independent showering he wants.
  4. The correct answer is A (Involve the discharge planner to arrange My Aged Care assessments for them both, including cost advice): Post-acute and community services are coordinated through the hospital discharge planner, and a person over 65 enters the aged care system through a My Aged Care assessment, which can also assess his wife's needs and any fees. The NDIS is mainly for people who acquire a disability before 65, so it is not the pathway here. A GP management plan funds only a limited number of allied health visits, so it would not cover all rehabilitation.

Practice Test 6 Answer Key

Case CHB76

Ethan Tuilagi, 3, admitted with left lower lobe pneumonia and wheeze. Background asthma. Nasopharyngeal swab is positive for influenza A. He is in a single room with a closing door and a droplet precautions sign; the room has no negative pressure. Observations: RR 38, HR 130, SpO2 93 percent on room air, temperature 38.2. Wet cough, left basal crackles and expiratory wheeze. His mother, who is also coughing, stays with him. Salbutamol via spacer was given by the nurse 20 minutes ago. Physiotherapy is requested for airway clearance, which may include nasopharyngeal suction. Another child on the ward, Hamish, 6, with leukaemia and neutropenia, is in protective isolation across the corridor and has also been referred to you today.

  1. The correct answer is B (Gown, gloves, eye protection and a fit checked P2 respirator, removed in the correct order): Influenza spreads mainly by droplets, but nasopharyngeal suction and induced coughing can generate aerosols, so a fit checked P2 respirator is added to gown, gloves and eye protection for the procedure. A surgical mask suffices only for routine droplet contact. Removal order matters to avoid contaminating yourself.
  2. The correct answer is A (Clean and disinfect it with a hospital grade wipe before it leaves the room): Equipment that touched an infected child must be cleaned and disinfected with a hospital grade wipe before it leaves the room, because carrying it out spreads contamination to the next child, including one who is neutropenic. A detergent wipe cleans but does not disinfect. Bagging it and treating it later still allows it to be used in the meantime on another patient.
  3. The correct answer is B (See Hamish first, then Ethan, with full PPE removal and hand hygiene between rooms): A neutropenic child is highly vulnerable to infection, so he is seen first, before any exposure to influenza. Full PPE removal, including mask and eye protection, and hand hygiene are needed between rooms. Seeing Ethan first because he seems sicker ignores that the referrals are equal priority, and reusing a mask between rooms defeats the protection.
  4. The correct answer is A (Use ISBAR to report the deterioration and observations, then request urgent medical review): A structured ISBAR handover gives the nurse the key facts at once: who you are, the deterioration with observations, your assessment and what you want done. With a falling saturation in a young child, urgent medical review is the recommendation. Waiting for routine handover or leaving the decision to the nurse delays escalation, and bypassing the nurse breaks team communication.
Case CHB77

Kofi Abara, 52, accountant, day 3 after craniotomy and resection of a left frontal high-grade glioma. Medications: dexamethasone, levetiracetam. He ran 5 km three times a week before surgery and wants to return to running and desk work. Observations: BP 128/78, HR 76, SpO2 97% on room air, GCS 15, headache 3/10, no seizures since surgery. Right shoulder abduction 3/5, right grip 4-/5, right ankle dorsiflexion 3/5 with the foot catching in swing. Sensation intact, tone normal, mild word-finding hesitation. Sits on the edge of the bed with supervision and is slightly impulsive. Neurosurgeon: mobilise as tolerated. His wife, Grace, attends sessions and asks what she should be doing. They live in a two-storey house with the bedroom upstairs. Discharge home is planned in three days.

  1. The correct answer is C (Damage near the left motor cortex or descending pathways reduces drive to right-sided muscles): Motor pathways from one cerebral hemisphere control the opposite side of the body, so a left frontal lesion or its surgical bed produces right-sided weakness. A peripheral nerve injury would not usually affect shoulder, grip and ankle together with a speech change. Steroid myopathy is proximal and symmetrical, so it does not explain a one-sided pattern.
  2. The correct answer is D (Walk a short distance with close guarding, cue foot clearance, and trial an ankle splint): A short, closely guarded walk lets you observe foot clearance and impulsivity safely, and an ankle splint addresses the catching foot that threatens a fall. Walking independently with a stick ignores both the impulsivity and the drop foot. Waiting for spontaneous recovery delays gait practice that supports early recovery, and relying on cueing alone leaves the tripping hazard in place.
  3. The correct answer is C (Lower him safely to sit, stay with him, and activate the urgent medical response): Sudden severe headache, vomiting tendency, drowsiness and new speech change after brain surgery suggest a possible bleed or raised intracranial pressure, which is time critical. He must be made safe where he is and the urgent response called while someone stays with him. Moving him further or waiting to see whether symptoms settle delays treatment.
  4. The correct answer is C (Follow a graded walking programme, progress by symptoms, and check driving advice with the team): A graded programme guided by symptoms builds fitness safely while fatigue, foot catching and seizure risk are still present, and driving and seizure precautions belong with the neurosurgical team. Returning to running on the basis of past fitness ignores the foot catching and the recent surgery. Prolonged rest causes deconditioning, and his wife should support rather than lead his programme.
Case CHB78

Daniel Nguyen, 34, software developer, video consultation from home. Three months of left jaw pain in front of the ear, worse with chewing firm food and during work deadlines. He reports clicking when opening and closing. No injury, fever, facial swelling, ear discharge or numbness. Pain is 3/10 at rest and 6/10 when chewing. He clenches when concentrating and sleeps face down. On video, with a ruler held against his incisors, maximum opening is 42 mm. The jaw deviates to the left in mid-opening, then returns to midline after an audible click. Lateral excursions are symmetrical and pain free. Neck screening is unremarkable. He takes no regular medication and lives alone. The clinic's allied health assistant, Priya, has capacity for video check-ins, and your caseload is full this month.

  1. The correct answer is A (Left disc displacement with reduction, where the disc recaptures as the condyle translates): Deviation that corrects to midline after a click, with near-normal opening, fits a disc that sits forward at rest but is recaptured as the condyle translates. If the disc were blocking translation, opening would be limited and the deviation would persist toward the affected side. Hypermobility usually shows a wide opening with a catch near the end of range rather than a mid-range click.
  2. The correct answer is D (Education, a relaxed jaw position with teeth apart, and gentle controlled opening exercises): First-line care for jaw pain without red flags is education, reassurance, habit change and gentle graded exercise, which suit home practice between video sessions. Splints are optional adjuncts and should not delay active self-management. Forced stretching and prolonged strict rest both aggravate or deactivate the joint when opening is already near normal.
  3. The correct answer is C (Go to the emergency department today for reduction, and arrange for someone to drive him): Jaw locked open after yawning suggests dislocation, which needs prompt medical assessment, usually with reduction, and he should not wait or force it. Reduction attempts over video cannot be supervised safely and can injure the joint or soft tissues. Heat and exercises do not address the mechanical block and only delay care.
  4. The correct answer is B (Delegate the check-ins with written instructions, while you keep reassessment and progression decisions): Delegating routine, well-defined tasks to a trained assistant with clear instructions is appropriate, but the physiotherapist remains accountable for assessment, clinical reasoning and changes to the plan. Letting the assistant progress exercises or reassess hands over decisions that need a physiotherapist's judgement. Declining outright wastes available support and leaves Daniel without follow-up.
Case CHB79

Linda Cheng, 52, bookkeeper, admitted to the acute ward for pain review. Seven weeks ago she fractured her left distal radius, treated in a plaster cast for six weeks. Since cast removal her hand has become increasingly painful, swollen and stiff. Examination: left hand and wrist swollen and shiny with patchy colour change, skin warmer and sweatier than the right. Light touch causes sharp pain (allodynia). Wrist flexion and extension each about 15 degrees, and she is unable to make a fist. Grip is not testable. Pain 8 out of 10, burning, disproportionate to the healed fracture. X-ray reports the fracture united. Neurology above the wrist is normal. The medical team suspects complex regional pain syndrome and has reviewed her analgesia. Linda is afraid to move the hand. The ward currently uses a numeric pain scale only.

  1. The correct answer is A (Allodynia with temperature, colour and sweating changes, and pain out of proportion to the injury): Complex regional pain syndrome is a clinical diagnosis recognised by pain out of proportion plus sensory (allodynia), vasomotor and sudomotor changes, as Linda shows. Stiffness after casting gives mechanical restriction with a firm end-feel and aching that eases with rest and elevation. Carpal tunnel symptoms follow a median nerve pattern without the skin and temperature changes seen here.
  2. The correct answer is B (Graded desensitisation and active, pain-limited use of the hand, building exposure step by step): Management emphasises early active use with graded exposure to touch and movement, since immobility and fear sustain sensitisation. Forceful stretching provokes pain and flares, while splinting and prolonged rest promote stiffness and disuse. Treatment is paced within tolerable pain and agreed with Linda.
  3. The correct answer is A (Withhold hand therapy and inform the medical team promptly about possible infection): Complex regional pain syndrome commonly causes warmth and redness, but broken oozing skin with a fever points to infection, which needs prompt medical assessment, and bathing or desensitisation could worsen it. Documenting and waiting delays treatment, and explaining the signs away as vasomotor change risks missing sepsis.
  4. The correct answer is A (A patient-reported arm function score such as QuickDASH, plus pain rating, recorded consistently): A validated patient-reported measure of limb function, paired with pain ratings and taken at the same time points for every patient, shows whether care changes outcomes that matter to patients. Grip strength is unreliable and distressing in this condition, and length of stay or satisfaction describe the service rather than patient function.
Case CHB80

Mr Emeka Banda, 28, a former builder, has C6 ASIA impairment scale A tetraplegia after a fall from scaffolding 14 months ago. He lives in a rented unit in Brisbane with part-time attendant carers funded through the NDIS, and you visit for community physiotherapy. Biceps and wrist extensors are 4/5, triceps 0/5 bilaterally, and he has useful tenodesis grip on both hands for feeding and writing. He uses a manual wheelchair indoors with power-assist wheels. He reports increasing right shoulder pain with transfers, pushing and overhead reaching. Skin is intact. His mood seems low: he has been using pain tablets more often and has not left home in weeks. His carer says he skipped his bowel routine twice this week. He tells you that you are the only person who listens, and asks to add you on social media and to meet for coffee on weekends.

  1. The correct answer is D (Some independent level transfers and upper body self-care with equipment, but help for lower body care): With C6 function and tenodesis, people can often achieve some level transfers and upper body self-care with adaptive equipment, though lower body care and bowel care usually need assistance. Expecting full independence overstates what the muscle function allows, and expecting total dependence underestimates it. The independence described at C8 depends on triceps and finger function he does not have.
  2. The correct answer is A (Review transfer and pushing technique, train scapular stabilisers, and adjust equipment to cut repetitive load): Shoulder pain in wheelchair users comes from repetitive high loading, so the best approach changes technique and equipment and strengthens the scapular stabilisers and external rotators. Resting then returning to the same loading simply resets the problem. Strengthening already dominant anterior muscles worsens imbalance, and a sling hinders safe transfers.
  3. The correct answer is B (Stretch fingers with the wrist flexed and wrist with fingers flexed, keeping some finger flexor tightness): Tenodesis works because wrist extension passively tightens the finger flexors and closes the fingers, so the finger flexors must stay slightly short across the combined range. Stretching fingers and wrist separately preserves joint range without losing that tension. Stretching both together or splinting them in full extension abolishes the grip, while stopping all stretching risks fixed wrist flexion.
  4. The correct answer is C (Decline the social contact kindly, ask about his safety, and arrange GP and peer support): A therapeutic relationship has boundaries, so the request is declined warmly, while his isolation and comment about being a burden call for a direct check on his wellbeing and links to his GP and peer support. Socialising or connecting online blurs the relationship even with safeguards. Discharging him abandons a vulnerable person and fails the duty of care.
Case CHB81

Hassan Khoury, 78, a retired grocer, attends community physiotherapy three weeks after hospital admission for decompensated heart failure. He has heart failure with reduced ejection fraction (30 percent), NYHA class III, and takes an ACE inhibitor, beta blocker, mineralocorticoid antagonist and frusemide. He lives with his wife, walks with a stick and has stopped his usual walks. Today: HR 76 regular, BP 108/64 mmHg, SpO2 95 percent, RR 18. He has mild bilateral ankle oedema and sleeps on two pillows. Six minute walk test: 240 m with a Borg dyspnoea score of 4 at the end, and no chest pain or dizziness. His wife says he weighed 80.9 kg three days ago and 83.1 kg this morning, but they have not been weighing him daily. He has not yet told anyone about the change. He asks whether exercise is safe for his heart.

  1. The correct answer is A (Limit today to light activity, and arrange same-day contact with his GP or heart failure service): A rise of about 2 kg over three days with ankle oedema and sleeping on two pillows suggests fluid retention, so he needs prompt medical review the same day and only light activity meanwhile. Telling the GP at some later point, or reviewing at the next visit, delays that review. Advising fluid and salt limits and carrying on is outside the physiotherapist's role and ignores the early warning.
  2. The correct answer is C (Interval walking at RPE 11 to 13 plus low-load resistance exercise, progressed gradually): In stable heart failure with reduced ejection fraction, supervised interval walking at light to somewhat hard effort combined with low-load resistance work improves function safely and can be progressed. Beginning with continuous hard exercise or high-intensity intervals is too demanding for NYHA class III. Excluding resistance training is outdated because low-load strength work is beneficial and well tolerated.
  3. The correct answer is A (Explain how daily weights catch fluid gain early, then agree a written action plan together): Self-management works best when the reason for each task is explained and the plan is agreed together, here showing that daily weights give early warning of fluid gain before symptoms appear. Weekly weights miss rapid changes like his recent gain. A printed chart without discussion does not address his wife's doubt, and waiting for symptoms ignores the early signal that weights provide.
  4. The correct answer is D (Stop exercise, sit him with legs raised, check observations, and arrange medical review): A fall in systolic pressure during exercise with symptoms is an abnormal haemodynamic response in heart failure and a criterion to stop, with observations and medical review before further exercise. Carrying on or resuming after a short rest risks collapse. A drink and breathing exercises do not address a possible cardiac cause.
Case CHB82

Mr Viliami Matagi, 42, a truck driver, has T4 ASIA A paraplegia after a work-related motor vehicle crash five weeks ago. He is on an acute spinal unit awaiting transfer to rehabilitation, and a compensable workers claim is open. He has an indwelling urinary catheter and wears an abdominal binder. Supine observations: BP 105/65, HR 66. Two days ago nursing staff noted a pounding headache with flushing and sweating above the level of injury during a bowel procedure; it settled when the procedure stopped. Today is his first session in a tilting wheelchair after weeks of bed rest, and the plan is progressive sitting. He and his wife are anxious about discharge and want to know what supports and equipment he will be able to access at home. A nurse is working beside you on the unit.

  1. The correct answer is C (Tilt him back and raise his legs promptly, then monitor blood pressure until symptoms settle): Symptomatic orthostatic hypotension after spinal cord injury is managed by immediately returning him toward a reclined position with the legs raised, which restores venous return, and by monitoring until recovery. Staying upright prolongs cerebral hypoperfusion. Fluids are a medical decision and should not delay the position change, and the binder is a preventive measure, not a rescue.
  2. The correct answer is D (An irritating stimulus below the injury triggers a reflex blood pressure rise the brain cannot control): In autonomic dysreflexia a painful or irritating stimulus below the lesion, such as a full bowel or bladder, provokes a reflex surge of sympathetic activity and high blood pressure that descending control cannot dampen. The headache comes from the rise in pressure, not a fall. Infection can be a trigger but is not the mechanism, and the heart rate usually slows rather than rises.
  3. The correct answer is D (Keep him sitting up, loosen binders and clothing, relieve the kinked tubing, and call the nurse): Autonomic dysreflexia is a medical emergency in which the upright position helps lower the pressure, so he stays sitting while tight items are loosened and the likely trigger, here the kinked catheter, is relieved and help is called. Lying flat is the response to low pressure and would raise it further. Waiting ten minutes delays treatment, and rectal procedures should be done only by trained staff after the bladder is addressed.
  4. The correct answer is C (Explain the compensable claim is the likely funder, and link them with the case manager): Costs of a work-related injury are normally met through the compensable scheme, so the case manager and the unit's social worker are the right contacts for equipment and modification approvals. The NDIS does not simply replace an open compensation claim. Medicare does not fund home modifications, and My Aged Care serves older people rather than a 42 year old.
Case CHB83

Kim Huynh is a 7 month old girl brought to the community child health centre by her mother on day 4 of a viral chest illness diagnosed by the GP as bronchiolitis. She was born at term and has no chronic lung disease. Her mother reports a wet cough, noisy breathing at night and feeds of about half the usual volume. Observations: RR 52 breaths per minute, HR 150, SpO2 96 percent in room air, temperature 37.6 degrees. She is alert and interacting, with mild subcostal recession, intermittent nasal flaring, widespread fine crackles and an expiratory wheeze. Her nose is blocked with visible secretions. The clinic manager has asked whether Tomasz, an allied health assistant with paediatric program experience, could take over Kim's sessions and home visits while the physiotherapist covers other clients.

  1. The correct answer is D (Routine percussion and vibration show no clear benefit, so supportive care is the focus): Reviews of airway clearance in otherwise well infants with bronchiolitis show that percussion, vibration and similar techniques do not shorten illness or improve outcomes, and they can distress the infant. Care is supportive: feeding support, clearing the nose when needed and watching for deterioration. Vibration over crackling zones is tempting, but the crackles reflect widespread small airway obstruction that manual techniques do not shift.
  2. The correct answer is B (Gentle nasal suction of visible secretions before feeds, with upright holding between smaller feeds): Infants breathe mainly through the nose, so a blocked nose directly worsens feeding and work of breathing. Gentle suction of visible secretions before feeds, with upright holding, is low risk and helps. Deep nasopharyngeal suction is not routine because it causes distress and mucosal trauma, and head-down or prone positioning adds reflux and safety concerns without proven benefit.
  3. The correct answer is C (Arrange urgent transfer to the emergency department with a handover of her findings): Poor intake with fewer wet nappies, drowsiness, grunting, marked recession and an SpO2 of 91 percent signal dehydration and impending respiratory failure that need hospital assessment and likely oxygen and fluid support. Waiting to reassess or booking a later GP review risks further deterioration. A clear handover lets the receiving team see the trajectory from the community findings.
  4. The correct answer is B (Retain assessment, delegate defined tasks within his training, and set supervision and escalation steps): The physiotherapist stays accountable for assessment, clinical reasoning and the plan, and may delegate only defined tasks within the assistant's training, with supervision matched to the risk. Because Kim's condition can change quickly, escalation steps must be explicit before any home visit. Experience, parental consent or a GP referral does not make independent assessment by an assistant appropriate.
Case CHB84

Wiremu Tane, 11 weeks old, was born at term in breech presentation. He is the first child and his parents are anxious. He was referred by the maternal child health nurse for head shape. Parents report he looks mostly to the left, tilts his head to the right and prefers lying with the right back of his head on the mattress. Flattening at the right back of the head has been noticed since about six weeks. Findings: head tilt to the right with chin turned left; passive rotation to the right limited to about 60 degrees (left full); left lateral flexion limited; right sternocleidomastoid feels tight without a discrete mass. From above, the right ear sits forward of the left. He tolerates tummy time for about one minute. He reaches and kicks symmetrically. Feeding well and growing along his centile. The parents ask for a plan and mention an online article about helmets.

  1. The correct answer is C (Right sternocleidomastoid, which pulls the head toward its own side and turns the face away): A tight sternocleidomastoid tilts the head to its own side and rotates the face to the opposite side, which matches his right tilt and left-facing chin, and it limits passive rotation toward the tight side. A tight left muscle would reverse the posture. Splenius capitis and levator scapulae turn the face toward the tilted side, so they cannot account for this combination.
  2. The correct answer is D (Gentle neck stretches, short frequent supervised tummy time, and toys placed to encourage turning right): Early torticollis is managed with gentle stretching, active positioning and handling that encourages turning toward the restricted side, plus supervised tummy time that builds neck strength and takes pressure off the flat area. Treating the neck alone ignores the contribution of lying position. Objects placed in the cot are not safe for sleep, and prolonged back-lying for play worsens flattening.
  3. The correct answer is C (Refer promptly to the GP or paediatrician for hip assessment and imaging, and tell parents why): Breech birth and torticollis are both associated with developmental hip dysplasia, and limited abduction with asymmetric folds is a concerning combination. Imaging and medical assessment are needed promptly, and the parents should understand the reason. Negative clinical tests do not exclude dysplasia, and waiting or advising swaddling alone delays confirmation.
  4. The correct answer is C (Acknowledge his concern, explain testimonials are weak evidence, and that repositioning is first-line for mild cases): Testimonials and commercial claims are low-quality evidence and cannot separate a helmet's effect from natural improvement as babies grow and become more mobile. For mild flattening, repositioning and physiotherapy are the recommended first-line measures. Respecting the father's concern and explaining the reasoning supports shared decisions, whereas dismissing him or endorsing helmets without evidence does not.
Case CHB85

Ms Linh Tran, 54, a primary school teacher, has non-cystic fibrosis bronchiectasis and attends a private respiratory physiotherapy practice. She produces about a cup of thick mucopurulent sputum daily, mostly on waking, and has had three exacerbations in the past year. History: gastro-oesophageal reflux, worse when lying flat or head down. Observations: RR 16, SpO2 96 percent on room air, HR 74, afebrile. Chest: coarse crackles at both bases that reduce after coughing, good air entry elsewhere. She coughs repeatedly with little result. A friend sent her an online article claiming a high-frequency chest wall oscillation vest 'doubles sputum clearance'; it cites one small, unblinded study of 12 people funded by the manufacturer. She asks whether she should buy a vest and wants an airway clearance plan she can complete twice a day, before work and in the evening.

  1. The correct answer is B (Thoracic expansion exercises: deep breaths with relaxed expiration, sometimes with a brief hold): Thoracic expansion exercises use deep inspiration to ventilate behind obstructed airways via collateral channels, helping loosen secretions. Breathing control only gives rest between cycles. Huffs at different volumes move secretions that are already loosened toward the mouth, so they do not mainly loosen them.
  2. The correct answer is B (Point out the study's weaknesses and compare the claim with guidelines and systematic reviews): Appraising the design (small sample, no blinding, no comparison group, commercial funding) shows a high risk of bias, and the claim should then be tested against guidelines and systematic reviews. Dismissing all devices skips appraisal, and accepting one favourable study ignores its flaws. Manufacturer data are not independent evidence.
  3. The correct answer is C (Modified drainage in horizontal side-lying or sitting positions, avoiding any head-down tilt): Head-down tilt aggravates reflux and raises the risk of aspiration, so drainage is better achieved in horizontal side-lying or sitting positions, using the breathing cycle to move secretions. Standard tilted drainage is the textbook habit but is unsuitable here. Percussion does not replace positioning, and prone lying over a pillow loads the abdomen and does not solve the reflux problem.
  4. The correct answer is A (Withhold the planned airway clearance, keep her sitting upright and arrange urgent medical review): A mouthful of fresh blood with breathlessness, dizziness and a falling saturation suggests significant haemoptysis, so airway clearance is paused, she stays upright and she needs urgent medical review, with an ambulance if bleeding continues. Gentle techniques or pressure devices can provoke more bleeding. Pausing and rechecking later still delays assessment of a possible haemorrhage.
Case CHB86

Meera Kapoor, 46, a primary school teacher, has had right neck and arm pain for five weeks, starting without injury. Pain runs from the neck over the shoulder blade to the lateral forearm, thumb and index finger, with pins and needles in the thumb. Pain 6/10, worse with looking up and turning right, eased with her hand on her head. Sleep is disturbed. No bilateral symptoms, gait change, fever or history of cancer.
