Medical history nil; her GP prescribed ibuprofen and referred her to you, asking for a progress letter. Examination: cervical extension and right rotation limited to about 30 degrees and reproduce arm symptoms, Spurling test positive on the right, distraction relieves arm pain, upper limb tension test (median bias) positive on the right, elbow flexion 4/5, wrist extension 4/5, reduced light touch over the right thumb, biceps reflex reduced on the right, Hoffmann sign negative.

  1. The correct answer is D (C6, supported by the thumb dermatome, biceps reflex change and elbow and wrist weakness): C6 supplies the thumb and lateral forearm, contributes to the biceps reflex and drives elbow flexion and wrist extension, so all her findings converge on this root. C5 would give shoulder abduction weakness and lateral upper arm sensory change. C7 involves the triceps and middle finger, and C8 the little finger side of the hand.
  2. The correct answer is D (Education, easing positions, deep neck flexor and scapular exercises, and gentle mobilisation within tolerance): Cervical radiculopathy usually responds to education, positions that open the foramen, gentle mobilisation and targeted strengthening, which also gives her a home plan. Repeated end-range loading into the provoking directions would aggravate the root. Thrust manipulation near an irritable root is not a sensible first step, and prolonged collar rest causes deconditioning.
  3. The correct answer is B (Stop cervical loading and manual techniques, and arrange urgent medical review for suspected cervical myelopathy): Hand clumsiness, gait unsteadiness, bilateral hyper-reflexia and a positive Hoffmann sign indicate upper motor neuron signs from cord compression, which needs urgent medical assessment and MRI. Reduced arm pain does not mean improvement here. Continuing treatment or ordering a routine X-ray delays recognition of a condition where timely surgery protects function.
  4. The correct answer is B (Findings and their change over time, urgency of concern, treatment given, and the action requested): Effective handover states what was found, how it has changed, why it is urgent, what has been done and what is being asked of the recipient. A long chronological account buries the key message. A vague note leaves the GP unaware of the urgency, and background detail about work and goals does not serve the immediate safety issue.
Case CHB87

Lan Do, 31, is 28 weeks pregnant with her second child and works in a nail salon. She has had asthma since childhood. Her obstetrician referred her from the antenatal clinic because of breathlessness on stairs and uncertainty about exercise. Her first language is Vietnamese and she speaks limited English. Her husband attended and offers to translate.

History: asthma well controlled with a preventer inhaler and a reliever used about once a week. No night cough. No leg pain or swelling. Non-smoker.

Observations: HR 92, BP 112/68, RR 18, SpO2 97 percent on room air. Chest clear with no wheeze. Talks in full sentences. Breathlessness on stairs rated 3 out of 10 on the modified Borg scale. Fundal height appropriate for gestation. She has not exercised since early pregnancy and wants to start walking.

  1. The correct answer is C (Progesterone raises ventilatory drive, so mild breathlessness is common and expected in healthy pregnancy): Progesterone increases the sensitivity of the respiratory centre, so resting ventilation rises and a mild sense of breathlessness is normal when the chest is clear and saturation is preserved. Tidal volume actually increases rather than falls, which is why a claim of reduced tidal volume is wrong. Normal saturation and a clear chest also argue against dilutional desaturation or a stiff chest wall as the cause.
  2. The correct answer is A (Moderate walking guided by the talk test, carrying her reliever, avoiding long spells lying flat): For a healthy pregnancy, moderate aerobic activity is encouraged, and the talk test is a practical gauge because fixed heart rate ceilings are no longer recommended. Carrying the reliever suits her asthma, and prolonged supine positioning is avoided later in pregnancy because the uterus can compress the vena cava and reduce venous return. Withholding exercise or pushing to vigorous levels does not fit her uncomplicated, well controlled picture.
  3. The correct answer is D (Stop the session, keep her resting, and arrange urgent medical assessment for suspected pulmonary embolism): Unilateral calf pain and swelling with pleuritic chest pain, tachycardia, tachypnoea and a saturation of 92% in pregnancy suggest deep vein thrombosis with possible pulmonary embolism, which is an emergency needing urgent medical assessment. Asthma treatment does not explain the calf signs, and booking review later or sending her to the GP delays imaging and anticoagulation she may need now.
  4. The correct answer is B (Book an accredited interpreter, brief them first, and address Lan directly throughout the session): Accredited interpreters are trained in accuracy and confidentiality, and briefing them first improves the session. Family members may filter, add or omit content and may inhibit a woman's own answers, which matters when she is hesitant. Speaking to the patient directly keeps her at the centre of care, whereas apps and untrained helpers risk errors in safety information.
Case CHB88

Mrs Giuseppina Rossi, 79, retired cleaner, attends private practice with her son Marco. She has had right hip osteoarthritis for three years, with pain 6/10 on walking and rising from low chairs and morning stiffness of about 20 minutes. X-ray last year showed moderate joint space narrowing. Her GP notes mild memory problems, but she lives independently, manages her own medication and shops by bus. Right hip flexion is 95 degrees, internal rotation 10 degrees, hip abductors 4/5, and she has an antalgic gait without an aid. She completes 7 sit-to-stands in 30 seconds. BMI is 31. Marco answers most questions and says, "Just do whatever you think, I will decide for her." Mrs Rossi looks at the floor and says she is worried about making it worse.

  1. The correct answer is A (Suitably dosed exercise strengthens muscles, eases pain and does not speed up joint damage over time): Exercise is a first-line treatment for hip osteoarthritis, and appropriately dosed loading improves pain and function without evidence of accelerating joint degeneration. Waiting for pain to resolve or avoiding strengthening leads to deconditioning, which worsens disability.
  2. The correct answer is A (Graded lower limb strengthening plus walking, allowing tolerable pain, with a home programme): Guidelines favour individualised graded strengthening and walking, with some pain during exercise accepted if it settles, plus a home programme to support adherence. Waiting for pain to disappear before loading, using water exercise only, or delaying strengthening until stiffness improves all keep a deconditioned older woman under-loaded and slow her gains in strength and function.
  3. The correct answer is B (Reassure her that flares settle, modify loads for a few days and keep moving gently): A transient, load-related flare without red flags is common in osteoarthritis and is managed by temporary load modification, education and continued gentle movement. Complete rest leads to deconditioning, urgent referral is not warranted without red flags, and ignoring a flare risks prolonging it.
  4. The correct answer is A (Presume capacity, explain the options and risks to her directly, and seek her own agreement): Adults are presumed to have capacity, and capacity is specific to each decision, so a mild memory problem alone does not remove her right to consent. The physiotherapist should communicate with her directly, support her understanding and let Marco assist only with her agreement. A family member does not hold automatic authority to consent.
Case CHB89

Despina Papas, 68, retired teacher who gardens daily, presents one week after slipping on a wet lawn with her left knee forced inward. She had immediate medial knee pain and now has swelling and bruising running down the medial calf. History: atrial fibrillation on warfarin, hypertension, type 2 diabetes. Examination: small effusion, flexion 5 to 115 degrees limited by pain, tender over the proximal medial collateral ligament, valgus stress at 30 degrees painful with mild opening and a firm end point, stable at 0 degrees. Lachman and posterior drawer negative, McMurray negative, no patellar or fibular head tenderness. She took four steps unaided. Calf soft and non-tender. Pain 4/10. She asks whether she should stop warfarin for a few days to settle the bruising, and whether you can recommend a stronger painkiller.

  1. The correct answer is C (Refer for knee radiographs, because her age alone meets a criterion of the rule): The Ottawa knee rule includes age 55 or older as a criterion for radiography after acute knee trauma, so Despina qualifies despite normal weight bearing and flexion. Absence of the other criteria does not cancel the age criterion. MRI is not the first test to exclude fracture, and delaying imaging leaves a possible fracture undiagnosed.
  2. The correct answer is B (Early pain-limited movement, quadriceps strengthening and weight bearing as tolerated, using a brace if needed): A partial medial collateral ligament sprain with a firm end point heals well with early protected motion, quadriceps work and weight bearing as tolerated, with a hinged brace for comfort if required. Rigid immobilisation causes stiffness and weakness. Friction with prolonged rest delays loading, and unrestricted kneeling and squatting stresses the healing ligament too early.
  3. The correct answer is A (Advise against stopping warfarin without medical advice, and direct her medication questions to her GP): Adjusting anticoagulants or choosing systemic medicines is outside physiotherapy scope, and stopping warfarin raises her stroke risk from atrial fibrillation. The physiotherapist should advise her not to change it herself and send medication questions to the GP or pharmacist. Recommending anti-inflammatory tablets also adds a bleeding risk for a person on warfarin.
  4. The correct answer is B (Arrange same day medical assessment to exclude bleeding, deep vein thrombosis or infection): A tense warm knee, rapidly increasing swelling, calf tenderness and loss of weight bearing in an anticoagulated patient could mean significant bleeding, a clot or infection, so same day medical assessment is needed. Compression is unsafe if a clot is possible. A negative bedside screen cannot exclude it, and a letter or waiting delays urgent care.
Case CHB90

Mrs Jean Walker, 61, a Wiradjuri woman, attends outpatient physiotherapy 8 weeks after a left middle cerebral artery stroke. She has travelled from a rural town with her sister. Her right arm is flaccid with shoulder flexion 1/5 and no active hand movement. There is a palpable gap below the acromion, and pain of 6/10 when the arm hangs unsupported or when carers help her up by the arm. Passive external rotation is limited to about 20 degrees by pain. There is no hand swelling or colour change. She walks with a stick. BP 138/84. She is quiet, but says family input matters to her health decisions and asks whether a Health Worker from her local Aboriginal Community Controlled Health Service can be involved.

  1. The correct answer is D (Weak rotator cuff and deltoid muscles let the weight of the arm stretch the joint capsule): In the flaccid stage the stabilising muscles that normally hold the humeral head in the glenoid are weak, so gravity stretches the capsule and ligaments. Spasticity belongs to later stages and tends to cause internal rotation contracture, not a gap. A shortened, stiff capsule would restrict rather than permit inferior translation.
  2. The correct answer is C (Support the forearm on a pillow, place the scapula forward, and assist from the trunk): Supporting the arm in a good position and avoiding traction through it protects the unstable joint, and carers need to learn safe handling. Overhead pulleys load the shoulder in a vulnerable direction, and prolonged slings encourage contracture and neglect without proving that they prevent subluxation. Fixed internal rotation promotes stiffness.
  3. The correct answer is A (Gentle pain-free passive movement within available range with scapular mobilisation, avoiding forceful end-range stretching): Gentle pain-free movement maintains range and scapular mobility without further stressing the stretched capsule of an unstable shoulder. Forceful stretching and pulleys risk damage and increase pain. Complete avoidance of passive movement allows stiffness to develop, which would limit later function.
  4. The correct answer is A (Welcome her sister and the Health Worker, check her preferences, and let her choose who joins): Culturally safe practice puts Mrs Walker in control of who supports her and treats community services as partners from the start. Delaying contact with the Health Worker takes control from her, and sending decisions through her sister does the same. Briefing family first shifts decision making away from the patient, who has capacity and decides who is involved.

Practice Test 7 Answer Key

Case CHB91

Sophia Stavrou, 77, is on day 2 after an open anterior resection for bowel cancer through a midline abdominal incision. She is a retired seamstress with mild hypertension and a 20 year history of smoking, stopped 30 years ago. Analgesia is by patient controlled analgesia pump and regular paracetamol. She has an abdominal drain and a urinary catheter.

Observations this morning: HR 96, BP 128/70, RR 22, SpO2 92 percent on 2 L via nasal prongs. Shallow upper chest breathing, reduced air entry at both bases with fine crackles on the right. She rates pain 7 out of 10 on coughing and avoids deep breaths. Currently in bed in a semi-recumbent position. Her ward is auditing physiotherapy outcomes after major abdominal surgery.

  1. The correct answer is D (Pain and reduced diaphragm movement cause shallow breathing, lowering lung volume and collapsing dependent airways): After laparotomy pain, splinting and diaphragmatic dysfunction produce shallow breathing, a lower functional residual capacity and closure of dependent airways, so the bases collapse. Mucus plugging, fluid and ageing can contribute, but none explains the pattern as directly in a patient who is avoiding deep breaths because of pain.
  2. The correct answer is C (Incidence of postoperative pulmonary complications within seven days, using a consistent written definition): An outcome measure should reflect the patient outcome the service aims to change, and postoperative pulmonary complications with a consistent definition directly capture this. Treatment counts measure activity not effect, satisfaction measures experience, and ward-wide length of stay is influenced by many factors beyond the physiotherapy service.
  3. The correct answer is B (Sit her out and mobilise, with sustained deep breaths and a supported cough over the wound): Upright positions and walking raise lung volumes and combine well with sustained deep breathing and wound support, and her pain is controlled enough right now to take advantage of it. Lying flat reduces functional residual capacity, percussion does not reinflate collapsed lung, and delaying exercise until she walks far enough misses the window.
  4. The correct answer is B (Stop treatment, rouse her, call for urgent medical help and tell the nurse about possible sedation): Reduced consciousness, a respiratory rate of 9, falling saturation and small pupils after repeated pump presses point to opioid induced respiratory depression, which is a medical emergency needing urgent medical help. Securing the handset or waiting to reassess oxygen delays that call. A glucose check is sensible later but does not explain pinpoint pupils and slow breathing, and sitting her up for breathing work treats atelectasis rather than sedation.
Case CHB92

Joan Whitby, 64, a retired seamstress, attends a community health centre with hand pain and stiffness. She has seropositive rheumatoid arthritis, diagnosed three years ago and managed by her rheumatologist with disease-modifying medication. Her GP has also noted mild memory difficulties. She lives with her husband, and her daughter Helen drives her to appointments and attends today. Findings: firm bony nodes at the distal interphalangeal joints of both hands, cool and non-tender. The right second to fourth metacarpophalangeal joints are warm, swollen and tender. Morning stiffness lasts about 90 minutes. Grip strength is reduced on both sides and jar opening provokes pain of 6 out of 10. Wrist extension is full. She wants to keep sewing and gardening. Helen asks that custom resting splints be made today and says her mother will agree to whatever is suggested. Joan looks unsure and says little.

  1. The correct answer is A (Heberden nodes typical of osteoarthritis, which coexists with her disease and is not active synovitis): Firm, cool, non-tender bony nodes at the distal joints are Heberden nodes, a sign of osteoarthritis that commonly sits alongside rheumatoid disease. Active rheumatoid synovitis looks different: warm, boggy swelling, as seen at her metacarpophalangeal joints. Bouchard nodes are the proximal equivalent, so naming them at the distal joints is wrong.
  2. The correct answer is D (Gentle active movement within pain limits, with night resting splints for the inflamed joints): During a flare, inflamed joints tolerate gentle active movement within pain limits, which maintains range without stressing the synovium, and resting splints at night support the joints. Resisted gripping and end-range stretching load inflamed capsules and can aggravate synovitis. Prolonged rigid immobilisation risks stiffness and weakness.
  3. The correct answer is C (Splint the fingers and urgently contact her rheumatologist about suspected extensor tendon rupture): Sudden loss of active finger extension with full passive range, in a person with rheumatoid arthritis, suggests extensor tendon rupture from synovial attrition. This needs a prompt specialist opinion because delay reduces repair options, and splinting protects the remaining tendons meanwhile. Exercise or waiting risks further rupture, and a nerve cause is unlikely when passive movement is full and pain is minimal.
  4. The correct answer is C (Presume capacity, explain the splints plainly, and let Joan make the decision herself): Adults are presumed to have capacity unless shown otherwise, and mild memory difficulties do not remove the right to decide. The physiotherapist should give clear information, check understanding and let Joan choose, with Helen supporting only if Joan wants that. A relative cannot consent for a person who has capacity, and formal assessment is not needed without real doubt about her decision-making ability.
Case CHB93

Daniel Pereira, 38, is a recreational runner and accountant. He has had right heel pain for five weeks, since raising his weekly running from 20 km to 45 km for a half marathon and changing to lightweight racing shoes. Pain is sharpest on his first steps each morning and after sitting, then eases, and builds a little late in runs. Examination: tender at the medial calcaneal tubercle, windlass test positive, great toe extension limited, calf tight, single leg heel raise reproduces pain at repetition 12. No numbness, night pain, trauma or systemic illness. Pain is 5/10 on first steps. During the session Daniel walks from the treadmill back to the plinth, catches his shoe on a curled edge of a rubber floor mat and falls onto one knee. He reports a grazed knee, no head strike, and says he is fine.

  1. The correct answer is B (Pain eases after some walking, and squeezing both sides of the heel does not provoke it): Plantar fasciopathy typically hurts most on first loading after rest and eases as the tissue warms up, and the heel squeeze test is usually negative. A stress fracture tends to worsen progressively with load, hurt at rest and be provoked by the squeeze test. Pain from tapping behind the medial malleolus would point to the tibial nerve at the tarsal tunnel instead.
  2. The correct answer is C (Temporary load reduction with progressive calf and plantar fascia loading, plus footwear and training review): The history shows a recent spike in running volume and a footwear change, so the tissue needs reduced but not absent load, with progressive strengthening of the calf and plantar fascia. Total rest and passive stretching do not build the tissue's load capacity. Continuing the same mileage ignores the overload that started the problem.
  3. The correct answer is B (Continue loading and build running volume stepwise, guided by symptom response the following morning): Morning pain settling does not mean the tissue can tolerate the full running load, as the pain after a long run shows. Strengthening should continue while running volume increases in steps, using next-morning symptoms as feedback. Stopping exercises or removing running altogether delays recovery and does not build capacity.
  4. The correct answer is D (Make the area safe, lodge an incident report, and follow up with Daniel about his knee): Removing the hazard, lodging a formal incident report through the organisation's system and checking on Daniel together meet the duty of care and allow review of the unreported mat hazard. A note in the clinical record or a verbal comment to the owner leaves no reviewable incident report. Telling the landlord about the hazard does not replace the practice's own reporting process.
Case CHB94

Tyrone Mason, 68, a Wiradjuri man, attends the pulmonary rehabilitation group at his Aboriginal Community Controlled Health Service. He has COPD, quit smoking six years ago and uses a long acting bronchodilator daily. He lives with his daughter, about 40 minutes from the nearest hospital, and has no home oxygen. Baseline assessment: resting HR 88, RR 18, SpO2 94 percent; 6 minute walk 285 m with SpO2 falling to 88 percent and Borg dyspnoea 5 out of 10 at the end. He enjoys yarning in the group and walks his grandchildren to school. Terry, an Aboriginal health worker, joins most sessions and helps with transport. The GP attends the service two days a week.

  1. The correct answer is A (Walking at a pace holding breathlessness moderate on the Borg scale, in interval bouts with rests): Symptom guided intensity using the Borg scale suits people with COPD, and interval bouts let him train at a worthwhile load despite exertional desaturation. A continuous target that preserves resting saturation is not achievable given his walk test. Maximal intervals are unsafe and heart rate is an unreliable guide in COPD, while seated work alone delays the benefits of walking training.
  2. The correct answer is D (Withhold exercise today and arrange same day GP review, informing Terry with Tyrone's agreement): New bilateral oedema, rapid weight gain and orthopnoea suggest possible fluid overload or heart failure, which needs medical assessment the same day and is beyond the scope of the physiotherapist to manage. The GP is on site, so escalation is easy, and sharing information with the health worker needs his agreement. A lighter session or waiting a week delays diagnosis, and advising fluid restriction is a medical decision.
  3. The correct answer is C (Walking distance, breathlessness and quality of life usually improve, though airflow limitation itself changes little): Pulmonary rehabilitation reliably improves exercise capacity, dyspnoea and health related quality of life, but it does not reverse the fixed airflow obstruction of COPD. Claiming reversal would set false expectations and could affect medication decisions. Reduced admissions are a recognised benefit but the symptom gains are the main expected effect, and these gains do not depend on home oxygen.
  4. The correct answer is B (Pursed lip breathing with slow exhalation while resting supported with the trunk inclined until settled): Pursed lip breathing with slow exhalation, in a supported inclined posture, reduces air trapping and the work of breathing during recovery from breathlessness. Continuing up the ramp keeps the load on while he is symptomatic. Deep inhalations with a hold increase air trapping, and diaphragmatic breathing while upright and unsupported does not unload the accessory muscles.
Case CHB95

Vikram Singh, 52, self-employed plumber, is on day 1 after posterior lumbar fusion L4 to S1 for degenerative spondylolisthesis with right leg pain. Surgical orders: mobilise from day 1, log roll, no bending, lifting or twisting, lumbar brace when out of bed. Medical history: type 2 diabetes, BMI 34, ex-smoker. Observations: HR 88, BP 118/72, RR 16, SpO2 96 percent on room air, afebrile. Wound dressing dry, pain 6/10 at the wound on oral analgesia. Neurology matches preoperative notes: sensation intact over L5 and S1, right ankle dorsiflexion and plantarflexion 4/5, no new deficit. Catheter removed this morning and he has voided once. He is anxious about walking and lives with his wife in a two-storey house. He tells you that you are the only one who listens, and offers to pay you cash for private home visits after discharge.

  1. The correct answer is C (Log roll, side-lying to sit with brace on, pausing at the edge before standing): Log rolling and moving from side-lying to sit keeps the spine in neutral and avoids flexion and rotation through the fused segments, with the brace applied as ordered before weight bearing. Pausing at the edge allows dizziness and pain to be checked. The other approaches load the spine through flexion or twisting or alter the ordered sequence.
  2. The correct answer is B (Stop the session and urgently inform the nurse in charge and the surgical team): Saddle numbness with altered bladder awareness after lumbar surgery suggests possible cauda equina compression, a surgical emergency where delay risks permanent loss of function. The physiotherapist's duty is immediate escalation. Anal tone testing is outside usual physiotherapy practice, and waiting for a later round or blaming the catheter delays definitive care.
  3. The correct answer is C (Short frequent walks, increased gradually each day within his pain and the surgeon's restrictions): Short frequent walks that increase gradually support circulation, lung function and confidence while respecting his fatigue, pain and the surgical restrictions. Waiting six weeks to progress adds deconditioning. A fixed daily target that only stops for severe pain ignores his tiring and wound pain, and trunk rotation stretches breach the ordered restriction on twisting.
  4. The correct answer is C (Politely decline, explain the community referral pathway, and keep all contact within the service): Taking a current hospital patient into a private cash arrangement uses a position of trust for personal gain and blurs the professional relationship, so the physiotherapist declines and directs him to the proper referral pathway. Accepting first and telling a manager later, or partly accepting the social contact, still crosses the boundary. Steering him to a colleague for private work only passes the problem on.
Case CHB96

Narelle Cooper, 15, a Year 10 student and 800 metre runner, attends the school sport clinic on her own. She was diagnosed with asthma at age nine. She has a prescribed daily preventer inhaler, which she admits she often skips, and carries a reliever. For six weeks she has coughed and felt chest tightness with wheeze about ten minutes into hard intervals, worse on cold mornings, settling about thirty minutes after stopping. At rest: RR 16, SpO2 98%, no wheeze, peak flow 380 L/min (her best is 400). She uses her inhaler without a spacer and breathes out as she presses it. Her parents have not returned the clinic consent form. She asks you not to tell them she skips her preventer.

  1. The correct answer is D (Cough, wheeze and tightness starting during hard intervals and settling about half an hour after stopping): Exercise induced bronchoconstriction typically appears during or soon after hard exertion, with cough, wheeze and tightness that resolve over a period after stopping, and it is worse in cold, dry air. Poor fitness gives breathlessness that scales with effort. Delayed night-time symptoms suggest reflux or poorly controlled asthma, and brief dizziness with palpitations points to a cardiac or hydration cause.
  2. The correct answer is D (Use a spacer, give one puff at a time, then breathe in slowly and hold briefly): A spacer lets the medicine be inhaled after the canister is pressed, and one puff per breath delivers a full dose, with a slow breath in keeping the drug from striking the throat. A fast forceful breath or exhaling straight away wastes the dose, and firing several puffs together into the chamber reduces how much reaches the lungs.
  3. The correct answer is B (Stop exercise, sit her upright, give asthma first aid and call an ambulance for severe symptoms): Speaking in short phrases, fast breathing and no relief from the reliever are signs of a severe attack, so she needs asthma first aid and an ambulance without delay. Calling her parents first or rechecking peak flow postpones emergency care. Moving indoors with breathing coaching suits a milder episode, and it does not treat a severe one.
  4. The correct answer is B (Assess whether she understands the assessment, its risks and alternatives, and document her decision): A young person can give valid consent when she has the maturity to understand the nature, risks and alternatives of the care, so capacity is judged for this decision rather than by age alone, and the assessment is documented. A missing parental form does not by itself bar care. Age alone does not confer capacity, and a school's authority does not replace the patient's own consent.
Case CHB97

Darren Kelly, 44, a farmhand, was brought to a small rural hospital 40 minutes after his quad bike rolled and the handlebars struck his chest. A Royal Flying Doctor Service retrieval is expected in about 90 minutes. Findings: HR 122, BP 88/60 mmHg, RR 28, SpO2 93 percent on 6 L/min by mask, distended neck veins, quiet heart sounds, equal breath sounds, bruising over the sternum, tenderness over right ribs 5 to 7, pain 8 out of 10. Chest X-ray is awaited. A nurse has asked the physiotherapist to give him some chest physiotherapy for suspected atelectasis. He is employed on the farm and a workers compensation claim has been lodged, and he asks whether his rehabilitation will be paid for. The nearest outpatient physiotherapy service is in a town 250 km from his home.

  1. The correct answer is C (Cardiac tamponade, where blood collecting in the pericardial sac restricts filling of the ventricles): Hypotension, raised venous pressure and muffled heart sounds form the classic triad of tamponade, and equal breath sounds argue against a tension pneumothorax. Haemothorax would show a dull chest and flat neck veins, and flail chest shows paradoxical chest movement. Tamponade is immediately life threatening and needs urgent medical intervention.
  2. The correct answer is B (Withhold chest techniques, tell the doctor of your concern, and support a position of comfort): An unstable patient with a possible life threatening cardiac injury needs medical resuscitation, not respiratory physiotherapy, and manual or breathing techniques may increase pain and add risk. The physiotherapist's role is to communicate the concern and keep him comfortable. Atelectasis is a secondary problem that can be addressed once he is stable.
  3. The correct answer is C (Explain insurer approval is needed, help him contact his claim manager, and raise telehealth options): Workers compensation insurers generally need to approve treatment, so he should contact his claim manager before committing to a provider, and telehealth can reduce the burden of 250 km. Starting with any provider risks costs being refused. Medicare does not usually fund compensable injuries, and delaying rehabilitation until acceptance can slow his recovery.
  4. The correct answer is D (Liaise with nursing to optimise timed analgesia, then teach splinted deep breathing and supported coughing): Pain from rib fractures drives shallow breathing and retained secretions, so effective analgesia timed before treatment is the first step, followed by splinted breathing and coughing. Percussion over fractured ribs is painful and unsafe, and coughing without support increases pain. Deferring respiratory care for a week lets atelectasis and pneumonia develop.
Case CHB98

Dorothy Hanlon, 78, a retired bookkeeper, lives alone in a unit and was referred by her GP to your community physiotherapy service. Yesterday she tripped on a kerb and inverted her right ankle. History: osteoporosis, hypertension and one fall six months ago. Observation: swelling and bruising over the lateral ankle and lateral foot, HR 76, BP 142/84. Palpation: tender at the posterior edge of the lateral malleolus and over the anterior talofibular ligament; midfoot and fifth metatarsal base not tender. In the clinic she managed only two painful steps. Dorsiflexion is 5 degrees, and the anterior drawer was not tested because of pain. Timed Up and Go is 16 seconds with a stick. She is a pensioner, has not been assessed through My Aged Care, and mentions she is worried about fees.

  1. The correct answer is A (Arrange ankle X-rays through her GP or an emergency department to exclude fracture first): Bony tenderness at the posterior edge of the malleolus and inability to take four steps make the Ottawa rules positive, so a fracture must be excluded with X-ray before any loading. Her age and osteoporosis raise that risk. Stress testing or protected walking could displace an undiagnosed fracture, and ultrasound shows ligament tissue rather than excluding a fracture.
  2. The correct answer is C (Protect with support, encourage early pain-limited walking and ankle movement, then progress balance and strength): Moderate lateral sprains do best with a short period of protection followed by early pain-limited movement and weight-bearing, then graded balance and strength work. Prolonged immobilisation delays recovery and promotes deconditioning, which matters in an older woman with a falls history. Aggressive friction or stretching irritates healing ligament and does not suit this stage.
  3. The correct answer is A (Explain fees openly, discuss subsidised options, and help her contact My Aged Care for assessment): Clear information about cost and help to be assessed through My Aged Care opens subsidised services suited to an older person. A reduced fee or a GP plan may help with some visits but does not set up ongoing assessed support. Sending her to a hospital clinic moves care without addressing her cost concern, and the NDIS is not a pathway for someone her age.
  4. The correct answer is D (Complete a multifactorial falls risk assessment, including postural blood pressure, and address home hazards): Falls in older people usually have several causes, so a multifactorial assessment covering blood pressure changes on standing, environment, balance and strength is the best approach. Postural light-headedness also needs medical follow-up, but exercise and home modification should not wait. Focusing on the ankle alone ignores her other risk factors.
Case CHB99

Mr Anil Sharma, 62, is on day three after insertion of a ventriculoperitoneal shunt for normal pressure hydrocephalus, on a neurosurgical ward. Before surgery he had a wide based, shuffling gait with start hesitation, urinary urgency and memory slowing. Today he is alert, GCS 15, HR 76, BP 138/84, SpO2 96 percent on room air. He has a mild headache at the incision site, rated 2 out of 10, and no nausea. Timed Up and Go was 24 seconds before surgery and is 19 seconds today with supervision. The neurosurgical team has ordered mobilisation as tolerated. A colleague gives you a small journal article describing 12 patients with this condition who improved after an intensive treadmill programme, with no control group, and suggests using it for Mr Sharma.

  1. The correct answer is B (It is an encouraging early sign of shunt response, to be tracked over weeks): Early gait improvement after shunting is a recognised favourable sign, but benefit can continue or fluctuate, so it is monitored with repeated measures during rehabilitation. Calling it full recovery is premature. Dismissing it as a practice effect or as failure ignores the clinical picture of reduced hesitation.
  2. The correct answer is C (Return him safely to bed and call the nurse in charge and medical team immediately): Severe headache, vomiting and a falling GCS after shunting suggest raised intracranial pressure from possible shunt malfunction, which is an emergency needing immediate medical assessment. Continuing therapy, waiting or treating it as simple nausea delays that review. The first priority is safety and escalation, not symptom relief by the therapist.
  3. The correct answer is B (Follow the neurosurgical activity and positioning orders, and avoid pressure over the valve): Early care follows the neurosurgical orders and avoids direct pressure or manipulation over the valve and tubing. Restricting normal neck movement to protect the tubing is an unsupported myth, and the other options either press on the device or interfere with it.
  4. The correct answer is B (Note the weak design, seek higher quality evidence, then apply cautiously with monitoring): A small uncontrolled series gives low confidence, so the sensible step is to look for stronger evidence and guidance and then combine it with his needs and preferences, monitoring his response. Accepting it because everyone improved ignores bias and natural recovery. Refusing all training until a trial appears would withhold reasonable care.
Case CHB100

Tahlia Morgan, 16, a Year 11 student and netball player, is brought to the emergency department by her mother after a low-speed rear-end collision. She was a front seat passenger wearing a seatbelt. Neck pain began within hours. She now reports neck pain 6 out of 10, stiffness and a mild occipital headache. There was no loss of consciousness, her Glasgow Coma Scale is 15 and she has not vomited. The emergency doctor assessed her clinically and cleared the cervical spine without imaging. No past history and no regular medications. Physiotherapy assessment in the department: guarded posture, active cervical rotation about 50 degrees each way limited by pain, and tenderness over the right upper trapezius and paraspinal muscles. Upper limb strength, reflexes and sensation are normal. Observations: HR 84, BP 112/70, SpO2 99 percent on room air. Tahlia and her mother are anxious about school and netball. Physiotherapy is asked to give advice before discharge.

  1. The correct answer is C (Grade II: neck pain with musculoskeletal signs such as reduced range, and no neurological deficit): Grade II combines neck pain with musculoskeletal signs, here reduced rotation and focal tenderness, without neurological deficit. Grade I has symptoms only and no objective signs, which does not fit her reduced range. Grade III needs neurological findings such as diminished reflexes, weakness or sensory loss, and headache alone does not qualify. Grade IV is a fracture or dislocation, which the doctor excluded clinically.
  2. The correct answer is C (Reassure about the usual good recovery and encourage gentle active neck movement and normal activity): Early reassurance, education about the favourable prognosis and encouragement to move and stay active produce better outcomes than rest. Collars prolong disability, and imaging has already been addressed by the doctor's clinical clearance. Passive treatment alone fosters dependence and is less helpful than active self-management.
  3. The correct answer is D (Stop testing, keep her supine and monitored, and alert the treating emergency doctor immediately): A sudden severe unusual headache with nausea and speech difficulty during neck loading raises concern for a vascular or neurological event, which is a medical emergency. Testing stops, she is kept safe and the emergency doctor in the department is told at once. Observing her or taking measurements before informing anyone loses time, and a GP review today is far too slow for this picture.
  4. The correct answer is C (Use a structured format: identify the patient, state the change, give brief history, request review): A structured handover such as ISBAR puts the identification, the new problem and the request for review up front, so a busy clinician grasps the urgency immediately. Starting with a long chronological history buries the key information. Passing the concern through a third person or relying on a written note adds delay and risks distortion.
Case CHB101

Ngoc Ngo, 7, has bilateral spastic cerebral palsy (GMFCS level II) and attends outpatient physiotherapy weekly with her mother. She walks indoors without aids and uses forearm crutches outdoors. Her goal is to walk further at school camp. The clinic has a treadmill with a body weight support harness. At assessment: crouch gait with in-toeing, hamstring length limited, ankle dorsiflexion 5 degrees short of neutral, knee extension strength 3/5. She tires after about 10 minutes of walking and her crouch deepens. Her mother mentions Ngoc has had right groin discomfort for three weeks, worse after school sport. Right hip abduction in hip flexion is about 20 degrees, left about 40 degrees. Her last hip X-ray was two years ago. Her mother asks whether Ngoc should keep doing treadmill training.

  1. The correct answer is A (It allows repetitive, task specific stepping practice with less load while she works on control): Partial unloading permits many more stepping repetitions with good quality than she could manage fully loaded, supporting task specific motor learning. The harness does not impose a normal pattern, and carryover needs overground practice. Unloading does not treat spasticity or substitute for strengthening.
  2. The correct answer is C (Give enough support for knee extension in stance without collapse, reducing it as control improves): Support should be titrated to the point where she can achieve good stance alignment and then reduced as her control improves, keeping practice both achievable and challenging. Too much support or speed degrades the pattern, and too little produces the crouch the training is meant to change. A fixed adult pace ignores her size and tolerance.
  3. The correct answer is A (Lower speed or add support and use shorter bouts with rests, as fatigue is degrading stepping): Practice is only useful while movement quality holds, so reducing demand and using short bouts with rest preserves a good pattern. Pushing on rehearses the crouch. Ending the active work or adding load increases difficulty when the problem is fatigue.
  4. The correct answer is B (Pause weight bearing training and promptly contact her paediatrician or GP to arrange imaging): Reduced abduction with new groin pain in a child with cerebral palsy raises concern about hip migration, which needs imaging and medical review, outside what the physiotherapist can diagnose or treat. Loading may harm a displacing hip, so weight bearing training should wait. Delaying review or treating it as soreness risks missed progression.
Case CHB102

Sipho Diallo, 34, bricklayer, is 10 weeks after a fall from a ladder with a complete T4 spinal cord injury (ASIA impairment scale A). He is an inpatient on a spinal rehabilitation unit. Sensation and movement are absent below T4; arm strength is 5/5. Resting seated BP is 100/60 mmHg and HR 62. He has an indwelling urethral catheter, and nursing staff manage his bladder and bowel programs. He wears an abdominal binder and compression stockings for upright sessions. Earlier this week on the tilt table he became light headed at a steep angle. He is progressing wheelchair skills and transfers, and discharge to the family home in regional Victoria with his wife Ngozi is planned in six weeks.

  1. The correct answer is A (Keep him upright, loosen tight clothing and binder, then check the catheter tubing for kinks): Autonomic dysreflexia is a medical emergency driven by a noxious stimulus below the lesion. Sitting him upright lets gravity lower his blood pressure, and removing constriction and checking for a kinked catheter looks for the cause quickly. Lying him flat suits orthostatic hypotension but would push his pressure higher.
  2. The correct answer is C (Lower the table toward horizontal until symptoms settle, then re-progress the angle more gradually): Lowering the table restores venous return and cerebral perfusion quickly, and the angle can then be raised in smaller steps. Holding the angle risks fainting. A steep head down tilt is unnecessary, and transferring him out first delays treating the fall in pressure.
  3. The correct answer is A (Pause the session, inform the nurse in charge and medical team, and document the episode): Autonomic dysreflexia can recur and needs medical review of the cause and of bladder and bowel management, so the nursing and medical team must be told promptly. Physiotherapists recognise, manage the immediate response and escalate, but changing catheters is outside their role. Resuming exercise or waiting for a meeting leaves the risk unreviewed.
  4. The correct answer is B (A noxious stimulus below the lesion provokes sympathetic outflow the brain cannot modulate): In injuries at or above about T6, a painful or irritating stimulus below the lesion triggers a sympathetic surge that descending brain control cannot dampen, so blood pressure climbs. Blood pooling on standing describes orthostatic hypotension, a different problem. The other explanations do not match the mechanism.
Case CHB103

Daniel Fraser, 27, a plumber, is eight weeks after a motorcycle crash that caused a right traction injury to the brachial plexus and a clavicle fracture treated with plating. A nerve conduction study at six weeks suggested an upper trunk injury with some preserved lower trunk function. The neurosurgeon is considering nerve transfer surgery. Findings (MRC grades): shoulder flexion and abduction 1/5, elbow flexion 1/5, wrist extension 2/5, grip 4/5. Passive shoulder flexion 90 degrees, external rotation 10 degrees. Reduced sensation over the lateral arm and forearm, with burning pain of 6/10 in the lateral forearm that is worse with light touch. The humeral head sits low in the joint. He is anxious about work. The neurosurgery registrar has asked for a physiotherapy update before next week's clinic.

  1. The correct answer is B (Maintain passive range gently within comfort and support the arm to limit inferior traction): Preserving passive range keeps the joint mobile for any later reinnervation, and supporting the weight of the arm reduces traction on the weak shoulder. Resisted work cannot be performed against grade 1 muscles, and prolonged immobilisation promotes stiffness. Forceful stretching risks damaging a joint that is already poorly supported.
  2. The correct answer is C (Weak shoulder abduction, elbow flexion and wrist extension, with relatively preserved hand grip): The upper trunk draws on C5 and C6 and supplies the deltoid, biceps and brachialis, with the wrist extensors partly involved, while the hand muscles from C8 and T1 are spared. The medial forearm and ulnar hand belong to the lower trunk. Scapular winging reflects a long thoracic nerve problem, and the middle finger relates to C7.
  3. The correct answer is C (Graded desensitisation and nerve pain education, with the pain flagged to the medical team): Allodynia and burning pain suggest neuropathic pain, which often improves with graded exposure to touch and clear education about what is happening. The physiotherapist should refer medication questions to the doctor and not manage them. Firm massage can provoke the pain, and total rest reduces tolerance.
  4. The correct answer is D (A concise structured summary of serial muscle grades, passive ranges, pain and function): Surgeons need objective, comparable data on recovery and any deterioration, delivered concisely and directly to the responsible clinician. Sending whole notes buries the key points, and messages through reception risk loss of detail and confidentiality. Reporting attendance without neurological findings does not inform the decision.
Case CHB104

Eddie Nona, 34, farm worker, fell from a quad bike 2 days ago. Right rib fractures (ribs 5 to 8) and a traumatic right pneumothorax. An intercostal catheter sits in the right chest connected to an underwater seal drain with no suction. The chamber fluid swings with breathing, there has been no bubbling for 12 hours and drainage is minimal. Chest X-ray this morning shows the lung largely re-expanded. RR 20, HR 88, BP 130/80 mmHg, SpO2 96 percent on room air. Pain 6 out of 10 on coughing despite regular analgesia, with shallow breathing and reduced right basal air entry. He is keen to return to work. This small rural hospital has one physiotherapist, who lives on the property next to Eddie's family. Eddie has asked the physiotherapist to be friends on social media.

  1. The correct answer is A (The air leak from the lung has probably stopped and the drain is still patent): Tidal swinging shows the tube is patent and connected to the pleural space, and absent bubbling with a re-expanded lung means the air leak has most likely sealed. A blocked or displaced tube would not swing with respiration. Shallow breathing alone would not hide a significant ongoing leak.
  2. The correct answer is B (Keep the drain below chest level, avoid tubing kinks and do not clamp the tube): The system stays below chest level so fluid cannot siphon back, tubing is kept free of kinks and the drain is not clamped so any air can escape. Clamping, even briefly, risks a tension pneumothorax if there is an air leak. Adding suction needs a medical order and is not part of mobilising a drain that is working on an underwater seal.
  3. The correct answer is A (Cover the wound with a hand or dressing, sit him up and call urgently): The immediate need is to cover the track to limit air entry, keep him upright to ease breathing and summon help for urgent medical management. Reinsertion is outside the physiotherapist's scope and risks harm. A sealed four-sided dressing can trap air and cause a tension pneumothorax, and lying flat worsens breathlessness.
  4. The correct answer is D (Decline kindly, explain why, and arrange documented follow-up through the hospital outpatient service): In a small community dual relationships are common, but care should still run through official, documented channels so boundaries, privacy and continuity are protected. Moving the relationship to personal social media or a private phone blurs the therapeutic role even if messages are recorded. Explaining the reason respectfully preserves trust and his access to care.
Case CHB105

Mr Dmitri Kowalski, 58, a builder, is 10 weeks after a right middle cerebral artery ischaemic stroke and is an inpatient in a rehabilitation unit. He has left hemiparesis: hip flexion 3/5, knee extension 3/5, ankle dorsiflexion 2/5, with mild increased tone in the plantarflexors. He walks 10 metres with a hemi-walker and moderate assistance from one person, showing circumduction and short left stance time. Berg Balance Scale 28/56. HR 74, BP 128/76, SpO2 97%. He takes an antihypertensive and a statin. He is motivated and has asked about the robotic gait trainer he saw advertised. The unit has a body weight support treadmill and an overground exoskeleton, and a colleague has forwarded a recent trial of robotic gait training for the team to appraise.

  1. The correct answer is B (It unloads part of his weight so stepping can be repeated safely with less handling): Harness support unloads part of his body weight so he can repeat many safe steps with less hands-on help, which suits high-repetition task-specific practice. The leg still has to bear load, only less of it. The belt speed does not correct the pattern on its own, so skilled feedback remains necessary. The harness is protective but does not make unsupported walking at higher speeds the goal at this stage.
  2. The correct answer is A (Lower the speed, review the unloading, and give targeted cueing or assistance for knee control): Hyperextension with a backward trunk lean shows the task is too fast or poorly controlled, so reduce difficulty and shape the movement with cueing or hands-on help. Speeding up would worsen the faulty pattern, and a rigid brace hides the problem rather than retraining control. Stopping task practice removes the training stimulus he needs.
  3. The correct answer is B (Judge whether participants and comparison treatment resemble him, then weigh results with guidelines and his preferences): Evidence-based practice means appraising how applicable a study is to this patient, here a different stage of recovery and a weak comparison group, and combining it with guidelines and patient values. A good design alone does not make a result transferable. Rejecting it for location, or copying it rigidly, ignores the integration of evidence, expertise and preference.
  4. The correct answer is D (Include plenty of progressively harder overground walking, using devices as an addition to that practice): Guidelines favour task-specific, repeated and progressively harder walking practice, with robotic or treadmill devices supplementing rather than replacing it. Waiting for strength limits practice that drives recovery, and passive device movement gives weaker learning than active effort. Speed alone ignores the balance and community demands of his goal.

Practice Test 8 Answer Key

Case CHB106

Marco Fontana, 78, retired tiler, lives alone. Two days ago he fell down stairs and sustained an undisplaced fracture of the C5 vertebral body. He is managed non-operatively in a rigid cervical collar, neurologically intact (ASIA impairment scale E), with medical orders for spinal precautions and mobilisation as tolerated. History: atrial fibrillation on anticoagulant medication, hypertension, osteoporosis. Observations lying flat: BP 128/76, HR 70 irregular, SpO2 96% on room air. Pain 4 out of 10 with movement. On the acute ward, physiotherapy is asked to begin sitting him out of bed. Marco is cheerful, says he has no visitors, and tells you nobody has ever looked after him as kindly as the ward staff.

  1. The correct answer is B (Confirm the spinal orders and collar instructions, then record a baseline neurological screen): Mobilisation of a cervical fracture follows the medical team's orders on stability, collar use and permitted activity, and a baseline neurological screen lets any later change be recognised. Testing neurology only after sitting him up would miss early deficits, and loosening the collar or maximal resisted testing risks the injury.
  2. The correct answer is B (Lay him flat, monitor until recovered, and inform the nurse before progressing him again): A fall in blood pressure with symptoms on sitting up suggests orthostatic intolerance, which is common after spinal injury and bed rest. Lying him flat restores perfusion, and the nurse needs to know before any further attempt so that the cause and the progression can be planned. Staying upright or repeating the same step leaves the cause unaddressed.
  3. The correct answer is A (Return him to bed in the collar, stop mobilising, and notify the medical team urgently): New bilateral tingling and reduced grip compared with baseline suggest neurological deterioration, possibly from cord compromise, which is time-critical. Returning him safely to bed in the collar and informing the medical team at once is required, since watching and waiting risks permanent loss. Collar pressure does not explain bilateral motor change.
  4. The correct answer is A (Decline the number and gift kindly, explain boundaries, and arrange community service follow-up): Keeping professional boundaries protects patients, and a personal number or gift can blur the relationship; declining kindly while still meeting his need for support honours the duty of care. Linking him with the community service gives safe ongoing contact. Accepting a gift, even to donate, or ending contact altogether each fail one side of that duty.
Case CHB107

Anjali Desai, 34, primary school teacher, relapsing-remitting multiple sclerosis for six years. Admitted three days ago with a relapse: new unsteadiness and double vision on left gaze. Receiving intravenous methylprednisolone under the neurologist, who has cleared her for rehabilitation. BP 118/72, HR 80, temperature 36.8, SpO2 98%. Reduced proprioception in both feet, truncal ataxia, wide-based gait, Romberg unsteady with eyes closed, Berg Balance Scale 38/56, lower limb strength 4+/5. Fatigue 8/10 by the afternoon, and her room on the sunny side of the ward is warm. She is waiting for a community neurological rehabilitation referral. She has told you she trusts you more than her usual team and would like you to be her private physiotherapist at home and a friend on social media.

  1. The correct answer is B (Short, supervised morning sessions with rests, in a cool space, stopping before fatigue builds): In multiple sclerosis, fatigue and heat worsen symptoms, so short cool sessions at her best time of day with planned rests allow quality practice without a setback. Training into fatigue or at high intensity during a relapse risks symptom worsening. Bed rest causes deconditioning and does not help balance recover.
  2. The correct answer is C (Walk with one person supervising and a wheeled walker, reviewed as her balance recovers): Her ataxia, low Berg score and unsteady turning mean she needs supervision and a stable wheeled aid, with the plan reviewed as she improves. A single-point stick gives too little support for ataxia. Two staff and a wheelchair for most journeys restricts her more than her findings justify, and independent walking leaves a falls risk unmanaged.
  3. The correct answer is D (Temporary heat-related symptom worsening, not a new relapse, so teach cooling and pacing strategies): Symptoms that appear with a rise in body temperature and settle fully with cooling are a pseudo-exacerbation, not new damage, so education on cooling, pacing and avoiding overheating is the right response. A new relapse would persist beyond a day. Infusion timing is not within the physiotherapist's role, and the pattern is tied to heat rather than general tiredness.
  4. The correct answer is C (Thank her, explain boundaries, decline the friend request, and organise follow-up through her usual team): A caring response acknowledges her trust, keeps a clear professional boundary by declining social media contact, and protects her continuity of care by using the community team arranged for her. Accepting a personal connection blurs the therapeutic relationship, and offering a private number does the same. Avoiding the conversation entirely leaves her without an explanation or a plan.
Case CHB108

Kyah Williams, 15, a Wiradjuri boy, was admitted overnight after a direct blow to his right calf in a tackle during a football game, 18 hours ago. Imaging excluded a fracture. The ward has requested physiotherapy for mobilisation and crutch training. His family drove in from a rural town, and his aunty and grandmother are at the bedside. Kyah is quiet, looks down when answering and says 'it's okay' when asked about pain. Observations: HR 108, BP 118/70, afebrile. The right calf is tense, swollen and shiny. Pain is 7/10 when assessed with a faces scale. Pain is felt on passive ankle and toe movement despite analgesia given two hours ago. Sensation is reduced between the first and second toes. Dorsalis pedis pulse is palpable and capillary refill is 2 seconds. The hospital has an Aboriginal Liaison Officer.

  1. The correct answer is C (Pain out of proportion to the injury that increases with passive stretch of the compartment's muscles): Pain out of proportion and pain on passive stretch of the involved muscles appear early, while distal pulses, refill and colour often stay normal until very late, when tissue damage is already established. Waiting for pulse loss or pallor delays recognition. Kyah's findings are therefore worrying even though perfusion signs look normal.
  2. The correct answer is B (Withhold mobilisation, keep the limb level with the heart and urgently escalate to the medical team): These findings suggest possible compartment syndrome, which is a surgical emergency, so activity is stopped, the limb is kept at heart level to protect arterial inflow and the medical team is told at once. Elevation above the heart, stretching and tight bandaging can all worsen perfusion or pressure. Crutch training can wait until the diagnosis is excluded.
  3. The correct answer is A (Tell staff if pain increases, the calf tightens, or numbness or toe weakness develops or spreads): Rising pain, increasing tightness and new or spreading sensory or motor change are the early signs a family member can notice and report quickly. A cold, pale foot or lost pulse are late signs when the limb may already be threatened. Bruising does not track pressure, and a numeric pain threshold is unreliable in a quiet adolescent who minimises pain.
  4. The correct answer is C (Ask who Kyah wants present, offer the Aboriginal Liaison Officer and use plain language): Culturally safe care asks the person about their preferences, offers access to Aboriginal support staff and avoids assumptions. Avoiding eye contact is often a sign of respect rather than disengagement. Speaking only to the aunty removes Kyah's voice, assuming what the family wants is a stereotype, and accepting a minimised pain report risks missing a deteriorating limb.
Case CHB109

Dinh Van Long, 58, a retired bus driver, has motor neurone disease diagnosed 18 months ago and is admitted with a right lower respiratory tract infection. He uses non-invasive ventilation (NIV) overnight at home. Today: RR 24, HR 98, SpO2 92 percent on room air, temperature 37.9 degrees. Reduced air entry and coarse crackles at the right base. His cough is weak and wet, with audible secretions he cannot clear. Vital capacity is 40 percent of predicted and lower when supine. Limb strength is 3/5 to 2/5, bulbar function is mildly affected, and a swallow assessment found a soft diet safe. He is alert and consents to treatment. His brother, who lives interstate, phones the ward asking for an update and says Mr Long is happy for him to know.

  1. The correct answer is D (Weak inspiratory and expiratory muscles reduce the volume breathed in and the force of expulsion): An effective cough needs a deep inspiration, glottic closure and strong expiratory muscle contraction, and in motor neurone disease the respiratory muscles are weak, as his reduced vital capacity shows. His bulbar involvement is only mild, so it is not the main cause, and the reflex itself is usually preserved.
  2. The correct answer is C (Mechanical insufflation-exsufflation via mask, with manually assisted coughing and suction if needed): Mechanical insufflation-exsufflation supplies the inspiratory volume and expiratory flow that his weak muscles cannot, and manual assistance adds expiratory force. Techniques that rely on his own muscle effort are unlikely to be effective, and head-down tilt increases the work of breathing and aspiration risk.
  3. The correct answer is C (Pause, rest him on NIV, reassess, then restart with fewer cycles and longer rests): Fatigue and desaturation in a person with weak respiratory muscles call for support and recovery time, and NIV gives that while the treatment is adjusted. Pushing on risks exhaustion and respiratory failure, while abandoning airway clearance leaves the secretions that caused the problem.
  4. The correct answer is A (Confirm Mr Long's wishes with him directly, and record who may receive information in the notes): Health information belongs to the patient, and a capable adult decides who receives it, so the physiotherapist should check with him and document that authorisation. A caller's claim is not consent, next of kin status gives no automatic entitlement, and releasing notes is outside the physiotherapist's role.
Case CHB110

Tuan Nguyen, 64, retired bus driver, fell from a stepladder five weeks ago and sustained a two-part surgical neck fracture of the left humerus, managed without surgery. Follow-up x-ray shows early callus and the orthopaedic surgeon's letter clears gentle passive and active-assisted movement, with no resisted exercise yet. He lives alone, smokes ten cigarettes daily and has never had a bone density scan. He has worn the sling constantly, including at rest, and has not moved his elbow or hand. Pain 4/10 at rest, 7/10 with movement. Passive range: flexion 70 degrees, abduction 60, external rotation 10. Radial pulse present, hand sensation intact, capillary refill normal, bruising resolving and shoulder swelling settled. Outpatient clinic.

  1. The correct answer is B (Light touch over the lateral deltoid and a palpable deltoid contraction during gentle isometric effort): The axillary nerve wraps around the surgical neck of the humerus and supplies deltoid and the skin over its lateral aspect, so sensation there plus deltoid activation reflects its integrity. The other findings test the radial, median and medial cord territories, which belong in a full neurological screen but do not reflect this particular nerve.
  2. The correct answer is C (Pendulum swings and supine assisted flexion within comfort, plus regular elbow and hand movement): Early gentle pendulum and assisted movement within comfort limits the stiffness that follows a fracture, while moving the elbow and hand prevents distal stiffness and swelling. Forcing elevation through pain risks the healing fragments. Banded strengthening is resisted work the surgeon has not yet cleared, and withholding all shoulder movement increases the risk of a stiff shoulder.
  3. The correct answer is C (Pause progression and contact his GP and orthopaedic team promptly to arrange further investigation): Constant night pain unrelated to position, with unexplained weight loss in a smoker, is a red flag for a serious underlying process, so progression pauses and the GP and orthopaedic team investigate promptly. Continuing or only reviewing at the next visit delays that. Lowering intensity and advising on smoking does not address the cause, and a callus check alone does not investigate weight loss.
  4. The correct answer is B (Agree a goal-based home plan, offer smoking cessation advice, and suggest his GP review bone health): Self-management is strengthened by a plan built around his own goals, brief advice on stopping smoking, and appropriate referral for bone health assessment after a fragility-type fracture. Recommending supplements strays towards medical advice beyond the physiotherapist's role. Leaving lifestyle to others misses a health promotion opportunity, and thrice-weekly supervision builds dependence rather than self-management.
Case CHB111

Mrs Lisa Prescott, 78, a widowed retired teacher, lives alone in a ground floor unit in Ballarat. Her GP referred her after two falls in six months, neither causing injury. Since the second fall she avoids leaving home, shops by delivery, and says she will fall again. History: generalised anxiety disorder, hypertension, mild osteoarthritis of both knees. Cognition screens normal. Resting HR 84, BP 134/80 sitting with no postural drop. Timed Up and Go 16 seconds without an aid. Five times sit to stand 18 seconds. Falls Efficacy Scale International shows high concern. Outdoors she becomes tremulous, breathes fast and wants to turn back after about 50 metres. Her GP chronic disease management plan has two Medicare subsidised allied health visits left this year, after podiatry and dietetics visits. She has not yet had a My Aged Care assessment.

  1. The correct answer is D (Fear of falling drives avoidance and deconditioning, so graded exposure with strength work fits): The picture is one of fear related activity avoidance with measurable physical decline, which responds to graded exposure combined with strength and balance training. Resting until confidence returns is a common but outdated habit that deepens deconditioning. Normal cognitive screening and no vestibular signs make the other explanations unsupported.
  2. The correct answer is D (Seat her briefly, guide slow breathing, then resume once settled over a shorter graded distance): Brief rest with controlled breathing lets the anxiety response settle, and finishing with a planned achievable step keeps exposure positive. Pushing on risks sensitising her, while abandoning outdoor practice reinforces avoidance. A pulse of 96 with a steady stance does not point to a cardiac problem needing review first.
  3. The correct answer is B (Progress strength and balance work to a challenging but safe level, raising dosage as confidence builds): Falls prevention evidence supports progressive, sufficiently challenging balance and strength exercise, which also builds confidence through mastery. Seated work under loads her and walking alone do not provide enough balance challenge. A group class may suit later, but dropping individual supervision now removes the safeguard while her confidence is fragile.
  4. The correct answer is D (Explain the visit limit, then refer her to My Aged Care for subsidised home support): People over 65 are generally directed to My Aged Care rather than the NDIS, and an assessment can open subsidised home support including allied health. Telling her openly about the visit limit respects her autonomy in funding decisions. Hiding the limit or simply discharging her either misleads her or leaves a clear need unmet.
Case CHB112

Zara Khan, 9 months, is on day 3 of an admission with bronchiolitis and a right upper lobe collapse on chest X-ray. She was born at term and was well before this illness, and her immunisations are up to date. She has 1 L/min oxygen by nasal prongs and nasogastric tube feeds; a bolus feed finished 30 minutes ago. RR 52, HR 150, SpO2 94 percent, mild subcostal recession, afebrile. Auscultation: reduced air entry in the right upper zone, transmitted upper airway noises and a wet cough. She is settled in her mother's arms. Her mother, Fatima, asks whether physiotherapy will help her recover faster, and says Zara vomited after treatment on another shift. The medical team has requested a physiotherapy review of the collapse.

  1. The correct answer is D (Defer treatment until the feed has settled, or schedule it before the next tube feed): Positioning and handling soon after a full feed increases the risk of reflux and vomiting, so treatment is timed before the next feed or after the feed has settled. Head-up positions reduce but do not remove that risk, and delaying until the end of the day wastes the treatment benefit for a collapse needing review now.
  2. The correct answer is B (Left side-lying with the head slightly raised, placing the right upper zone uppermost): Positioning the affected lung uppermost favours ventilation and drainage of that region, so left side-lying suits a right upper lobe collapse. A head-up tilt limits reflux and the work of breathing. Prone positioning is unsafe for an unobserved infant, and head-down tilt increases reflux and respiratory effort.
  3. The correct answer is D (Stop treatment, return her to a supported upright position, and alert the nurse for medical review): A fall in saturation with increased respiratory distress means she is not tolerating treatment, so the safest step is to stop, support her breathing and get medical review. Continuing or altering oxygen without an order risks further deterioration, and suction without review may not address the cause.
  4. The correct answer is C (Alert the nurse so the tube is checked before any use, then enter an incident report): A displaced feeding tube must be checked by a person authorised to confirm position before anything is given through it, and the event must be reported through the incident system even without harm so the cause can be reviewed. Repositioning it herself is outside the physiotherapist's role, and listening over the stomach is not a reliable position check.
Case CHB113

Thuy Ly, 10 weeks old, was born at term and is an inpatient on the paediatric ward after a mild bronchiolitis admission. Her parents say her head tilts to the right and she mostly looks to the left. Findings: head tilted to the right with the chin turned to the left; passive rotation to the right limited; a small firm lump in the right sternocleidomastoid; flattening of the left back of the head with the left ear sitting forward. Her hips were examined by the ward doctor and are normal. She is alert and feeding well, with oxygen saturation 98 percent on room air. Her parents avoid tummy time because of reflux and she spends most of the day on her back or in a car seat. During handling, her right arm lies limp beside her with the elbow extended and she moves it much less than her left.

  1. The correct answer is B (She favours turning left, so the left back of her head bears sustained pressure): In right sided torticollis the infant favours rotating the head to the left, so the soft skull rests on the left back of the head for long periods and flattens there. This is positional moulding secondary to the neck asymmetry rather than direct pull of the muscle on bone. Early positioning change and stretching address both problems.
  2. The correct answer is D (Alternate her head direction, hold her upright often, and offer brief supervised tummy time): Varying head direction, more upright holding and short supervised tummy time reduce sustained pressure on one part of the skull and support neck and motor development, and they suit an infant with reflux when timed away from feeds. Helmets are not a first line approach at ten weeks, and a bouncer increases pressure on the back of the head. Waiting passively loses the period when positioning has most effect.
  3. The correct answer is A (Tilt the ear toward the left shoulder, then rotate the chin toward the right shoulder): The right sternocleidomastoid tilts the head to the right and turns the chin to the left, so lengthening it needs the opposite combination: ear toward the left shoulder with the chin turned toward the right. The other combinations either shorten the muscle or lengthen only one of its two actions.
  4. The correct answer is A (Pause stretching and request urgent paediatric medical review of the arm, handling it gently meanwhile): Reduced spontaneous movement of one arm with pain on shoulder movement in an infant is outside typical torticollis and may reflect a fracture, brachial plexus injury or another condition. Entry level physiotherapists recognise this as needing prompt medical assessment before treatment continues, and stretching or exercise could worsen an unrecognised injury. Monitoring, or starting a nerve injury programme without a diagnosis, oversteps the physiotherapy role.
Case CHB114

Amira Haddad, 41, secondary school teacher, lives with her husband and two children in a single-storey home. Three years ago she fell from a ladder and has a complete T4 spinal cord injury (ASIA Impairment Scale A). She uses a manual wheelchair and intermittent self-catheterisation four times daily. Her usual seated blood pressure is 95/60 mmHg with heart rate 70. In the past fortnight she has had two episodes of pounding headache with facial flushing, each after she delayed catheterising. Today at the home visit she feels well. On moving from supine to sitting, her BP falls to 82/50 mmHg with light-headedness that settles within a minute of reclining. A standing frame programme for bone health and circulation has been recommended by her rehabilitation physician, and the first trial is planned today. Her husband is present and keen for her to start. Amira is orientated and understands the information provided.

  1. The correct answer is D (A marked rise above her baseline consistent with autonomic dysreflexia, which needs prompt management): In injuries at or above about T6, autonomic dysreflexia is recognised by a rise well above the person's own low baseline, with headache and flushing, and it needs prompt management of the trigger. Comparing 140/88 with a population threshold hides how large this rise is for her. Her usual postural drop on sitting is a separate issue, and waiting to recheck delays treating a possible hypertensive emergency.
  2. The correct answer is C (Keep her sitting up, loosen tight clothing, then check her bladder for fullness and arrange emptying): Staying upright uses gravity to pool blood in the legs and lower blood pressure, while removing the noxious trigger is the definitive treatment. A full bladder is the most likely cause given her history, so checking and emptying it comes early. Lying flat would raise the pressure further, and calling an ambulance is for when removing triggers fails.
  3. The correct answer is D (Respect her refusal, explore her fears, offer a graded alternative later, and document the discussion): A person with capacity decides about her own treatment, and family members or a referring doctor cannot give or override consent for her. Exploring her fears and offering a gradual, lower-risk way to try again keeps the relationship and the treatment goal alive. Documenting the informed refusal protects both parties.
  4. The correct answer is A (Return her toward supine, recheck BP and symptoms, then trial a slower progression with abdominal binder): Symptomatic orthostatic hypotension after high thoracic injury is managed by lowering the angle, rechecking and then progressing more gradually, often with an abdominal binder or compression to support venous return. Holding or increasing the angle while she is symptomatic risks fainting. One episode is expected in this group and does not by itself justify abandoning the programme.
Case CHB115

Mr Binh Le, 54, a taxi driver, is on day 2 after an open right hemicolectomy through a midline incision. The epidural was removed this morning and he is on regular oral analgesia. BMI 33, smokes 15 cigarettes a day. Observations: RR 24, HR 102, BP 118/70, temperature 37.9, SpO2 91% on room air. Auscultation: reduced air entry with bronchial breathing at the left base. Chest wall expansion is shallow and he splints his abdomen. Pain on deep breathing is 6 out of 10 and 3 at rest. He has spent most of the time in bed. The chest X-ray report describes left lower zone collapse. The ward is reviewing post-operative pulmonary complications and asks physiotherapists to record a standard outcome at every treatment.

  1. The correct answer is B (Reduced air entry and bronchial breathing at the left base, matching the X-ray collapse): Localised signs, reduced air entry with bronchial breathing over one base that match the imaging, identify a collapsed lobe. A fast heart and respiratory rate, shallow expansion and a mild temperature all occur with pain, infection or simple splinting, so none of them separates collapse from pain.
  2.
