The correct answer is A (Teach supported deep breathing with a held inspiration, sitting upright on the edge of the bed): Collapse after surgery is mainly a volume problem, so upright positioning with active, wound-supported deep breaths and a held inspiration reopens the lung once analgesia has taken effect. He can still breathe actively, so a mechanical device is not the first step, and there are no secretions described that justify percussion or forced coughing in a fresh midline wound.
  3. The correct answer is A (Return him to bed, supported sitting, stay with him and activate the medical emergency response): Sudden pleuritic pain, faintness, tachycardia, a sharp fall in saturation and a swollen calf after major surgery suggest a pulmonary embolus, a time-critical emergency. The priority is safe positioning, staying with him and activating the emergency response immediately. Continuing breathing exercises treats the known collapse and misses the new picture, and splinting with a routine nursing review or rechecking first delays life-saving care.
  4. The correct answer is B (Respiratory rate, room air saturation, auscultation findings and pain score during deep breathing): Measures for a quality review should be objective, repeatable at the bedside before and after treatment, and tied to the complication being studied, which is reduced aeration limited by pain. Vital signs taken once do not show a treatment effect, and chest X-rays are not repeated every session. Counting exercises measures effort rather than effect, and a weekly rate misses the response to each session.
Case CHB116

Mrs Helen Kovacs, 86, lives in residential aged care. Three weeks ago she felt sudden mid back pain after sneezing, and imaging showed a T12 compression fracture. A DEXA scan last year showed osteoporosis, and she has not been started on osteoporosis treatment. Other history: mild kyphosis, hypertension, wrist fracture at age 78. She walks 30 metres with a four wheeled frame. Pain is 6/10 moving from sit to stand and 2/10 at rest. There is tenderness over T12. Neurological screen is normal: lower limb power 4+/5, sensation intact, reflexes normal. Berg Balance Scale is 38/56. The nurse unit manager asks about her care plan, and the family asks whether the physiotherapist can recommend a medication to prevent more fractures.

  1. The correct answer is C (Back and hip extensor strengthening, balance work and walking, avoiding loaded spinal flexion and twisting): Extensor strengthening, balance training and walking improve posture, function and falls risk while avoiding loaded flexion and twisting that stress the anterior vertebral body. Waiting for imaging to show healing means unnecessary inactivity and bone loss. Adding trunk flexion exercises is a recognised risk for another compression fracture, and a rigid brace worn all day weakens the trunk muscles she needs.
  2. The correct answer is A (A multifactorial plan combining night lighting, scheduled toileting, supervised balance training and bed height review): Falls prevention in older people works best as a multifactorial plan that targets the actual cause, here night toileting, poor lighting and impaired balance. Bed rails are a restrictive practice that raise injury risk when climbed over. Walking alone or protectors alone do not address the causes.
  3. The correct answer is A (Roll to side lying, then push through the arms as the legs lower): Rolling to the side and pushing up as the legs descend keeps the spine in a neutral position and avoids trunk flexion and rotation, which load the fractured vertebra. Sitting straight up or curling the trunk, even with arm assistance, concentrates compressive load through the anterior spine.
  4. The correct answer is D (Explain that prescribing is outside physiotherapy scope, and request the GP review her osteoporosis treatment): Physiotherapists do not prescribe or recommend specific drugs, but they should recognise untreated fracture risk and ask the GP to review her treatment. Staying within scope while taking no action on the gap leaves her osteoporosis untreated. Sending the family to a pharmacist or asking for medication under standing orders still places a medication decision outside the physiotherapist's role and the GP's review.
Case CHB117

Declan Murphy, 16, a school first-grade rugby union player, attends a private physiotherapy practice. Eight weeks ago he had a first anterior dislocation of his right (dominant) shoulder in a tackle, reduced in the emergency department, followed by two weeks in a sling. There was no fracture on X-ray and he has no other medical history. Current findings: full passive range; scapular dyskinesis on lowering the arm from elevation; external rotation strength 4+/5; axillary nerve sensation intact; sulcus sign negative. He is worried about missing the school finals and wants to resume contact training in three weeks. Last week, during a supervised band exercise at 90 degrees of abduction, his shoulder partly subluxed and he reported brief pins and needles in his hand, which settled in minutes. The practice has an incident reporting policy. The event was written only in his clinical notes.

  1. The correct answer is D (Anterior glenohumeral instability, because the posterior force limits perceived forward translation of the humeral head): Apprehension in abduction and external rotation that eases with a posterior force is a positive relocation response, consistent with anterior instability after his dislocation. Impingement tends to produce pain rather than apprehension. A posterior or labral problem would not behave this way in this position.
  2. The correct answer is A (Cuff and scapular strengthening with dynamic stabilisation drills, then graded contact exposure once criteria are met): Full passive range does not mean the shoulder is dynamically stable, so strength, scapular control and neuromuscular drills come first, followed by staged contact exposure when objective criteria are met. A fixed three week return ignores those criteria. Stretching into external rotation is unnecessary because his range is full and it stresses the anterior structures, and heavy loading in the vulnerable position risks another subluxation.
  3. The correct answer is D (Lodge a factual incident report, inform Declan and his father openly, and review the exercise progression): An adverse event during treatment should be reported through the practice system, disclosed honestly to the patient and family, and used to review risk, whatever the final harm. A clinical note alone does not feed learning. A mandatory notification to the regulator is not triggered by a minor transient event, and the GP can be informed in addition but not instead.
  4. The correct answer is D (Not cleared for contact; recurrent instability warrants orthopaedic referral before any decision on return to play): A further subluxation despite rehabilitation signals recurrent instability, so surgical opinion should be sought and contact play deferred. Strength symmetry alone does not show functional stability. Braces and taping give limited protection in a tackling sport, and return decisions belong to the clinical team with Declan, not the coach.
Case CHB118

Ms Priya Nair, 34, primary school teacher, has had relapsing-remitting multiple sclerosis for five years. She was admitted to subacute rehabilitation three days ago after a relapse with new left leg weakness and unsteadiness, treated by her neurologist with intravenous corticosteroids. Examination: left hip flexion 3+/5, left ankle dorsiflexion 3/5, right side 5/5. Mild left leg spasticity. Proprioception reduced in the left foot and Romberg unsteady with eyes closed. Berg Balance Scale 38 out of 56. She walks 30 metres with a single point stick and reaches Borg 15 after one lap. Temperature 36.7 degrees Celsius. She says heat makes her worse and she felt weaker after a hot shower this morning. She lives with her partner and hopes to return to teaching in eight weeks. She has avoided exercise since diagnosis because she fears it triggers relapses.

  1. The correct answer is B (Heat slows conduction in demyelinated nerves, causing temporary symptoms rather than new inflammatory damage): A small rise in core temperature further impairs conduction along demyelinated axons, so old symptoms reappear and settle as she cools, without a new lesion. Her unchanged examination and normal temperature argue against an extending relapse. Central fatigue and blood pressure changes are possible in other situations but do not fit this pattern.
  2. The correct answer is C (Short supervised walking bouts with rest breaks in a cool room, progressing as fatigue allows): Fatigue and heat sensitivity call for short, repeated bouts with planned rests in a cool environment, increased gradually. Pushing to fatigue in warmth risks symptom worsening and discourages practice. Enforced rest from walking promotes deconditioning, and a fixed-speed treadmill ignores her fatigue pattern.
  3. The correct answer is D (Supported standing with eyes open, practising weight shift and narrowed stance near a bench): Balance training starts with tasks she can control, using vision and hand support while she builds weight shift and base of support changes. Removing vision and adding unstable surfaces is too difficult with impaired proprioception. Avoiding standing practice has no evidence-based rationale, since exercise does not cause relapses.
  4. The correct answer is B (Exercise is safe and beneficial, so agree a graded plan with pacing and cooling strategies): Regular exercise is safe in multiple sclerosis and supports fitness, mood and fatigue, so a graded, individualised plan with pacing, energy conservation and cooling matches her work goals. Fear-based rest reinforces deconditioning. High intensity only and indefinite deferral both ignore her fatigue and heat sensitivity and her need for a plan now.
Case CHB119

Hoa Vo, 13, is referred by her GP for cough and chest tightness during netball training over the past six months. Asthma was diagnosed two years ago. She takes a daily low dose inhaled preventer and carries a reliever, which she rarely uses at training. Her father attends today. Resting observations: HR 84, RR 16, SpO2 98 percent on room air, chest clear. Her teacher reports she often drops out after about five minutes of running. Hoa says she is embarrassed to use her inhaler in front of teammates. Her father wants a supervised treadmill challenge test today to prove it is asthma, while Hoa sits quietly and looks away when it is mentioned. She has a written asthma action plan from her GP.

  1. The correct answer is A (Chest symptoms provoked by hard exertion and relieved by a reliever inhaler or by resting): Exercise induced bronchoconstriction is airway narrowing triggered by vigorous exertion, with symptoms that ease with a reliever or rest, which matches Hoa's history. Frequent night cough and symptoms lasting days point to poorly controlled asthma rather than an exertional trigger. Noisy inspiratory breathing that settles within a minute suggests inducible laryngeal obstruction, and symptoms driven by cold air or pollen without exertion describe other triggers.
  2. The correct answer is C (Graded warm up with brief high intensity bursts, with reliever use timed per her action plan): A warm up with short intense bursts can produce a refractory period in which exertional bronchoconstriction is reduced, and her action plan guides when the reliever is used. A long gentle warm up does not give that effect, and waiting for symptoms means treating after bronchoconstriction has started. A scarf can warm cold air but does not replace a planned warm up and reliever timing, and changing preventer doses without the plan is outside her prescribed regimen.
  3. The correct answer is D (Stop exercise, sit her upright, and help her take her reliever as her action plan states): Symptomatic bronchoconstriction during exercise calls for stopping the trigger, an upright posture that eases breathing, and prompt reliever use following her action plan. Continuing to walk or delaying the reliever lets the airway narrowing progress. Lying flat worsens breathlessness in asthma, and observations should be taken while she is being treated, not before.
  4. The correct answer is C (Explain the test to both, explore her concerns, proceed only with her agreement and parental consent): A young person who understands the information is entitled to have her views weighed heavily, and a physiotherapist can assess understanding as part of obtaining consent. Good practice is to explain, address her fears, and proceed only when both she and her parent agree. Relying on the parent alone overrides a capable child, deferring to the GP avoids a core professional skill, and discharging her ignores the parent's consent and the clinical need.
Case CHB120

Giovanni Rossi, 64, retired bus driver. Referred to outpatient cardiac rehabilitation 3 weeks after an uncomplicated percutaneous coronary intervention with a stent for a non-ST elevation myocardial infarction. Medications: aspirin, ticagrelor, a beta blocker, an ACE inhibitor and a statin. Resting HR 62 beats per minute, BP 126/78 mmHg, SpO2 97 percent on room air. No chest pain at rest; walks 20 minutes daily with mild breathlessness. Baseline 6 minute walk test today: 380 metres, peak Borg breathlessness 4 out of 10, rating of perceived exertion 13 on the 6 to 20 scale, peak HR 98 beats per minute. Workload this morning: the physiotherapist has 90 minutes of unallocated time. Waiting are a patient who phoned yesterday reporting chest discomfort climbing stairs, a routine discharge summary, an equipment audit due this month, and preparation for a new referral booked on Thursday. A group exercise class starts at 10:30.

  1. The correct answer is D (Use perceived exertion and symptoms to guide intensity, because the beta blocker blunts heart rate): Beta blockade lowers the heart rate response to exertion, so heart rate targets from age-predicted maximum or from today's peak would under-prescribe intensity. Perceived exertion, breathlessness ratings and symptoms remain valid guides in this setting. Walking speed alone ignores the safety information provided by his responses.
  2. The correct answer is C (Use the same course length, standard instructions and encouragement, and similar time of day): The walk test is sensitive to course length, wording of instructions, encouragement and time of day, so these must match for the two distances to be compared. Checking resting observations is sensible safety practice but does not make the two results comparable. A different corridor changes the number of turns and the distance, and comparison with predicted values does not repair that. Pacing him alters his effort and removes the self-paced design of the test.
  3. The correct answer is B (Stop exercise, follow the chest pain protocol and report the symptom to his medical team): New exertional chest tightness after a recent coronary event needs medical review even when it settles fast and the monitor is unchanged, because a single lead may not show ischaemia. Exercise must stop and the programme's protocol be followed. Resuming at a lower level only delays the review and exposes him to risk.
  4. The correct answer is C (Contact and triage the caller, since new exertional symptoms may need prompt medical review): Prioritisation rests on risk to patient safety and time sensitivity. New chest discomfort on exertion in a person after a cardiac event may need prompt medical review, so contacting and triaging the caller comes first. A routine slot delays that screening, and the discharge summary, group class preparation, audit and referral planning can all be completed after the risk has been dealt with.

Practice Test 9 Answer Key

Case CHB121

Ngoc Bui, 9, Year 4 student, brought by her mother, Linh, to the hospital outpatient physiotherapy clinic ten days after sudden right facial weakness. Two days earlier she had mild right ear pain. She is afebrile, with no headache, limb weakness, speech change or vision loss, and the GP has diagnosed Bell palsy. Findings: unable to wrinkle the right forehead or fully close the right eye, with the eyeball rolling upward on attempted closure. Right nasolabial fold flattened, mouth droops on smiling, left face normal. The right eye looks red and she says it feels gritty. Slight voluntary movement of the right mouth corner is present. The clinic uses a wall-mounted mirror for facial exercises.

  1. The correct answer is A (Involvement of the forehead on the affected side, since it receives bilateral cortical input): The upper face receives input from both cerebral hemispheres, so a central lesion usually spares the forehead. Forehead weakness therefore points to damage at or below the facial nucleus. Lower face droop occurs in both central and peripheral lesions, and unilateral onset does not separate them.
  2. The correct answer is B (Lubricate the eye often, tape the lid at night, and report any worsening redness or pain): An eye that does not close dries and the cornea can be damaged, so lubrication by day and gentle lid closure at night protect it, with prompt review if it worsens. A firm patch over an eye that cannot close can scratch the cornea. Forced blinking and tap water rinses do not protect the surface.
  3. The correct answer is C (Small slow symmetrical mirror movements, stopping before fatigue or any synkinetic eye movement): During reinnervation, effortful or exaggerated movements encourage unwanted co-contraction, so exercise uses small controlled symmetrical movements and stops before fatigue. Strong resisted work and electrical stimulation are not supported for facial nerve recovery and risk poor movement patterns. Firm stretching of weak facial muscles adds nothing and may irritate the tissue.
  4. The correct answer is A (Report it in the incident system, inform the family openly, and document the facts): A near miss that could have caused harm is reported through the organisation's incident system so the hazard is reviewed across the service, and the family is told openly. Repairing the bracket informally or waiting for a complaint loses the learning. The clinical record holds care details, while the incident report is the proper place for the event itself.
Case CHB122

Daniel Kowalczuk, 34, a plumber, was admitted to intensive care two days ago after a car rollover. He has right rib fractures 4 to 8 with a small lung contusion, and a small pericardial effusion on echocardiography. The medical team is monitoring for cardiac tamponade with serial echocardiograms. He breathes spontaneously on 4 L/min nasal prongs and has no intercostal catheter. Observations at rest: HR 98, BP 118/72, RR 24, SpO2 94%, JVP not elevated, heart sounds normal. Pain is 7/10 on coughing and 3/10 at rest, managed with a multimodal regimen. Auscultation shows reduced air entry at the right base and no wheeze. He is anxious, avoids deep breaths and has a weak, shallow cough with little sputum. His treatment is covered by the compulsory third party insurer, and the insurer's case manager has asked for a treatment plan. He lives with his partner and wants to return to work within three months.

  1. The correct answer is B (An exaggerated fall in systolic blood pressure during inspiration compared with expiration): Pulsus paradoxus is an exaggerated inspiratory fall in systolic pressure, and it is one of the bedside signs associated with tamponade because the effusion limits ventricular filling. A sustained fall after standing describes orthostatic hypotension, a different mechanism that is also easy to confuse with a haemodynamic warning sign.
  2. The correct answer is C (Supported deep breathing in upright sitting with a pillow splint, timed after analgesia): Upright sitting with splinted deep breaths after analgesia improves lung volume and cough effectiveness without loading unstable ribs. Percussion over fractured ribs risks pain and further injury, and strapping restricts expansion, which promotes atelectasis. Lying on the affected side reduces expansion of that lung, so it works against the aim.
  3. The correct answer is B (Lay him back safely, continue monitoring, and alert the intensive care medical team immediately): Hypotension with tachycardia and distended neck veins in a patient with a known effusion is a possible tamponade, which is a medical emergency. The physiotherapist makes the patient safe and escalates at once. Waiting several minutes to recheck delays the response, and attributing the change to pain or position ignores the pattern that points to a cardiac cause.
  4. The correct answer is C (Send the insurer a written plan with goals, expected sessions and outcome measures for approval): Compensable schemes generally fund treatment that has been justified and approved in advance, so a written plan with goals, expected sessions and outcome measures is the standard step. Medicare chronic disease items are not intended for an injury covered by a compensable insurer. Treating first and invoicing afterwards risks sessions being declined, and relying on a discharge summary leaves the insurer without the treatment plan it has asked for.
Case CHB123

Thuy Truong, 72, a retired market vendor, is on a general medical ward after 18 days in intensive care for septic shock from pneumonia, including 12 days of mechanical ventilation. She was decannulated from a tracheostomy last week. She speaks Vietnamese and limited English, and her daughter Mai usually translates. Weakness was first noticed when sedation was lightened; she had no limb symptoms before admission. Examination: symmetrical weakness of proximal and distal limbs, MRC sum score 38 out of 60, reduced deep tendon reflexes, intact sensation and cranial nerves. She can sit unsupported for two minutes but cannot stand. Cough is weak. HR 88, BP 118/70, RR 20, SpO2 95 percent on room air. She lives with Mai and her husband in a two-storey home. She says she is very tired and 'cannot lift her legs'. The team plans transfer to rehabilitation.

  1. The correct answer is D (Intensive care acquired weakness, as the sum score is below the usual diagnostic cut-off of 48): A sum score below 48 with symmetrical, generalised weakness, reduced reflexes and preserved sensation after prolonged ventilation and sepsis is the typical picture of intensive care acquired weakness. Guillain-Barre would normally begin with ascending symptoms before admission, and simple deconditioning does not produce a score this low or marked weakness of both proximal and distal muscles.
  2. The correct answer is B (Frequent short sessions of sitting out, supported standing and graded active limb exercises): Early, progressive and frequent mobilisation within her tolerance is the main intervention for intensive care acquired weakness, using short sessions because she fatigues quickly. Passive work alone and waiting for strength to return delay active recovery. One long session to fatigue overloads a patient who cannot yet stand, and limiting ward input to chest care wastes the time before transfer when limb work could begin.
  3. The correct answer is D (Pause mobilisation, document the neurological changes and request urgent medical review for possible spinal pathology): New sensory loss, bladder disturbance and legs weakening more than arms are red flags for spinal cord or cauda equina involvement. These are time critical and are not features of intensive care acquired weakness, where sensation is spared. Mobilisation should pause and urgent medical review be requested. Continuing with monitoring, screening only for infection or neuropathy, or referring only the bladder symptom each delay the urgent assessment she needs.
  4. The correct answer is A (Book an accredited Vietnamese interpreter for assessment and consent, and invite Mai to stay for support): Informed participation and consent need an accredited interpreter, because family members may filter, omit or add information and may not know medical terms. Inviting Mai keeps her support role. Apps, family-only interpretation and demonstrations with after-the-fact explanation all leave Thuy's own understanding and consent unchecked.
Case CHB124

Daniel Yarran, 58, an Aboriginal man from a remote community 900 kilometres away, is on a neurosurgical ward on day 3 after a left basal ganglia intracerebral haemorrhage. An external ventricular drain (EVD) is in place and intracranial pressure (ICP) is monitored. Glasgow Coma Scale 10 (E3 V2 M5), right hemiparesis. Head of bed is at 30 degrees; medical orders permit bed-level physiotherapy but not sitting out. BP 150/85 within the team's target, HR 70, SpO2 96 percent on room air. Several family members have travelled to the ward and are waiting in the corridor, distressed and unsure what is happening. Daniel's sister says the community usually decides important things together and asks to be present when you treat him. An Aboriginal Liaison Officer is available on the ward.

  1. The correct answer is A (Keep the head neutral at the ordered angle, avoiding neck rotation and tight hip flexion): Neutral head alignment and the prescribed elevation promote venous drainage from the head, while neck flexion, rotation and strong hip flexion can impede it and raise pressure. Lying flat or turning the head can increase ICP, and the positioning must respect the prescribed head angle.
  2. The correct answer is D (Stop handling, keep the head neutral and elevated, and alert the nurse managing his drain): A sustained rise during handling means the treatment is probably contributing, so activity stops and the team who manage the drain are told straight away. Finishing the movement or silencing the alarm ignores a safety signal, and lowering the head can increase intracranial pressure.
  3. The correct answer is A (Invite the family to sessions, ask how they wish to help, and engage the liaison officer): Culturally safe care respects collective decision making, asks the family how they wish to be involved, and works with the Aboriginal Liaison Officer while keeping Daniel's interests and privacy central. Excluding the family or limiting contact to one spokesperson ignores how decisions are made in many communities, and waiting for formal arrangements delays communication.
  4. The correct answer is D (Clarify the orders with neurosurgery and nursing, and plan progression around the drain status): Activity level with an EVD in place is set by the medical team, and the drain status must be confirmed so that line management and monitoring are safe during any progression. Improved scores alone do not authorise sitting, and refusing all progression until removal may delay appropriate early mobilisation.
Case CHB125

Ms Alicia Fonoti, 44, is 14 months after a right middle cerebral artery stroke and lives with her sister in a two-storey house. She attends community rehabilitation. Two weeks ago she had botulinum toxin injections to the left gastrocnemius, soleus and tibialis posterior for plantarflexion and inversion spasticity that causes toe drag and ankle rolling. Findings today: left ankle passive dorsiflexion 0 degrees with the knee extended and 8 degrees with the knee flexed. Modified Ashworth Scale 2 at the plantarflexors. Active dorsiflexion 1/5. She walks 10 metres with a stick and a rigid ankle-foot orthosis that she finds uncomfortable. Skin is intact and she has no pain at rest. The doctor has requested serial casting. You also have an urgent new post-operative referral, a family meeting at 2 pm and an overdue progress note.

  1. The correct answer is A (It temporarily reduces muscle overactivity, giving a window for stretching, casting and training): Botulinum toxin weakens the injected overactive muscles for a limited period, and the therapy team uses that window to lengthen tissue with casting and stretching and to train movement. It does not change muscle length by itself, so stretching and casting remain needed. Waiting for the effect to wear off wastes the window, and the toxin acts at the muscle rather than repairing or improving drive from the brain lesion.
  2. The correct answer is B (Check skin, circulation and sensation, and teach her when to seek urgent cast removal): Casting carries risks of pressure injury and circulatory compromise, so skin, circulation and sensation must be checked and the patient and carers must know when to have it removed. Casting is used precisely because tone and range are limited, and it does not require active control. Orthosis review is useful later but not a prerequisite.
  3. The correct answer is B (Have the cast removed urgently at a clinic or at the emergency department today): Severe pain under the heel with pale, cold toes suggests compromised circulation or a pressure area under the cast, which can cause lasting tissue damage within hours. Prompt removal and assessment today is required. Elevation, medicine and a recheck, or a visit tomorrow all build in a delay that she cannot safely afford, and she is alone at home.
  4. The correct answer is C (See the urgent referral first, explain and rebook Alicia's cast, then meeting and note): Clinical urgency should drive the order of work, and because the cast can wait a day, rebooking with a clear explanation is acceptable. Documentation and booking order matter but do not outrank an urgent new post-operative patient. Casting is not appropriate to delegate to an assistant.
Case CHB126

Giuseppe (Joe) Romano, 47, accountant and masters sprinter (100 m), has state championships in six weeks. Five days ago he felt a sudden pull at the back of his right thigh while accelerating in a race, stopped immediately and limped for two days. He now walks normally. Bruising is visible along the posterior thigh. History: left calf strain three years ago, no medical conditions, no regular medications. Examination: pain 3/10 walking and 6/10 on attempted fast running. Tender over the proximal myotendinous junction of biceps femoris with no palpable gap. Resisted knee flexion at 90 degrees is 4/5 and painful. Passive straight leg raise is 60 degrees right and 80 degrees left. Slump test negative, lumbar screen clear. Joe asks you to film his running next visit so his coach can review technique, and he is happy for you to do so.

  1. The correct answer is D (Pain-tolerable isometrics progressing to isotonic loading, with pain-free cross training to maintain fitness): Early pain-tolerable loading, progressed from isometric to isotonic work, promotes tissue healing while cross training preserves fitness. Prolonged rest delays loading and delays recovery. Aggressive stretching into pain and friction can aggravate a healing myotendinous junction, and jogging with moderate pain risks re-injury.
  2. The correct answer is B (Stop loading and refer promptly for specialist review and imaging of suspected avulsion): A sudden pop with marked weakness, a palpable defect near the ischial tuberosity and extensive bruising suggest a proximal hamstring tendon avulsion or rupture, where early specialist opinion and imaging matter. Rest with ice and compression treats a muscle strain, not a structural tendon injury. Waiting 48 hours or starting isometric loading delays referral and risks a worse outcome.
  3. The correct answer is C (Graded sprint exposure from submaximal speeds, reaching full speed only when symptoms stay settled): Return to sprinting is criterion-based and staged: strength alone does not show that the muscle tolerates the high strain of maximum speed, so exposure builds from submaximal runs with symptoms monitored. Waiting for imaging or an arbitrary time point does not show readiness. Full speed now skips the staged exposure that protects against re-injury.
  4. The correct answer is B (Explain purpose, sharing and storage, get documented consent, and use a secure clinic system): Valid consent for recording and sharing needs an explanation of what is recorded, who will see it and how it is stored, and it should be documented. Footage is health information and belongs in a secure system, not a personal phone or messaging app. A form that does not cover disclosure to the coach, or partial identification measures, does not meet these requirements.
Case CHB127

Eileen Walsh, 71, a retired shopkeeper, attends community health with 18 months of bilateral knee pain, the right worse than the left. Pain is 6/10 after walking 15 minutes or climbing stairs, and morning stiffness lasts about ten minutes. There is no locking, no night pain and no history of injury. BMI 31. An X-ray two years ago showed moderate medial joint space narrowing. She takes medication for hypertension, well controlled. Her daughter, who attends, mentions some memory lapses but there is no formal diagnosis. Right knee flexion 120 degrees, extension 0, no effusion, crepitus on movement; quadriceps strength 4/5; single leg stand 5 seconds; 30 second chair stand 7 repetitions. A friend told her she needs surgery and that exercise wears out the joint. Her daughter says, 'Just do whatever you think best, Mum agrees to anything.'

  1. The correct answer is D (Education, strengthening and aerobic exercise are first-line, with weight management as support): Current guidance for knee osteoarthritis places education, exercise and weight management first, because progressive strengthening and aerobic work reduce pain and improve function without accelerating joint damage. Deferring strengthening until pain settles removes the main treatment for her pain. Limiting strengthening leaves her quadriceps weakness unaddressed, and leading with passive treatments leaves the main modifiable factors untreated.
  2. The correct answer is C (Short-lived soreness that settles within a day is acceptable, so continue and progress load gradually): A temporary rise in knee ache after new exercise is common in osteoarthritis and does not indicate structural harm if it settles within about a day, so the programme continues with gradual progression. Stopping or switching to unloaded work reinforces her fear of harm and slows strength gains. Repeat imaging is not indicated by exercise soreness alone.
  3. The correct answer is D (Pause loading exercise and refer for medical review for a possible mechanical knee injury): Sudden swelling after a twist, catching and blocked extension suggest a possible meniscal tear or loose body rather than a simple osteoarthritis flare. Loading, forced mobilisation or warm water exercise could worsen a mechanical block, so exercise is paused and she is referred for medical review. Treating it as a routine flare risks missing a surgical problem.
  4. The correct answer is B (Re-explain simply, check understanding with teach-back, and presume capacity unless assessment shows otherwise): Capacity is presumed in adults and is decision-specific, so memory lapses alone do not remove her right to decide. The physiotherapist adjusts communication, uses teach-back and gives time, involving the daughter only as support if Mrs Walsh wishes. A formal capacity assessment is justified only if understanding remains impaired despite these supports, and a nod is not informed consent.
Case CHB128

Trang Dang Nguyen, 64, a retired seamstress who speaks Vietnamese and limited English, is on day 16 in intensive care after septic shock from a perforated bowel and laparotomy. She was ventilated for 11 days and now has a tracheostomy on a trachy mask with FiO2 0.28. She is alert (RASS 0), follows simple gestured commands and is stable: HR 88, BP 118/70 mmHg, SpO2 96 percent, RR 20, afebrile. Her Medical Research Council (MRC) sum score is 34 out of 60, with symmetrical weakness that is greater proximally: hip flexion 2, shoulder abduction 2, ankle dorsiflexion 3. Reflexes are reduced, facial strength is normal and light touch sensation is intact. She has spent most of the admission in bed. Her daughter Mai has been interpreting for staff, but Mrs Nguyen nods and seems to defer to every request. A telephone interpreter service is available.

  1. The correct answer is A (Use a professional interpreter, confirm understanding by teach-back, and include Mai if her mother wishes): Informed consent needs accurate, impartial interpretation, so a professional interpreter is used and understanding is checked with teach-back, with family present only if the patient chooses. A relative may filter or soften information and may not be free of influence, and nodding is not evidence of understanding. Untrained bilingual staff are not accredited interpreters and carry the same accuracy risks.
  2. The correct answer is C (A sum score below 48 out of 60 is consistent with significant intensive care acquired weakness): The MRC sum score grades six muscle groups on each side of the body, three in the arms and three in the legs, giving twelve groups and a maximum of 60. The total below 48 out of 60 is the accepted bedside marker of significant intensive care acquired weakness. The score does not separate neuropathy from myopathy, and electrophysiology is not required before the clinical diagnosis is made. The low score is a reason to rehabilitate with suitable grading, not to withhold active work.
  3. The correct answer is C (Daily active-assisted exercise and graded sitting out of bed with a hoist, monitoring vital signs throughout): She is stable and cooperative, so early graded active rehabilitation with appropriate lifting aids and monitoring is safe and reduces further deconditioning. Waiting for a score threshold delays benefit, and heavy loading is unsuited to muscles graded two. Delaying chair sitting until decannulation withholds a safe, useful upright activity because a tracheostomy alone is not a barrier.
  4. The correct answer is B (Stop, return her to a supported reclined position, and monitor until observations recover): Combined desaturation, tachypnoea and tachycardia are signs of intolerance, so activity stops and she is placed in a supported reclined position that eases breathing while observations are monitored. Continuing risks further deterioration. Lying flat can worsen breathlessness in a weak patient, and suctioning has no indication without signs of retained secretions.
Case CHB129

Tomasi Vaka, 38, long-distance truck driver, attends a private practice. Five days ago he woke with a right facial droop after a cold the week before. His GP examined him, excluded other causes and started treatment; the referral letter states Bell palsy. Findings: unable to wrinkle the right forehead or raise the right eyebrow; the right eye closes about halfway with the eyeball rolling upward; right nasolabial fold flat; the mouth corner droops and saliva escapes on the right when drinking; sounds seem louder on the right; taste mildly altered. Limb strength, sensation, reflexes and speech are normal. BP 128/78 mmHg. House-Brackmann grade IV. The practice employs an allied health assistant, Priya, who started last month and works under your supervision.

  1. The correct answer is B (Weakness involves the upper face including the forehead, indicating a peripheral facial nerve lesion): The forehead receives input from both sides of the brain, so it is spared in a central palsy such as stroke but weak when the facial nerve itself is damaged. Onset over days and mouth droop occur in both conditions, so they do not separate them. Preserved eyebrow elevation would point toward a central cause.
  2. The correct answer is C (Use lubricating drops by day, ointment or gentle taped closure at night, and sunglasses outdoors): Incomplete lid closure and reduced blinking leave the cornea exposed, so regular lubrication, protection at night and sun or wind protection are the priority. Patching can trap debris and scratch the surface, and rubbing the lid does not help the eye. Waiting for pain before using drops delays protection of a cornea that may already be dry.
  3. The correct answer is C (Gentle low effort mirror exercises suit this stage, and electrical stimulation has limited supporting evidence): Gentle, low effort movement is consistent with current practice, whereas vigorous exercise may encourage unwanted movement patterns later and the evidence for electrical stimulation is limited. Avoiding movement altogether is not required. Stimulating to maintain bulk is not supported by good evidence.
  4. The correct answer is D (Delegate the agreed exercise demonstration only, and keep the eye reassessment with the physiotherapist): An allied health assistant can carry out and reinforce clear, routine tasks within their training once the physiotherapist has assessed the patient and set the plan. Reassessing eye closure and judging the risk to the cornea needs clinical reasoning, so it stays with the physiotherapist, who remains accountable. A signed form does not transfer that accountability.
Case CHB130

Amara Okafor, 15, plays school netball and attends outpatient physiotherapy with her mother. History: recurrent left ankle sprains over 2 years and two right shoulder subluxations that reduced themselves. Her GP suspects a hypermobility spectrum disorder. Examination: Beighton score 7 of 9, thumb to forearm bilaterally, elbow and knee hyperextension, left ankle anterior drawer laxity without pain, right shoulder apprehension in abduction and external rotation, and poor scapular control. Neurological screen is normal. Today, during a single leg balance drill on a wobble board that you were supervising, her left ankle gave way and she fell onto her outstretched right hand. She now has wrist pain 4/10, tenderness in the anatomical snuffbox, mild swelling, and wrist movement limited by pain. Her pulse and capillary refill are normal. There was no head strike. Her mother is in the waiting room.

  1. The correct answer is D (It records generalised hypermobility, but diagnosing a connective tissue condition needs medical assessment): The Beighton score is a screening measure of generalised joint laxity at nine sites. It does not diagnose a specific syndrome, predict dislocations or measure instability, and the diagnosis of a connective tissue disorder rests with medical practitioners. Strength training can improve control but does not change the score in the way a retest would suggest.
  2. The correct answer is C (Scapular control and rotator cuff work in mid-range, progressing load slowly and avoiding end range): In a hypermobile shoulder the aim is dynamic stability, so scapular control and cuff strength are trained in controlled mid-range positions and load is progressed gradually. Heavy loads from the first session outpace her motor control, and training at end range exposes the position in which she has already subluxated. Delaying cuff work until apprehension resolves leaves the shoulder without its main dynamic stabilisers.
  3. The correct answer is B (Immobilise the wrist and thumb in a splint and arrange same-day medical assessment for imaging): Snuffbox tenderness after a fall on an outstretched hand raises suspicion of a scaphoid fracture, which can be missed early and carries a risk of non-union or avascular necrosis. Immobilising the wrist and thumb and arranging same-day assessment is the safe response. Routine imaging a week away or waiting for symptoms to persist delays diagnosis, and treating a normal first film as a sprain ignores that early radiographs can miss a scaphoid fracture.
  4. The correct answer is A (Explain what happened openly, document it factually, and report it via the practice incident system): An adverse event during care should be disclosed honestly to the patient and family, recorded factually in the clinical notes, and reported through the organisation's incident system so the practice can learn and reduce risk. Incident systems cover patient events as well as staff injuries. Waiting for a diagnosis before reporting weakens transparency, and the physiotherapist who was supervising the drill carries their own reporting duty, which cannot be passed to the doctor.
Case CHB131

Divya Gupta, 27, a graphic designer working from home, has a video consultation from her bedroom. She has hypermobile Ehlers-Danlos syndrome, diagnosed by a rheumatologist, who recorded a Beighton score of 7 out of 9. Her right shoulder subluxates a few times a month with overhead reaching, and both wrists ache after mouse use. A previous course of general stretching made her shoulder feel looser. On video she demonstrates active elevation: the right scapula wings and the humeral head appears to shift forward. Her pain with reaching is 3 out of 10 and she has none at rest. She mentions feeling light-headed and noticing her heart race when she stands up quickly. Her employer's human resources manager has emailed you directly asking for her diagnosis and a statement about her work capacity, saying Divya told them you would help. Divya has not mentioned this to you. A flatmate is in the next room.

  1. The correct answer is A (It screens for generalised hypermobility and neither confirms a syndrome nor predicts instability): The Beighton score is a screening measure of generalised joint hypermobility, scored from nine simple manoeuvres. Diagnosis of a hypermobility disorder also needs history, clinical features and exclusion of other conditions, so the score cannot diagnose on its own. It does not grade severity or predict how often she will subluxate, and it does not show which joints are unstable, because the manoeuvres test only a few sites.
  2. The correct answer is B (Slow low load scapular and cuff strengthening with motor control cues within stable mid-range): In hypermobility, joint stability depends on muscle control, so slow, low load strengthening with attention to scapular position and cuff co-ordination in mid-range is the safe starting point and can be progressed. Loading at end range works in the position where the joint already gives way. Avoiding overhead tasks and delaying strengthening leaves control untrained, and fast overhead drills are too demanding for an unstable shoulder at this stage.
  3. The correct answer is D (Pause standing work, keep her seated, and advise GP review for possible orthostatic intolerance): Light-headedness and a marked heart rate rise on standing that settle with sitting suggest orthostatic intolerance, which is common alongside hypermobile Ehlers-Danlos syndrome and needs medical evaluation. Standing work should pause until the GP has reviewed her, while seated exercise can continue. Pushing through symptoms risks a fall in a person with unstable joints, and an ambulance is not warranted when symptoms settle promptly.
  4. The correct answer is C (Release nothing yet, confirm with Divya what she consents to share, and document it): Health information may be disclosed only with the patient's informed consent or where the law requires it, so a third party's statement that Divya agreed is not enough. The physiotherapist should confirm directly with Divya what she wants shared, release only that, and document the consent and the disclosure. Sending limited details or using secure email does not replace consent.
Case CHB132

Baby Tevita Fifita, 6 weeks old, is seen in the hospital outpatient clinic with his parents, Ana and Sione. Born at term by vaginal birth with shoulder dystocia, birth weight 4.3 kg. His right arm has moved little since birth. Observations: right arm rests adducted and internally rotated with the forearm pronated, wrist and fingers move well and grasp is present. Moro response is asymmetrical, with no active right shoulder abduction or external rotation and weak elbow flexion; left side normal. Clavicle is not tender, no crepitus, and breathing and facial movement are symmetrical. Passive shoulder external rotation reaches neutral with a soft end feel. Ana is exhausted, breastfeeding every two to three hours, and worried she will hurt him. Sione works night shifts and rarely attends clinic.

  1. The correct answer is B (Upper trunk (C5 and C6) injury, sparing the hand because lower roots are intact): Loss of shoulder abduction, external rotation and elbow flexion with preserved hand function is the classic upper trunk pattern, often called Erb palsy. A lower trunk injury affects the hand first, and a total injury would leave a flail arm with absent grasp. An isolated axillary nerve lesion would spare elbow flexion.
  2. The correct answer is A (Gentle passive movements with the scapula supported, short and frequent, stopping before resistance): Gentle, frequent passive movement with the shoulder blade supported keeps the joint mobile and prevents contracture without stressing the injured plexus or the joint. Forceful stretching risks injury, and strapping the arm encourages stiffness. Pulling through the hand loads a vulnerable shoulder and nerve, and he cannot cooperate with resisted work.
  3. The correct answer is B (Refer promptly to a specialist brachial plexus service to consider surgical options): Absent antigravity elbow flexion at around three months suggests poor spontaneous recovery, and early specialist review matters because surgical timing affects results. Waiting longer wastes the window for nerve surgery. Home electrical stimulation is not established practice for nerve regeneration, and constraint is not suitable for an infant with a flaccid limb.
  4. The correct answer is D (Demonstrate handling, let both parents practise with feedback, and agree a simple routine): Hands-on practice with feedback builds skill and confidence, and a simple routine that fits their life is more likely to be sustained than a long checklist. Involving both parents shares the load, and teaching Ana alone leaves her carrying it. Delaying teaching withholds support when it is needed most.
Case CHB133

Mr Graham Pollard, 72, a retired diesel mechanic, attends a hospital outpatient pulmonary rehabilitation programme in a regional town. Diagnosis: COPD, FEV1 42 percent predicted, ex-smoker. Medications: a long-acting inhaled bronchodilator (tiotropium) and a reliever. He lives with his wife, who drives him. Week 1 assessment: resting SpO2 94 percent on room air, HR 82, RR 18, BP 138/84. Six minute walk test 280 m, SpO2 fell to 88 percent, Borg dyspnoea 5 of 10, leg fatigue 4 of 10. Chest: reduced air entry bilaterally, prolonged expiration, no added sounds. Goals: walk to the local bowling club (about 400 m) and carry his shopping. Program: three supervised sessions a week, walking and cycling at a target of Borg dyspnoea 3 to 4, plus strength work and education. He says at the end of the first session that his mouth has been very dry since starting the inhaler.

  1. The correct answer is A (Stop the treadmill, seat him and coach pursed-lip breathing until SpO2 and dyspnoea settle, then reassess): A saturation of 86 percent with dyspnoea well above target means exercise should pause, with seated rest and breathing control until values recover. Slowing slightly or pressing on leaves him still desaturated and breathless. Abandoning the whole session is unnecessary because he can usually resume at a lower intensity once recovered.
  2. The correct answer is A (Supervised progressive endurance training of the legs, with strength work and self-management education alongside): Supervised exercise training, particularly progressive lower limb endurance work supported by strength training and education, has the most consistent evidence for better exercise capacity and quality of life. Inspiratory muscle training adds little when used alone. Education helps self-management but does not by itself change walking capacity, and training below a breathless threshold gives too small a stimulus.
  3. The correct answer is D (Increase treadmill speed or incline in small steps to bring dyspnoea back to the target range): Dyspnoea of 2 sits below the 3 to 4 range, so the training stimulus is too low and intensity should rise gradually to reach it. Holding the load or only lengthening walks leaves the stimulus under target. His saturation of 91 percent leaves ample room to progress, so it is not a reason to hold back.
  4. The correct answer is B (Encourage him to discuss it with his GP or pharmacist before changing anything, offering a note): Changing or stopping prescribed medication is outside physiotherapy scope, and swapping a maintenance inhaler for a reliever could worsen his airflow limitation and raise exacerbation risk. The safe response sends the question to the prescriber or pharmacist, who can adjust treatment, while the physiotherapist shares observations. Reassurance alone leaves a real adherence problem unaddressed.
Case CHB134

Tahlia Wilson, 41, a Wiradjuri woman and school teacher's aide, was referred by her Aboriginal Community Controlled Health Service GP three months after COVID-19. She reports ongoing breathlessness and fatigue. After a long walk on Saturday she felt worse for two days afterwards. She has no known lung disease and does not smoke. Resting HR 78, BP 118/72, SpO2 98 percent on room air, RR 16. Lung auscultation is clear. Six minute walk test: 380 m, SpO2 nadir 95 percent, Borg breathlessness 5 out of 10, peak HR 128. She breathes with upper chest dominance and sighs often, especially when talking. She answers briefly, looks down often, and says her aunty usually helps her decide about health matters. Her aunty is in the waiting room.

  1. The correct answer is B (Symptom guided pacing with small, gradual increases, avoiding activity levels that trigger next day symptom flares): Worsening of symptoms a day or two after exertion suggests post exertional symptom exacerbation, so activity is paced and increased cautiously according to symptoms. Fixed schedules and high intensity work risk repeated flares. Prolonged rest followed by a standard graded programme does not respect the delayed response.
  2. The correct answer is C (No significant exertional desaturation, so gas exchange failure is unlikely to explain her breathlessness): A small drop from 98 to 95 percent is not clinically significant desaturation, so impaired gas exchange is an unlikely cause of her symptoms, and factors such as deconditioning or breathing pattern deserve attention. One normal saturation result does not exclude all lung disease, so it cannot replace medical review. The fall is too small to need oxygen assessment or a repeat test with supplemental oxygen.
  3. The correct answer is B (Teach relaxed lower chest breathing at rest and while talking, then link it to pacing): An altered breathing pattern with normal lung findings is addressed by retraining relaxed, low effort breathing first at rest and then in speech and activity. Upper chest dominance does not prove weakness, and high load training could provoke a flare. Rebreathing bags are outdated and forced deep breathing tends to increase overbreathing.
  4. The correct answer is A (Offer to include her aunty if she wishes, allow unhurried yarning, and let her set goals): Cultural safety means the patient decides how care is delivered, so she is offered family involvement, time to build trust through conversation, and control of her own goals. Directing questions to her aunty removes her voice in her own care. A standard checklist of beliefs treats culture as a fixed item, and postponing to arrange a worker without asking what Ms Wilson wants today puts a decision for her ahead of her own preferences.
Case CHB135

Lucia Cattaneo, 68, retired shop assistant, referred by her GP to hospital outpatient physiotherapy. Right heel pain for three months, worst with first steps in the morning and after 20 minutes of walking. History: type 2 diabetes (metformin), bilateral knee osteoarthritis, and a course of ciprofloxacin for a urinary infection finished two weeks ago, after which her pain worsened. Examination: tender thickening 4 cm above the calcaneus, painful arc sign, Thompson test negative, single leg heel raise six repetitions on the right and fourteen on the left, VISA-A score 48/100. No sudden event or palpable gap. She walks with a stick and lives alone, managing her own medications. Her daughter Elena, a nurse, drives her to appointments and has asked to speak with you.

  1. The correct answer is B (Midportion tendinopathy with an intact tendon and reduced calf capacity, suited to progressive tendon loading): A negative Thompson test, thickening in the midportion and loaded pain with reduced calf endurance fit midportion tendinopathy with an intact tendon. A partial tear would not be expected without a sudden event, and insertional pain is felt at the heel itself. Bursitis causes pain deep to the insertion rather than thickening above it.
  2. The correct answer is A (Mild pain resolving by the next day is acceptable and guides how load is progressed): Pain that is mild and settles by the next day indicates the tendon is tolerating the load, so it can guide progression. Stopping for any pain removes the stimulus the tendon needs, and zero pain is not required. A higher pain level or slow recovery suggests the load is too high.
  3. The correct answer is A (Continue with monitoring, advise reporting sudden pain, and inform the GP about the antibiotic association): Fluoroquinolone antibiotics are linked to tendon problems, so the physiotherapist informs the GP who prescribed the drug and teaches her what sudden change to report. With a stable examination and no sudden event, continuing monitored loading is reasonable. Stopping care or sending her to the emergency department is not supported without red flags.
  4. The correct answer is B (Decline to share details, explain privacy obligations, and ask Lucia about consent at her next visit): Information may be shared only with the patient's consent or where the law allows, and Elena's profession or role does not change that. The physiotherapist should decline politely, explain the reason and invite Lucia to give consent. Partial disclosure or email verification still releases information without her permission.

Practice Test 10 Answer Key

Case CHB136

Helen Papadopoulos, 58, office manager. Three months of right lateral hip pain, 5/10, worse lying on the right side at night, climbing stairs and standing on one leg. BMI 31, postmenopausal, no trauma, fever or night sweats, no leg symptoms from the back. Examination: focal tenderness over the right greater trochanter, pain within 30 seconds of single leg stance on the right, mild pelvic drop on walking, hip flexion and internal rotation full and pain-free, lumbar screen normal. Hip radiographs ordered by her GP are unremarkable. Her GP has offered a corticosteroid injection; Helen prefers to try exercise first and wants to return to walking groups. The practice employs Sam, an allied health assistant (Certificate IV), who will supervise her exercise sessions.

  1. The correct answer is A (Gluteal tendon related lateral hip pain, with an intra-articular hip source and lumbar referral less likely): Focal trochanteric tenderness, pain on single leg stance and pain-free hip rotation and flexion point to gluteal tendon related lateral hip pain. Pain-free rotation and normal radiographs make an intra-articular source less likely, and a normal lumbar screen argues against referral. Describing it as acute bursitis misses the tendon loading cause.
  2. The correct answer is D (Avoid compressive postures, then progress from isometric to gluteal strengthening within tolerable symptoms): Compressive postures such as side lying on the painful hip and crossing legs load the gluteal tendons and should be reduced while loading progresses from isometric work. Stretching into adduction adds compression, long rest causes deconditioning, and heavy painful loading is too provocative at this stage.
  3. The correct answer is C (Injection gives early relief, but education with exercise offers similar or better later outcomes): Evidence suggests injection gives earlier relief, while education with exercise gives similar or better outcomes later. Sharing this lets Helen decide with her GP. Claiming injection is superior at every point ignores the later results, and the claim of tendon damage is overstated. Requiring a rest period is not supported.
  4. The correct answer is D (Give written progression and stopping limits, confirm Sam's competence, and reassess Helen at set intervals): Delegation needs clear parameters, a check of the assistant's competence and ongoing review by the physiotherapist, who stays accountable for the plan. An assistant's qualification does not allow independent prescription decisions. Reviewing only on request or signing notes at the end leaves oversight gaps.
Case CHB137

Lui Tapueluelu, 28, plumber from regional Queensland. Ten weeks ago he was injured in a road crash and has a complete C6 tetraplegia (ASIA Impairment Scale A) with a stabilising spinal fusion. He is in a spinal rehabilitation unit, medically stable and off the ventilator. Strength: wrist extensors 4/5, elbow flexors 5/5, triceps 0/5, no finger movement, no sensation below C6. Sitting balance is poor without arm support and two staff are needed to transfer him. Straight leg raise reaches 80 degrees bilaterally. He wants to return to his two-storey home in a small town and to work. A compulsory third party claim is open, his NDIS application is pending, and the team plans discharge in about three weeks. His custom wheelchair prescription awaits approval and his family are asking why.

  1. The correct answer is B (Manual wheelchair propulsion on level surfaces and sliding-board transfers, possibly with some assistance): At C6 the wrist extensors and elbow flexors allow manual propulsion on level ground and sliding-board transfers, though many people still need help with some tasks. Without triceps, uneven terrain and kerbs usually need power assistance or a helper. Functional walking is not expected after a complete cervical injury, and hoist-only dependence underestimates his potential.
  2. The correct answer is B (Stretch finger flexors with the wrist flexed, and avoid stretching them while the wrist is extended): Tenodesis grip depends on the long finger flexors being slightly tight over the fingers when the wrist extends, so closing the fingers happens passively. Stretching the fingers with the wrist flexed keeps finger joints supple while preserving that tightness across the wrist. Stretching over both joints together removes the grip, and doing nothing risks fixed contractures.
  3. The correct answer is A (Write a report justifying the equipment need and coordinate with the case manager and occupational therapist): Funders under compensable schemes and the NDIS decide on documented, reasoned evidence of need, so a clear functional report coordinated with the case manager and occupational therapist is the most effective support. A verbal statement carries little weight, a generic chair may not meet his needs, and delaying discharge is a team decision rather than a way to fix a funding gap.
  4. The correct answer is C (Manually assisted cough, with an abdominal thrust timed to his expiratory effort after a deep breath): A C6 injury paralyses the abdominal and intercostal muscles needed for a forceful expiration, so he cannot generate an effective cough alone. An assisted cough supplies the missing expiratory force once secretions are mobilised. The other techniques depend on his own expiratory effort and leave the main problem unaddressed.
Case CHB138

Hoa Ngo is a 58-year-old retired seamstress with pulmonary arterial hypertension and right ventricular dysfunction (WHO functional class III). She takes a diuretic and a prescribed pulmonary vasodilator and has home oxygen for exertion only. Her respiratory physician has referred her to the outpatient cardiopulmonary rehabilitation programme. At initial assessment: resting HR 84, BP 104/66 mmHg, SpO2 94% on room air, RR 18, mild bilateral ankle oedema, and raised jugular venous pressure noted in the cardiology letter. She reports breathlessness on one flight of stairs and light-headedness when hurrying. A 6 minute walk test on room air gave 280 m, with HR rising to 112, SpO2 falling to 86% and Borg dyspnoea 6 of 10. The clinic manager has asked the physiotherapists to choose outcome measures so the programme can be audited and improved.

  1. The correct answer is A (Short low intensity walking bouts guided by Borg rating, using prescribed oxygen with SpO2 monitoring): Low intensity work in short bouts keeps the load on the right ventricle manageable, and Borg ratings let effort be set by symptoms rather than heart rate. Her walk test showed marked desaturation, so her prescribed oxygen with SpO2 monitoring is needed from the start of exertion. Continuous cycling to a heart rate target and waiting for severe symptoms before oxygen both risk right heart strain and hypoxaemia, while seated work only wastes the supervised training she has been approved for.
  2. The correct answer is A (Straining raises intrathoracic pressure, reducing venous return and output from an already strained right ventricle): Holding the breath against effort raises pressure inside the chest, which falls venous return and limits output from a right ventricle already working against high pulmonary pressure. Light loads with continuous breathing avoid this effect. The mechanism involving shunting away from the pulmonary capillaries does not describe what happens during straining.
  3. The correct answer is C (Stop exercise, seat her with oxygen, monitor observations, and contact the treating cardiology team today): Rapid weight gain, rising oedema, near-fainting, low blood pressure and desaturation despite oxygen suggest decompensating right heart failure, so exercise must stop and she needs medical review the same day. Seating her with oxygen and monitoring keeps her safe while the team is contacted. Lying flat can worsen breathlessness in right heart failure, and carrying on or delaying the report postpones urgent care.
  4. The correct answer is D (Repeat identical measures and protocol at discharge, adding a patient-reported quality of life questionnaire): Repeating the same walk test protocol, Borg rating and functional class at discharge, together with a patient-reported quality of life measure, gives comparable before and after data for an audit. Attendance and satisfaction describe the service but not clinical change. Resting heart rate and SpO2 do not reflect exercise capacity, and comparing with healthy norms ignores each patient's own baseline.
Case CHB139

Mele Fifita, 15, netball player, fell during a game eight weeks ago and had open reduction and internal fixation of a left bimalleolar ankle fracture. She was non-weight bearing for six weeks and has just been cleared by her orthopaedic surgeon for partial weight bearing, 20 kilograms through the left leg, and ankle movement. The wound is healed. She attends a paediatric rehabilitation day programme with her mother and uses elbow crutches. Findings: figure-of-eight swelling 1.5 cm greater than the right and worse by the evening; active dorsiflexion 5 degrees with a firm end feel, plantarflexion 30 degrees; calf and quadriceps wasting on the left; pain 3/10; lower limb function questionnaire 38/80. She wants to return to netball and is anxious about falling.

  1. The correct answer is A (The foot takes load up to the limit, practised on bathroom scales before walking): Partial weight bearing means a prescribed amount of load is allowed, so the patient should feel the limit on scales before relying on it during gait. Touching down for balance describes a more restricted order. Pain is an unreliable guide to load, and a percentage of body weight is a different instruction from a stated kilogram limit.
  2. The correct answer is C (Active ankle pumps and gentle assisted dorsiflexion, with elevation and compression between sessions): Active and gently assisted movement restores range without breaching the load limit, and elevation with compression controls the swelling that limits dorsiflexion. Stretching on a step loads the leg beyond the order. Aggressive end-range mobilisation risks irritating healing tissue, and resisted work is premature when range and swelling are the main limits.
  3. The correct answer is C (Stop exercise, avoid calf massage and arrange urgent medical assessment for suspected thrombosis): Recent surgery, reduced mobility, a long trip and a warm, tender, rapidly swelling calf make deep vein thrombosis a real possibility, so exercise and massage should stop and she needs urgent medical assessment. Normal observations and no chest symptoms do not exclude it. Compression, walking or waiting for a scheduled review all delay diagnosis.
  4. The correct answer is D (Repeat the same measure at discharge for all patients and review grouped change together): Using one validated measure at the start and end of care for every patient allows change to be compared across the whole group and over time, which is what quality improvement needs. Satisfaction ratings do not measure function. Mixed measures cannot be combined, and adult norms are a poor benchmark for adolescents recovering from fracture.
Case CHB140

Mr Koby Simpson, 74, retired stockman, Aboriginal man from a remote community 600 km away, admitted three days ago under the general medical team with five days of low back pain after lifting a heavy tank lid. Constant 8/10 pain, worse at night and not eased by rest; unintentional weight loss of about 6 kg over three months. History: prostate cancer treated three years ago, type 2 diabetes, ex-smoker. Observations: HR 82, BP 138/84, temperature 36.8, SpO2 96 percent on room air. Examination: percussion tenderness over the L2 spinous process, lower limb power 5/5, reflexes normal, saddle sensation intact, no bladder or bowel change. The team has requested physiotherapy to mobilise him. His niece Rhonda is at the bedside. He has not yet met the hospital's Aboriginal Liaison Officer and seems uneasy discussing private matters.

  1. The correct answer is D (Features suggest possible spinal metastasis or pathological fracture, so mechanical back pain should not be assumed): Night pain unrelieved by rest, unexplained weight loss, previous cancer, age and spinal percussion tenderness are red flags for serious spinal pathology such as metastasis or fracture. Normal power, reflexes and saddle sensation with no bladder or bowel change argue against cauda equina syndrome at present. An acute disc injury or mechanical strain would not explain constant night pain with weight loss, and inflammatory spondyloarthropathy usually starts in younger adults.
  2. The correct answer is C (Hold exercise and spinal loading, and discuss the findings with the medical team before mobilising): With red flags and no imaging, spinal loading or mobilisation could worsen an unstable lesion, so the physiotherapist holds treatment and communicates findings to the medical team first. Braces, extension exercises or manual therapy assume mechanical pain. Mobilising before medical review risks harm.
  3. The correct answer is D (Keep him lying, stop the session and urgently notify the treating team of the new signs): New saddle numbness, urinary retention and increasing weakness suggest cauda equina syndrome or cord compromise, which needs emergency medical review. Delay for rechecks, a bladder scan or an upright assessment wastes time and risks irreversible loss. The session stops and he stays lying.
  4. The correct answer is D (Offer Koby the choice of having Rhonda or the Liaison Officer present, and allow unhurried time): Culturally safe practice lets the person choose who supports him and respects his comfort, so Koby is offered Rhonda or the Aboriginal Liaison Officer and given unhurried time. Speaking only to family removes his voice, and gender matching is a preference to ask about, not an assumption. Leaving cultural support to discharge is too late.
Case CHB141

Divya Banerjee, 31, a software developer, swims freestyle with a masters squad (about 20 km a week) and has had right shoulder pain for six weeks since adding hand paddles to her sessions. Pain is 5 out of 10 during the pull-through and with overhead reaching, with an occasional ache at night when lying on that side. No trauma and no neck pain. Examination: painful arc between 90 and 120 degrees of abduction, Hawkins-Kennedy positive, empty can painful but strong, resisted external rotation painful with 5 out of 5 strength, no lag signs, passive external rotation full, mild scapular dyskinesis. Neurological screen and cervical screen are clear. SPADI score is 38 percent. Her coach has advised a cortisone injection so she can compete in three weeks.

  1. The correct answer is C (Load-related rotator cuff tendinopathy, with no features suggesting a significant full-thickness tear): Painful but strong resisted testing, a painful arc and a recent increase in training load fit rotator cuff tendinopathy, and the lack of weakness or lag makes a large tear unlikely. Full passive external rotation argues against early frozen shoulder. Bursitis cannot be separated clinically from the tendon and would not explain pain on resisted testing alone.
  2. The correct answer is D (Explain injections and medication decisions belong to her doctor, and offer a referral letter with findings): Prescribing, recommending drugs and performing injections are outside the scope of an entry-level physiotherapist, so these decisions go to a medical practitioner with her consent. Offering a letter of clinical findings supports that referral. Arranging an injection through a colleague or advising a stronger medication still assumes a role the physiotherapist does not hold, and sharing findings with the coach needs her agreement.
  3. The correct answer is C (Modify swim volume and paddle use, and start graded rotator cuff and scapular loading exercises): Tendinopathy responds best to education, relative load reduction and progressive loading, so she can keep some training while the tendon adapts. Complete rest deconditions the tendon and her fitness. Passive treatments alone rarely change tendon capacity, and heavy loading in a painful range without reducing volume risks flaring symptoms.
  4. The correct answer is A (Pause progression and refer her promptly to her GP to investigate possible non-musculoskeletal causes): Constant night pain unrelated to movement with systemic symptoms and unexplained weight loss are red flags for serious non-musculoskeletal disease, so loading should stop and she needs prompt medical investigation. Treating as a worsening tendon or neck problem delays diagnosis. Imaging for a cuff tear does not address the systemic features.
Case CHB142

Mr Jamal Haddad, 19, has Duchenne muscular dystrophy and lives with his parents in western Sydney. He lost independent walking at 12 years and now uses a power wheelchair. He receives NDIS-funded home physiotherapy fortnightly and takes daily oral corticosteroids prescribed by his neurologist. Assessment today: elbow flexion 3/5, grip 3/5, hip and knee extensors 1/5. Bilateral plantarflexion contractures, with ankle dorsiflexion lacking 25 degrees to neutral. Mild scoliosis. Forced vital capacity was 38 percent of predicted at his last clinic review. He reports morning headaches, daytime tiredness and a weak cough when he has a cold. SpO2 96 percent on room air, RR 18. His mother asks about increasing strengthening exercises. Your service is reviewing which outcome measures it records for people with progressive neuromuscular conditions.

  1. The correct answer is D (Night-time hypoventilation from respiratory muscle weakness, needing medical review for ventilatory support): Morning headache and daytime sleepiness with a markedly reduced vital capacity point to nocturnal hypoventilation, which can occur while awake saturations remain normal. It warrants referral for sleep and ventilation assessment. A chest infection would present with acute features, and the other explanations do not fit the usual pattern in this condition.
  2. The correct answer is D (Daily gentle passive stretching with night splints or ankle foot orthoses to maintain length): In Duchenne muscular dystrophy the aim is to slow contracture progression with regular gentle stretching and night positioning, which a family can sustain at home. Resisted work damages dystrophic muscle and he cannot stand to do heel raises. Casting or surgery first, and forceful stretching to restore length, are not realistic or appropriate starting points.
  3. The correct answer is B (Submaximal low resistance activity in less affected muscles, avoiding eccentric and heavy resistance work): Dystrophic muscle is vulnerable to damage from eccentric and high resistance loading, so submaximal, low resistance activity within comfortable limits keeps function without overwork. Delayed soreness lasting days signals too much load. Restricting him to passive movement is unnecessarily limiting, since gentle active use is encouraged.
  4. The correct answer is B (Use validated, condition-specific measures at set times, track each person's trend and audit data completeness): Validated measures suited to the condition and its stage, repeated at consistent intervals, let the physiotherapist see each person's trajectory, and auditing completion lets the service judge its own quality. A single symptom score misses function and strength. Walking measures do not suit someone who is non-ambulant, and unstructured notes are not comparable over time.
Case CHB143

Mrs Mai Vo Nguyen, 78, speaks Vietnamese and very little English. She was admitted two days ago to the acute orthopaedic ward after a fall at home. History: breast cancer treated six years ago, osteoporosis, hypertension. She describes left thigh pain for three weeks, worse at night, not eased by rest and now aggravated by weight bearing. The X-ray report shows a lytic lesion in the proximal left femur with a possible impending fracture. Orthopaedic review is pending. Observations: HR 88, BP 130/76, SpO2 96% on room air, afebrile. Left hip flexion is limited by pain and she cannot straight leg raise. The medical note says mobilise as tolerated. Her daughter is present and offers to translate. A telephone interpreter service is available on the ward.

  1. The correct answer is D (Night pain unrelieved by rest, together with a past breast cancer diagnosis): Night pain unrelieved by rest, together with a cancer history known to spread to bone, is the classic red flag pattern. Pain that eases with rest and age related risk are common to mechanical and osteoporotic problems and do not discriminate. Groin tenderness only localises the pain.
  2. The correct answer is B (Clarify weight bearing status with the orthopaedic team and keep her resting meanwhile): An impending pathological fracture must not be loaded or stressed until the surgeon has defined the weight bearing status. Pain tolerance is not a safe guide, and resisted or end range work adds torque through weakened bone.
  3. The correct answer is A (Stop movement, support the limb as it lies, and alert the nurse in charge): A sudden pain with shortening and external rotation suggests a pathological fracture through the known lesion. The priority is to prevent further movement and get medical review immediately. Realigning the limb risks displacement, and extra checks or history taking before alerting the nurse delay care that cannot wait. Examination can follow once help is on the way.
  4.
