The correct answer is A (Book a professional interpreter, let the daughter stay for support, and use teach-back): Informed consent about a safety restriction needs accurate and impartial interpretation, which a professional interpreter provides, while family presence can still comfort the patient. Family members may soften or omit information, and gestures cannot convey risks.
Case CHB144

Brandon Rankine, 15, a Wiradjuri boy, was admitted to a regional hospital five weeks ago with bacterial meningitis complicated by a left cerebellar infarct and right-sided sensorineural hearing loss. He is now an inpatient on a metropolitan rehabilitation ward, a long way from his family in Dubbo. Previously well, he played football and attended Year 10. Findings: wide-based ataxic gait, left finger-to-nose dysmetria, falls after two seconds on tandem stance, left lower limb strength 4/5. Fatigue limits sessions to about 30 minutes. HR 78, BP 110/68 mmHg, afebrile. He is quiet in sessions, avoids eye contact with staff and says he wants to go home. His aunty visits daily and the hospital has an Aboriginal Liaison Officer. His goals are to return to school and football.

  1. The correct answer is D (Left cerebellar ataxia affecting coordination and balance, matching the infarct more than the weakness): Ipsilateral limb dysmetria with a wide base and poor tandem stance points to a cerebellar lesion, matching the left infarct. Grade 4/5 weakness is too mild to explain the incoordination, so strengthening alone would miss the main problem. Hearing and vestibular injury or deconditioning may add to his imbalance, but neither produces left finger-to-nose dysmetria.
  2. The correct answer is D (Task-specific balance and gait practice in short bouts with rests, progressed as safety allows): Motor recovery after cerebellar injury responds to repeated, specific and progressively harder practice, but fatigue and falls risk call for short bouts, rests and supervision. Waiting for ataxia to resolve delays the practice that drives recovery. Prolonged high-intensity or unrested work raises fatigue and the chance of falls.
  3. The correct answer is B (Stand on his left, face him, reduce noise and ask him to demonstrate his understanding): Positioning on the hearing side, facing him so he can see the speaker, lowering noise and checking understanding by demonstration all improve comprehension. Speaking louder from his deaf side or from behind does not help. Written-only instructions remove helpful cues and are not needed when verbal communication is adapted.
  4. The correct answer is A (Offer Brandon and his family the choice of involving the liaison officer and aunty): Culturally safe practice means offering choices and following the patient's and family's preferences, rather than assuming what they want. Avoiding eye contact can be a sign of respect and not disengagement. The liaison officer supports the team and family but does not replace the physiotherapist, and Brandon's own voice in his goals still matters.
Case CHB145

Baby Aarav Sharma, 4 months old, was born at 34 weeks and has post-haemorrhagic hydrocephalus. A ventriculoperitoneal shunt was inserted 3 weeks ago, with the valve palpable behind the right ear. He is on the paediatric ward for a feeding review, and the physiotherapist has been asked to assess his development. HR 140, RR 38, afebrile, anterior fontanelle soft and flat. Head circumference is tracking along the 90th centile. In prone he lifts his head about 30 degrees, he has head lag when pulled to sit, and there is mild extensor tone in his legs. He is irritable after prolonged prone. His mother, Priya, is at the bedside with a friend. The medical team has asked for a physiotherapy entry in the chart.

  1. The correct answer is D (Possible shunt malfunction with raised intracranial pressure, so stop treatment and alert the medical team): Drowsiness, vomiting, a tense fontanelle and downward eye deviation (sunsetting) with a rising heart rate are classic signs of raised intracranial pressure, which suggests the shunt is blocked or failing. This is an emergency needing immediate medical review. Crying can briefly tense a fontanelle, but it does not account for drowsiness and eye signs. Infection also needs medical review, and finishing the assessment first delays it.
  2. The correct answer is C (Offer short supervised tummy time while avoiding sustained pressure over the valve site): Tummy time supports head control and development and is generally encouraged with supervision, but pressure over the valve should be avoided. Stopping it altogether denies him needed practice. Lying on or turning toward the valve increases pressure on the device.
  3. The correct answer is B (Report the rapid centile change to the medical team promptly, since it needs review without symptoms): Crossing centile lines upward can be an early sign of inadequate CSF drainage even before obvious symptoms appear, so it needs timely medical assessment. Waiting for symptoms or repeating the measurement at home delays review. Catch-up growth is a possible explanation but is the doctor's call, not an assumption to act on.
  4. The correct answer is A (Discuss his care with Priya, and share information with the friend only with her explicit consent): Health information about a child is released by the parent or guardian, and silence is not consent. The friend has no right of access, and a verbal summary discloses the same information. Obtain Priya's clear agreement first and document what was shared.
Case CHB146

Huong Bui, 6 years, day 2 of admission with right lower lobe community acquired pneumonia. Previously well and fully immunised. On intravenous antibiotics. Wet-sounding cough but she cannot clear secretions. Observations: RR 28 breaths per minute, HR 110, temperature 37.6 degrees, SpO2 95 percent on room air. Auscultation: coarse crackles and reduced air entry at the right base, clear on the left. No recession or grunting. Eating small amounts, drinking well. Physiotherapy was requested for sputum clearance. Her mother, who speaks fluent English, is present and anxious. Her father smokes inside the house, and Huong has had two chest infections this year. Discharge is expected in 2 days. She enjoys blowing bubbles and playing with her toy rabbit.

  1. The correct answer is A (Play-based huffing and bubble blowing with left side lying, so the right lung is uppermost): A cooperative school-age child can use huffing and blowing games, and the affected lung should be uppermost so secretions drain toward the central airways. Lying on the affected side reverses this and lowers ventilation to that lung. Head-down tilt and suction are not needed when she can participate.
  2. The correct answer is C (Withhold treatment, stay with her and request an urgent medical review through the escalation process): Grunting, recession, marked tachypnoea and falling saturation signal respiratory distress and possible deterioration, which needs immediate escalation. Starting airway clearance adds work of breathing to a tiring child, even if the nurse has been told. Repositioning and a fifteen minute wait delay medical review in a child who is already worsening.
  3. The correct answer is B (Air entry and crackles on auscultation, work of breathing, oxygen saturation and sputum quantity): Response to airway clearance is judged by changes in the physical signs the technique targets: auscultation, breathing effort, saturation and the amount of sputum cleared. Colour, cultures, blood tests and imaging reflect the infection and medical care rather than the effect of that session. Satisfaction and session length say little about lung function.
  4. The correct answer is D (Keep the home smoke free, encourage play and fluids, and seek review if breathing worsens): Smoke exposure at home increases the risk of repeated chest infections, so a smoke free home is the key prevention message, alongside normal activity, fluids and clear return criteria. Prolonged rest adds little once she is eating and drinking. Smoking outside still exposes her through clothing and surfaces, and home percussion has no evidence of benefit once she is clearing secretions.
Case CHB147

Joanne Garner, 81, a widow who lives alone, attends a community falls clinic after fainting twice in 3 weeks when standing from a chair. History: Parkinson's disease, type 2 diabetes, hypertension, mild knee osteoarthritis. Her GP recently changed her medications, including an increased antihypertensive dose and a new diuretic. Sitting BP 142/80 mmHg, HR 74. One minute after standing, BP 108/62 mmHg, HR 78, with light-headedness and greying of vision; symptoms ease when she sits. Timed Up and Go 16 seconds. Lower limb strength 4-/5. She drinks little fluid because of urinary urgency. Her daughter has brought printed articles about compression garments and wants a programme based on the latest research.

  1. The correct answer is B (A fall of 20 mmHg systolic or 10 mmHg diastolic within three minutes of standing): Orthostatic hypotension is defined by a sustained drop of at least 20 mmHg systolic or 10 mmHg diastolic within three minutes of standing. Her readings exceed both. A large heart rate rise with a small pressure fall describes a different pattern, and symptoms alone do not define the criteria.
  2. The correct answer is B (Sit at the edge first, pump the ankles, then stand slowly with support nearby): Staged rising with ankle pumping uses the calf muscle pump to return blood and gives the circulation time to adjust. Breath holding and straining can lower venous return further, and fast rising or walking immediately risks another faint in a person with proven postural pressure falls.
  3. The correct answer is A (Appraise the trial's design, sample and relevance to her, then weigh it against current guidelines): Evidence-based practice combines the best available research with clinical expertise and the patient's circumstances, so a small trial in younger adults needs critical appraisal for relevance to an 81-year-old with Parkinson's disease. Dismissing the article unread skips that appraisal, and trying the garment without it ignores risks such as skin and mobility issues. Appraising research is a core physiotherapy skill, not a task to hand to the GP.
  4. The correct answer is B (Lower her to a supine position with the legs raised, and monitor her until recovered): Lying her flat with the legs elevated restores cerebral perfusion fastest in a postural faint, and she then needs observation and notification of the GP. Keeping her upright or walking her risks a further drop in pressure and a fall. A sugary drink is not first-line unless low blood glucose has been confirmed and she is alert enough to swallow safely.
Case CHB148

Mrs Jennifer Okafor is a 66 year old retired teacher attending outpatient physiotherapy three weeks after coronary artery bypass grafting (three grafts) through a median sternotomy, with a saphenous vein harvest from the right leg. She has type 2 diabetes and a BMI of 34. Today her sternum feels stable with no click, the wound is clean and dry, wound pain is 3 out of 10 and increases when she coughs, and her cough is weak. She walks 10 minutes continuously at RPE 12. Shoulder elevation is stiff and restricted by pain. HR 78, BP 130/80 mmHg. She hugs a pillow when coughing. The clinic is busy: the physiotherapist has a student on placement and a waiting list of post-operative referrals.

  1. The correct answer is D (To limit shearing forces on the healing sternum and reduce the risk of instability): Pulling, pushing and coughing create shearing and distracting forces across the healing sternal edges, which can cause instability and wound breakdown, especially with diabetes and a high BMI. The grafts are not displaced by chest wall movement. Precautions exist for the bone and wound rather than for the lungs or the shoulders.
  2. The correct answer is C (Take the patient's call first, arrange urgent medical assessment, then reorganise remaining tasks): A hot, swollen calf with new breathlessness twelve days after surgery raises concern about deep vein thrombosis with pulmonary embolism, which is life threatening and time critical. The physiotherapist takes the call and arranges urgent assessment. Advice to rest, elevate or compress and a review later in the week delays that, and compression is unsafe if a clot is suspected. The ward call, student meeting and report can be reorganised afterwards.
  3. The correct answer is D (Progress active shoulder movement in comfort, avoiding heavy lifting and pushing until healing is confirmed): Active shoulder movement within comfort prevents stiffness and is acceptable once the sternum is stable, but loaded pushing and pulling still shear the sternum until the surgeon confirms healing. Resisted bilateral exercise is premature even when the sternum feels stable on palpation. Holding the arms low leads to avoidable shoulder stiffness.
  4. The correct answer is D (Withhold exercise and arrange same day review by the cardiothoracic team or emergency department): A clicking, mobile sternum with wound redness and fever suggests sternal instability and possible deep wound infection, which can progress to mediastinitis. Exercise is withheld and she needs same day cardiothoracic or emergency review. Withholding exercise is right, but a GP review in two days or a routine surgical appointment delays definitive care. A binder and continued walking load an unstable sternum.
Case CHB149

Rosa Mancini, 72, retired seamstress, lives alone in a regional town. Diagnosis: metastatic breast cancer with a recurrent right malignant pleural effusion, managed at home with an indwelling pleural catheter drained three times a week by a community nurse. She is under the palliative care team (GP and community palliative service). Goal: stay at home and walk to her letterbox and garden. Today: breathless after washing, modified Borg 5/10, RR 22, SpO2 93% on room air, HR 92, afebrile. Chest: reduced air entry over the right lower half. Catheter site clean, dressing intact. She says she has stopped private physiotherapy because the cost is too high and asks who can help pay for visits at home. She is a part pensioner with no previous aged care assessment.

  1. The correct answer is A (Stony dull percussion, reduced breath sounds and reduced vocal resonance at the right base): Fluid in the pleural space separates the lung from the chest wall, producing a stony dull note and diminished breath sounds and vocal resonance over the fluid. Hyper-resonance with tracheal shift describes a tension pneumothorax. Bronchial breathing with increased vocal resonance fits consolidation, and diffuse wheeze points to airflow obstruction rather than effusion.
  2. The correct answer is A (Supported forward lean sitting, pursed lip breathing and a handheld fan aimed at her face): Forward lean positions, pursed lip breathing and a fan on the face are simple, evidence-based ways to reduce the sensation of breathlessness in advanced disease, and she can use them alone. Lying supine tends to worsen breathlessness with a large effusion. Starting oxygen is outside the physiotherapist's role and a saturation of 93 percent does not call for it. Walking to a high Borg score is not the aim during an episode of breathlessness.
  3. The correct answer is D (Withhold exercise and urgently report the findings to her GP or palliative team): Local inflammation, cloudy fluid, fever and tachycardia suggest a catheter-related pleural infection, which needs prompt medical assessment and may need antibiotics or drain review. Exercise adds physiological load to an unwell patient. Holding exercise is right, but waiting for the next nursing visit or for a further rise in observations delays treatment of a possible sepsis picture.
  4. The correct answer is D (Help her register with My Aged Care for assessment of subsidised in-home services): Older Australians who need help at home are assessed through My Aged Care, which links them to subsidised services such as allied health. The NDIS generally does not take new participants who first apply after age 65. Palliative patients are not excluded from Medicare arrangements, and nothing in the history supports a compensable claim.
Case CHB150

Hamza Khalil, 16, has bilateral spastic cerebral palsy, GMFCS level III. He is in Year 10 at a mainstream school, uses a rollator indoors and a manual wheelchair for longer distances, and lives with his parents and two siblings. Over the past year of rapid growth his walking has become more crouched and tiring. Findings: popliteal angle 60 degrees bilaterally, hip flexion contracture of 10 degrees, hip and knee extensor strength 3+/5, no fixed ankle contracture, and good sensation. He is an NDIS participant with a plan that includes a capacity building line. His team includes a GP, paediatrician, occupational therapist, speech pathologist and school learning support officer. Hamza wants to walk with his friends on the school camp next term; his mother worries about contractures; the school wants him in the wheelchair on excursions.

  1. The correct answer is B (Agree shared, measurable goals built around what matters most to him, with team roles clear): Goals that reflect the young person's own priorities and are agreed with family, school and clinicians are more meaningful and more likely to be followed. Each concern can be addressed within a shared plan. Letting one voice decide, whether family, school or the adolescent alone, loses the collaborative element.
  2. The correct answer is C (Progressive strengthening of antigravity muscles with task-specific walking practice and regular stretching): Weak antigravity extensors contribute to crouch, so progressive strengthening combined with practising the walking task itself, plus stretching, has the best support. Stretching alone does not build the strength needed to hold the knee extended. Resistance training does not worsen spasticity, so avoiding it is outdated advice.
  3. The correct answer is B (Refer promptly to his GP or paediatrician for hip imaging and orthopaedic review): New groin pain with reduced hip movement in a young person with cerebral palsy raises concern for hip displacement or another hip problem, and surveillance is overdue. Imaging and orthopaedic opinion should come before loading the joint further. Treating it as muscle tightness or waiting risks missing a progressive condition.
  4. The correct answer is B (Provide a goal-linked functional report and explain that the NDIS decides what is funded): The physiotherapist can supply evidence linking each request to his goals and function, but the NDIS decides what is reasonable and necessary and the therapist should not promise outcomes. Referring the family on without a report withholds the evidence the planner needs. Medicare items are limited and do not replace NDIS funded therapy for a current participant, so that route misdirects the family.

Practice Test 11 Answer Key

Case CHB151

Ayaan Sharma, 5 months, is brought to the emergency department by his mother after three days of runny nose and cough, and a day of poor feeding. He was born at term and is otherwise well. He has taken about half his usual feeds and had two wet nappies today. Observations: RR 58, HR 160, SpO2 93 percent in room air, temperature 37.4 degrees C. He has nasal flaring, mild subcostal recession and bilateral fine crackles with expiratory wheeze. His nose is blocked with secretions. The doctor's working diagnosis is bronchiolitis and physiotherapy review has been requested. The department is busy and an allied health assistant is rostered to support you for the shift. Ayaan's mother speaks English and is anxious. She asks if he needs chest physiotherapy to clear his lungs.

  1. The correct answer is D (Routine percussion is not recommended, so the focus is supportive care, nasal suction and feeding support): Evidence does not support conventional chest physiotherapy in uncomplicated bronchiolitis, where it does not shorten illness or reduce oxygen need. The useful input is supportive: nasal suction when secretions block feeding, positioning and feeding support. Techniques that compress the infant chest add handling stress and have no proven benefit.
  2. The correct answer is A (Gentle shallow nasal suction before feeds, with few passes while monitoring his colour and SpO2): A young infant breathes mainly through the nose, so a blocked nose makes feeding hard. Gentle, shallow suction before feeds, limited to a few passes with monitoring, relieves this with the least trauma and stimulation. Deep or repeated suction causes mucosal swelling and distress, and suctioning after the feed does little to help the feed itself.
  3. The correct answer is B (Call urgently for medical help and stay with him, supporting his airway position): Grunting, pauses in breathing, drowsiness, a rate of 74 and a saturation of 88 percent are signs of impending respiratory failure in an infant and need immediate medical review. Oxygen and suction are reasonable supports, but each used alone delays escalation by minutes he does not have, and prone positioning of a drowsy infant adds risk. The physiotherapist calls for help and stays with him, supporting his airway position.
  4. The correct answer is D (Retain suction and respiratory assessment, delegating only feeding position support with clear instructions and review): The physiotherapist stays accountable for delegated tasks and delegates only when the task is within the assistant's competence, the patient is predictable and supervision is adequate. Suction and respiratory assessment in an infant with borderline saturation need clinical judgement about deterioration, so they stay with the physiotherapist, while simple feeding support can be delegated. One demonstration or a checklist does not make the task safe to hand over.
Case CHB152

Hoa Do, 14 months, day 3 of admission with bronchiolitis. Born at term, previously well. Weight 9.8 kg, afebrile, drinking small amounts. Nasogastric tube feeds since admission because she tires with oral feeds; the last feed finished 20 minutes ago. Observations: RR 52, HR 150, SpO2 94 percent on room air, temperature 37.4. Mild subcostal recession, noisy nasal breathing, widespread wheeze and coarse crackles. Parents are at the bedside. The registrar has requested chest physiotherapy for secretions. Nursing handover mentions that on the previous shift Hoa was placed head-down in her cot, her SpO2 fell to 89 percent, and she settled after repositioning. No incident report was lodged. Paediatric ward, tertiary hospital.

  1. The correct answer is B (Avoid routine airway clearance techniques, and provide nasal suction and comfortable positioning with her parents): Current evidence and guidelines for uncomplicated bronchiolitis do not support routine airway clearance techniques, which add handling and distress without shortening the illness. Nasal suction, comfortable positioning and involving parents are supportive. Percussion, vibration and forced expiratory techniques suit specific problems such as lobar collapse or chronic suppurative disease, which Hoa does not have.
  2. The correct answer is D (Enter the event in the hospital incident system and inform the nurse unit manager): A patient safety event that caused or could have caused harm should be reported through the hospital incident system by any staff member who knows of it, because reporting drives learning and system fixes. Informing the unit manager supports follow-up. A private conversation or a note in the chart leaves the event uncaptured, and Hoa's recovery does not remove the need to report.
  3. The correct answer is A (Upright or semi-upright in a parent's arms or cot, head neutral, avoiding head-down tilt): Infants with bronchiolitis breathe more easily upright or semi-upright, where the diaphragm works better and recession reduces, and this also suits the period after a feed. Head-down and prone positions increase reflux, aspiration and desaturation risk, as Hoa showed on the previous shift. Lying flat reduces diaphragm efficiency.
  4. The correct answer is A (Stop suction, sit her upright, apply oxygen and call the nurse and registrar urgently): A falling saturation with rising respiratory rate and grunting signals acute deterioration, so the procedure stops and the likely cause is removed first. Upright positioning and oxygen are given while help is called, because an infant can tire quickly. Continuing suction or waiting to reassess delays the escalation she needs.
Case CHB153

Lui Salesa, 74, retired builder, was admitted four days ago with acute decompensated heart failure. Diagnosis: heart failure with reduced ejection fraction, EF 30 percent, ischaemic cardiomyopathy. Medications include frusemide, an ACE inhibitor, a beta blocker and spironolactone. He has diuresed and is now 3 kg below his admission weight, with mild pitting oedema at the ankles. Today: HR 78 sinus, BP 108/66 mmHg, RR 18, SpO2 95 percent on room air, bibasal fine crackles reduced from admission. He sleeps on three pillows. He walked 40 metres in the corridor with a Borg dyspnoea rating of 13 (somewhat hard) and one rest. He lives with his wife and adult son in a two-storey house and has never kept a record of his weight. Discharge is planned in two days, and you are asked to support his self-management.

  1. The correct answer is C (Several short walks at an effort of 11 to 13, building duration before speed): Heart failure exercise is usually prescribed as frequent short bouts at a light to somewhat hard effort, increasing duration before intensity, with rests as needed. Rating of perceived exertion suits him because he takes a beta blocker. Waiting for echocardiographic improvement withholds beneficial mobilisation, and driving him to high exertion or demanding zero breathlessness are both inappropriate.
  2. The correct answer is B (Hold progression, sit him upright, and report these signs of fluid retention to the medical team): Rapid weight gain, increased oedema, extra pillows and returning crackles point to worsening fluid overload, so progressing exercise is unsafe until he is reviewed. Sitting upright eases orthopnoea and the medical team needs to know promptly. Airway clearance does not treat crackles caused by fluid, and lying flat worsens orthopnoea.
  3. The correct answer is D (Straining raises chest pressure and afterload, making it harder for a weak heart to pump): Holding the breath against a closed airway raises intrathoracic pressure and sharply changes loading conditions on the heart, which is poorly tolerated when the ventricle is weak. Exhaling during effort avoids this. A brief breath-hold does not drop oxygen enough to cause arrhythmias, and it does not move fluid into the lungs or change kidney handling of fluid.
  4. The correct answer is C (Weigh each morning after toileting, before eating, on the same scale, and record it): Weighing at the same time each morning after emptying the bladder, before food, on the same scale and written down gives comparable readings, so a rapid gain stands out. Evening weights are affected by meals and drinks, irregular weighing misses early changes, and waiting for symptoms delays action because weight often rises before swelling or breathlessness appears.
Case CHB154

Jess Tran, 29, primary school teacher and recreational runner training for a half marathon in eight weeks, running 40 km a week. Four months of bilateral anterior knee pain, right worse, 6/10 on downhill running and descending stairs, with stiffness after prolonged sitting. No trauma, locking or swelling. Examination: no effusion, ligament tests negative, patellar tracking appears normal. Pain with squatting to 60 degrees, tenderness of the medial and lateral patellar facets, positive patellar compression, normal inferior pole. Hip abduction strength 4/5 with dynamic knee valgus on single leg squat. During treadmill gait analysis today the belt stopped abruptly and Jess stumbled, grazing her palm on the rail. She says she is fine and wants to finish the session.

  1. The correct answer is C (Pain with patellar compression and tenderness of the patellar facets, with a normal inferior pole): Pain on patellar compression with facet tenderness and a normal inferior pole fits patellofemoral pain. Inferior pole tenderness with loaded decline squat pain points to patellar tendinopathy, and warm-up pattern with tendon thickening also suggests tendon. Lateral pain at a set distance suggests iliotibial band involvement.
  2. The correct answer is A (Hip and knee strengthening with load management, reducing downhill running and weekly volume as needed): Patellofemoral pain responds to combined hip and knee strengthening with load management, which keeps her running at a tolerable level. Prolonged rest weakens her and taping alone does not address the deficits. Stretching ignores the hip weakness, and deep heavy squats raise patellofemoral joint stress.
  3. The correct answer is A (Reassess for intra-articular injury, and refer to her GP for imaging if findings support it): Swelling and giving way are not typical of patellofemoral pain and suggest an intra-articular problem, so the knee needs reassessment and referral for imaging if findings support it. Continuing the programme or bracing assumes the original diagnosis still holds. Delaying examination loses the chance to identify an injury early.
  4. The correct answer is B (Take the treadmill out of use, document the event, report it internally and check on Jess): A malfunctioning machine is a hazard even when no one is hurt, so it should be taken out of use, the event documented and reported through the practice's incident process, and the patient followed up. Recording only in the clinical notes leaves the hazard in place. Waiting for symptoms or sending the patient elsewhere does not manage the risk to others.
Case CHB155

Arjun Mehta, 15, a Year 10 student, plays school rugby union. Two days ago he was tackled and hit his head on the ground, with no loss of consciousness. He was seen by his GP yesterday, who found no signs of structural injury and no red flags. Today: headache 4/10, mild light sensitivity, difficulty concentrating in class, and a feeling of fogginess. Neurological screen normal, oriented, GCS 15, tenderness over the right upper cervical muscles, and dizziness on rapid head turning. He sleeps normally and is keen to return to training. The sport coordinator wants him to play on Saturday. The clinic is short staffed today, with several students waiting for the single physiotherapist.

  1. The correct answer is B (Follow a graded return plan, progress only while symptom-free, and avoid contact until medically cleared): Concussion in adolescents calls for a conservative graded return, with each step started only once symptom-free, and no contact sport until medically cleared. A normal screen or a scrum cap does not show recovery, and headache with dizziness means he is not ready. Strict rest in a dark room is outdated, since light activity and gradual return are encouraged.
  2. The correct answer is D (Gentle cervical mobility work and graded head-turn exercises that keep symptoms mild and settling quickly): Neck-related and vestibular symptoms after concussion respond to gentle, graded cervical and head-turn exercises that keep symptoms low. High-velocity manipulation of the upper cervical spine carries risk and is not indicated, vigorous exercise this early is likely to aggravate symptoms, and a collar causes stiffness and deconditioning.
  3. The correct answer is B (Attend with adjustments such as rest breaks and reduced screen time, building load as symptoms allow): Current practice supports a brief period of relative rest, then a gradual return to learning with adjustments scaled to symptoms. Pushing through a rise to 6/10 aggravates symptoms, while prolonged absence and a blanket exemption from assessments delay recovery and reintegration into school.
  4. The correct answer is A (Pause Arjun's review, assess the drowsy Year 11 boy immediately and arrange urgent medical transfer): A head-injured student who is drowsy and has vomited repeatedly shows red flags for serious intracranial injury, so he takes priority over stable injuries and a nearly complete review. He needs immediate assessment and urgent medical transfer rather than waiting. The ankle can be screened with the Ottawa ankle rules afterwards, since he can bear weight.
Case CHB156

Kofi Mensah, 62, was admitted with severe pneumonia three weeks ago and is now in a rehabilitation unit for deconditioning. He takes an ACE inhibitor and a diuretic for hypertension and has type 2 diabetes. He walks 10 metres with a frame and one assistant, and reports light-headedness on standing. Lying for 5 minutes: BP 134/80, HR 76. After 3 minutes standing: BP 102/64, HR 80. He then looks pale and says his vision is greying. Chest is clear, SpO2 97% on room air, and oral intake has been poor for two days. A newer graduate colleague has found an online article about abdominal binders and wants to use one for Kofi. It is a single-centre study with 14 participants and no control group.

  1. The correct answer is B (The systolic fall of 20 mmHg or more within three minutes meets criteria for orthostatic hypotension): A sustained systolic fall of at least 20 mmHg, or diastolic fall of at least 10 mmHg, within three minutes of standing meets the usual definition, and Kofi's readings exceed both. A blunted heart rate response does not exclude the diagnosis and may point to a medication or autonomic contribution. Symptoms are common but are not needed for the definition.
  2. The correct answer is C (Treat it as weak evidence, check guidelines or systematic reviews, and weigh it with Kofi's circumstances): A single small study without a control group is low in the evidence hierarchy and cannot show that the binder caused any improvement. The sound approach is to look for higher quality syntheses or guidelines and combine them with the patient's situation and preferences. Rejecting all small studies is too blunt, and a personal trial cannot separate the binder's effect from natural recovery.
  3. The correct answer is C (Lower him into the chair, raise his legs, and recheck blood pressure until symptoms settle): Presyncope means cerebral perfusion is falling, so the priority is to remove gravity's effect by sitting or lying him down immediately and raising his legs, then monitoring. Marching or continued standing risks a fall or full syncope, and walking him to a bed adds more upright time while he is at his weakest.
  4. The correct answer is A (Progress in stages from sitting to standing with slow transitions, checking pressure and symptoms each time): Graded exposure with measurement at each stage lets the physiotherapist progress while he remains safe, and slow position changes reduce the pressure drop. Keeping him in bed would worsen deconditioning, a long immediate stand invites another presyncope, and relying on symptoms alone misses a pressure drop before it becomes dangerous.
Case CHB157

Colin Barrett, 34, a Wiradjuri man, works as a delivery driver and attends your private practice with right jaw pain for 8 weeks. His GP referred him with temporomandibular dysfunction. He reports clicking on opening, pain when chewing steak, and morning headaches over the right temple. He clenches when stressed about deadlines. Examination: maximal mouth opening 42 mm, with the jaw deviating to the right at mid-opening and straightening before full opening. A soft click is felt over the right joint on opening. Masseter and temporalis are tender on the right. Cervical rotation is full and pain free. There is no swelling, trauma, fever or locking history, and the cranial nerve screen is normal. He felt rushed at a previous clinic and did not return after one visit. His sister drove him today and asks if she may sit in on the session.

  1. The correct answer is B (Right disc displacement with reduction, with muscle pain linked to his clenching): A deviation that corrects before full opening with a soft click points to a disc that clicks back into place on opening, and the muscle tenderness fits his clenching. A closed lock would limit opening and the deviation would persist. Coarse crepitus and a fixed deviation would be expected in degenerative joint disease.
  2. The correct answer is D (Education, a softer diet, relaxed jaw posture and gentle graded jaw exercises): Most disc displacement with reduction and associated muscle pain settles with conservative care: reassurance, load modification, posture and graded exercise. Thrust techniques are not first-line, and forceful end range stretching can aggravate a sensitive joint and muscles. A full-time splint is not a first step and can alter the bite, so it is better left until simple measures have been tried.
  3. The correct answer is A (Explain a likely non-reducing disc, trial gentle mobilisation, and refer if opening stays limited): The new loss of the click with a limited, deviating opening suggests the disc is no longer reducing, which is managed conservatively first with gentle techniques and exercise, and referred if it persists. Forcing the jaw can injure the joint. With no fever or swelling, an emergency infective cause is not suggested.
  4. The correct answer is D (Welcome her, ask Colin what support he prefers, explain each step, and check his comfort): Culturally safe care starts with asking the person what he wants, giving him control over who attends, and taking time to explain. Assuming how a family makes decisions, or arranging support without asking, replaces his own decision with the clinician's assumption.
Case CHB158

Mrs Sofia Karagiannis, 84, born in Greece, lives in a residential aged care home. Moderate Alzheimer's dementia; she speaks mostly Greek with limited English. Three falls in four weeks: two in the afternoon in the corridor, one at 3 am walking to the toilet. Previously independent with a four-wheeled frame, she now needs standby assistance and often leaves the frame behind. Medications: donepezil, sertraline and temazepam at night. Lying BP 128/76, standing at one minute 102/66 with unsteadiness and pallor noted by staff. No injuries. Gait: reduced step length, turns in many steps, slow. Care staff say she is agitated late afternoon. Her daughter works full time and has offered to interpret, and the nurse suggests asking a Greek speaking cleaner.

  1. The correct answer is D (Postural blood pressure drop and a sedating night medication, both open to prescriber review): The drop on standing with symptoms and a sedative taken before a night fall are reversible contributors that the GP can review. Dementia is a risk factor but cannot be changed, and gait changes may be a result of these factors rather than isolated weakness. The frame is useful when she remembers to use it, so the problem is prompting, not the device.
  2. The correct answer is B (Embed practice into daily routines, such as walks to meals, with demonstration and cueing): People with dementia learn best through repeated, routine, cued activity that does not rely on remembering instructions, and staff can deliver it every day. A written sheet depends on recall and self-direction. Complex multi-step tasks exceed her processing, and restricting her to a chair would speed decline and does not address the cause.
  3. The correct answer is C (Book a qualified interpreter by phone, and invite her daughter to stay as support): A qualified interpreter gives accurate, impartial translation and protects confidentiality, while the daughter can still provide comfort and background. Family members and untrained staff may filter or change what is said and are not bound by professional standards. Using English alone excludes her from decisions and wrongly discounts her own voice.
  4. The correct answer is D (Staged rising with a pause at the bed edge and standby assistance until steady): Rising in stages with a pause lets blood pressure adjust, and standby assistance protects her if she becomes dizzy, especially as she forgets her frame. Leaving her to walk alone after the pause removes that protection. Quick rising increases the drop, and bed rails are a restrictive practice that raises injury risk if she climbs over.
Case CHB159

Raymond Williams, 68, a Gamilaraay man and retired shearer, attends the allied health clinic at his local Aboriginal Community Controlled Health Service. He has had six weeks of right buttock pain spreading down the back of the leg to the outer foot, which began after lifting a water tank. Pain is 7/10 with sitting and coughing. Medical history: type 2 diabetes and hypertension, both managed by the clinic GP. Examination: right straight leg raise reproduces leg pain at about 40 degrees, crossed straight leg raise negative, right ankle plantarflexion 4/5, right Achilles reflex reduced, reduced sensation along the lateral border of the right foot, left side normal. Saddle sensation, bladder and bowel function are normal. Raymond answers briefly and looks away when asked direct questions. He says his niece, who drives him, usually helps him decide about health matters. He is worried about leaving Country for the surgical clinic in the city.

  1. The correct answer is A (The S1 root, affected by a posterolateral disc herniation at the L5 to S1 level): Weakness of plantarflexion, a reduced Achilles reflex and sensory loss on the lateral foot are the classic S1 pattern. L5 involvement would more often show great toe extensor weakness and dorsal foot sensory change, and L4 affects the knee reflex and medial leg. Pain with coughing and a positive straight leg raise favour a root rather than a peripheral nerve trunk.
  2. The correct answer is B (Most people improve over weeks to months by staying active, with neurological checks at each visit): Most lumbar radiculopathy with a mild deficit improves over weeks to months with conservative care, and staying active within comfort is preferred to bed rest. Neurological monitoring covers the small risk of progression. Surgical opinion is reserved for severe or progressive deficit or pain that fails to settle.
  3. The correct answer is D (Arrange emergency transfer by ambulance for urgent assessment of suspected cauda equina syndrome): Saddle numbness, urinary difficulty and bilateral leg symptoms are red flags for cauda equina syndrome, which needs emergency imaging and surgical assessment within hours. The distance to hospital makes ambulance transfer more appropriate, because it provides monitoring and pre-notifies the receiving team. An appointment tomorrow, waiting a day for advice, or a long private drive risks permanent bladder, bowel and sexual dysfunction.
  4. The correct answer is C (Ask Raymond who he wants involved, offer an Aboriginal health worker, and allow time without rushing): Cultural safety means asking the person about their preferences, supporting involvement of people he trusts such as an Aboriginal health worker, and allowing unhurried yarning, while consent still comes from Raymond himself. Treating silence as agreement is unsafe. Excluding his niece or accepting her consent in his place both override his choice.
Case CHB160

Hala Khoury, 72, retired schoolteacher, has had Parkinson disease for six years (Hoehn and Yahr stage 3) and takes levodopa four times daily. She has fallen twice in six months, both near doorways. She reports gait freezing at doorways and when turning, worse in the hour before her next dose and when anxious. Findings: shuffling steps with reduced arm swing, Timed Up and Go 17 seconds, and a pull test showing she would fall if not caught. No orthostatic blood pressure drop; cognition intact. She walks only to the letterbox now because her husband, who is anxious about falls, discourages longer walks. She asks about community exercise groups and what she can do herself.

  1. The correct answer is B (Rehearse stopping, shifting weight side to side, then taking a large deliberate first step): An internal attentional strategy of stopping, weight shifting and a deliberate large step needs no equipment or helper and can be rehearsed until automatic. External cues are useful but depend on a person or device being present. Hurrying with small quick steps encourages festination and worsens freezing.
  2. The correct answer is A (Schedule sessions when medication works best, and report the wearing-off pattern to her doctor): Exercise is safest and most productive when she is in a good medication phase, and a predictable wearing-off pattern is information her doctor needs to review the regimen. Training in the pre-dose period raises falls risk, and both her falls happened then. Changing medication timing is outside physiotherapy scope, and stopping exercise on poor days removes a valuable intervention.
  3. The correct answer is A (Set shared goals with both of them, agree a graded programme, and teach safe self-monitoring): Self-management is supported by shared, specific goals, a graded plan and skills to monitor her own safety, with her husband included so his concerns are addressed. Limiting her to supervised exercise or letting him decide removes her autonomy and deconditions her further. A generic leaflet and any class give no tailoring to her freezing and falls risk.
  4. The correct answer is B (Turn in a wide arc using fewer, larger steps instead of pivoting on the spot): A wide arc with larger steps keeps her base of support wide and avoids the tight pivot that triggers freezing and imbalance. Small quick steps encourage shuffling, and rotating on one foot or spinning with arm momentum challenges balance further. Guarding in clinic allows safe practice before she tries it at home.
Case CHB161

Margaret Ioannou, 58, retired shop owner, right hemiparesis from a left middle cerebral artery stroke 14 months ago. Attends a community rehabilitation centre as an outpatient. Ten days ago she received botulinum toxin injections to the right gastrocnemius and tibialis posterior. She walks with a single point stick and an ankle foot orthosis that has become hard to fit. Right ankle passive dorsiflexion lacks 15 degrees from neutral with the knee extended; plantarflexors score 3 on the Modified Ashworth Scale; 10 metre walk speed 0.5 metres per second; one fall last month. Medications: aspirin, atorvastatin, ramipril. Sensation in the right foot is intact. She has been sitting most of the day since the stroke and is unsure what she may do between visits. Her husband works long days and cannot attend sessions.

  1. The correct answer is D (Considerable tone increase through most of the range, with difficult passive movement): A score of 3 means tone is considerably increased through most of the range and passive movement is difficult. A score of 2 is more marked tone but easy movement, and a score of 1 is only a slight catch, while a rigid, fixed limb is a score of 4.
  2. The correct answer is A (Combine stretching, serial casting if indicated and task-specific gait training during the toxin window): Botulinum toxin lowers tone for a limited window, which is the best time to lengthen soft tissue and practise better movement patterns. Waiting wastes that window, and heavy strengthening of spastic plantarflexors or ice alone does not address range or walking.
  3. The correct answer is A (Attend urgently so the cast can be split and circulation and sensation checked): Tightness, pins and needles and pale toes suggest compromised circulation or nerve compression, which can cause lasting damage within hours. The cast must be released and reviewed promptly. Elevation, exercise or waiting until tomorrow for advice could delay relief of pressure, even if symptoms briefly ease.
  4. The correct answer is C (Agree personal goals, teach a home walking routine, and link her to a stroke group): Self-management works best when goals come from the person, the routine is taught and practised with feedback, and social support is arranged for when family cannot help. A generic sheet, delayed activity or an unscreened gym class does not build confidence or safety.
Case CHB162

Mr Anthony Pappas, 79, retired electrician, is six weeks after a right middle cerebral artery ischaemic stroke and is an inpatient in a rehabilitation unit. He has left hemiparesis (hip flexion 3/5, knee extension 3/5, ankle dorsiflexion 2/5), left foot drop and mild left inattention. He walks 10 metres with two people assisting and an ankle foot orthosis. Berg Balance Scale 22 out of 56. History: atrial fibrillation on an anticoagulant, hypertension, low bone density. Resting HR 72 irregular, BP 138/82, SpO2 96 percent. At the previous session his standing BP fell to 105/60 with dizziness. His wife, Sophia, visits daily. The team proposes body weight supported treadmill training using a harness, and a robotic gait trainer later. Mr Pappas is friendly and says yes to everything offered, but staff note he has reduced insight into his weakness.

  1. The correct answer is D (Begin with substantial body weight support, slow speed and blood pressure checks between short bouts): High support, a slow pace and short bouts with blood pressure checks suit someone with orthostatic symptoms, fall risk and fragile bones, and can be progressed as he tolerates. Minimal support and a brisk pace overload a weak limb. Checking pressure only afterwards misses the chance to stop early.
  2. The correct answer is B (Stop the treadmill, support him in the harness, then sit him and recheck observations): Symptoms with a clear drop in blood pressure and a faster irregular rate are a stop criterion, so the priority is to halt the activity, support him and get him seated before reassessing. Slowing down or adding support still keeps him upright when his circulation is compromised. Standing and breathing exercises delay safe positioning.
  3. The correct answer is B (It allows safe, high-repetition, task-specific stepping and works best alongside overground walking practice): Support systems let people with significant weakness practise many safe repetitions of stepping early, but benefit comes when combined with real overground walking. The evidence does not show machines outperform overground training for everyone. Passive movement does not retrain active ankle control, and aerobic conditioning needs its own programme.
  4. The correct answer is C (Assess his capacity for this decision with simple, supported explanations and document it): Capacity is decision specific and presumed unless shown otherwise, so the therapist should support his understanding and assess whether he can understand, retain, weigh and communicate a choice. A stroke diagnosis does not give a spouse automatic authority, and forgetting does not alone prove incapacity. Agreement without understanding is not valid consent.
Case CHB163

Mr Tam Ly, 57, a retired factory worker who speaks Vietnamese and limited English, attends a hospital outpatient physiotherapy clinic. Six weeks of left leg pain from the buttock down the posterolateral thigh and calf to the top of the foot, with tingling in the big toe. Pain 7/10 in sitting, 4/10 walking. Medical history: type 2 diabetes (well controlled), hypertension. No bladder or bowel change, no weight loss. Examination: left straight leg raise reproduces leg pain at 40 degrees, crossed straight leg raise negative, ankle dorsiflexion 4/5, great toe extension 4/5, reduced sensation over the dorsum of the foot, ankle and knee reflexes present and equal. A family friend who brought him has been interpreting but says she is not sure of some medical words. An accredited interpreter service is available by telephone.

  1. The correct answer is B (L5, indicated by weak hallux extension, dorsal foot numbness and an intact Achilles reflex): Great toe extensor weakness and dorsal foot sensory loss are the classic L5 pattern, and the preserved ankle reflex argues against S1. A positive nerve tension test shows root irritation but does not identify the level. The knee reflex is equal and the sensory change is lateral and dorsal, which does not fit L4 or L3.
  2. The correct answer is D (Education about usual recovery, graded walking, active exercise, and regular rechecks of neurological signs): Most lumbar radiculopathy improves with time, and stable mild weakness is managed conservatively with education, activity and active exercise while monitoring for change. Urgent surgery is reserved for progressive or severe deficits. Traction has limited support, flexion is likely to aggravate sitting pain, and bed rest slows recovery.
  3. The correct answer is B (Contact the referring doctor the same day to arrange urgent specialist review of the progressive weakness): Motor loss that is worsening over two weeks is a progressive neurological deficit and needs prompt specialist review, even though pain has eased. Falling pain with falling power can mean the nerve is failing, so it must not be read as recovery. An orthosis or strengthening may help later but delays the urgent decision about decompression.
  4. The correct answer is A (Engage an accredited interpreter, and check his understanding by asking him to explain the advice back): Valid consent and safe red flag advice need accurate communication, which an accredited interpreter provides and an untrained family friend cannot guarantee. Teach-back then confirms he has understood. A signature or a handout does not prove understanding, and delaying the explanation leaves him without safety information in the meantime.
Case CHB164

Jordan Nguyen, 22, university student, was thrown from his motorbike yesterday. Diagnosis: fractures of right ribs 4 to 7 and a right pneumothorax, treated 18 hours ago with an intercostal catheter connected to an underwater seal drain. Observations: HR 96, BP 118/74, RR 22, SpO2 95% on 2 L/min via nasal prongs, pain 7/10 with coughing despite regular analgesia. Auscultation: reduced air entry at the right base, left clear. Drain: water column swings with breathing, brief bubbling only on coughing, 80 mL serosanguinous drainage overnight. He sits on the edge of the bed, taking shallow breaths and avoiding coughing. He tells you he is lonely, has followed your clinic's social media page, and would like to catch up for coffee after discharge.

  1. The correct answer is C (The drain is patent, and a small residual air leak on coughing is expected early): Tidal swinging of the water column shows the drain communicates with the pleural space and is patent. Brief bubbling on coughing reflects air being expelled from the pleural space, which is common soon after insertion with a small leak. A blocked or displaced drain would show no swing, no drainage or a sudden change in the patient's condition, and only the medical team decides removal.
  2. The correct answer is A (Teach supported deep breathing and a towel splinted cough over the right ribs): Timing treatment after analgesia and teaching splinted deep breaths and coughing restores lung volume while protecting the fracture sites. Percussion and vibration over fractured ribs are painful and inappropriate. Rest and delay increase the risk of atelectasis, and a drain does not prevent safe breathing exercises or mobilisation when it is managed correctly.
  3. The correct answer is D (Stop walking, sit him upright, give oxygen per protocol and urgently call the medical team): Sudden hypoxia, tachypnoea and new subcutaneous emphysema suggest the drain is blocked, displaced or the pneumothorax is recurring, which is an emergency needing immediate medical review. Clamping a drain in a patient with an air leak can cause a tension pneumothorax. Raising the bottle risks backflow into the pleural space, and waiting or coughing delays urgent care.
  4. The correct answer is C (Decline kindly, explain the professional boundary, document the conversation and inform your supervisor): Physiotherapists must keep professional boundaries because the power imbalance and the patient's vulnerability continue beyond discharge, so a personal relationship is not appropriate. A kind, clear refusal, a record of the exchange and supervisor support protect both people. Ignoring the invitation leaves the boundary unclear, and meeting him in public still crosses it.
Case CHB165

Mr Sione Tupou, 83, a retired boilermaker, lives in a residential aged care facility with moderate Alzheimer's disease. He speaks English and Tongan, and his wife visits daily. He fell in his room five days ago and the next day had a cemented hemiarthroplasty (posterior approach) for a left intracapsular neck of femur fracture. He is transferred to subacute rehabilitation today. Surgeon's orders: full weight bearing, no hip movement restrictions. History: hypertension, atrial fibrillation on an anticoagulant, previous mild stroke. Before the fall he walked with a four-wheeled walker and needed supervision to shower. On the ward he is oriented to person only, follows one-step commands with repetition, and is calm when his wife is present. Observations: HR 78 irregular, BP 128/74, SpO2 96 percent. Wound clean and dry. Left hip movement is limited by pain. He says 'no pain' when asked but grimaces on transfers and guards the left hip. In acute care he only sat at the bedside. The unit is reviewing its hip fracture pathway, with results audited quarterly.

  1. The correct answer is D (Use an observational tool such as PAINAD, with family and nursing input, before and during activity): When a person with dementia cannot reliably report pain, behavioural observation tools such as the Pain Assessment in Advanced Dementia scale, combined with information from family and nursing staff, are recommended. His verbal denial conflicts with his behaviour. Charted analgesia does not prove relief, and simplifying a self-report scale still depends on abilities he lacks.
  2. The correct answer is A (Mobilise daily with assistance, using short, simple, consistent cues and familiar routines): Early, daily weight bearing mobilisation improves outcomes after hip fracture, including in dementia, and works best with simple repeated cues, a consistent routine and familiar people. Waiting for cognition to improve causes deconditioning and prolongs delirium. Written rules rely on learning he cannot do, and hoisting removes the practice he needs.
  3. The correct answer is A (Stop mobilising, support the limb in bed, inform medical staff urgently and withhold weight bearing): A new shortened, rotated limb with sudden pain and refusal to bear weight after a hemiarthroplasty suggests dislocation or a periprosthetic fracture. The limb should be supported without manipulation, loading stopped and medical staff informed immediately. A neurovascular check is useful but should not hold up the report, and retrying the transfer or waiting a day risks further injury.
  4. The correct answer is C (Apply the Elderly Mobility Scale consistently at admission and discharge, then audit pooled results): A validated observed performance measure that works for older adults with cognitive impairment, applied the same way at the same time points, gives comparable data for pre and post audit. Self-report questionnaires exclude many patients. Length of stay reflects flow rather than mobility, and therapist-chosen tests cannot be pooled.

Practice Test 12 Answer Key

Case CHB166

Enzo Santoro, 67, a retired bricklayer, is seen at home by the community rehabilitation team 5 weeks after a right transtibial amputation for peripheral arterial disease. History: type 2 diabetes, hypertension, ex-smoker. Residual limb: the distal suture line is mostly healed with a 1 cm dehiscence and scant serous drainage, skin warm, mild oedema, no erythema. He wears a shrinker sock. Knee extension lacks 10 degrees and hip extension strength is 3+/5. Left foot: cool, toes dusky, capillary refill 4 seconds, dorsalis pedis pulse not palpable, and new burning pain at night that eases when he hangs the foot over the bed. HR 78, BP 138/82, temperature 37.1 degrees. He hops short distances with a frame indoors and uses a wheelchair outdoors.

  1. The correct answer is D (Worsening arterial supply to the remaining limb, with rest pain suggesting threatened limb viability): Cool dusky toes, slow capillary refill, absent pulse and rest pain that eases when the leg is dependent are classic signs of critical arterial insufficiency. Neuropathy causes sensory loss rather than ischaemic rest pain, and compression or elevation would worsen perfusion in an ischaemic foot.
  2. The correct answer is B (Support the residual limb in the wheelchair and keep the knee straight when lying): A support board or similar device holds the knee extended in sitting, and lying with the knee straight stretches the hamstrings and prevents shortening. Pillows under the knee and hanging the limb encourage flexion and oedema. Flexion exercises alone do not address the position held for most of the day.
  3. The correct answer is B (Bridging with a roll under the thigh, avoiding pressure on the distal residual limb): Hip extensor strength is needed for prosthetic gait, and bridging with a roll under the thigh trains it while keeping all load off the unhealed distal wound. A band around the distal limb adds shear to the dehiscence, and pool work with an open wound carries an infection risk. Kicks on the left leg train the wrong limb and load a foot with poor perfusion and rest pain.
  4. The correct answer is C (Pause prosthetic preparation, document the findings and arrange same-day medical review for suspected infection): Purulent drainage, spreading erythema and a raised temperature in a diabetic person with vascular disease suggest a developing wound infection that needs prompt medical assessment. Diagnosing and treating infection is outside physiotherapy scope, and waiting or applying more compression delays care and may worsen perfusion.
Case CHB167

Divya Iyer, 54, an accountant in regional Queensland, has non cystic fibrosis bronchiectasis with three chest infections last year. She is seen by video from home. She has a productive cough most mornings with about two tablespoons of mucopurulent sputum and no haemoptysis. Home pulse oximeter SpO2 95 percent at rest; RR 16 counted on video; she speaks in full sentences. She does the active cycle of breathing technique sitting, for ten minutes once daily, and finds it tiring. She walks 30 minutes daily without trouble, works from home and lives alone, with a neighbour who checks on her at weekends. She has emailed a company brochure for a chest wall oscillation vest she saw on social media.

  1. The correct answer is C (Add relaxed breath control between cycles and continue until huffs are dry and non productive): Relaxed breath control between cycles reduces fatigue and bronchospasm, and carrying on until the huffs are dry means the session ends when the airways are clear rather than at a fixed time. A longer session without breath control adds to the tiredness she already reports. A costly vest is not shown to be better and is not the first adjustment to trial, and a fixed ten minute stop leaves secretions behind.
  2. The correct answer is D (Seek same day medical review, as these signs suggest an exacerbation, and continue airway clearance): Increased purulent sputum with new breathlessness, fever and lowered saturation indicates an infective exacerbation that needs medical assessment and likely antibiotics, and airway clearance continues alongside that treatment. Intensifying physiotherapy alone delays treatment. Stopping clearance allows secretions to build up, and a pharmacist product does not address the infection.
  3. The correct answer is D (Explain that small uncontrolled studies give weak evidence, then compare the device with guideline supported techniques): Evidence based practice weighs study design, size and bias, and a small unblinded study without controls cannot show that a device works better than existing techniques. The best response is to explain this and compare alternatives against guideline recommendations and her preferences. Treating any publication as proof is uncritical, refusing to engage ignores her values, and similar participants do not remove the design flaws.
  4. The correct answer is B (Stop, have her sit down, stay connected to monitor her, and call 000 if she worsens): Sudden pallor and dizziness during a remote session is a safety event, so the technique stops and she is placed somewhere safe. Staying connected lets the physiotherapist watch her and activate emergency services at once if she deteriorates, which matters because she is alone. Continuing in position or resuming risks a faint, and ending the call removes the only observer.
Case CHB168

Kwame Mensah, 38, is a delivery driver who felt a sudden blow to the back of his left calf while playing touch football and fell. He arrived in the emergency department two hours ago and is otherwise well, with no regular medication and no history of clots. Examination: a palpable gap in the Achilles tendon about 4 centimetres above the calcaneus, bruising and moderate swelling, weak active plantarflexion, a resting left ankle that sits in more dorsiflexion than the right, normal sensation and foot pulses, and no calf tenderness. The emergency physician suspects a complete rupture and has asked you to fit a splint and teach crutches before he goes home to await orthopaedic review. The corridor you planned to use for crutch practice has just been mopped by cleaning staff.

  1. The correct answer is C (A positive Thompson test, indicating loss of continuity between the calf muscle complex and the calcaneus): Failure of the foot to plantarflex on calf squeeze is a positive Thompson test and indicates the tendon no longer transmits force to the heel. With a palpable gap and a more dorsiflexed resting posture it strongly supports complete rupture. Tendinopathy keeps the fibres intact, so the foot would still move.
  2. The correct answer is D (Hold the ankle in gentle plantarflexion and keep him non-weight-bearing on crutches until orthopaedic review): Resting the foot in plantarflexion brings the torn tendon ends together, and protecting from load avoids lengthening the healing tendon. Dorsiflexion or stretching pulls the ends apart and weight-bearing in a neutral position strains them. Early guided loading may follow later, but only under the specialist's plan.
  3. The correct answer is D (Warn that calf swelling, breathlessness or chest pain need urgent care, leaving medication choices to doctors): Immobilisation after Achilles rupture raises the risk of venous thromboembolism, so warning signs and when to seek urgent help belong in the advice. Recommending medicines is outside physiotherapy scope. Calf massage can be harmful if a clot is present, and good fitness does not remove the risk.
  4. The correct answer is A (Enter the near miss in the incident system, note facts and tell the nurse in charge): Near misses are reported so hazards are fixed before someone is hurt, and the report should be factual and made promptly through the organisation's system. The senior nurse should be told so the area is made safe. Waiting for an injury or shifting the risk to the patient does not meet risk management duties.
Case CHB169

Ravi Subramanian, 71, retired accountant, had a glioblastoma resected four months ago and is now on a palliative care unit with a prognosis of weeks to a few months. He takes dexamethasone and levetiracetam. The agreed goals of care are comfort and function, with no further tumour treatment. Findings: proximal lower limb strength 3/5 bilaterally, left ankle dorsiflexion 2/5, sit to stand needs one-person assistance, one fall last week, mild left-sided inattention, SpO2 95 percent, HR 82, pain 3/10, high fatigue. His wife wishes to take him to the unit garden and to learn safe transfers. Today the physiotherapist also has a new patient with acute breathlessness, a discharge home needing equipment, and a family meeting at 2 pm, with two hours available.

  1. The correct answer is D (See the breathless patient first, prepare for the meeting, then arrange equipment and see Mr Subramanian): A new acute problem with a potential safety risk is seen first, then the fixed-time commitment is prepared for, with the remaining tasks done in order of urgency. Mr Subramanian is stable, so his session can be shortened or shifted without harm. Handing the unassessed breathless patient to nursing staff or deferring review for paperwork leaves a safety concern unexamined.
  2. The correct answer is A (Show a simple assisted transfer, supervise her practice, and check she repeats it safely): Carer training works best through demonstration, supervised practice and confirmation that she can repeat the transfer safely, matched to his present ability. The single demonstration with unsupervised practice gives no check of technique. Teaching many methods at once overloads a tired carer, and a hoist for every transfer goes beyond what one person assistance currently requires and cuts across his goal of function.
  3. The correct answer is D (Steroid myopathy, so use short, low-load functional exercises like sit to stand within his fatigue limits): Symmetrical proximal weakness with intact sensation and reflexes in a patient on long-term corticosteroids points to steroid myopathy. In palliative care, short functional tasks within fatigue limits preserve independence without overloading him. Cord compression would be expected to give long tract signs or sensory change, and intensive resistance work is unrealistic at this stage.
  4. The correct answer is D (Apply wheelchair brakes, protect his head, time the seizure and call for help): The priorities are to keep him safe where he is, protect his head, note the duration and summon help. He is already secured in a belted chair with head support, so moving him adds risk on an uneven path. Restraining limbs or placing objects in the mouth causes injury, and wheeling him during the seizure is unsafe.
Case CHB170

Darren Kowalczyk, 46, plasterer. Four days of worsening low back pain with left leg pain after lifting sheeting at work. Yesterday he noticed difficulty starting his urine stream, and today he says he feels numb when wiping after toileting. He mentions that erections have been difficult this week. Previous L5-S1 disc bulge two years ago, managed conservatively. Medications: paracetamol and ibuprofen. Observations: HR 78, BP 130/82, afebrile. Examination: lumbar flexion limited by pain; left straight leg raise reproduces leg pain at 30 degrees; left ankle plantarflexion 4/5, right 5/5; left ankle reflex reduced; reduced light touch over the left lateral foot and sole; reduced pinprick sensation around the anus. Seen at a private practice outpatient clinic. His GP, Dr Ahmed Rahman, has rooms nearby. The nearest emergency department has an on-site spinal surgical service.

  1. The correct answer is B (Altered perianal sensation with new urinary hesitancy and sexual dysfunction beginning this week): Saddle sensory change with bladder and sexual dysfunction points to compression of the sacral nerve roots in the cauda equina. Unilateral radicular pain, a reduced reflex and mild weakness fit a single root problem and are common in ordinary disc radiculopathy, so they do not raise the same urgency.
  2. The correct answer is A (Stop treatment and arrange urgent emergency department assessment for same-day imaging): Saddle numbness with new urinary hesitancy points to suspected cauda equina syndrome, which needs same-day emergency assessment and imaging because delay lowers the chance of recovering bladder, bowel and sexual function. A GP visit tomorrow or an outpatient scan this week is too slow. Waiting for symptoms to worsen is not appropriate when the red flags are already present.
  3. The correct answer is D (Arrange an ambulance or a lift with another driver, and ask him not to drive): His altered foot sensation and weakness, plus anxiety and distraction, make driving unsafe, and any delay in reaching hospital counts. An ambulance or a supported lift gets him there directly. Detouring via the GP or home adds delay that this time-critical condition cannot afford.
  4. The correct answer is D (Give a structured verbal summary of onset, red flags and neurological findings, then send notes): A structured handover of timing, red flags and objective neurological findings lets the receiving team prioritise him without repeating tests. Relying on a letter, a GP relay or the diagnosis alone delays or loses the specific information needed to act quickly.
Case CHB171

Mrs Lucia Conti, 71, a retired seamstress, lives alone in a single-storey home. Six weeks ago she slipped on wet tiles and fell onto her outstretched right (dominant) hand. A distal radius fracture was treated with closed reduction and a plaster cast, which was removed today, and she is referred to the post-immobilisation outpatient clinic. History: hypertension, no previous fractures, post-menopausal, never had a bone density scan, walks without an aid. Examination: mild hand and finger swelling, finger stiffness with an incomplete fist, wrist flexion 35 degrees, wrist extension 30 degrees, supination limited, grip about 40 percent of the left, pain 4 out of 10 with movement. No numbness, normal capillary refill, and skin colour and temperature match the left hand. Her surgeon has cleared her to move. Goals: sew, cook and open jars. She asks what she can do to stop falling and breaking more bones.

  1. The correct answer is B (Fall on an outstretched hand with a distal radius fracture displaced and tilted dorsally): A Colles fracture is a distal radius fracture, usually from falling on an outstretched hand, with the distal fragment displaced and tilted dorsally. The reverse pattern with palmar displacement follows a fall on a flexed wrist. Scaphoid and radial head fractures share the outstretched hand mechanism but are different injuries with different signs.
  2. The correct answer is D (Active wrist and forearm exercises, finger mobility and oedema control, progressing to graded strengthening): After cast removal the priorities are active range of motion, finger and forearm movement and oedema control, then progressive strengthening as pain and healing allow. Forceful passive stretching risks pain, swelling and a flare of symptoms. Early heavy resistance is too much for the tissues, and prolonged splinting keeps the wrist and fingers stiff.
  3. The correct answer is B (Notify the referring doctor of suspected complex regional pain syndrome and pace activity by pain): Disproportionate burning pain, allodynia, swelling and skin and temperature changes are the pattern of complex regional pain syndrome, which needs early medical review while therapy continues with pain-paced graded activity and desensitisation. Pushing harder aggravates the condition, and reassuring her ignores new red flags. Nothing here suggests the fracture has moved.
  4. The correct answer is B (Encourage a GP review of bone health and a regular strength and balance exercise programme): A wrist fracture from a fall at her age is a fragility fracture, so she should be directed to her GP for bone health assessment, and regular strength and balance training reduces falls risk and supports bone. Avoiding weight bearing weakens bone, restricting outings reduces fitness and confidence, and treating the fracture as isolated misses the chance to prevent the next one.
Case CHB172

Neha Iyer, 12, is brought by her parents after the school nurse noticed uneven shoulders. She has not started menstruating, is growing quickly, and has no back pain or neurological symptoms. Examination: right thoracic rib prominence on forward bending that persists on sitting and side bending, mild right shoulder elevation, level pelvis, equal leg lengths, normal reflexes, strength and sensation, and no skin markings. A standing radiograph arranged by her GP shows a 28 degree right thoracic curve and a Risser grade of 1. Her parents have found a website selling an exercise programme that claims to cure scoliosis, with testimonials and before and after photographs, and they ask whether it can replace a brace. Neha is anxious about wearing a brace at school.

  1. The correct answer is A (Vertebral rotation within a structural curve, which does not correct with posture or position change): A rib prominence that persists across positions reflects fixed vertebral rotation, which is the hallmark of a structural scoliosis. Postural curves disappear on forward bending or position change. Muscle spasm and core weakness do not produce a fixed rotational deformity.
  2. The correct answer is D (Orthopaedic referral to consider bracing, with regular radiograph monitoring through the growth spurt): A moderate curve in a child with substantial growth remaining carries a real risk of progression, so specialist review for bracing and serial monitoring is standard. Observation alone at a single late review would miss progression during the growth spurt. Surgery is reserved for much larger or progressing curves, and exercises are an adjunct rather than a replacement for bracing.
  3. The correct answer is A (Look for controlled studies comparing curve progression with usual care, and check for commercial interest): Claims of cure should be tested against controlled studies that measure curve progression, along with the risk of bias from people selling the product. Testimonials and photographs are selected and cannot show what would have happened without treatment. Reputation or a short trial judged by appearance can delay effective treatment during the window when the curve can progress.
  4. The correct answer is C (Explain that mild fading redness is expected, check the fit with the orthotist, and encourage wear): Mild redness over a pressure point that fades soon after the brace comes off is common with early use. Brace effectiveness depends on adherence to the prescribed hours, and fit adjustments belong with the orthotist. Cutting wear time reduces benefit, stopping outright overreacts to normal skin changes, and adding padding yourself alters a prescribed device.
Case CHB173

Eddie Papadopoulos, 72, retired builder, is on day 3 after an open right lower lobectomy for lung cancer. History: COPD, 40 pack year smoking history, weight loss of 11 kg in six months, BMI 17.5, low albumin. Observations: RR 22, HR 96, SpO2 93 percent on 2 L/min via nasal prongs, afebrile. A right intercostal catheter is on water seal. Pain is 5 out of 10 on coughing, managed with regular oral analgesia. Auscultation: reduced air entry and fine crackles at the right base; weak, unproductive cough. He sat out of bed yesterday with assistance and fatigues after two minutes of standing. He is a high falls risk. The ward has allied health assistants who support mobilisation.

  1. The correct answer is B (Active cycle of breathing with supported huffing, timed after analgesia and given in upright sitting): Pain limits deep breathing and cough after thoracotomy, so treatment is timed after analgesia and uses wound support with active cycle of breathing and huffing in an upright position. Percussion over a fresh thoracotomy before analgesia adds pain and has limited benefit. Suction is reserved for patients who cannot cooperate, and huffing with splinting is safe for the wound.
  2.
