The correct answer is C (Carry it upright below chest level, avoid kinked tubing and keep every connection intact): The drainage bottle stays upright and below the level of the chest so that fluid and air drain away from the pleural space, and the closed system is kept intact. Clamping can trap air and cause a tension pneumothorax, and disconnecting breaks the seal. Raising the bottle above the chest allows fluid to flow back toward the patient.
  3. The correct answer is A (Short, frequent walking and low load resistance exercise with rest breaks, avoiding exhaustion while nutrition improves): Cachexia and postoperative fatigue call for brief, frequent, low intensity activity that preserves muscle without depleting limited energy reserves, alongside nutritional support. A single long session or high intensity intervals exceed his tolerance. Restricting him to bed until weight recovers would accelerate loss of strength and function.
  4. The correct answer is A (Assess his condition, confirm the assistant is competent, give clear instructions and stop triggers, and review): The physiotherapist remains accountable for delegated care and must match the task to the patient's current condition and the assistant's demonstrated competence, with clear instructions, escalation triggers and ongoing supervision. A written sheet alone leaves judgement with an unqualified worker, and a role description does not mean every patient is suitable.
Case CHB174

Albert Walker, 79, Wiradjuri man, admitted to the acute medical ward after a fall at home with a urinary tract infection, now treated. Known Alzheimer disease with mobility decline over six months. He lives with his granddaughter Shannon in a country town 250 km from the hospital. Oriented to person only, follows one-step commands with cues. Hearing and vision are adequate. Walks 15 metres with a four-wheeled frame and stand-by assistance, short steps, two near falls on the ward. He becomes restless in the evenings. Lying BP 130/80, HR 76, SpO2 96%. Hip pain 2/10, X-ray shows no fracture. Medications include donepezil and an antihypertensive. An Aboriginal Liaison Officer is available, and Shannon visits daily.

  1. The correct answer is C (Postural hypotension: sit him down, inform the nurse and doctor, and progress position changes gradually): A systolic fall of 20 mmHg or more on standing with symptoms is postural hypotension, a modifiable falls risk that the medical team needs to know about. He should be seated and position changes graded. Treating it as normal ageing or continuing exercise ignores the symptoms, and nothing here suggests an arrhythmia.
  2. The correct answer is A (Supervision with scheduled toileting, a low bed and his frame positioned within reach): People with dementia cannot reliably learn or remember to use a call bell, so safety relies on environment and supervision that meet needs such as toileting. Bed rails and belts are restraints that raise injury risk and distress. Keeping him seated leads to deconditioning and does not address why he is getting up.
  3. The correct answer is D (Sit beside him, yarn informally, and ask whether he wants family or a liaison officer present): Culturally safe practice adapts the style to the person: relaxed conversation, sitting alongside and inviting family or a liaison officer, with his agreement. Speaking louder does not address the mismatch, and having family answer for him removes his own voice. Delaying care until a worker is free is not needed when the approach itself can be changed now.
  4. The correct answer is B (Train Shannon in safe assisting, advise on home equipment, and link him with local health services): Returning to Country and family supports wellbeing, so the role is to make it safe: carer training, equipment and a link to local services. Recommending placement away from family overrides his and the family's wishes, and waiting for full independence may never be realistic in dementia. A sheet and phone call do not give Shannon practical support.
Case CHB175

Anjali Kapoor, 41, a primary school teacher and mother of two, attends your private practice. She leaks small amounts of urine when she coughs, sneezes, jogs or jumps on the trampoline with her children. This has continued for two years since her second vaginal birth three years ago. She has no urgency, no burning, no blood in the urine and no sense of vaginal bulge. BMI 29. She drinks about one litre of water and four coffees daily. Her bladder diary shows seven voids per day, one at night, and two leaks daily. With her informed consent, vaginal palpation finds a pelvic floor muscle contraction of 2/5 on the Modified Oxford scale, a hold of 3 seconds, and she bears down when asked to lift. She wants to return to jogging and trampolining.

  1. The correct answer is D (Stress urinary incontinence from reduced pelvic floor support when abdominal pressure suddenly rises): Leakage with raised intra-abdominal pressure and no urgency is the stress pattern, and seven voids per day is within a normal range. Urgency, constant dribbling and a weak stream are absent, which makes the other types less likely.
  2. The correct answer is D (Teach a correct inward lift without bearing down, with feedback, before adding longer holds): Many women contract incorrectly, so technique and feedback come before volume. Bearing down worsens leakage, midstream stopping can disturb emptying, and crunches raise abdominal pressure without training the pelvic floor specifically.
  3. The correct answer is C (Keep training, use a pre-contraction before impact, and return to jogging gradually, adjusting load): Progress is encouraging but she is not yet fully controlled, so a graded return with a pre-contraction and load adjustment lets her stay active while strength improves. Complete avoidance or a pad alone does not build capacity, and her strength is not yet sufficient for unrestricted impact.
  4. The correct answer is A (Drink water steadily through the day, cut coffee gradually, and keep the bladder diary): Adequate fluid keeps urine dilute and the bladder comfortable, while a gradual caffeine reduction avoids rebound and is a modest bladder irritant measure. Fluid restriction concentrates urine and irritates the bladder, and over-frequent voiding trains a smaller capacity.
Case CHB176

Heather Sutherland, 68, a retired primary school teacher, attends your private practice with 8 months of bilateral hand pain. She has hypertension managed with an ACE inhibitor and has not seen her GP about her hands. Examination: Heberden nodes at the distal interphalangeal joints of both index and middle fingers, bony enlargement at the base of the right thumb with a positive grind test, and reduced grip strength bilaterally. Over the last 3 weeks both hands have also felt stiff for about 90 minutes on waking. The 2nd and 3rd metacarpophalangeal (MCP) joints are warm and softly swollen on both sides, and the metacarpal squeeze test is positive. Wrist extension is 40 degrees bilaterally. Pain is 6/10 with jar opening. She is afebrile and keen to keep gardening.

  1. The correct answer is D (Prolonged morning stiffness with warm swollen MCP joints and a positive metacarpal squeeze test): Morning stiffness lasting well beyond about 30 minutes, warm soft joint swelling and a painful squeeze across the MCP joints point to synovitis, which is typical of inflammatory arthritis such as rheumatoid arthritis. Heberden nodes and thumb base changes are classic osteoarthritis findings and do not by themselves suggest inflammation. Reduced grip strength occurs in both conditions so it does not separate them.
  2. The correct answer is A (A thumb base support worn for provoking tasks, plus joint protection education and pain-free movement): For thumb base osteoarthritis, a support during aggravating tasks combined with joint protection and gentle movement reduces load and pain while keeping function. Heavy resisted pinch and forced end range stretching compress or stress the painful carpometacarpal joint. Long continuous splinting leads to stiffness and weakness without extra benefit.
  3. The correct answer is C (Explain your concern and promptly send your findings to her GP for inflammatory arthritis review): Possible inflammatory arthritis needs prompt medical diagnosis because early treatment protects joints, and in Australia the GP coordinates pathology and the specialist referral that attracts the Medicare rebate. Ordering and interpreting investigations for diagnosis sits outside physiotherapy scope. Waiting six weeks delays time-sensitive care, which is the main flaw in continuing osteoarthritis management first.
  4. The correct answer is A (Keep gentle pain-free active movement, pace tasks, and avoid forceful gripping through the swollen joints): Inflamed joints do best with gentle, pain-free movement and task pacing, which maintain range without adding load. Complete rest promotes stiffness and weakness. Resisted gripping and firm deep friction over swollen joints increase stress on inflamed synovium and can aggravate the flare.
Case CHB177

Nabil Nasser, 15, has cystic fibrosis with pancreatic insufficiency and cystic fibrosis related diabetes. He is on day 3 of a pulmonary exacerbation on a respiratory ward of a children's hospital: IV antibiotics through a PICC in his right arm, increased productive cough and a poor appetite. Observations: RR 22, HR 104, SpO2 92 percent on room air, temperature 37.6 degrees Celsius, blood glucose 5.8 mmol/L before breakfast. Chest: coarse crackles throughout, worse at the right upper zone. His sputum grows Pseudomonas aeruginosa chronically. He plays football and wants to stay fit. He asks to use the hospital's shared gym with another teenager with CF he has met on the ward. His mother is present. A teacher from his school telephones the ward, asking the physiotherapist for details of his illness and expected return date.

  1. The correct answer is C (Arrange individual exercise in his room and explain why people with CF are kept apart): People with CF are kept apart because organisms such as Pseudomonas can spread by droplets, shared surfaces and close contact, so individual exercise in his own room with cleaned equipment is safest. Masks and wiping reduce but do not remove that risk. Exercise is encouraged during an exacerbation when tolerated, and peer support does not outweigh cross-infection.
  2. The correct answer is A (Have fast-acting carbohydrate available and recheck his glucose before and during the session): Exercise with active insulin and little carbohydrate intake raises the risk of hypoglycaemia, so fast-acting carbohydrate should be at hand and glucose checked before and during. Waiting for the insulin peak or relying on symptoms delays detection of a fall. PICC care matters, but it is not the most urgent hazard here.
  3. The correct answer is D (Avoid resisted or repetitive loading of the right arm, using the left arm and legs instead): A PICC sits in a vein of the upper arm, so resisted or repetitive use of that limb risks irritating the vein or displacing the line. Left arm and leg training still meets his fitness goals. Light weights or push-ups still load the arm, and banning all upper limb activity is more restrictive than needed.
  4. The correct answer is D (Decline to share details and refer the request to the team to seek Nabil's consent): Health information is confidential and may be released only with consent, and a 15 year old who understands the issues should be part of that decision with his mother. Passing the request to the treating team avoids improvised disclosure to an unverified caller. Even a return date or general summary reveals information about his health.
Case CHB178

Hung Tran, 71, a retired builder with idiopathic pulmonary fibrosis, is admitted with progressive breathlessness. The palliative care team is involved and his goals of care are comfort focused, with a wish to return home with community palliative care. Observations: SpO2 89 percent on 4 L/min oxygen via nasal prongs, RR 28, HR 104, BP 118/72. Fine bilateral basal crackles. Borg dyspnoea 8 out of 10 after sitting out of bed. He fears suffocating at night and his wife Linh stays with him. Morphine is prescribed by the medical team. Yesterday during mobilisation his oxygen tubing caught on the bed rail and he stumbled; a nurse caught him and there was no injury.

  1. The correct answer is B (Supported forward leaning sitting with relaxed shoulders, plus a handheld fan directed at his face): Supported forward leaning reduces the work of breathing and a fan on the face eases the sensation of breathlessness, which suits comfort focused care at night. Upright sitting with diaphragmatic breathing helps less in stiff fibrotic lungs and lacks the fan. Walking practice with a Borg rating of 8 is too demanding, and changing the oxygen flow is outside the prescription and the comfort goals.
  2. The correct answer is B (Scarred, stiff lung tissue thickens the barrier for oxygen transfer, so levels fall more with exertion): Pulmonary fibrosis replaces normal lung with scar tissue that is stiff and thickens the gas exchange membrane, so oxygen transfer is limited and worsens when exercise increases demand. Airway collapse with air trapping describes COPD. Heart failure and primary respiratory muscle weakness are different mechanisms and do not explain this disease.
  3. The correct answer is D (Tell Hung and Linh, enter the near miss in the incident system, and document the facts): Near misses are reported because they reveal hazards before someone is harmed, and the staff member who was involved or supervising should enter the report. Open disclosure to the patient and family and a factual record complete the response. Recording only in the notes loses the learning for the organisation, handing it off leaves a gap, and delaying the report for a care review has no justification.
  4. The correct answer is B (Offer a paced walk with portable oxygen and a chair for rests, stopping when he asks): In comfort focused care the aim is to support activity that matters to the person with the least distress, so a short paced walk with portable oxygen, seating and his own stopping signal respects his wishes. Deferring removes a valued activity without reason. A progressive training programme does not match his goals, and changing oxygen flow is outside the physiotherapist's remit and is guided by prescription.
Case CHB179

Jayden Nguyen, 14, has Duchenne muscular dystrophy diagnosed at age 4. He attends the paediatric outpatient clinic with his mother every three months and takes daily corticosteroids prescribed by his neurologist. He still walks short distances but has had two falls this month and tires on school stairs. Findings: calf pseudohypertrophy, toe walking, hip extensor and knee extensor strength 3/5, ankle dorsiflexion reaching only neutral with the knee extended, tight hamstrings bilaterally, and a 10 metre walk time of 14 seconds. A respiratory review was last done 14 months ago. He enjoys online gaming and adapted sport with friends. His mother is grateful for the clinic, has no family nearby and often talks with the physiotherapist about her own stress. Jayden has attended this clinic for three years.

  1. The correct answer is D (Proximal weakness of the hip and knee extensors, with the arms substituting to lift the trunk): Rising by walking the hands up the thighs is the classic Gowers manoeuvre. It substitutes arm and trunk work for weak gluteal and quadriceps muscles. Calf tightness contributes to his toe walking but does not explain the hand use, and sensation and tone are not the problem in this condition.
  2. The correct answer is A (Daily gentle sustained calf and hamstring stretching, combined with night ankle-foot orthoses): Regular gentle stretching with night orthoses is the standard way to slow contracture in Duchenne muscular dystrophy while keeping him walking. Heavy eccentric work is avoided because dystrophic muscle is easily damaged by high-load lengthening contractions. Forceful stretching risks injury, and prolonged casting adds weakness from immobility.
  3. The correct answer is B (Decline both kindly, explain the professional boundary, and keep contact within clinic channels): A long treating relationship with a vulnerable adolescent and a stressed parent makes blurred boundaries a real risk to objectivity and to his care. Declining warmly, naming the reason and keeping contact in clinic channels protects the relationship. Private settings or a short attendance do not remove the dual relationship, and recording it afterwards does not make it appropriate.
  4. The correct answer is C (Contact the respiratory team promptly to request urgent review for possible nocturnal hypoventilation): Morning headache, disturbed sleep and orthopnoea in a boy with declining respiratory muscle strength suggest sleep-disordered breathing or hypoventilation. This needs prompt medical review for assessment of ventilatory support, which is outside physiotherapy to start. Airway clearance training helps but does not replace urgent referral, and a routine letter or GP visit delays it.
Case CHB180

Ngoc Huynh, 6 years, attends a hospital outpatient clinic. She has spastic diplegic cerebral palsy, GMFCS level II, and walks independently at school with toe contact first in stance. Six weeks ago she received botulinum toxin injections to both gastrocnemius muscles, followed by two weeks of serial below-knee casting. Casts were removed one week ago. Today passive ankle dorsiflexion with the knee extended is 0 degrees on the right and 5 degrees on the left (before injection: minus 15 and minus 10). Modified Ashworth Scale for the plantarflexors is 1+ bilaterally. Gait shows improved heel strike on the left only. Her mother says Ngoc has not worn her night ankle-foot orthoses since the casts came off because they feel tight. The clinic holds Gross Motor Function Measure scores from baseline and wants to use them to review the service. Ngoc is cooperative and reports no pain.

  1. The correct answer is B (Check fit and skin, adjust if needed, then build up nightly wear gradually): Gains from toxin and casting are lost without ongoing stretch, and a tight feel after casting usually settles once fit and skin are checked and wear is built up gradually. Waiting for a remake leaves her without overnight stretch, and full nights straight away risks skin problems and further refusal. Daytime stretching cannot replace the long sustained stretch that night orthoses give.
  2. The correct answer is B (It blocks acetylcholine release at the junction, wearing off over months as terminals regrow): Botulinum toxin blocks release of acetylcholine at the neuromuscular junction, and the effect fades over months as new nerve terminals sprout. That is why stretching, strengthening and orthoses are used while tone is reduced. It does not lengthen muscle directly, and it acts locally rather than centrally.
  3. The correct answer is A (Start graded strengthening and task practice now, using the window of reduced tone for learning): Reduced tone after toxin and casting is a window in which weak antagonist muscles can be trained and new movement patterns learned. Waiting until the effect fades wastes that window, and passive work does not build the active control she needs for walking at school. Endurance walking alone ignores the calf and ankle weakness she reports.
  4. The correct answer is C (Repeat the same measures at set intervals and add goal attainment scores for comparison): Repeating the same standardised measures at fixed intervals, together with individual goal attainment scores, lets the clinic compare change fairly across children and over time. Using the Gross Motor Function Measure alone ignores what families set as their goals. Goal scores alone lose the standardised comparison, and a published reference group does not reflect this clinic's children or the effect of the episode.

Practice Test 13 Answer Key

Case CHB181

Mrs Tracey Pearce, 78, a retired schoolteacher, is on day 3 of intravenous antibiotics for right lower lobe community acquired pneumonia. History: type 2 diabetes, osteoarthritis of both knees, ex-smoker. She lives alone and loves her garden. Observations today: RR 22, HR 96, BP 128/74, temperature 37.6, SpO2 94% on 2 L/min via nasal prongs. Auscultation: coarse crackles and reduced air entry at the right base, with secretions audible on deep breaths. Sputum is thick and yellow. Her cough is weak and she says she is too tired to cough. She sits out of bed with supervision and walks 10 metres with a frame before stopping breathless (Borg 4). Before discharge she asks what she can do herself to avoid another chest infection.

  1. The correct answer is A (Assist secretion clearance using positioning, active cycle of breathing and supported huffing with cough): Audible secretions, thick sputum and a weak cough point to retained secretions as the problem that physiotherapy can change today. Techniques that mobilise and move secretions, with support for a weak cough, address it directly. Endurance training and rest matter later, but neither clears the airway, and volume work without clearing secretions is less effective.
  2. The correct answer is D (Left side-lying with the bed flat, letting gravity assist drainage of the right base): Drainage needs the affected lobe uppermost, so left side-lying suits a right lower lobe, and the head-down tilt can be dropped because she does not tolerate it and gravity still helps in a flat position. Keeping the tilt ignores her intolerance. Lying on the right puts the affected lung down, which does not drain it.
  3. The correct answer is A (Stop walking, seat her safely, check the oxygen delivery and monitor until saturation recovers): A clear fall in saturation with high perceived effort and a rising heart rate means exercise should stop, with the patient seated and the equipment and her recovery checked. Walking on risks further desaturation. Changing the oxygen flow is a nursing and medical decision made under the treating team's orders, so the physiotherapist reports the response rather than adjusting it.
  4. The correct answer is D (Stay active daily, practise the cough and breathing techniques, and discuss vaccinations with her GP): Self-management after pneumonia combines regular activity, rehearsed breathing and cough techniques she can use if symptoms return, and referral of vaccination questions to the person who can advise on them, her GP. Prolonged rest promotes deconditioning. A device has no evidence as daily prevention, and waiting for a fever alone delays early review.
Case CHB182

Salvatore Ferraro, 74, retired mechanic, lives alone in a two-storey home. He has had type 2 diabetes for 20 years, with hypertension and mild chronic kidney disease. His GP referred him to outpatient physiotherapy for burning feet, unsteadiness and two falls in the past year, neither causing injury. Findings: 10 g monofilament sensation absent at the forefoot and toes bilaterally; vibration absent at the big toes; ankle reflexes absent; ankle strength 4/5; Berg Balance Scale 38/56. Skin is dry with a small heel fissure and a callus under the right first metatarsal head, with a healed ulcer scar at the same site. He wears worn backless slippers at home. The clinic gym has exercise mats; another patient told the front desk last week that one mat edge was curling.

  1. The correct answer is C (Stop weight-bearing exercise and arrange same-day medical review for possible acute Charcot neuroarthropathy or infection): Warmth, redness and swelling in an insensate foot suggest acute Charcot neuroarthropathy or infection, and the lack of pain reflects the neuropathy. Weight-bearing must stop and he needs medical review the same day. A podiatry visit next week or a routine GP review delays care for a limb-threatening problem, and reviewing only the heel fissure misses the warm swollen midfoot, which is the main concern.
  2. The correct answer is A (Supported standing and weight-shift tasks with eyes open on a firm surface, then progress difficulty gradually): With little sensation from his feet he relies on vision and the vestibular system, so early work keeps eyes open on a firm surface with support, then progresses in small steps. Removing vision on foam or walking tandem without support is too hard and risks a fall. Postponing standing work leaves his falls risk untreated, since sensation may not return.
  3. The correct answer is B (Check his feet daily, wear closed supportive shoes indoors, and see a podiatrist for the callus): Daily foot inspection, supportive closed footwear indoors and podiatry care for the callus reduce the chance of unnoticed injury and ulcer recurrence. Soaking and filing at home risks burns or skin damage in feet that cannot feel it. Going barefoot or wearing open sandals exposes the skin to trauma.
  4. The correct answer is A (Remove the mat, report the incident in the clinic's system, and openly discuss it with him): After a fall linked to equipment, particularly when the hazard was already known, the hazard is removed, the event is reported through the organisation's incident system so it can be reviewed, and the patient is told openly what happened. A note in his record alone does not trigger a hazard review. A quick repair or reporting externally first bypasses the clinic's own governance.
Case CHB183

You are the emergency department physiotherapist on a busy afternoon. Sione Faleolo, 52, a bus driver, presented with a hot, red, swollen right knee since yesterday and cannot bear weight. He has had previous episodes of toe pain. Observations: temperature 38.6 degrees, HR 104, BP 128/80. The knee has a large effusion, is warm, and any passive movement in any direction causes severe pain. Range is 10 to 40 degrees. Joint aspiration and blood tests are pending. You have three requests. The doctor asks you to mobilise Sione. Mrs Ruth Hollis, 84, medically cleared after a minor fall, needs a mobility assessment before discharge because her bed is needed. A young man with a mild ankle sprain and negative Ottawa ankle rules has waited three hours for crutches.

  1. The correct answer is B (Septic arthritis, because infection can destroy the joint surface quickly if untreated): A hot joint with fever and pain through all movement is septic arthritis until proven otherwise, since delay risks irreversible cartilage loss. Crystal arthritis can look identical but is not the dangerous diagnosis to miss, and the picture is not typical of a minor injury.
  2. The correct answer is A (Do not mobilise the knee, document the findings, and report them to the doctor): A possibly infected joint is not a physiotherapy target until the diagnosis is made, and loading or moving it adds pain without benefit. Reporting findings clearly helps the team, whereas teaching discharge mobility or exercise assumes a benign cause.
  3. The correct answer is D (Teach crutches with weight bearing as pain allows, with elevation and gentle active movement): In a crystal flare the joint is protected while pain is high, but prolonged immobilisation causes stiffness and weakness, so comfort-guided loading with aids is preferred. Resisted work and heat or deep friction would aggravate an acutely inflamed joint.
  4. The correct answer is D (See Sione first, because he is the sickest patient and the doctor's request is unsafe): Sione is the sickest patient and the doctor's request to mobilise a possibly infected joint is unsafe, so the physiotherapist should first see him and advise the doctor against mobilisation before the aspirate result. Mrs Hollis's discharge assessment and the sprain can follow.
Case CHB184

Rosa Tuilagi, 71, had a left transtibial amputation three weeks ago for critical limb ischaemia. She has type 2 diabetes with peripheral neuropathy, and her right foot has dry skin and a callus under the first metatarsal head. She is on a subacute rehabilitation ward, transfers with supervision and uses a wheelchair with a residual limb support. Residual limb: sutures removed, a 2 cm area of the distal wound edge has opened with cloudy ooze, surrounding redness extends about 3 cm, and the skin feels warm. Temperature 37.9 degrees Celsius, HR 92, BP 136/78. Knee extension lacks 10 degrees and hip extension is limited. She keeps the knee bent over a pillow for comfort. Rosa wants a prosthesis fitted as soon as possible, and her family is keen to know the date. She lives with her daughter.

  1. The correct answer is B (Keep the knee straight in bed and use a residual limb support board when seated): Holding the knee in extension in bed and supporting the residual limb in sitting keeps the hamstrings and knee capsule from shortening. A pillow under the knee, hanging the limb down and resting it bent all encourage flexion, which delays prosthetic fitting. Elevation on pillows in a wheelchair is an old habit that also promotes a bent knee.
  2. The correct answer is A (Hold limb loading and compression, report to the treating surgical team today, and document findings): Spreading erythema, warmth, ooze and a raised temperature suggest wound infection in a person with diabetes, which needs same day medical assessment. The physiotherapist protects the wound by holding compression and loading, then escalates and documents. Wound debridement or ordering tests falls outside entry-level physiotherapy scope here, and waiting for a weekly meeting delays care.
  3. The correct answer is A (Active knee and hip extension exercises in lying, plus right leg and trunk strengthening): Active extension work in lying maintains range and strength while keeping force off the open wound. Distal pressure, weight acceptance and firm massage are all valid pre-prosthetic techniques once the wound has healed, but each would load or shear the compromised tissue today.
  4. The correct answer is C (Check both feet daily with a mirror or helper, wear protective shoes, and report skin breaks): With neuropathy and reduced sensation, unnoticed skin damage is the main route to ulceration, so daily inspection, protective footwear and prompt reporting are the core protection. Soaking, heat and self-trimming can burn or break insensate skin, and moisturiser between the toes leaves moisture that favours infection.
Case CHB185

Kiran Mehta, a 24-year-old apprentice electrician, was the front-seat passenger in a car collision two hours ago and has been brought to the emergency department. Findings: seatbelt-pattern bruising across the sternum, tenderness over right ribs 4 to 7, HR 118, BP 92/60 mmHg, RR 26, SpO2 93% on 6 L/min via mask, temperature 36.5 degrees. Jugular veins are distended, the registrar describes heart sounds as muffled, and BP falls noticeably on inspiration. Breath sounds are reduced at the right base. Pain is 8 of 10. A bedside echocardiogram and chest X-ray are being arranged. The emergency physician has asked the physiotherapist to see Kiran for chest physiotherapy. Kiran's mother says the family has no private health insurance and is worried about how his physiotherapy will be funded after he leaves hospital.

  1. The correct answer is C (Withhold treatment, escalate the unstable signs to the emergency physician, and wait for cardiac assessment): Hypotension, distended neck veins, muffled heart sounds and worsening instability suggest cardiac tamponade, an emergency where chest physiotherapy could worsen his condition and delay definitive care. The physiotherapist should hold treatment and escalate immediately. Breathing exercises, basal positioning and coughing techniques target the lung findings, which are secondary to the cardiac threat.
  2. The correct answer is A (Low blood pressure, distended neck veins and muffled heart sounds on auscultation): Beck's triad is low blood pressure, distended neck veins and muffled heart sounds, and it indicates cardiac tamponade. Hypotension with absent breath sounds and a hyper resonant chest describes tension pneumothorax. Pulsus paradoxus is a supporting sign of tamponade but is not part of the triad. Raised pressure with a slow heart rate and irregular breathing is the Cushing response to raised intracranial pressure.
  3. The correct answer is B (Optimise analgesia first, then deep breathing and supported coughing with incentive spirometry and early mobilisation): Rib fracture pain drives shallow breathing and atelectasis, so analgesia timed before treatment allows deep breathing, supported coughing, incentive spirometry and early mobilisation, which lower the risk of pneumonia. Strapping and shallow breathing promote secretion retention. Percussing a fracture site adds harm, and delaying mobilisation removes a key part of recovery.
  4. The correct answer is A (Explain a compulsory third party claim may fund care, then ask social work to assist lodgement): Compulsory third party schemes pay reasonable treatment costs for people injured in motor vehicle crashes, so lodging a claim early is the correct pathway, and hospital social work routinely helps families with this. Medicare offers only limited rebates for allied health and does not cover private physiotherapy in full. The NDIS requires a permanent and significant disability, and schemes usually approve treatment as the claim proceeds rather than only after it is finalised.
Case CHB186

Kavita Gupta, 31, a primary school teacher, is 30 weeks pregnant with her first child and attends the antenatal clinic physiotherapy service. For two weeks she has had tingling and numbness in her right thumb, index and middle fingers that wakes her at night and eases when she shakes her hand. Her hands feel puffy by evening. She has no neck pain, headache or visual change. BP 118/72 mmHg. Examination: Phalen's test positive at 40 seconds, Tinel's sign positive at the wrist, reduced light touch over the thumb, index and middle fingers, thenar bulk normal, grip strength mildly reduced, neck movements full and pain free. Both hands show mild oedema. She marks books and types for several hours daily, wants to avoid medication and asks whether it will go away after the baby arrives.

  1. The correct answer is A (Night waking relieved by shaking the hand, with symptoms confined to three radial digits): Night waking that eases on shaking the hand, with symptoms confined to the median supplied digits, is the classic carpal tunnel picture and fits fluid retention in pregnancy. Full neck movement makes a cervical cause less likely but does not exclude one. Grip weakness can occur with either lesion, and a short duration of symptoms does not point to the neck over the wrist.
  2. The correct answer is D (Neutral wrist night splint, workstation changes and planned review of symptoms and function): Carpal tunnel syndrome in pregnancy is usually self-limiting, so a neutral night splint, workstation changes and planned review suit mild to moderate symptoms without muscle wasting. Splinting around the clock with no planned review restricts work and misses progression. An injection conflicts with her wish to avoid medication and is not a first step, and daytime splinting with strengthening does not protect the nerve overnight.
  3. The correct answer is C (Pause treatment, recheck blood pressure and arrange urgent same-day medical or obstetric assessment): A new headache, facial puffiness and a raised blood pressure in late pregnancy are red flags for pre-eclampsia, which needs same-day medical review whatever the hand findings. Treatment is paused because the priority has changed. Waiting for the next antenatal visit or offering reassurance delays assessment of a condition that can deteriorate quickly.
  4. The correct answer is D (Wear the splint overnight, vary tasks, and report new weakness or constant numbness promptly): Safe self-management combines the night splint, varying repetitive tasks and early reporting of warning signs such as new weakness or constant numbness, which can signal progressing nerve compression. Keeping tasks unchanged and waiting for pain misses those signs. Stopping work and waiting until after the birth removes the monitoring she needs, and firm hourly stretching can irritate the nerve.
Case CHB187

Mohammed Hassan, 8, Duchenne muscular dystrophy diagnosed at age four, on long-term corticosteroids under a paediatric neurologist. Attends mainstream primary school and is seen at the community health centre with his mother. Gowers' sign positive, calf pseudohypertrophy, waddling gait with increased lumbar lordosis. Ankle dorsiflexion 0 degrees with the knee extended on both sides, hip flexor tightness. Six minute walk distance 340 m, down from 390 m six months ago. He tires after school, has leg pain the day after long play, and wants to play football at lunch. His mother pays privately for weekly physiotherapy, has a GP care plan, and has no current NDIS plan after a first application was declined. She asks what funding exists for therapy, night splints and a wheelchair for long distances.

  1. The correct answer is A (Gluteal weakness (hip extensors and abductors), with lordosis shifting body weight behind the hips): In Duchenne muscular dystrophy weakness begins proximally, and weak hip abductors allow the pelvis to drop so the trunk sways side to side, while weak hip extensors are compensated by leaning back into lordosis. Distal strength is preserved, so dorsiflexor weakness does not explain the pattern. Quadriceps weakness affects the knee, and tightness alone does not produce a waddle.
  2. The correct answer is D (Daily gentle calf stretching at home, with night resting ankle-foot orthoses reviewed regularly): Regular gentle stretching, ideally daily with a parent's help, plus night orthoses is the standard way to slow contracture in Duchenne muscular dystrophy. Forceful overpressure and heavy resistance work risk damaging weakened muscle fibres. Serial casting is a more invasive step that is not the first approach for a child who is still walking.
  3. The correct answer is A (Encourage self-paced play and swimming with rest breaks, and reduce activity causing next-day pain): Submaximal, self-paced activity maintains function and participation, and prolonged pain or fatigue the next day signals that the load was too high. Total rest causes avoidable deconditioning, and high-intensity or eccentric work such as jumping can damage dystrophic muscle. Banning weight-bearing sport removes valued peer activity without evidence of benefit.
  4. The correct answer is B (Support a new NDIS request with a functional report, and discuss GP plan rebates): The child with a permanent, progressive condition may qualify for the NDIS, and a first decision can be revisited with a new request backed by a functional report, while the GP plan can provide Medicare rebates for allied health visits. The declined application can be reviewed, so rebates alone are not the whole answer. Deferring the NDIS loses the main source of funding, and pausing therapy while waiting would let his function decline.
Case CHB188

Divya Desai, 58, accountant, attends the community health centre with three days of brief spinning vertigo when rolling over in bed or looking up to hang washing. Each episode lasts under a minute, with mild nausea. She has no hearing loss, tinnitus, headache, neck pain, double vision, speech change or limb weakness, and no recent head injury. Mild hypertension, controlled. BP 128/78, HR 72. Neurological screen normal, eye movements smooth, no spontaneous nystagmus. Right Dix-Hallpike reproduces her vertigo with nystagmus; left Dix-Hallpike and supine roll test are negative. She drives to work and is anxious about falling. An allied health assistant, Jordan, works in the team and is rostered at her follow-up.

  1. The correct answer is C (Right posterior semicircular canal benign paroxysmal positional vertigo, from free-floating canal debris): Short latency, up-beating torsional nystagmus on the right Dix-Hallpike that fatigues on repeat fits posterior canal canalithiasis on that side. A horizontal canal problem would show on the supine roll test, and an anterior canal problem gives down-beating nystagmus. Vestibular neuritis causes continuous vertigo, not brief positional episodes.
  2. The correct answer is C (Epley manoeuvre, beginning with the head turned 45 degrees toward the right ear): The Epley canalith repositioning manoeuvre starts with the head turned toward the affected ear, which is the right side here, to move debris out of the posterior canal. Starting toward the left would reposition the wrong way. Habituation exercises are less effective than a manoeuvre, and the log roll treats the horizontal canal.
  3. The correct answer is A (Keep it with the physiotherapist, since re-assessment and selecting the manoeuvre require clinical reasoning): Repeating the diagnostic test, deciding which canal is involved and choosing or modifying the manoeuvre are clinical judgments that cannot be delegated to an assistant. Competence in the technique or patient consent does not change that. Jordan can support with agreed tasks, such as reinforcing a written exercise programme, under direction.
  4. The correct answer is A (Suspect stroke or transient ischaemic attack and arrange emergency department assessment by ambulance): Vertigo that does not change with position, together with double vision and slurred speech, are brainstem warning signs even if they have settled, and need emergency assessment by ambulance. Positional vertigo does not cause diplopia or dysarthria. Repeating tests or doing another manoeuvre delays time-critical care, and a GP visit later in the week is too slow for a possible transient ischaemic attack.
Case CHB189

Luca Esposito, 74, has moderate COPD and attends a pulmonary rehabilitation programme at a community health centre. He is an ex-smoker, lives with his wife and takes a long-acting inhaled bronchodilator. He is starting week 1 of an eight-week programme.

Initial assessment today: resting HR 82, BP 134/80, SpO2 94 percent on room air, modified Borg dyspnoea 1 out of 10. Spirometry on file shows a post-bronchodilator FEV1 well below predicted with an obstructive pattern. A six minute walk test is planned in the corridor.

Your afternoon is busy: this baseline test, a group exercise class supervised with an allied health assistant, and a new referral waiting for a phone triage. Luca's wife is waiting in the reception area.

  1. The correct answer is B (Call Mrs Bianchi promptly, assess her safety by phone and advise same day medical review): New green sputum, fever and worse breathlessness suggest an infective exacerbation that can worsen quickly, so the time-critical contact comes before the routine test or class. Assessing her by phone and directing her to a doctor the same day is within scope. Finishing the test first delays the call, and an assistant should not assess or advise on an unwell patient. A message to self-refer leaves her safety unchecked.
  2. The correct answer is C (Use standardised instructions, the same corridor and the same encouragement phrases at each test): The six minute walk test is sensitive to how it is delivered, so the course, instructions and encouragement must be standardised to allow a valid comparison over time. The walk is self-paced by design, but the conditions around it must stay constant. Free encouragement, individual protocols or a treadmill change the conditions and make the later result incomparable.
  3. The correct answer is C (Stop the test, seat him, monitor vital signs and arrange medical review before further exertion): Chest tightness with pallor and sweating are accepted reasons to stop an exercise test, and the cause needs medical exclusion of cardiac ischaemia before any more exertion. Seating him and monitoring keeps him safe while help is arranged. Continuing, pausing and resuming, or giving oxygen without a prescription all keep him exercising while a possible cardiac event is unassessed.
  4. The correct answer is A (Report the exertional desaturation to his doctor for oxygen assessment, pacing exercise by symptoms meanwhile): Exertional desaturation in COPD can be significant enough that a medical review of the need for ambulatory oxygen is warranted, and the physiotherapist's role is to report objective findings while continuing symptom limited training. Stopping all exercise denies him the benefit of rehabilitation, and ignoring the finding misses a treatable problem. Authorising oxygen is a medical decision and outside physiotherapy scope.
Case CHB190

Mitchell Quinn, 47, is a ceiling plasterer who identifies as Aboriginal. He reports four months of gradually worsening right shoulder pain with overhead work, which now wakes him when he rolls onto that side. There was no trauma, no neck pain and no pins and needles. He is otherwise well and takes no regular medication. Examination: painful arc between about 70 and 120 degrees of abduction, full and symmetrical passive range, painful resisted external rotation and abduction with strength graded 4 out of 5, positive Hawkins-Kennedy test, negative drop arm sign, normal neurological screen and cervical clearing. He has not had imaging. He brings a printout from a clinic advertising a new injection therapy that cites a single small trial with no comparison group, and asks whether he should have it instead of exercises.

  1. The correct answer is D (Rotator cuff related shoulder pain consistent with tendinopathy, without findings that suggest a large full-thickness tear): A painful arc, pain on resisted cuff testing and full passive range fit rotator cuff related pain. A negative drop arm sign and mild weakness argue against a large tear, and full passive range with a normal neck screen argues against capsulitis or radiculopathy. Night pain is common in tendinopathy and does not by itself indicate a tear.
  2. The correct answer is A (Education on load management, then a progressive graded loading programme for the cuff and scapular muscles): Guidelines favour education, load management and progressive exercise as first-line care for cuff tendinopathy without red flags. Slinging and prolonged rest cause deconditioning, and routine imaging does not change initial management. Stretching and friction do not address the tendon's capacity to tolerate load.
  3. The correct answer is D (Pause progression and arrange imaging with medical or surgical review, since strength has declined despite rehabilitation): Declining strength and failure to elevate with full passive range after adequate rehabilitation suggest a cuff tear or other structural lesion that needs imaging and specialist opinion. Loading harder or waiting longer delays that review. Manual therapy cannot restore strength lost to a structural cause.
  4. The correct answer is A (Explain that an uncontrolled trial cannot separate benefit from natural recovery, so stronger evidence is needed): Without a control group, improvement could reflect natural history, placebo or regression to the mean, so the trial cannot show that the treatment works. Physiotherapists are expected to appraise evidence and help patients make informed decisions, so deferring to the seller fails that duty. Participant improvement in an uncontrolled trial is weak support, and a larger trial without a comparison group would have the same flaw.
Case CHB191

Neil Fletcher, 38, a Wiradjuri man, works as a self-employed carpenter. Three weeks of central low back pain after lifting sheet material on site, referring to the left buttock but not below the knee. Pain 6/10 with bending, 2/10 at rest, eased by walking. No night pain, weight loss, fever, bladder or bowel change or saddle numbness. Medication: paracetamol as needed. He has not seen a physiotherapist before and says he is wary of clinics. He attends with his aunt, who says the family usually discusses health decisions together. He is worried about losing income. Examination: lumbar flexion to mid-shin with end-range pain, extension full, left side-flexion mildly painful, paraspinal spasm L3 to L5, neurological examination normal, straight leg raise negative, hip range normal. Oswestry Disability Index 32 percent.

  1. The correct answer is D (Non-specific mechanical low back pain with somatic referral, suitable for active physiotherapy management): Pain that follows lifting, stays above the knee and comes with normal neurology and no warning features fits non-specific mechanical back pain with somatic referral. Radiculopathy needs a dermatomal or myotomal deficit or tension sign, which he lacks. Inflammatory features such as morning stiffness easing with activity and night pain are absent, and the diagnosis of instability is not supported by his findings.
  2. The correct answer is B (Reassurance, advice to stay active with modified duties, and graded exercise and movement confidence work): For non-specific back pain without red flags, evidence supports reassurance, staying active, graded exercise and addressing fear of movement. Bed rest delays recovery and braces encourage protective behaviour. Imaging does not change management without warning features, and avoiding flexion reinforces his belief that bending is dangerous.
  3. The correct answer is D (Arrange urgent transfer to an emergency department and explain the concern about cauda equina compression): Saddle sensory change with new urinary hesitancy are red flags for cauda equina syndrome, which needs emergency assessment and imaging the same day. The GP review, even an urgent one, or an outpatient scan adds delay and risks permanent bladder, bowel and sexual dysfunction. Watching at home over two days also postpones decompression, and the improving back pain does not lessen the concern.
  4. The correct answer is C (Ask Neil how he wants family involved, listen without rushing, and explain findings in plain language): Cultural safety means the patient decides how involved family and community are, and the clinician builds trust through respectful listening and clear communication. Excluding the aunt or speaking only to her both take that choice away from Neil. Treating everyone identically ignores his individual preferences and his wariness of clinics.
Case CHB192

Mr Giorgos Papadakis, 81, lives with his wife in a single-storey home. He was diagnosed with Alzheimer's disease two years ago and has fallen three times in four months, each time walking to the toilet at night. He has hypertension and mild osteoarthritis of both knees, and his GP recently changed his antihypertensive. Findings: Timed Up and Go 18 seconds using a four-wheeled walker that he will not use at home. Lying BP 138/80 mmHg, standing BP at three minutes 118/70 mmHg, no dizziness reported. He follows one-step commands but forgets instructions within minutes. Hip abduction 4/5 and ankle dorsiflexion 4/5 bilaterally. His wife is exhausted and anxious. The physiotherapist plans a home programme and wants an allied health assistant (AHA) from the team to help deliver sessions twice weekly.

  1. The correct answer is C (A significant postural drop despite no symptoms; inform the GP and teach slow position changes): A fall of 20 mmHg systolic on standing meets the usual criteria for orthostatic hypotension, and many older people with it report no dizziness. It also fits the timing of his falls and the recent medication change, so the GP needs to know. Dismissing it because he is symptom free would miss a modifiable falls risk.
  2. The correct answer is D (Modify the environment with sensor lighting, a clear path and a walker placed within reach): With moderate memory loss, strategies that depend on him recalling instructions at night are unlikely to work, so changing the environment is the most reliable choice. Lighting, a clear route and a visible walker act without new learning. A bed alarm adds to his wife's burden and does not prevent the fall itself.
  3. The correct answer is D (Short repeated functional tasks tied to daily routines, with simple cues and prompting from his wife): People with dementia usually retain procedural learning better than explicit recall, so repeated, simple, functional practice embedded in routine works best. Frequent novelty, written material and self-monitoring all rely on memory and insight that he is losing. Carer prompting makes the practice happen between visits.
  4. The correct answer is B (Give an individualised written plan with clear limits, plus supervision and review of his response): The physiotherapist remains accountable for assessment, planning and outcomes, so delegation needs a tailored plan, defined parameters and ongoing supervision with review. Generic sheets, report-only-on-falls arrangements and handing over responsibility all weaken oversight. His cognitive decline and falls history make close monitoring especially important.
Case CHB193

Thuy Le, 12, is seen in the hospital outpatient physiotherapy clinic with her mother, referred by her GP after a school screening found an uneven back. She is premenarchal and grew about 6 cm over the past year. The orthopaedic clinic report describes a right thoracic curve with a Cobb angle of 28 degrees and an immature skeleton on X-ray. On forward bending there is a right rib prominence measuring 9 degrees on a scoliometer. Leg lengths are equal. Lower limb neurological screening is normal, with symmetrical reflexes, and she has no pain. The orthopaedic surgeon has prescribed a thoracolumbar brace, to be fitted by the orthotist. Thuy is anxious about wearing it at school. Her mother asks whether exercise could replace the brace and whether you can decide when she may stop wearing it.

  1. The correct answer is C (Vertebral rotation that persists on bending, indicating a structural rather than postural curve): A rib prominence that persists on forward bending reflects vertebral rotation, the hallmark of a structural scoliosis. A postural curve corrects when the trunk flexes. Muscle tightness does not produce fixed rotation, and rotation alone does not imply neurological risk.
  2. The correct answer is B (Check skin daily, add a seamless cotton layer, and tell the orthotist if redness lingers): Transient redness that fades after removal is an expected pressure response, so monitoring the skin, using a seamless cotton layer and involving the orthotist if it persists is appropriate. Stopping the brace for days reduces its effect on a curve in a growing child. Adding padding alters the fit that the orthotist set and may shift pressure elsewhere.
  3. The correct answer is C (Pause exercise and escalate the same day to the orthopaedic team for urgent neurological review): Night pain with new motor weakness in a child with scoliosis is a red flag for a neural axis problem such as a spinal cord tumour or syrinx, which an idiopathic curve does not explain. This needs same day escalation rather than waiting. Routine GP review or watchful waiting delays diagnosis, and treating it as a muscle strain misses a possibly serious cause.
  4. The correct answer is A (Acknowledge her concerns, explain the orthopaedic team decides on bracing, and relay them promptly): The surgeon who prescribed the brace is responsible for changing the management plan, so the physiotherapist should respect Thuy's feelings, explain who decides and make sure her concerns reach that team quickly. Evidence for exercise-only care does not allow a physiotherapist to override a prescription. The orthotist fits and adjusts braces but does not decide to discontinue treatment.
Case CHB194

Lakshmi Pillai, 68, a retired school librarian, had paralytic polio in her right leg at age 3 in India and lives with her husband in a two-storey home. Over 18 months she reports increasing fatigue, new aching and weakness in the right leg and in the left calf, and muscle twitching. She walks with a right knee-ankle-foot orthosis and a stick. Right quadriceps 3/5, left quadriceps 4+/5 (5/5 in old records). Fatigue builds through the day, rated 7/10, and is worse after shopping. She sleeps poorly and reports breathlessness lying flat and morning headaches. Resting SpO2 is 96 percent, RR 16, chest clear on auscultation. Six minute walk: 190 metres with two rests, needing 20 minutes to recover. She has stopped gardening and has no home supports. She is not on a compensable scheme and has not used the NDIS. Her GP referred her for exercise advice, and she asks how she can afford ongoing physiotherapy and a home assessment.

  1. The correct answer is B (Refer her promptly to the GP for sleep and ventilation assessment of possible respiratory weakness): Breathlessness lying flat, poor sleep and morning headaches suggest nocturnal hypoventilation from respiratory muscle weakness, which is recognised in post-polio syndrome. A normal daytime saturation does not exclude it, so prompt medical referral is needed. High-load breathing training or more aerobic exercise could add fatigue and delay the diagnosis.
  2. The correct answer is C (Low-intensity submaximal exercise with planned rest breaks, stopped before fatigue or next-day symptoms): Overuse can damage already stressed motor units in post-polio syndrome, so exercise should be submaximal, include rest breaks, and stop before fatigue or symptoms the next day. High-intensity resistance or pushing through fatigue risks further weakness. Stopping exercise altogether leads to deconditioning, which adds to her limits.
  3. The correct answer is A (See the GP about a care plan for rebated visits, then contact My Aged Care): At 68 she is past the age for new NDIS access, so a My Aged Care assessment is the route to subsidised home supports and a home assessment. A GP management plan for a chronic condition gives access to Medicare rebated allied health visits. A childhood onset does not override the age rule, relying on her husband leaves out available services, and paying privately ignores subsidies she can use now.
  4. The correct answer is C (Pace tasks with planned rests, and use aids to reduce effort on shopping trips): Pacing tasks with planned rests and using aids lowers the energy cost of daily activity and protects overused muscle in post-polio syndrome. Packing activity into one block or building stamina by pushing on increases fatigue and next-day symptoms. A brisk daily walk progressed weekly raises load too fast for weakened motor units.
Case CHB195

Enzo Bruno is a 76-year-old retired baker with peripheral arterial disease, type 2 diabetes and hypertension. He stopped smoking six months ago. He reports cramping pain in the left calf after about 150 m of flat walking, easing within three minutes of standing still. Ankle brachial index is 0.62 on the left and 0.85 on the right. Left foot pulses are not palpable, the skin is shiny and hairless, and there are no ulcers or rest pain. Resting BP 138/82 mmHg, HR 72, SpO2 97%. His GP letter notes mild memory difficulties. His daughter, who attends, urges him to join a supervised exercise programme, but Enzo says he is unsure he wants to do it and repeats that walking has never hurt him before.

  1. The correct answer is C (Walk to moderate calf pain, rest until it eases, then repeat, gradually building total session time): Claudication training works best when walking reaches moderate calf pain, followed by rest until relief, repeated to accumulate time on his feet. Staying pain-free gives too little stimulus for adaptation, while pushing through severe pain is unnecessary and discouraging. Walking is the preferred mode, and a low ankle brachial index alone is not a contraindication when there is no rest pain or tissue loss.
  2. The correct answer is B (Pause walking and arrange same-day vascular or medical review for possible critical limb ischaemia): Night pain relieved by hanging the leg down, a cool pale foot and a dark patch on the toe suggest chronic limb-threatening ischaemia with tissue loss, which needs same-day vascular assessment and exercise must stop. A podiatry referral next week or a GP review next week delays limb salvage. Reduced walking still stresses the limb, and waiting for the toe to worsen lets tissue loss progress.
  3. The correct answer is D (Inspect both feet daily, wear well-fitting supportive shoes with socks, and report any skin break promptly): Diabetes with reduced sensation and poor perfusion makes small injuries dangerous, so daily foot inspection, well-fitting footwear and prompt reporting of skin breaks form the core advice. Barefoot walking exposes the foot to unnoticed injury, hot soaks can burn skin that senses poorly, and compression stockings can further limit arterial flow.
  4. The correct answer is D (Presume capacity, explain simply with teach-back and written notes, and seek his own decision): Capacity is presumed and is specific to each decision, so the physiotherapist should adapt communication with plain language, teach-back and written notes, and seek his own decision, involving his daughter only with his agreement. Family cannot consent for an adult who may have capacity, and mild memory difficulty alone does not justify formal testing first. Enrolling him on her advice bypasses his autonomy.

Practice Test 14 Answer Key

Case CHB196

Mr Marco Conti, 82, a widower, was admitted with breathlessness and a large left pleural effusion due to heart failure. An ultrasound guided intercostal catheter drained 1.2 L two days ago and was removed this morning. History: heart failure, atrial fibrillation, hypertension. He uses a walking stick. Today: RR 18, HR 82 and irregular, BP 134/80, SpO2 95% on room air. A chest X-ray shows a small residual effusion. He walks 40 metres with his stick, needs one rest and rates breathlessness Borg 3. He lives alone in a two-storey house and his daughter lives interstate. He holds a Medicare card and a pension, has no private health insurance, and asks how he will pay for physiotherapy and help at home after discharge.

  1. The correct answer is C (Stony dull percussion, reduced or absent breath sounds and reduced chest expansion at the left base): Fluid in the pleural space damps sound and breath transmission and limits expansion on that side, giving stony dullness, quiet or absent breath sounds and reduced movement. Hyper-resonance describes a pneumothorax. Bronchial breathing with increased vocal resonance fits consolidation, and crackles with resonance suggest secretions in an otherwise air-filled lung.
  2. The correct answer is B (Short repeated walks with seated rests, guided by Borg 3 to 4 and regular saturation checks): In atrial fibrillation a heart rate target is unreliable, so intensity is set by perceived exertion. Short intervals with rests suit his tolerance and let him build up safely while saturation is checked. Waiting in bed for imaging clearance causes deconditioning, and a hard continuous effort exceeds what is appropriate for a frail man with heart failure.
  3. The correct answer is D (Withhold exercise, sit him upright, monitor observations and inform the medical team promptly): A new fall in saturation, a faster respiratory rate and a return of stony dullness with absent breath sounds suggest the effusion is re-accumulating, which needs medical review rather than physiotherapy techniques. Exercise should be paused and he should be supported while the team is told. Airway clearance targets secretions, which this picture does not show, and deferring review risks further deterioration.
  4. The correct answer is C (Refer him for My Aged Care assessment and ask his GP about chronic disease management items): Older Australians access government-funded home support through a My Aged Care assessment, and his GP can advise whether chronic disease management arrangements apply for subsidised allied health visits. The NDIS is for people who first apply before the age of 65. Medicare rebates are limited and need a GP-initiated plan, and funded community services do exist after discharge.
Case CHB197

Mai Tran, 10 weeks old, first child of Hoa and Minh, was born at term after a prolonged second stage and an assisted delivery. She is seen at the community child health centre after referral by the maternal and child health nurse for a head tilt. Feeding and weight gain are normal; she prefers feeding on the left. At rest her head tilts to the right with the chin turned left. Passive rotation to the right is about 20 degrees short of the left, and passive tilt to the left is also limited. A small firm lump is palpable in the right neck. The left back of her head is flat. Hips are symmetrical. She sleeps on her back and tolerates tummy time poorly. The family speaks Vietnamese at home and a telephone interpreter was used. Mai's aunt lives nearby and often asks about her.

  1. The correct answer is C (Congenital muscular torticollis from a shortened right sternocleidomastoid muscle limiting right rotation): A tilt to one side with rotation to the other, a palpable mass and restricted passive range fit a tight sternocleidomastoid. Positional preference would show normal passive range. Bony or ocular causes do not produce a palpable muscle mass with restricted passive rotation.
  2. The correct answer is D (Continue safe supine sleeping, add brief frequent tummy time, and encourage looking to her right): Safe supine sleeping stays unchanged, while short frequent supervised tummy time and prompting her to turn to the right lengthen the tight muscle and offload the flat area. Turning to the left shortens the tight right side further. A reclined bouncer keeps pressure on the flattened area, and a helmet is not a first step at this age.
  3. The correct answer is C (Send her to the emergency department promptly, as these signs may indicate raised intracranial pressure): A full, tense fontanelle with vomiting, irritability and poor feeding in an infant raises concern for raised intracranial pressure, which needs emergency medical assessment. Stretching, a routine GP visit or orthopaedic imaging would delay urgent care. The physiotherapist should escalate immediately and document the findings.
  4. The correct answer is A (Decline to share details without the parents' consent, and offer to pass on her message): Health information about a child is held for the parents as guardians, so a relative needs their consent before receiving details. Confirming identity or family relationship does not replace consent, and passing her to the manager does not change this. Offering to pass on her message is courteous, and the call should be noted in the record.
Case CHB198

Hoang Van Duc, 68, retired factory worker, born in Vietnam, had paralytic polio in the right leg at age three. He walked without aids for decades. Over two years he has noticed increasing fatigue, aching in both thighs and new weakness in the right leg; he now struggles with stairs and has had two near falls. Seen at a community health service with a telephone interpreter. Right hip flexion 3/5, knee extension 3+/5, left leg 4/5; right calf wasted and shorter; no sensory loss; reflexes reduced in the right leg; no upper motor neuron signs. Swallowing and breathing are normal. His GP has excluded thyroid disease and anaemia. He lives with his daughter, who works in a bakery. He offers the physiotherapist a bag of fruit and says the service waiting list is long.

  1. The correct answer is A (Slow new weakness and fatigue in muscles affected decades ago, after stable function): Post-polio syndrome appears decades after the original illness as slow, new weakness, fatigue and pain in muscles already affected. Sudden onset suggests stroke, fluctuating eyelid droop suggests a neuromuscular junction disorder, and spasticity with brisk reflexes points to an upper motor neuron condition.
  2. The correct answer is D (Low-intensity exercise with rest breaks, activity pacing and energy conservation strategies at home): Overuse of already stressed motor units can worsen weakness in post-polio syndrome, so exercise should be submaximal, paced and stopped short of fatigue. Strict rest causes deconditioning, and intense training risks lasting loss of strength.
  3. The correct answer is A (Keep the professional interpreter, use short sentences with teach-back, and let the daughter support him): A professional interpreter protects accuracy and confidentiality, while teach-back checks real understanding rather than polite agreement. Family members may omit or alter content, and English handouts do not meet his language needs. The daughter can still be present as support.
  4. The correct answer is C (Politely decline private arrangements, explain service processes, and ask your supervisor about earlier review options): Private payment from a person on your own service's waiting list creates a conflict of interest and breaks professional boundaries, and documenting it does not fix that. His concern about delay is legitimate, so the duty of care is met by raising earlier review through the service, not by closing the conversation.
Case CHB199

Bruce Walker, 72, a retired cattle station manager, attends a remote community clinic hundreds of kilometres from the nearest hospital. He has had type 2 diabetes for 18 years, with a recent HbA1c of 9.1 percent, and hypertension. He reports numbness and burning in both feet, worse at night, and two near falls on uneven ground. Findings: 10 g monofilament sensation absent at 5 of 10 sites on each foot; vibration sense absent at the great toes; ankle reflexes absent; Berg Balance Scale 41/56; callus under both first metatarsal heads; skin intact; pedal pulses palpable. He wears worn thongs around the yard. The podiatrist visits every six weeks and the clinic has telehealth. He has seen an online advertisement claiming a magnetic insole and pain device cure neuropathy, citing customer testimonials, and asks whether he should buy them.

  1. The correct answer is A (Absent response to the 10 g monofilament at multiple sites on each foot): Inability to feel a 10 g monofilament at multiple sites is the standard clinical sign of loss of protective sensation and ulcer risk. Absent reflexes and vibration support neuropathy but do not directly show that he cannot feel pressure or injury. Calluses reflect abnormal loading and are a consequence, not a sensory test.
  2. The correct answer is A (Explain testimonials are weak evidence, review trial findings together, and keep proven care first): Testimonials are anecdotal and cannot show that a product works, so the best response teaches him to weigh the quality of evidence, looks at trial findings with him and keeps established care for glucose and feet as the priority. Dismissing him damages trust, and endorsing a product on the strength of a placebo response or a company registration does not meet evidence based practice.
  3. The correct answer is C (Progressive standing balance and leg strengthening tasks beside the rail, in supportive shoes, done regularly): Progressive balance and strengthening practice with a rail close by allows challenge with a safe barrier against falls, and supportive footwear protects feet that cannot sense injury. Barefoot uneven walking risks unnoticed wounds, seated only exercise gives no balance challenge, and unsupported advanced tasks raise falls risk for a man with loss of protective sensation.
  4. The correct answer is B (Wear closed supportive shoes, inspect both feet daily, and arrange podiatry for the calluses): With absent protective sensation, daily visual inspection, closed supportive footwear with socks and professional callus care reduce the chance of unnoticed ulcers. Home pumice use can injure insensate skin, going barefoot or wearing thongs exposes the feet, and omitting socks increases friction. Loss of sensation means pain cannot be relied on to prompt a check.
Case CHB200

Tomasz Kowalski, 34, a plumber, is in subacute rehabilitation three weeks after right ACL reconstruction (hamstring autograft) and a mild traumatic brain injury from a motorbike fall. Medication: paracetamol. Knee: trace effusion, flexion 95 degrees, passive extension to 0, active extension lag of 12 degrees, poor quadriceps contraction. Wound healed, calf soft and non-tender, resting pain 3 out of 10, HR 72, BP 118/76. He walks with two crutches, weight-bearing as tolerated per the surgeon's protocol. His job requires kneeling and carrying loads. Nursing notes record that he is oriented but forgets conversations within minutes. His wife, Anna, visits daily. You plan to add neuromuscular electrical stimulation (NMES) to his quadriceps.

  1. The correct answer is B (Restore quadriceps activation using NMES or biofeedback with low-load exercises, keeping full passive extension): An extension lag with poor quadriceps contraction points to inhibition after surgery, so activation work with NMES or biofeedback and low-load exercise is the priority while maintaining full extension. Resisted open chain extension through full range stresses the graft too early. Jogging is far beyond his current control, and early hamstring strengthening loads the harvest site.
  2. The correct answer is A (Assess his capacity for this decision with supported communication, and involve a substitute decision-maker if lacking): Consent must be informed and ongoing, and capacity is decision-specific, so it should be assessed for this treatment using simple, repeated explanations. Only if he lacks capacity does a legally recognised substitute decision-maker decide, according to state or territory law, and a spouse is not automatically that person. A brain injury alone does not remove capacity, and earlier agreement he cannot recall is not valid consent.
  3. The correct answer is C (Explain that running starts when objective strength, control and effusion criteria are met, not by date): Return to running after ACL reconstruction is best guided by objective criteria such as strength, control and absence of effusion, rather than time alone. Time since surgery matters but does not show he is ready for impact. A rigid twelve month delay is unnecessarily conservative, and surgeon clearance does not replace functional testing.
  4. The correct answer is B (Withhold exercise and stimulation and urgently notify the medical team to assess for deep vein thrombosis): A swollen, warm, tender calf after surgery and reduced mobility suggests possible deep vein thrombosis, which needs urgent medical assessment and imaging. Massage or vigorous activity could dislodge a clot. Homans sign is unreliable and a negative result cannot exclude thrombosis.
Case CHB201

Mosese Pouono, 15, is a Year 10 student and rugby league player who attends with his mother. He has had three months of right sided headaches that start at the base of the skull and spread behind the eye, three to four times a week, lasting hours, worse with long laptop use and neck movement. Pain is 6/10. No aura, nausea, visual change, fever, night waking, or head injury in the last year. The school counsellor has noted exam stress. Examination: forward head posture, restricted right rotation on the flexion rotation test, his headache reproduced by right C1 to C2 passive accessory pressure, reduced deep neck flexor endurance, normal neurological screen and normal vital signs. His GP referred him for physiotherapy and asked to be kept informed. His mother asks whether he can still play rugby.

  1. The correct answer is B (Reproduction of his headache with C1 to C2 pressure and a restricted right flexion rotation test): Cervicogenic headache is supported when the usual headache is reproduced by upper cervical segment testing and the flexion rotation test is restricted. Nausea and light sensitivity favour migraine. Posture and reduced neck flexor endurance are common in many headache types and in people without headache, so they are not specific.
  2. The correct answer is D (Upper cervical mobilisation with deep neck flexor training, ergonomic changes and a graded return to activity): Evidence supports combining manual therapy of the upper cervical segments with neck flexor and scapular exercise and advice on posture and workstation set up, keeping him active. Thrust manipulation alone is a higher-risk option with less support in adolescents. Passive-only care and prolonged rest do not address the underlying muscle endurance deficit.
  3. The correct answer is C (Withhold treatment, keep him under observation, and arrange emergency medical assessment for suspected head injury): A worsening headache with repeated vomiting and increasing drowsiness after a head impact are red flags for serious intracranial injury, so he needs emergency assessment and observation. A GP visit or home observation is too slow and unsupervised for these signs. Unchanged neck findings do not make the headache cervicogenic, and waiting until tomorrow could miss a bleed.
  4. The correct answer is C (Findings, treatment and response, outcome measures, and recommendations for ongoing care or medical review): A useful handover is relevant, accurate and within consent: what was found, what was done, how he responded, measured outcomes and clear recommendations. Passing on third-party counsellor information goes beyond the consent given. A one-line note gives the GP no basis for follow up, and advising on medication falls outside physiotherapy scope.
Case CHB202

Mr Dimitri Kostas, 68, retired builder, presented to the emergency department after falling from a ladder at home 6 hours ago. Imaging confirms right rib fractures 5 to 8 with no pneumothorax or haemothorax. History: mild COPD, ex-smoker, lives with his wife. Analgesia was charted by the medical team and a dose was given 40 minutes ago. On assessment he sits upright with RR 22, SpO2 94 percent on room air, HR 88, BP 140/85. Pain is 8/10 on deep inspiration and 4/10 at rest. Breathing is shallow with splinting of the right chest, and his cough is weak and suppressed. Auscultation shows reduced air entry at the right base, with no added sounds. He is keen to go home today. As you finish, he thanks you, offers you cash to visit his home privately next week, and asks to add you on social media.

  1. The correct answer is B (Chest infection from retained secretions and basal atelectasis, driven by pain limited shallow breathing): Pain causes shallow breathing and a suppressed cough, so secretions pool at the base and the lung under-inflates, which is the classic route to atelectasis and chest infection, and COPD raises this risk. Delayed pneumothorax and flail chest are possible with multiple rib fractures but are not suggested by his current findings, which show no pneumothorax on imaging and no paradoxical chest movement.
  2. The correct answer is D (Supported deep breathing in upright sitting, splinting the injured side with a towel during coughs): Timing treatment after analgesia and using upright positioning with manual or towel splinting lets him breathe deeply and cough effectively without loading the fractures. Percussion and vibration over fractured ribs are inappropriate. Supine positioning reduces basal expansion, and delaying breathing work leaves secretions unmanaged.
  3. The correct answer is D (Stop treatment, keep him sitting upright and urgently escalate to the medical team for review): Sudden breathlessness, falling saturation, tachycardia, falling blood pressure and a hyperresonant note suggest a pneumothorax that may be under tension, which is a medical emergency needing immediate review.
