Watching and waiting costs time he does not have, and lying flat worsens his breathing. Waiting for imaging before escalating delays decompression, which is the definitive treatment.
  4. The correct answer is B (Politely decline both, explain the formal follow up pathway, and document the conversation clearly): Accepting payment outside the service or blurring the relationship on social media compromises boundaries, so the offers are declined kindly while his follow up needs are still met through the formal pathway, and the exchange is recorded. Reporting him or abandoning his care is an overreaction that fails the duty of care.
Case CHB203

Lenny Coulthard, 68, an Aboriginal man, lives in a remote community and attends the health clinic where you work on a fly-in fly-out basis. He has type 2 diabetes and smokes. For six months he has had cramping in his left calf after walking about 150 m, relieved by standing still for two to three minutes. An Aboriginal health practitioner measured his ankle brachial index: right 0.9, left 0.6. Left foot pulses are weak and the skin is cool and shiny, with no ulcers, no tissue loss and no rest pain. BP 142/84, HR 76. English is his third language; the clinic has access to a telephone interpreter and an Aboriginal health worker. You offer a walking programme. During the explanation Lenny nods and says yes to everything, but he cannot say back what the programme involves. His family is in the waiting room.

  1. The correct answer is D (Use the interpreter, explain in small steps, check by teach back, and let him decide): Adults are presumed to have capacity, and an inability to repeat information most often reflects language or communication barriers rather than lack of capacity. The physiotherapist should remove those barriers with an interpreter and plain steps, confirm understanding, and let Lenny decide himself. A relative cannot consent for a competent adult, and polite agreement is not informed consent.
  2. The correct answer is C (Intermittent claudication from arterial disease in the left leg, suited to structured walking): Reproducible calf pain on walking that settles within minutes of standing still, with a reduced ankle brachial index and weak pulses, fits claudication from peripheral arterial disease. Neurogenic claudication eases with sitting and flexion, and limb threatening ischaemia involves rest pain or tissue loss, which he does not have. Neuropathy does not produce pain reliably related to walking distance.
  3. The correct answer is D (Walk until calf pain is moderate, rest until it eases, then repeat across the session): Repeated walking to moderate claudication pain, with rest until it settles, is the basis of structured exercise therapy and improves walking distance over time. Stopping at the first discomfort gives too little stimulus, and pushing through the pain or doing one occasional long walk is less effective and less tolerated. The programme needs regular frequent sessions.
  4. The correct answer is B (Pause the walking programme and arrange urgent medical assessment for possible critical limb ischaemia): Pain at rest that wakes him and eases when the foot hangs down, with a cold pale foot, indicates critical limb ischaemia, which threatens the limb in a person with diabetes. He needs urgent medical assessment and the walking programme should stop. Shorter walks, a podiatry referral or seated exercises all leave an ischaemic limb without timely medical review.
Case CHB204

Karim Haddad, 38, a builder, had a right transtibial amputation 10 weeks ago after a crush injury at work (workers' compensation claim). He is an inpatient in a rehabilitation unit. Arabic is his first language and his English is limited. His wife usually interprets and often answers for him. Medical history: nil; he is reducing smoking. The residual limb wound is healed with mild distal oedema; knee extension lacks 10 degrees; hip extension strength is 3+/5 and hip flexion 4/5; single leg stance on the left is steady for 30 seconds. He has intermittent burning phantom limb pain, 5/10, mostly at night. He wears a shrinker sock that tends to roll down, and a preparatory prosthesis fitting is planned. He says yes to most questions and appears anxious. His goals are to walk again and return to work. Interpreter bookings are available through the hospital.

  1. The correct answer is D (Book an accredited Arabic interpreter, address Karim directly and use teach-back to confirm understanding): Informed consent for a significant treatment needs an accredited interpreter who is independent of the family, with the clinician speaking to the patient directly and confirming understanding in his own words. Family members may filter or change information. Translation applications and untrained staff are not reliable for consent, and handouts do not confirm comprehension.
  2. The correct answer is D (A correctly sized shrinker worn consistently, with daily skin checks and measures to stop rolling): A well fitting shrinker applies graded compression that reduces oedema and shapes the limb, and a roll at the top acts as a tourniquet, so fit and skin checks matter. Bandaging with the greatest pressure at the top restricts return flow and increases distal swelling. Resting the limb on a pillow under the knee encourages a knee flexion contracture, which he already shows.
  3. The correct answer is B (Education that phantom pain is common, with mirror therapy or graded motor imagery and desensitisation): Phantom limb pain is common after amputation and is understood as a central phenomenon, so explanation lowers anxiety and mirror therapy, motor imagery and desensitisation are physiotherapy options with supporting evidence. Telling him to ignore it dismisses a real symptom. Treating the scar does not address a centrally driven pain, and wearing the prosthesis is generally helpful rather than harmful.
  4. The correct answer is B (Pause prosthesis use, check the limb, and liaise with the prosthetist about socket fit before resuming): Redness that does not fade soon after removing the socket shows excessive pressure that could progress to a pressure injury, so loading is paused and the prosthetist, who alone alters the socket, is involved. Adding socks can change fit further and mask the problem. Shortening sessions or using cream treats the skin but not the cause, and modifying a socket is outside physiotherapy scope.
Case CHB205

Paolo Bruno, 68, retired bus driver, is on day 2 after an anterior STEMI treated with primary PCI and a stent to the left anterior descending artery through the right radial artery. Troponin has been falling since yesterday. Echocardiogram: ejection fraction 45 percent. Medications: aspirin, ticagrelor, metoprolol, ramipril and atorvastatin. He has no chest pain today. Resting HR 62 bpm in sinus rhythm on telemetry, BP 124/76 mmHg lying, SpO2 96 percent on room air, RR 16. The right wrist puncture site is clean with no haematoma. The cardiology registrar has written an order to mobilise as tolerated with telemetry. He has been in bed or the chair since admission and is anxious about straining his heart. You are the ward physiotherapist reviewing him at 10:00, and you will hand over to the afternoon physiotherapist and the nurse in charge.

  1. The correct answer is D (Explain the plan, then progress from sitting to standing to a short walk, watching symptoms): Early graded mobilisation with telemetry is appropriate after uncomplicated PCI, and a stepwise progression allows a first standing blood pressure and symptom check before walking. Explaining the plan also eases his fear of straining. Walking straight out risks missing a postural drop on new cardiac medications, and resisted arm work loads the recent radial puncture site.
  2. The correct answer is D (Metoprolol blunts heart rate response to exertion, so heart rate poorly reflects his true effort): Beta blockers such as metoprolol dampen the heart rate rise with exertion, so a heart rate target would understate how hard he is working. A rating of perceived exertion tracks effort independently of the drug. A low resting rate does not itself prevent a range being used, his mildly reduced ejection fraction does not make heart rate unreliable, and heart rate targets are not limited to outpatient settings.
  3. The correct answer is B (Stop and sit him down, lie him flat if symptoms persist, and recheck his observations): A fall of this size with light-headedness and pallor is symptomatic hypotension, and metoprolol stops the heart rate rising to compensate, so the rate looks falsely reassuring. Stopping, sitting or lying him and rechecking observations is the safe first step. Continuing to walk or standing him again risks syncope, and the team then needs to be told.
  4. The correct answer is C (Observations before and after the episode, distance walked, and a review request before further walking): A useful handover is specific and structured: the objective changes before and after the event, what he achieved, and a clear request that he be reviewed before further walking. Leaving the review decision to others, or reporting only that he recovered, means the afternoon team may repeat the same plan. A vague summary that he recovered quickly invites the same symptomatic hypotension again.
Case CHB206

Luca Santoro, 81, a retired baker, was admitted 2 days ago with a left middle cerebral artery infarct. He has right arm weakness (2/5), a right lower face droop, mild expressive aphasia and a wet voice after speaking. He is nil by mouth until a speech pathologist assesses his swallow tomorrow, and is being fed by nasogastric tube. Observations today: HR 78, BP 146/84, RR 18, SpO2 95 percent on room air, temperature 37.4 degrees C. Auscultation reveals scattered crackles at the right base, and he gives a weak, ineffective cough on request. He sits out of bed with the assistance of one person. His daughter Elena, visiting, asks you quietly to let him have a few hot chips from the cafe because he is miserable, and offers you a gift voucher to thank you for the extra attention.

  1. The correct answer is A (Sit him upright, reassess breathing and chest findings, and inform the medical team promptly): Wet voice, a weak cough, new basal crackles and a raised temperature suggest possible aspiration, so the priority is safe positioning, reassessment and prompt medical notification. Percussion in head-down positions risks further aspiration and is not appropriate after an acute stroke. Routine suction and continuing the mobility plan delay review of a possibly worsening chest.
  2. The correct answer is D (Decline both politely, explain the aspiration risk, and offer to pass her concerns on): Duty of care means protecting a patient on a nil by mouth order, and the swallow risk is real until the speech pathologist has assessed him. Gifts of value blur professional boundaries, so declining politely and explaining the reason keeps the family's trust. Offering to pass on her concerns gives her a legitimate route without breaching the restriction.
  3. The correct answer is C (Wet voice suggests secretions pooling near the larynx, and aspiration can occur without coughing): A wet or gurgly voice suggests secretions pooling at the laryngeal inlet, and stroke patients may aspirate silently without a cough, so a reflex cough on testing does not prove the airway is protected. That is why formal screening comes before any oral intake. Treating the sign as normal for age would miss a serious risk.
  4. The correct answer is D (Reposition him upright and symmetrical with supports, then tell nursing staff he is ready): Upright, symmetrical positioning supports safer swallowing and matches the speech pathologist's recommendation, which the physiotherapist can achieve with supports. Semi-reclined feeding raises aspiration risk even when the diet is modified. Leaving him slumped ignores the recommendation, and a long balance session would add fatigue just before eating.
Case CHB207

Vincenzo Rossi, 58, a bricklayer, was admitted two days ago with an anterior STEMI and had primary PCI with a stent to the left anterior descending artery via the right radial artery. Echocardiography shows an ejection fraction of 40 percent. He takes aspirin, ticagrelor, a beta blocker, an ACE inhibitor and a statin. Today he is pain free: HR 70 in sinus rhythm, BP 122/74 mmHg, SpO2 97 percent on room air, RR 16, chest clear. Troponin is falling and telemetry has shown no arrhythmia for 24 hours. He has smoked for 25 years, lives with his wife and hopes to return to heavy work. He walked to the bathroom yesterday with mild fatigue. You have been asked to begin inpatient cardiac rehabilitation education and exercise. The cardiologist plans transfer to the ward tomorrow, where another physiotherapist will continue his program.

  1. The correct answer is C (A rating of 11 to 13, fairly light to somewhat hard, while still able to talk): Early post-MI inpatient exercise is kept at a light to somewhat hard effort, around 11 to 13 on the Borg scale, with the patient able to talk. Higher ratings are for later outpatient phases and work-specific conditioning. Restricting him to a rating of 6 to 8 is overly cautious for a stable patient and delays recovery.
  2. The correct answer is B (His activity level, exercise response by RPE and symptoms, precautions, and planned progression): A useful physiotherapy handover states what he can currently do, how he responded in terms of effort, symptoms and observations, any precautions, and the plan for progression, so the next clinician continues safely without repeating or regressing. Copying the record duplicates other disciplines' handover. Restarting at day one and repeating all baseline tests wastes the gains made and slows recovery.
  3. The correct answer is B (Stop, sit him down, check observations, and call the nurse or doctor for urgent assessment): Chest tightness resembling his presenting pain in the early days after infarction may signal recurrent ischaemia, so exercise stops and medical review is sought urgently with observations taken. Continuing or restarting exercise exposes the myocardium to further demand. Treating the pain as musculoskeletal without ruling out a cardiac cause is unsafe at this stage.
  4. The correct answer is A (Use RPE and symptoms as the main guide, because the beta blocker blunts his heart rate): Beta blockers limit the heart rate rise with effort, so heart rate based targets underestimate his workload and RPE with symptoms becomes the reliable guide. Pushing to an age-predicted maximum risks overexertion. Withholding a prognostic medication for exercise is outside the physiotherapist's role and unsafe for a patient after infarction.
Case CHB208

Joyce Kennedy, 63, an Aboriginal woman from a regional town, is receiving palliative care at home. Her daughter Tanya is the main carer. Metastatic breast cancer with bone metastases. Recent imaging shows a lytic lesion in the left proximal femur with cortical erosion. After discussion with her family and oncologist she declined surgical fixation. Pain is 7/10 in the left groin and thigh when she stands or walks, and 3/10 at rest. She takes regular opioid analgesia. Observations are stable. She walks 5 metres with a frame, and her verandah is about 8 metres from her bed. Her goal is to sit outside with family. Several relatives usually attend visits, and Joyce says family are part of her decisions. The oncology team has advised protected weight bearing on the left.

  1. The correct answer is D (Pain that is provoked by loading and relieved by rest, in a lesion eroding the cortex): Pain that comes on with weight bearing and settles at rest points to mechanical failure of the bone, and cortical erosion removes much of its load-bearing strength, so together these predict fracture. Constant night pain and widespread deposits reflect disease burden but do not describe mechanical risk. Reduced range from weakness says nothing about structural weakening of the femur.
  2. The correct answer is B (Protected weight bearing with a frame, supervised transfers, and a wheelchair for longer distances): Following oncology advice, protected weight bearing with a frame and supervised transfers limits load through the lesion while still letting Joyce work toward her goal, with a wheelchair for the longer trip. Full weight bearing and resisted work add bending and twisting loads that can break a weakened femur. Bed rest alone ignores her goal and her quality of life.
  3. The correct answer is B (Ask Joyce how she wants family involved, offer an Aboriginal health worker, and yarn first): Culturally safe practice puts Joyce in control of who is involved, and for many Aboriginal people family and community are central to decisions and care. Asking her, offering an Aboriginal health worker and taking time to yarn builds trust and respects that. Sending family out or speaking only to Tanya both assume what Joyce wants without asking her.
  4. The correct answer is C (Stop movement, support the leg as found, and urgently call the palliative care team): Sudden pain with a shortened, externally rotated leg and loss of active lift strongly suggests a pathological femoral fracture. The priority is to prevent further movement and get urgent medical input so analgesia and decisions about care match Joyce's wishes. Delaying the call, applying traction or rolling her can worsen the fracture and cause severe pain.
Case CHB209

Eleni Kostas, 78, a retired seamstress, has idiopathic pulmonary fibrosis diagnosed four years ago and is now under a community palliative care service. She lives with her husband Nick in a single-storey home. She uses home oxygen at 2 L/min via a concentrator with 15 metres of tubing. Resting SpO2 is 91 percent on oxygen, RR 24, with a dry cough and bibasal fine inspiratory crackles. Breathlessness reaches Borg 7 after walking to the toilet and she becomes anxious during attacks. She has an advance care plan: remain at home, no intubation, no ventilation. Her goal is to sit in the garden with Nick. At this visit Nick mentions that Eleni caught her foot on the tubing in the hallway last week and nearly fell, with no injury. The tubing still crosses the hallway.

  1. The correct answer is A (Forward-leaning supported sitting, a handheld fan directed at her face, and slow relaxed breathing): Upright forward lean, airflow from a fan across the face and slow relaxed breathing are low-burden ways to ease breathlessness in advanced lung disease, and her husband can use them too. Her cough is dry, so secretion clearance has little to offer. Incentive spirometry and progressive walking targets do not fit a palliative goal of comfort and staying at home.
  2. The correct answer is C (Sit to wash, pace the task in stages with rests, and dry with a towelling robe): Sitting to wash, spacing the task into stages and avoiding arm-raised towel drying reduce the oxygen cost of the activity while keeping her independence, which matches her goals. Cutting showers or doing everything in one go either removes independence or concentrates the effort when recovery is already slow. Steam does not treat the fibrosis and can add to breathlessness.
  3. The correct answer is D (Support comfort with positioning and a fan, and urgently contact the palliative team for same-day review): Her plan is to stay at home without ventilation, so comfort measures and rapid review by the team who can treat a possible chest infection or adjust symptom management fit her goals. Automatic ambulance transfer overrides the plan she made, and directing oxygen flow is outside physiotherapy scope. Waiting a week leaves a distressed patient without timely medical review.
  4. The correct answer is D (Reduce the hazard with the family, and report the near miss through the organisation's incident system): A near miss shows a real hazard, and organisational policy and safe practice expect it to be reported so the risk can be reviewed, even when no one was hurt. Acting on the hazard with the family protects Eleni now. Fixing it quietly, passing it to the supplier or changing equipment without review leaves the hazard unrecorded for the care team.
Case CHB210

Kim Truong Tran, 46, cleaner, Vietnamese-speaking with limited English, day 12 of admission for pneumococcal meningitis, treated with intravenous antibiotics and now medically stable. Temperature 36.8, HR 82, BP 118/74, RR 16, SpO2 98%. Neck stiffness has resolved. Pupils equal and reactive, orientated and following commands, but slowed in processing. Headache 3/10 and stable, photophobia improving, fatigue after about ten minutes of activity. Gait wide-based with mild unsteadiness, supervision needed over 20 metres; dizziness with head turns; Berg Balance Scale 38/56. Strength and sensation normal in all limbs. Her husband speaks little English; her 15-year-old daughter has interpreted so far. A professional telephone interpreter service is available. She does ladder and bucket work as a cleaner.

  1. The correct answer is A (New focal limb weakness with increasing drowsiness, suggesting a brain abscess, infarct or hydrocephalus): New focal deficit with falling alertness points to a structural or vascular complication such as abscess, infarct or raised pressure from hydrocephalus, so therapy stops and she is reviewed urgently. A headache that settles with rest, vestibular symptoms that are stable and mild neck discomfort without systemic or neurological change fit the recovery picture already recorded. They warrant monitoring rather than an urgent stop.
  2. The correct answer is B (Book a professional interpreter, speak to Mrs Tran directly, and check understanding with teach-back): Informed understanding of precautions requires accurate interpretation, so a qualified interpreter is used rather than a child or a relative with limited English, and teach-back confirms she understood. Gestures and demonstration help but cannot convey risks and warning signs. Machine translation can be inaccurate and is not a substitute for a two-way conversation.
  3. The correct answer is D (Supervised graded balance and gait practice in short sessions with rests and gaze stability exercises): Post-meningitis imbalance and dizziness respond to graded, supervised balance, gait and gaze stability work, with sessions short enough to respect her fatigue. Prolonged rest delays recovery, and heavy exercise is poorly tolerated this early. Avoiding head movements removes the very stimulus that drives vestibular compensation.
  4. The correct answer is B (Advise a graded, staged return guided by her GP and rehabilitation team, with ladder tasks last): Return to work after meningitis is best graded by task demand, leaving the highest risk tasks such as ladders until balance and endurance are reliable, and coordinated with her doctors. A full immediate return risks falls, while waiting for complete recovery delays useful gradual loading. Leaving the timing to rest alone gives no guidance on safe progression.

Practice Test 15 Answer Key

Case CHB211

Kwame Mwangi is a 47-year-old man with cystic fibrosis, nine days after bilateral lung transplantation via a clamshell incision. He is on the transplant ward taking tacrolimus, mycophenolate and prednisolone. Observations: HR 92, BP 128/76 mmHg, RR 20, temperature 36.8 degrees, SpO2 96% on 2 L/min nasal prongs. Chest drains are out. Auscultation shows reduced air entry at both bases with scattered coarse crackles. He has a moderately thick productive cough, pain 4 of 10 on coughing, and expresses sputum with encouragement. He walks 30 m with a frame and one seated rest, Borg 5 of 10 afterwards. Kwame lives alone, says he feels isolated, and has come to value the daily physiotherapy visits and the friendly rapport with his treating physiotherapist.

  1. The correct answer is A (Active cycle of breathing in supported sitting with wound splinting, and treatment timed after analgesia): The active cycle of breathing with huffing in supported sitting clears thick secretions without the high pressures that stress a fresh incision and airway anastomoses, and splinting plus treatment after analgesia lets him cough effectively. Percussion in head-down tilt adds pain and risk, and suction is not first-line in a patient who can still expectorate. Holding off on huffing allows secretions to accumulate.
  2. The correct answer is A (Possible acute rejection or infection, needing prompt medical review before further exercise or airway clearance proceeds): Increasing breathlessness, fever and a falling lung function reading in a recipient on immunosuppression can signal acute rejection or infection, and either needs urgent medical review. Treating it as deconditioning or retained sputum risks missing a serious problem and could expose him to harmful exertion. Pain-related guarding may contribute but does not explain the fever or the drop in measured function.
  3. The correct answer is C (Follow the surgical team's sternal precautions, using leg-driven transfers and avoiding heavy arm loading until cleared): Following the surgical team's precautions protects the healing chest wall, so transfers should rely on leg drive and arm loading should wait for clearance. Pushing through the arms early can disrupt healing. Banning all arm movement is unnecessary and promotes stiffness and weakness, which is why progression is guided by the surgeons.
  4. The correct answer is A (Politely decline both, explain professional boundaries, and refer him to social work for support): Accepting gifts or sharing personal contact details blurs the professional relationship and can disadvantage him if boundaries are later crossed. Explaining boundaries kindly while recognising his loneliness meets the duty of care, and a social work referral gives real support. Offering a personal number even for exercise questions sets an unsafe precedent, because follow-up should go through the service's own channels.
Case CHB212

Tuan Pham, 42, builder, speaks Vietnamese and limited English. Five weeks ago he lacerated his right volar wrist on glass. The median nerve was repaired surgically, and the surgeon has now cleared active movement and discontinued the splint. He is referred for hand rehabilitation. Findings: reduced sensation over the thumb, index and middle finger pads, with normal sensation over the little finger. Thumb abduction is 2/5 (movement only with gravity eliminated). The thumb web space is tightening. Tinel's sign at the wrist crease reproduces tingling into the thumb and index finger. Grip is weak. At the first visit his 15 year old son interpreted, and Mr Pham appeared unsure about the exercises. He uses power tools at home, cooks for his family each evening, and worries about getting back to the building site.

  1. The correct answer is B (Book a professional interpreter, address Mr Pham directly, and confirm understanding with teach-back): A professional interpreter gives accurate, impartial communication and protects Mr Pham's autonomy, whereas family members, especially children, may omit or change information and should not carry this role. Speaking to him directly and using teach-back confirms that he understands before he consents. Apps and English handouts cannot verify understanding or support valid consent.
  2. The correct answer is D (Axons regrow slowly, around one millimetre a day, so hand recovery takes many months): Regenerating axons advance at roughly one millimetre per day from the repair site, so reinnervation of the thenar muscles takes months, and results depend on distance and age. A faster rate and a short timeline set unrealistic expectations, and recovery is often incomplete even with the right rate. Muscle retraining supports function but cannot replace axonal regrowth.
  3. The correct answer is C (Gravity-eliminated thumb abduction practice, web space stretches, and a night web spacer splint): With 2/5 power the muscle cannot move against resistance, so gravity-eliminated work avoids overload while stretches and a night splint keep the web space supple and prevent the contracture that denervated thumb muscles invite. Resisted gripping overloads weak muscle and encourages substitution by stronger muscles. Complete rest allows contracture, and forceful tensioning risks the fresh repair.
  4. The correct answer is C (Check the numb fingertips daily, watch hot or sharp objects closely, and use protective gloves): Skin without protective sensation can burn, cut and ulcerate without pain, so daily inspection, care around heat and sharp tools, and protective gloves are the priority. Heat on numb skin is dangerous, and deliberate exposure to warm items risks burns rather than retraining. Immobilising in a sling would worsen stiffness and does not protect the skin.
Case CHB213

Giovanna Rossi, 58, retired hairdresser, lives at home with her husband Carlo and has motor neurone disease with limb and mild bulbar involvement, diagnosed 14 months ago. You are her community physiotherapist. She recently finished oral antibiotics for a chest infection. Findings today: sitting forced vital capacity 48 percent predicted, falling to 36 percent supine. Peak cough flow 150 L/min. SpO2 95 percent on room air, RR 20, HR 82. Breath sounds are clear apart from coarse upper airway sounds that she cannot clear. She reports poor sleep, morning headaches and needing three pillows. Neck flexion and shoulder girdle strength 3 out of 5. She has told you she wants only Carlo to receive information about her health, and you have documented her wishes. Her daughter, who lives interstate, phones you asking for a full update.

  1. The correct answer is A (Diaphragm weakness with possible nocturnal hypoventilation, needing prompt medical referral for respiratory assessment): A large postural fall in vital capacity indicates diaphragm weakness, and morning headaches with poor sleep and orthopnoea point to nocturnal hypoventilation. This needs medical review for respiratory function testing and consideration of ventilatory support. Secretion management and exercise do not address the underlying ventilatory failure.
  2. The correct answer is C (Begin at low pressures, increase gradually to comfort and effective clearance, and train Carlo to assist): Pressures are titrated individually, starting low so the patient learns the technique and tolerates it, then raised until the cough is effective. Training the carer makes it usable when secretions build up. Using it only during infections delays skill and misses early clearance, and fixed settings ignore her bulbar involvement.
  3. The correct answer is B (Stop using the device, keep her upright and arrange urgent medical assessment for possible pneumothorax): Sudden pleuritic pain, desaturation and unilateral reduced air entry in someone using pressure based cough assistance raise a pneumothorax, which is a contraindication to continuing airway pressures. Urgent medical assessment takes priority over any secretion technique. Treating this as sputum retention delays diagnosis and could worsen the leak.
  4. The correct answer is D (Decline to share details without Mrs Rossi's consent and suggest the daughter ask her mother directly): Health information belongs to the patient, and a competent adult's documented instruction on who may receive it must be respected. A family relationship or an assurance from the caller does not replace consent. Even general progress or confirmation of a diagnosis is a disclosure, so the daughter is directed back to her mother.
Case CHB214

Hannah Brooks, 34, an accountant, sits at a desk for about nine hours a day. She reports a right-sided headache for four months, starting at the base of the skull and spreading to the forehead and behind the eye. Headaches occur most days, last hours, and are provoked by prolonged sitting and neck movements. No aura, nausea or light sensitivity, no visual disturbance, dizziness or drop attacks, no recent trauma, no fever and no change in character. Her GP has excluded other causes. Medication: paracetamol as needed. Examination: reduced right upper cervical rotation on the flexion-rotation test (about 30 degrees right, 45 left), tenderness at right C1 to C3 joints, headache reproduced by sustained C2 pressure, poor deep neck flexor endurance, forward head posture, thoracic stiffness, normal neurological examination. Today the clinic is fully booked, a colleague is absent, and you also have a new patient assessment, an insurer progress report and a student supervision session due.

  1. The correct answer is B (Upper cervical joint dysfunction is a probable source, so physiotherapy for cervicogenic headache is appropriate): A unilateral headache provoked by neck movement, with a restricted flexion-rotation test and reproduction on upper cervical palpation, points to a cervical source that physiotherapy can treat. Migraine and tension-type headache have different features and do not explain the movement restriction or the reproduced symptoms. There are no neurological signs to suggest nerve irritation needing a block, and no vascular warning signs that would stop treatment.
  2. The correct answer is A (Upper cervical mobilisation combined with deep neck flexor and scapular exercise, plus workstation and posture breaks): Evidence supports combining manual therapy with specific exercise for the impaired muscles and addressing the sustained postures that drive the problem. Passive treatments such as massage and heat give the same short relief she has already had. Traction with a brace deconditions the muscles, and repeated thrust techniques with stretching do not correct the endurance deficit.
  3. The correct answer is B (Explain risks and benefits, obtain informed consent, use graded techniques, and monitor symptoms): Screening is based on history and ongoing symptom monitoring, and informed consent covering material risks is required before manual therapy. Provocation tests have poor validity and do not make treatment safe. A negative screen does not remove the duty to discuss risks, and consent should be revisited when techniques or findings change.
  4. The correct answer is D (Triage the caller's urgency first, arrange same-day medical review, order the rest by risk): New bilateral leg weakness may signal serious spinal pathology, so patient safety takes priority over administrative deadlines and teaching commitments. The caller should be triaged immediately and directed to same-day medical assessment. Delaying the call, booking later in the day, or assessing in the clinic all postpone the medical review that is needed, and remaining tasks can then be ordered by risk and deadline.
Case CHB215

Mr Paolo Fontana, 71, retired bricklayer, lives with his wife in regional Victoria and joins by tablet for a telehealth session. Parkinson disease for six years (Hoehn and Yahr stage 3), referred by his GP after two falls in three months, both while turning in the kitchen. He takes levodopa four times daily and says the benefit fades before the next dose, especially in the late afternoon. On video: reduced arm swing, short shuffling steps, stooped posture, and freezing at the kitchen doorway. Timed Up and Go 16 seconds from a dining chair. He reports lightheadedness on standing from his chair, sometimes before a fall. His wife has a home blood pressure cuff but has never used it for him. Cognition is intact. Your afternoon list also holds three more telehealth clients, including a postoperative client who phoned with a new concern, and a GP report is due today.

  1. The correct answer is A (External visual cues such as strips of tape placed across the floor to step over): External visual cues give a cortically driven target that helps bypass the impaired automatic stepping of the basal ganglia, so they are the first strategy for freezing at thresholds and turns. Counting breaths is an internal strategy that relies on the same automatic system and is less reliable. Dual-task practice tends to worsen freezing, and a walker does not address initiation.
  2. The correct answer is B (During his ON phase after a dose, when movement is best and his wife can supervise): Training is best placed in the ON phase, when he can move well enough to practise quality movement with cues and a lower risk of falling. Practising at the end of the dose cycle raises fall risk and rehearses poor movement. Assessing the unmedicated state can be useful, but it is not the best timing for training sessions.
  3. The correct answer is A (Guide the wife to measure lying and standing blood pressure and report to the GP): Lightheadedness before falls suggests orthostatic hypotension, which is common in Parkinson disease, so objective lying and standing readings should be obtained and passed to the GP before standing exercises progress. Coping strategies, a diary or seated work are useful but leave the cause unmeasured and the GP uninformed. Carrying on with standing exercises without data leaves a safety risk unaddressed.
  4. The correct answer is C (See the postoperative concern first, then the due GP report, then routine reviews): Workload is prioritised by clinical urgency and risk first, so a possible postoperative complication comes before administrative deadlines and routine care. The report is then completed because it has a same-day deadline. Delegating assessment of a new, unexplained concern to an assistant is outside appropriate delegation.
Case CHB216

Mr Khoa Dang, 67, a retired bus driver, is 4 months after a right pontine stroke. He lives with his wife in a two-storey home and receives community rehabilitation. He has mild left hemiparesis: ankle dorsiflexion 3+/5, knee extension 4/5, with reduced proprioception in the left foot. Timed Up and Go is 24 seconds with a single-point stick and Berg Balance Scale is 41/56. He has had two near falls in the past month, on the stairs and in the bathroom, and fatigues after 10 minutes of walking. BP 134/80, HR 72. He takes aspirin, atorvastatin and amlodipine. His goals are to walk to the local shops and manage the stairs. The team has an allied health assistant (AHA) with a Certificate IV who can run exercise sessions twice weekly.

  1. The correct answer is B (They indicate increased falls risk, supporting a targeted balance and strength programme with a home review): A reduced Berg score, slow Timed Up and Go and recent near falls together point to a genuine falls risk that warrants intervention and environmental review. A score above the midpoint is not reassuring when the test is out of the normal range. Nothing in the case points to a vestibular cause, and near falls should not be waved away as ageing.
  2. The correct answer is C (Progressive task-specific walking and stair practice with balance challenge and leg strengthening, graded to fatigue): Recovery is most specific to the tasks practised, so his programme should combine functional walking and stairs with balance and strengthening, graded around fatigue. Seated strengthening alone does not transfer well to standing balance, and continuous treadmill walking ignores stairs and balance. Eyes-closed static holds are not matched to his goals.
  3. The correct answer is D (Provide a written individualised programme with stop criteria, demonstrate it, and arrange ongoing review and supervision): The physiotherapist remains accountable, so delegation needs an individualised plan, defined limits, stop criteria, training in the tasks and an agreed supervision and review process. Assessment and progression decisions require clinical reasoning and stay with the physiotherapist. A generic handout and an on-demand phone call do not provide adequate oversight.
  4. The correct answer is C (Stop exercise today, monitor until symptoms settle, and arrange review before sessions restart): Symptomatic low blood pressure on standing in someone with falls risk is a stop criterion for the assistant, who should not make the decision to continue. The physiotherapist should review him and consider whether the GP needs to be informed. Continuing after one improved reading skips that review, and asking the GP to change medication is not the immediate step.
Case CHB217

Mr Desmond Walker, 71, a Wiradjuri man and retired fencing contractor, attends a private practice on GP referral with his niece. For three weeks he has had brief spinning episodes lasting under a minute when he rolls onto his right side in bed or looks up to a shelf. No hearing loss, tinnitus, headache or new weakness. Medications: ramipril and metformin. Seated BP 138/82 with no postural drop. Cranial nerve screen, limb power, coordination and gait are normal, with no spontaneous nystagmus. Head impulse test is normal bilaterally. Dizziness Handicap Inventory score is moderate. He mentions that his last clinic visit felt rushed and he left unsure what had been done. Dix-Hallpike testing is planned today.

  1. The correct answer is A (Right posterior canal BPPV, with canalithiasis suggested by the latency and brief fatigue): A short latency, torsional upbeating nystagmus toward the undermost ear and fatigue within about a minute are the typical signs of debris in the posterior canal on the tested side. Anterior canal BPPV produces downbeating nystagmus and is uncommon. Vestibular neuritis causes continuous vertigo with a positive head impulse test, which is normal here.
  2. The correct answer is C (Canalith repositioning (Epley) manoeuvre to the right, with a slow controlled head sequence): The Epley manoeuvre moves debris out of the posterior canal and is the first line treatment for the confirmed side, and it can be performed slowly with adequate neck range. Habituation exercises are kept for people who cannot tolerate or do not respond to repositioning. A horizontal canal roll targets a different canal, and gaze stabilisation treats vestibular loss rather than canal debris.
  3. The correct answer is A (Stop the manoeuvre, sit him up, and call an ambulance for suspected stroke): Diplopia, dysarthria, arm drift and nystagmus that changes with gaze point to a central cause such as posterior circulation stroke, which needs emergency care. Positioning must stop and he should be kept safe and monitored while an ambulance is called. Waiting, a GP appointment or private transport all delay time-critical treatment and leave him unmonitored.
  4. The correct answer is D (Welcome his niece and the Health Worker with his agreement, and allow unhurried time): Cultural safety is judged by the patient's experience, so honouring who he wants present, with his consent, and allowing time to build trust is the best step. Excluding family or splitting the Health Worker's role ignores his request. A fixed script puts the clinic's process ahead of the person.
Case CHB218

Marco Cattaneo, 67, retired bus driver, referred by his GP to the community health centre for balance problems. He has had type 2 diabetes for 22 years and takes metformin and gliclazide. He reports burning feet at night and two near falls on uneven paths. Findings: light touch and 10 g monofilament sensation reduced at the toes and forefeet bilaterally, vibration absent at both great toes, ankle reflexes absent. Ankle dorsiflexion and plantarflexion 4/5 bilaterally. Small callus under the right first metatarsal head, skin intact. Standing with feet together: more sway with eyes closed, no fall. Berg Balance Scale 44/56. He lives with his wife, who attends with him and keeps the household paperwork.

  1. The correct answer is B (Reduced somatosensory input is increasing his reliance on vision for postural control): Absent vibration, reduced monofilament sensation and absent ankle reflexes point to a large fibre sensory neuropathy, so the feet are giving poor position information and vision compensates. Closing the eyes removes that compensation and sway increases. Weakness is only mild (4/5) and would not explain the eyes-closed change, and nothing in the history suggests a central or vestibular cause.
  2. The correct answer is A (Inspect both feet first and require fitted closed shoes for all weight-bearing practice): With loss of protective sensation, repeated pressure or friction over a callus can break the skin without him feeling it, so feet are checked before and protected during weight-bearing. Barefoot work gives him little useful feedback and exposes the skin. Heat is unsafe where temperature sensation is reduced, and socks alone give no protection or stability.
  3. The correct answer is C (Stop weight-bearing exercise on that foot and arrange urgent medical or foot service review): Warmth and redness over a callus in an insensate diabetic foot suggest tissue breakdown, infection or early Charcot change, and the absence of pain is misleading because sensation is lost. Continued loading risks an ulcer, so same-day review is needed. Waiting weeks or reassuring him delays treatment of a limb-threatening problem.
  4. The correct answer is B (Explain that his consent is needed first, and offer to ask him directly): Health information belongs to the patient, and being a spouse or carer does not by itself give a right to it. The physiotherapist should seek his permission and record what he agrees to share. Sharing partial information, or pointing her to a records process, still discloses or facilitates access without his consent.
Case CHB219

Meera Reddy, 42, is an accountant who works at a computer for most of the day. She has eight weeks of right lateral elbow pain that began after a weekend of painting at home. Pain is worst when lifting a full kettle or gripping a mouse. Rest alone has not helped. Examination: tenderness at the lateral epicondyle and common extensor origin, pain on resisted wrist and middle finger extension with the elbow straight, full elbow and wrist range, normal neck and neurological screens, painful and reduced grip strength compared with the left, and a Patient-Rated Tennis Elbow Evaluation score of 58 out of 100. She uses paracetamol occasionally. Her employer's human resources officer has telephoned the clinic asking for her diagnosis and how long she will need modified duties. Meera has not mentioned this and has not signed any release.

  1. The correct answer is C (Load-related tendinopathy of the common extensor origin, most often involving the extensor carpi radialis brevis tendon): Localised tenderness at the extensor origin that is provoked by resisted wrist extension is the typical picture of lateral epicondylalgia. Radial tunnel pain lies more distally and is not reproduced by resisted wrist extension in this way. A normal neck screen and stable elbow make radiculopathy and ligament injury unlikely.
  2. The correct answer is B (Activity modification with a progressive loading programme for the wrist extensors, plus education about expected recovery): Education, load modification and progressive tendon loading are the mainstay of care and build capacity to return to normal tasks. Rest and constant bracing deload the tendon without improving it. Corticosteroid injection tends to give short-term relief with poorer outcomes later, and stretching or friction lacks strong support.
  3. The correct answer is D (Increase load in small steps with recovery days between sessions, guided by next-day symptom response): Tendons adapt to gradual increases in load, and mild pain that settles quickly is acceptable during rehabilitation. Doubling the load and removing recovery days risks flaring the tendon. Waiting for zero pain or abandoning loading prolongs deconditioning.
  4. The correct answer is D (Decline to share details without consent, pass the request to Meera and record the call factually): Health information, including attendance and expected duration, can be released to an employer only with the patient's consent or where the law requires it. The appropriate response is to decline politely, tell Meera about the request, and document the contact accurately. Verifying identity does not create consent, and sharing partial details or leaving the call out of the notes still breaches her privacy or weakens the record.
Case CHB220

Mason Nguyen, 12 years, plays football three times weekly and school basketball. He attends a community health centre with his mother after 4 months of right anterior knee pain, worse with jumping, kneeling and stairs. He has grown about 6 cm this year. There was no injury, night pain, fever or locking. Medications: nil. Examination: prominent, tender right tibial tuberosity; no knee effusion; knee flexion and extension full range; heel to buttock distance 12 cm on the right and 6 cm on the left; resisted knee extension strong but mildly painful; single leg squat shows dynamic knee valgus. Pain is 5/10 after training and 1/10 at rest. Hip and ankle examination is normal. Mason wants to keep playing. This morning the school football coach emailed the front desk asking for Mason's diagnosis and when he can play. Mason's mother has not been asked about sharing information.

  1. The correct answer is A (Traction apophysitis of the tibial tuberosity from repeated quadriceps loading during rapid growth): Localised tuberosity tenderness with a growth spurt, jumping load and tight quadriceps is the classic picture of traction apophysitis at the patellar tendon insertion. Patellar tendinopathy centres on the inferior patellar pole and occurs after skeletal maturity. Patellofemoral pain is diffuse rather than tender at the tuberosity, and a physeal injury would need a traumatic history.
  2. The correct answer is A (Symptom-guided load modification, quadriceps stretching and graded strengthening, with reassurance it resolves at skeletal maturity): This is a self-limiting condition, so management is relative load modification guided by symptoms, flexibility and strengthening, plus education that it settles as growth finishes. Total rest is rarely needed and deconditions him. Imaging is not routine without trauma or red flags, and pushing through pain with heavy loading aggravates the apophysis.
  3. The correct answer is D (Protect the knee in extension and arrange urgent medical review for suspected tuberosity avulsion fracture): Sudden pain after a forceful landing with loss of active extension and a changed contour suggests avulsion of the tuberosity, which needs prompt imaging and medical management. Resisted testing or isometric loading would stress the extensor mechanism further. Waiting a week delays a potentially surgical problem.
  4. The correct answer is A (Obtain parental consent with Mason's input, then share only activity advice and record the agreement): Health information about a child is shared with the parent's informed consent, involving the child as appropriate, and only to the extent needed, here activity advice rather than the full diagnosis. A safety interest alone does not override privacy. Ignoring the request leaves it undocumented, and a verbal disclosure is still a disclosure.
Case CHB221

Mariam Haddad, 44, works full time on her feet in a supermarket. Eight weeks of right heel pain, worst with the first steps each morning and after sitting (7/10), settling to 3/10 once she has walked. BMI 31. No trauma, no night pain, no numbness. No medical history of note and no regular medications. Examination: tenderness at the medial calcaneal tubercle, windlass test reproduces her pain, ankle dorsiflexion with the knee extended 4 degrees, both calves tight, mildly pronated foot posture, no swelling or bruising, sensation normal, 10 single-leg heel raises. She wears thin flat sandals at work and has tried rest and ice with little change. You plan a progressive loading and stretching programme. The clinic has an allied health assistant (AHA) who could supervise her gym exercises, and the practice owner suggests the AHA also complete her reassessment and update her goals to save your time.

  1. The correct answer is A (Tenderness at the medial calcaneal tubercle with pain reproduced by the windlass test): Localised tenderness at the plantar fascia origin plus pain with the windlass test loads the fascia directly and fits plantar fasciopathy. Pain on calcaneal squeeze would point towards a stress injury, and burning paraesthesia suggests tibial nerve involvement. Calf tightness is common in plantar heel pain but is a contributing factor, not a finding specific to the fascia.
  2. The correct answer is C (Supportive footwear, progressive fascia loading with calf stretching, and advice on pacing standing time): Current practice combines footwear change, progressive tendon and fascia loading, calf flexibility work and load management, which suits someone who cannot stop work. Prolonged rest is an outdated habit that does not address loading capacity. An injection is a later consideration rather than a first step, and expensive rigid orthoses without a loading programme do not fix the underlying capacity problem.
  3. The correct answer is A (Progress to slow heavy heel raises with toes elevated, adding load gradually while monitoring next-morning pain): Fifteen easy repetitions means the tissue is ready for heavier, slower loading, and a mild next-morning ache is an acceptable monitoring signal rather than a reason to stop. Waiting for pain to reach zero delays adaptation. More light repetitions do not provide the stimulus the fascia needs, and passive modalities replace the active loading that drives recovery.
  4. The correct answer is A (Delegate supervising the set programme, but keep reassessment and progression decisions with the physiotherapist): Assessment, clinical reasoning, goal setting and progression remain the physiotherapist's accountability and cannot be handed to an assistant. Delegating a defined exercise programme with appropriate supervision is acceptable. Reviewing decisions after the fact still lets the assistant make clinical decisions, and refusing all delegation ignores that supervised assistants can safely deliver set programmes.
Case CHB222

Bruno Ferraro, 71, retired builder, stage IV non-small cell lung cancer with bone metastases, admitted to the inpatient palliative care unit for symptom control. Left lobectomy two years ago. Weight loss of 12 kg in six months, BMI 17.5, poor appetite, taking oral nutrition supplements. Resting SpO2 93 percent on room air, RR 20, HR 96. After walking 10 metres with a frame he scores breathlessness 4 on the Borg scale. Pain is 3 out of 10 at rest and 6 out of 10 with weight bearing through the right hip, which has a known lytic lesion in the proximal femur; the medical team has not yet advised on fracture risk. Fatigue is worse in the afternoons. Prognosis is weeks to months. His goals are to sit in the garden with his wife and walk to the bathroom independently. An allied health assistant, Priya, who has completed general mobility training, works on the unit.

  1. The correct answer is D (Supported forward-lean sitting, relaxed pursed lip breathing and a handheld fan to the face): Forward-lean sitting with arm support, relaxed pursed lip breathing and a fan on the face are first line strategies for breathlessness in advanced cancer, and they reduce both the sensation and the panic. Holding deep breaths increases effort, walking on the spot adds exertion during an episode, and saving the fan for later withholds one of the most effective measures when it is needed.
  2. The correct answer is A (Provide written instructions with stop criteria and escalation steps, and review him regularly yourself): Delegation keeps accountability with the physiotherapist, so the task must be specific, written, matched to the assistant's training and backed by stop criteria and escalation steps. His symptoms change daily, so ongoing review is needed. Leaving decisions to the assistant or handing over responsibility removes the supervision that a changing palliative patient requires.
  3. The correct answer is B (Walk with a frame, avoid resisted femoral loading and seek medical advice on fracture risk): A lytic lesion in a weight bearing bone with loading pain carries a risk of pathological fracture, so resisted and twisting loads through the femur are avoided until the medical team advises, while walking with an aid keeps him moving toward his goals. Stopping weight bearing altogether is more restrictive than needed. Walking unaided and ignoring the femur leaves the fracture risk unmanaged.
  4. The correct answer is C (Cachexia is cancer driven, so weight gain is unlikely, though gentle activity may help his function): Cancer cachexia is a metabolic syndrome driven by inflammation and tumour effects, so extra food or exercise does not reverse it, particularly near the end of life. An honest answer avoids false hope while explaining that gentle activity can support strength, mood and comfort. Resistance training and high protein intake do not restore weight here, and rest alone speeds deconditioning.
Case CHB223

Gary Whitfield, 67, a retired plumber, attends outpatient cardiac rehabilitation after a hospital admission for decompensated heart failure. Diagnosis: heart failure with reduced ejection fraction (echo EF 30 percent), NYHA class II to III. Medications include a beta blocker, an ACE inhibitor and a diuretic. Resting HR 68 bpm, BP 108/66 mmHg, SpO2 96 percent on room air, RR 16. Mild bilateral ankle oedema, no orthopnoea. His dry weight at discharge was 84.0 kg. Six minute walk test: 280 m, finishing at Borg RPE 14 and HR 92, with no symptoms of concern. Gary is motivated but lives alone, has never owned bathroom scales, and stopped attending his old exercise group because he was told to take it easy. His wife died last year; his daughter lives nearby and helps with shopping. The aim of the programme is supervised exercise plus support to manage his condition at home.

  1. The correct answer is D (Prescribe by Borg RPE of about 11 to 14, with symptoms guiding progression): Beta blockade flattens the heart rate response, so a heart rate ceiling or percentage of predicted maximum underestimates his true effort. Perceived exertion in the light to somewhat hard range, combined with symptom review, gives a reliable guide in heart failure. Anchoring to a single heart rate from the walk test ignores that the same effort can produce different rates between days.
  2. The correct answer is C (Withhold exercise and arrange same day medical review, given the rapid weight gain): A rapid gain of about 2 kg over a few days with worsening swelling and breathlessness suggests fluid retention and possible decompensation, which needs prompt medical review before exercise. Stable resting observations do not exclude this, and reducing intensity or giving diet advice delays treatment. Changing his fluid or diet plan is outside the physiotherapist's role.
  3. The correct answer is D (Systolic blood pressure dropping during effort, with new light-headedness or pallor appearing quickly): A fall in systolic pressure with increasing effort indicates the failing ventricle cannot raise output, and symptoms such as pallor or light-headedness confirm poor perfusion. Rising heart rate, moderate breathlessness that settles quickly, mild fatigue at a moderate rating and mild ankle swelling are expected in this group and are not reasons to stop, though they should be monitored.
  4. The correct answer is B (Weigh every morning after the toilet and before breakfast, and report rapid gain): Daily weighing under the same conditions, first thing after passing urine and before eating, detects fluid gain early enough to act on. Weekly readings or readings only when swelling is visible miss the rise that precedes symptoms. Evening weights after meals and exercise vary too much to compare, and weight gain, not loss, is the warning sign to report.
Case CHB224

Mrs Hoa Tran, 76, a retired seamstress, is four weeks after a right hemisphere stroke. She speaks Vietnamese and limited English. Her daughter Linh usually translates and often answers on her mother's behalf. Findings: flaccid left arm (shoulder muscle strength 1/5), a palpable gap below the acromion, and shoulder pain of 7/10 during passive flexion past 90 degrees and external rotation. The left hand is warm, with no swelling or colour change. Sitting balance is fair, and blood pressure and cognition are stable. Nursing staff lift her under the left arm during transfers, and in the recliner chair her left arm hangs at her side. A professional Vietnamese interpreter can be booked for tomorrow.

  1. The correct answer is D (Cue forward lean over her feet, guide at trunk and pelvis, and support the arm): A flaccid arm is vulnerable to traction, so staff should not pull on it or lift under it, and guiding at the trunk and pelvis lets her use her own legs while the arm is supported. Pulling on the hand or lifting under the armpit stresses the unsupported joint. A hoist for every transfer removes the participation that drives recovery.
  2. The correct answer is B (Book the professional interpreter, address Mrs Tran directly, and let Linh attend as support): Consent and a pain history need accurate, impartial communication, which a qualified interpreter provides, and speaking directly to the patient preserves her voice and autonomy. Family can stay for reassurance, but relatives may filter, shorten or answer for the patient. Using staff with partial language skills or relying on basic English risks misunderstanding and invalid consent.
  3. The correct answer is B (Inform the medical team promptly, and keep the hand elevated while avoiding painful passive movement): Burning pain, swelling, warmth, shiny skin and pain from light touch in a limb after stroke suggest complex regional pain syndrome, which needs prompt medical review because early treatment matters. Elevation and avoiding painful passive movement prevents aggravation in the meantime. Vigorous stretching can worsen the condition, and putting the signs down to positioning delays diagnosis.
  4. The correct answer is A (Rest the arm on a pillow with shoulder forward, and elbow and hand supported): A flaccid arm should be supported in a forward, well-supported position so the weight of the limb does not drag the humeral head down or stretch the soft tissues. Forcing abduction and external rotation stresses the unsupported joint, and binding the arm against the chest encourages stiffness. An arm left flat at the side lets gravity pull on the shoulder.
Case CHB225

Hassan El-Masri, 52, presented to the emergency department with three days of fever, right sided chest pain on breathing in and a productive cough. Chest x-ray shows right lower lobe consolidation and he has been diagnosed with community acquired pneumonia. He is a taxi driver, smokes about fifteen cigarettes a day and has no other medical history. Antibiotics have started.

Observations: T 38.2, HR 100, BP 124/76, RR 24, SpO2 93 percent on room air. Coarse crackles and bronchial breathing at the right base. He reports thick green sputum he is unable to clear and a pain score of 6 out of 10 when coughing. The emergency physician has requested physiotherapy for sputum retention. He hopes to go home today and asks what he should do at home.

  1. The correct answer is A (Active cycle of breathing technique in left side lying, with hydration advice and encouragement to mobilise): Because he has retained secretions, an airway clearance technique is indicated, and active cycle of breathing with the affected right lung uppermost uses gravity to assist drainage. Percussion with the consolidated side down works against drainage and is not shown to help consolidation. Deep breathing alone ignores the sputum, and suction is for patients unable to cooperate.
  2. The correct answer is C (Time treatment after analgesia takes effect and support his right chest with a towel when huffing): Pain limits effective breathing and coughing, so coordinating treatment with analgesia and splinting the painful side lets him take larger breaths and clear sputum more effectively. Starting before analgesia works makes treatment painful and less effective, and repeated forced coughing raises pain and fatigue. Holding back active airway clearance leaves a patient with retained sputum and borderline saturation without effective treatment.
  3. The correct answer is D (Teach a home clearance routine and warning signs, and offer cessation support without pressing the issue): Brief, respectful intervention supports behaviour change: physiotherapists give practical self-management advice, ask about smoking and offer help, and accept that he is not ready to quit now. Withholding help, pressuring him or deferring smoking advice entirely as outside scope all miss an opportunity, since brief advice and referral to the GP or Quitline are well within physiotherapy practice.
  4. The correct answer is A (Stay with him and activate the rapid response process, supporting the oxygen already ordered): Drowsiness, confusion, tachypnoea, tachycardia, falling saturation and cool skin indicate severe deterioration, possibly sepsis or respiratory failure, which needs immediate medical response rather than more treatment. The physiotherapist remains with the patient and calls the response team. Repeating treatment, waiting ten minutes or starting ventilation without medical direction delay appropriate care.

Practice Test 16 Answer Key

Case CHB226

Mr Robert Hayes, 74, a retired bus driver, was admitted 36 hours ago with a right middle cerebral artery ischaemic stroke. He did not receive thrombolysis. History: hypertension, newly diagnosed atrial fibrillation (anticoagulation not yet started) and type 2 diabetes. The medical team has cleared him for mobilisation and nursing staff report he has not yet been out of bed. Today he is alert and oriented. BP 148/84 mmHg, HR 78 irregular, SpO2 96% on room air. Left arm strength 2/5, left leg 3/5, reduced left-sided sensation, and he often ignores objects on his left. He sits on the edge of the bed only with moderate assistance and leans to the left. An allied health assistant, Priya, is rostered on the ward today and has offered to help with his mobilisation.

  1. The correct answer is A (Assist sitting over the edge of bed, then transfer to a chair, checking observations): He is medically cleared and stable, so frequent short out-of-bed sessions starting with supported sitting and a chair transfer suit his current trunk control and allow monitoring of blood pressure and symptoms. Standing and stepping in parallel bars exceeds his sitting balance and risks a fall onto the weak left side. Prolonged bed rest delays recovery and raises the risk of complications.
  2. The correct answer is C (Return him safely to bed and alert medical staff immediately with his current observations): A sudden severe headache, falling consciousness, new weakness and markedly raised blood pressure suggest possible haemorrhagic transformation or extension, which needs urgent medical assessment. The priority is a safe return to bed and immediate escalation, with the observations ready to hand over. Rechecking first, waiting ten minutes or monitoring in the chair all delay time-critical care.
  3. The correct answer is A (Cue him to look left and progressively place key items further into the left side): Inattention is managed by drawing attention progressively into the neglected space, so cueing him to scan left and graded placement of items encourage use of that field. Placing everything on the right or approaching only from the right reinforces the habit of ignoring the left. Reducing input from the left lessens his chance of noticing it further.
  4. The correct answer is A (Give clear instructions on assistance level and stop signs, then review his response afterwards): The physiotherapist remains accountable for delegated tasks, so the patient must be assessed, the task matched to the assistant's competence, and instructions specific about assistance, precautions and when to stop. Follow-up review of the patient and of the assistant's performance completes the supervision. Handing over the whole programme or shifting accountability to another worker is not acceptable, while refusing delegation outright ignores a legitimate role for trained assistants.
Case CHB227

Mrs Kim Dang is a 54 year old woman who speaks Vietnamese and very little English. She was transferred to inpatient rehabilitation three days ago after 24 days in intensive care for septic shock from a perforated bowel, with prolonged ventilation, corticosteroids and four days of neuromuscular blockade. The tracheostomy was removed two days ago. She is alert and cooperative. Examination: symmetrical limb weakness, MRC grade 3 at shoulder abduction and elbow flexion, grade 2 at hip flexion and knee extension, grade 3 at ankle dorsiflexion. Sensation is intact, reflexes are reduced and facial movements are normal. Resting HR 96, BP 118/70 mmHg, SpO2 95 percent on room air. She sits on the edge of the bed with moderate assistance of two. Her husband has been interpreting but looks exhausted. Her goal is to care for her grandchildren.

  1. The correct answer is B (Book a qualified interpreter for consent and goals, with her husband attending as support): A qualified interpreter protects accuracy, privacy and informed consent, and lets Mrs Dang speak for herself about her goals. Family members may filter information and carry their own emotional strain. Using her husband for instructions, or a ward colleague who is not a trained interpreter, still leaves exercise safety and symptoms poorly communicated, so he is best kept as support.
  2. The correct answer is A (Sepsis, multi-organ failure, prolonged ventilation, immobility, and steroid or neuromuscular blocker drug exposure): Intensive care acquired weakness is linked to sepsis and multi-organ failure, prolonged ventilation, immobility, hyperglycaemia and exposure to corticosteroids and neuromuscular blocking agents. Sedation and poor nutrition contribute mainly through immobility and illness severity rather than by damaging motor neurons directly. Immobility matters, but sepsis and drug exposure are strong recognised contributors, and age and a tracheostomy are not main drivers.
  3. The correct answer is B (Short, frequent sessions of active assisted and functional exercise, progressing from supported sitting): Her weakness limits loading, so early rehabilitation uses short, frequent sessions of active assisted and functional work, graded against fatigue and heart rate and blood pressure responses. High-load programs overload weak muscle and worsen fatigue. Passive-only work or prolonged bed rest adds to deconditioning and delays recovery.
  4. The correct answer is D (Lower her to lying with legs raised, recheck observations, and await medical review): A fall in blood pressure with a rise in heart rate, dizziness and pallor is an orthostatic response, so she is returned to a position that restores cerebral perfusion and her observations are rechecked. Further standing waits for review of the cause, such as volume status or autonomic dysfunction. Resuming after a short rest, or changing equipment, ignores the unresolved cause.
Case CHB228

Aarav Patel, 7, has bilateral spastic cerebral palsy, GMFCS level III. He walks short distances at school with a posterior walker, wears solid ankle foot orthoses for walking, and uses a wheelchair for longer distances. He is seen at the community health centre with his mother Kavita. Passive ankle dorsiflexion past neutral with the knee flexed: right 10 degrees, left 12 degrees; with the knee extended: right 0 degrees, left 2 degrees. Modified Ashworth Scale is 2 in both calf muscles and hip adductors. He is pain free. At school he sits for most of the day and the standing frame is rarely used. His hip migration is monitored by the paediatric orthopaedic clinic. Kavita has found a website promoting an expensive overseas programme claiming to cure spasticity and wants your advice.

  1. The correct answer is C (Gastrocnemius shortness is the main limitation, so stretching should target the two-joint muscle): Range that improves with the knee flexed shows the two-joint gastrocnemius is limiting, because flexing the knee slackens it while soleus remains on stretch. Stretching and positioning are therefore applied with the knee extended. A capsular restriction would not change with knee position.
  2. The correct answer is B (Introduce supervised standing in the frame on most school days, with feet flat and hips abducted): Regular supervised standing gives a sustained stretch to the calves and hip adductors and loads the hips, which is thought to support hip development in children at this level. Using it on most school days is achievable. Occasional use gives too little stimulus, and standing for most of the day is impractical and tiring.
  3. The correct answer is A (Review the quality of the evidence together, explain its limits, and involve the treating team): Good practice is to appraise the evidence with the family: study design, size, independence and how it compares with accepted guidelines, then involve his treating team in the decision. Dismissing it outright or endorsing testimonials both skip the appraisal. Declining to comment leaves the family without support for an informed choice.
  4. The correct answer is B (Liaise with the paediatric rehabilitation team about spasticity management while continuing stretching and orthoses): Loss of range despite good adherence shows the current approach is no longer enough, so the team should consider further spasticity management that can be combined with ongoing stretching and orthoses. Forceful stretching risks pain and soft tissue injury. Stopping effective measures removes protection, and waiting delays treatment during growth.
Case CHB229

Fadi Daher, 58, builder, post-operative day 1 after open right hemicolectomy for bowel cancer via midline incision. BMI 33, ex-smoker with 20 pack-years. Thoracic epidural infusing, pain 3 out of 10 at rest and 7 out of 10 on coughing. Observations: RR 24, HR 96, BP 118/70 mmHg, temperature 37.8, SpO2 92 percent on 2 L per minute via nasal prongs. He has been mostly supine in bed and breathes shallowly. Examination: reduced chest expansion, bronchial breathing at the left base, reduced air entry at the right base. Chest X-ray shows left lower lobe volume loss. He follows instructions. The HDU is currently auditing post-operative pulmonary complications and has asked staff to record consistent outcome measures.

  1. The correct answer is C (Trachea shifted towards the left side with reduced chest expansion and a dull percussion note): Collapse pulls the trachea and mediastinum toward the affected side, and the airways stay open so bronchial breathing can be heard over dull lung. A large effusion pushes the trachea away and gives stony dullness with absent vocal resonance. Hyperresonance suggests air, and widespread crackles suggest fluid on the lungs.
  2. The correct answer is A (Upright sitting, deep breaths with an inspiratory hold, and coughing supported by a pillow): Upright positioning raises functional residual capacity and sustained deep breaths with a hold re-expand collapsed segments, while a splinted cough protects the wound and reduces pain. A stable blood pressure means the epidural does not prevent sitting up, and avoiding coughing leaves secretions in place. A tight binder restricts expansion, and waiting for secretions to appear misses the aim of re-expanding the collapsed segments.
  3. The correct answer is D (Lie him back, stay with him and alert the nurse and anaesthetic team): Hypotension with tachycardia on mobilising can reflect epidural-related sympathetic block, bleeding or hypovolaemia, so he must be laid back to restore perfusion and the team informed. Remaining upright or finishing the transfer risks syncope and a fall. Extra oxygen does not treat a perfusion problem.
  4. The correct answer is A (Oxygen saturation with the stated oxygen flow, respiratory rate and a consistent complication definition): A useful audit measure is objective, quick and recorded the same way by everyone, which saturation with its oxygen context, respiratory rate and an agreed complication definition provide. A walk test is not feasible in the first days after major abdominal surgery, repeated imaging at every review is impractical, and bedside spirometry is effort dependent and hard to standardise on a busy unit.
Case CHB230

Mele Tuipulotu, 31, Tongan-Australian primary school teacher and mother of two, is 12 weeks after a vaginal birth with a repaired second degree tear. Her GP referred her for leaking urine. She leaks small amounts when she coughs, sneezes, lifts her toddler and runs for the bus. She has no urgency, nocturia, pain passing urine, bowel problems, heaviness or bulge. GP urine dipstick was clear. The perineal scar is healed and non-tender. With consent, vaginal palpation shows a gentle contraction of 2/5 (modified Oxford scale) held for 3 seconds, 4 repetitions, without abdominal bracing. A cough produces a small leak. It is 1 pm, and your afternoon list also includes an unreturned message from a woman two weeks postnatal who reports a swollen, painful left calf, and a routine report that is due today.

  1. The correct answer is B (Stress urinary incontinence linked to reduced pelvic floor strength and endurance after childbirth): Leakage that occurs only with raised abdominal pressure, with no urgency, normal voiding and a clear dipstick, fits stress incontinence, and a weak, short contraction explains the poor urethral support. There is no urgency to suggest a mixed or overactive bladder picture. A second degree tear does not involve the sphincter, so surgical referral first is not needed and pelvic floor training is the first line.
  2. The correct answer is A (Call her now, screen for red flags, and arrange same day medical assessment if indicated): Swelling and pain in one calf shortly after birth raises concern for deep vein thrombosis, which can lead to pulmonary embolism, so this is the most time-critical task. Only a clinician speaking to her can screen for red flags and arrange same day medical assessment. Reception cannot triage symptoms, a text message cannot confirm she has read it or is safe, and waiting until Mele's session ends leaves a possible emergency unassessed while a stable, non-urgent patient is seen.
  3. The correct answer is A (Supervised pelvic floor muscle training, building holds and quick contractions, with a contraction before coughing): Supervised, progressive pelvic floor muscle training is first-line treatment for stress incontinence and suits her weak, short contraction. Contracting before a cough gives immediate protection while strength builds. A handout alone cannot confirm she is contracting correctly, abdominal work first delays the muscles that need training, and high load or impact training adds pressure on a pelvic floor that is not yet strong enough to cope.
  4. The correct answer is C (Start with graded low-impact loading, progress impact gradually, and monitor leakage and heaviness): Her strength and endurance are good and she has no leakage with coughing, so she is ready for a graded return rather than a wait for perfection, using impact tolerance as feedback. Leakage with impact is common but should not be ignored, and surgery is not considered after only six months of improving conservative care. Waiting for zero leakage on a test delays a safe, progressive loading programme.
Case CHB231
