Mrs Trang Bui Tran, 74, a retired cleaner who speaks Vietnamese with very little English, is referred by her GP to a community health physiotherapy clinic. Her daughter attended the first visit. For 12 months she has had bilateral buttock and thigh heaviness after walking about 150 metres, easing within minutes of sitting or leaning forward on a shopping trolley. Walking uphill is easier than downhill. She rides a stationary bike at the community centre for 20 minutes without symptoms. No rest pain and no bladder or bowel change. History: hypertension, type 2 diabetes, bilateral knee osteoarthritis. MRI report: moderate central canal narrowing at L3-4 and L4-5. Examination: lumbar extension reproduces thigh heaviness, flexion is comfortable. Straight leg raise is negative. Lower limb strength is 5/5, reflexes are symmetrical, sensation is intact and dorsalis pedis pulses are palpable. Goal: walk to the local shops (about 400 metres) and back.

  1. The correct answer is A (Symptoms ease in spinal flexion, like leaning on a trolley, and cycling is tolerated): Relief in spinal flexion and comfortable cycling are typical of neurogenic claudication, because a flexed spine enlarges the canal and relieves pressure on the cauda equina roots. Vascular claudication is tied to a reproducible walking distance and settles within seconds of simply stopping, whatever the spinal posture. Her palpable pulses also argue against a vascular cause.
  2. The correct answer is C (Graded walking with flexed rest breaks, plus stationary cycling and trunk strengthening to build endurance): A programme that builds aerobic capacity in a flexed, tolerated posture (cycling, graded walking with seated or leaning rests) and trunk strength matches the mechanism of stenosis and her goal. Extension based work narrows the canal and provokes her symptoms. Traction has little support, and prolonged rest from walking leads to deconditioning.
  3. The correct answer is C (Arrange urgent emergency department assessment for suspected cauda equina compression and notify the GP): New urinary hesitancy, saddle numbness and bilateral leg weakness are red flags for cauda equina compression, which needs same-day emergency assessment and possible surgical decompression. A GP visit, even on the same day, may not give access to the imaging and surgical team she needs, and an outpatient scan or next day appointment risks irreversible bladder and motor loss. Exercise is not appropriate until the emergency is excluded.
  4. The correct answer is A (Engage a qualified interpreter, in person or by phone, to explain the plan to Mrs Tran): Valid consent requires that Mrs Tran herself understands the risks, options and plan, which needs an impartial qualified interpreter. A relative may filter or soften information, even with good intentions. Translated paperwork or cooperation alone does not show that she has understood or agreed.
Case CHB232

Farid Haddad, 59, an electrician, is 7 weeks after a left middle cerebral artery ischaemic stroke and is an inpatient in a rehabilitation unit. He has right hemiparesis (arm 2/5 with little active wrist movement, leg 3/5), walks 15 metres with a four-wheeled frame and moderate assistance, and has no aphasia. Over the last fortnight he has missed sessions, saying 'there is no point, I will never work again'. He sleeps poorly, eats little and tells staff he feels like a burden to his family. The unit psychologist has diagnosed post-stroke depression, the PHQ-9 score is 16, and he is starting an antidepressant prescribed by the rehabilitation physician. MoCA is 22/30 with errors in attention and delayed recall. This morning he refused his session and told you he wants to go home on Friday. His wife Rana says she will sign whatever is needed on his behalf. BP 132/80, afebrile.

  1. The correct answer is C (Presume capacity, give clear information, and ask him to explain the decision and risks): Capacity is presumed, is specific to each decision, and is not removed by a diagnosis of depression or a low cognitive screening score. The first step is to give clear information and check that he can understand, weigh and explain the decision and its risks. Relatives cannot consent for a person who has capacity, and arranging discharge without that discussion skips the consent process.
  2. The correct answer is D (Ask directly about thoughts of self-harm and inform the treating team the same day): Statements about being a burden and having no point are warning signs, and asking directly about self-harm does not increase risk. The treating team needs to hear the same day so the risk can be assessed properly. Reassurance alone or avoiding the topic delays that assessment, and extra exercise does not address safety.
  3. The correct answer is B (Depression lowers participation and outcomes, so physiotherapists adapt goals, grade tasks and support referral): Depression after stroke is associated with poorer engagement and functional outcomes, so physiotherapists adapt goals, grade activity and support referral within the team. Delivering psychological therapy is outside physiotherapy scope, and pausing rehabilitation removes the activity that helps recovery. Leaving everything to the psychologist ignores that attendance and motivation are affecting his physiotherapy and that his goals need reviewing.
  4. The correct answer is A (Co-design staged goals around hand use, with meaningful task practice and recorded small wins): Goals tied to something he values, such as holding tools for work, tend to improve motivation and participation. Staged targets give early wins, and active task practice is more likely to promote recovery than passive work. Deferring arm work or limiting it to passive range removes the activity he cares about, and keeping a walking goal he calls pointless risks further disengagement.
Case CHB233

Sam Okafor, 29, non-binary (they/them), works in a call centre. They present with 18 months of right neck and shoulder pain, averaging 5/10 and worse at the end of long shifts. Sam has taken testosterone for two years under their GP and wears a chest binder most days for 8 to 10 hours. They report rib tightness on deep breaths when the binder is on. No arm symptoms, no night pain, no weight loss. Examination: cervical rotation 60 degrees left and 50 degrees right with end-range pain, upper limb neurology normal, shoulder range full, upper trapezius and levator scapulae tender, right lower rib springing mildly tender. SpO2 98 percent. Sam asks how many sessions they can afford. Their GP completed a chronic disease management plan last month. They have no private health extras cover and no NDIS plan.

  1. The correct answer is A (Use harm reduction: check binder size, plan breaks and avoid exercising in it): Binding is important to Sam, so a harm reduction approach works with their goals: correct size, breaks, shorter wear and not exercising in a binder reduce rib and breathing problems. Telling them to stop ignores their needs and is unlikely to be followed. A tighter binder worsens the problem, and imaging is not indicated by mild tenderness without trauma.
  2. The correct answer is C (Pain education, graded neck and thoracic exercise, with workstation and binder wear adjustments): Persistent mechanical neck and shoulder pain is best managed with education, graded active exercise and addressing contributing load such as posture at work and binder wear. A collar and rest promote deconditioning. Thrust manipulation as the lead treatment and passive modalities have little lasting benefit and do not match Sam's wish to understand and self-manage.
  3. The correct answer is C (Stop the session, keep Sam resting and arrange urgent emergency department assessment by ambulance): Pleuritic chest pain, breathlessness, tachycardia, lowered oxygen saturation and a tender calf after a long flight suggest a possible pulmonary embolism, which needs emergency assessment now. Exertion or travel by taxi adds risk, and waiting to see whether symptoms settle or saturations fall further wastes time. Removing the binder addresses the known rib problem but does not explain the calf and cardiorespiratory findings, and a same day GP visit cannot provide urgent imaging or monitoring.
  4. The correct answer is D (A capped number of rebated visits is available yearly, with Sam paying any gap): A GP chronic disease management plan allows a limited number of Medicare rebated allied health visits each year, and the rebate often does not cover the whole fee, so Sam may pay a gap. Medicare does not fund unlimited visits or pay full fees automatically. A specialist referral is not required when a GP plan is in place.
Case CHB234

Fatima Haddad, 41, a cleaner, was in a rear-end car collision five days ago. Her GP diagnosed concussion and a neck strain, and she holds a compensable scheme claim. She has frequent headache, dizziness when turning her head quickly, light sensitivity, fatigue and poor concentration. No loss of consciousness reported by bystanders. Neurological screen is normal: pupils equal and reactive, cranial nerves intact, limb power and sensation normal, Romberg steady. Neck rotation 50 degrees each side with pain at end range and tight suboccipital muscles. Resting HR 78, BP 125/78. She speaks Arabic and limited English. Her 16-year-old son is present and has been translating at the reception desk. She wants to return to cleaning offices full time next week and asks you to complete the work capacity form today.

  1. The correct answer is C (Book an accredited interpreter for the session and explain to the family why this helps): Accurate communication about symptoms, risks and consent needs a trained, impartial interpreter, and a teenage family member should not carry that role or hear private health details. Splitting the session or relying on a summary in English leaves gaps in understanding, and untrained staff are not a substitute for an accredited interpreter.
  2. The correct answer is B (Worsening headache, repeated vomiting, drowsiness, confusion, new weakness or a seizure needs an emergency visit): Progressive headache, repeated vomiting, drowsiness, confusion, new focal weakness or seizure can indicate intracranial bleeding or other serious complications and need emergency care. Listing the right warning signs but waiting for the next review delays that care. Symptoms that are common after concussion, such as light sensitivity, tired concentration and a sore neck, are expected to settle gradually and are managed through her GP rather than as emergencies.
  3. The correct answer is A (Light symptom-limited activity such as walking, plus gentle neck movement, kept below symptom worsening): After the first few days, gradual symptom-limited activity supports recovery from concussion and gentle neck movement addresses the strain, while keeping activity below symptom worsening. Prolonged strict rest can delay recovery, and pushing to full effort or loading the neck heavily risks exacerbating symptoms.
  4. The correct answer is B (Advise a graded return with modified duties and share findings with her GP, with her consent): Ongoing symptoms with head movement suggest a gradual return with modified tasks is safer than full duties, and communication with the GP, with her consent, supports consistent advice. Clearing her for full duties ignores her symptoms, and complete time off until she has no symptoms is not the recommended approach.
Case CHB235

Mrs Claire Lawson, 74, retired primary school teacher, lives alone on a rural property and attends physiotherapy by video call on a tablet, with her neighbour nearby. She had paralytic poliomyelitis at age 6 affecting her right leg and trunk, and has walked independently with a right ankle foot orthosis for decades. Over two years she reports increasing fatigue from mid-afternoon, new right quadriceps weakness, aching in the right thigh and calf, a cold right foot, and two falls in six months. She has stable hypothyroidism and osteoporosis. Her neurologist has confirmed late effects of polio and excluded other causes. Observed by video: five sit to stands in 22 seconds using her arms, right knee hyperextension in stance, steady gait over 6 metres with her orthosis. Evening fatigue is 7 out of 10. Goals: keep gardening and walk to her letterbox. The clinic wants to start using standard outcome measures across its telehealth clients.

  1. The correct answer is C (Gradual failure of overworked, enlarged motor units that once compensated for lost anterior horn cells): Late effects are thought to come from long-term strain on surviving motor units that took over for neurons lost in the original illness. The virus does not reactivate, and the pattern is not simple disuse or demyelination. Recognising this explains why standard heavy strengthening can backfire.
  2. The correct answer is B (Pace activity with short walks and planned rests, stopping before pain or fatigue builds): Energy conservation and pacing, with exercise kept below the level that provokes pain or lasting fatigue, protects overworked motor units while keeping her active. Adding rest breaks to the same walk but progressing distance still pushes through the overnight pain. Pool work with progression ignores the need to limit load, and restricting walking to the house cuts the activity she values and does not match her goals.
  3. The correct answer is B (Arrange prompt medical review with her GP or neurologist and send them a written summary): New bulbar symptoms, meaning swallowing and voice change, can occur with late effects of polio but also signal other serious conditions, so prompt medical review is needed. Waiting, reassuring or starting swallowing exercises delays assessment and falls outside physiotherapy scope. A written summary helps the doctor act quickly.
  4. The correct answer is C (Repeat the same validated measures with one remote protocol at set intervals): Valid, reliable measures used the same way each time, with a standard remote set-up, let the clinic compare people and detect real change. Letting clinicians choose different tools means results cannot be compared across clients. Self-report scales alone leave out performance measures such as the timed sit to stand, and testing only at the start and end with an in-person protocol cannot track change through a telehealth service.
Case CHB236

Hassan Khalil, 66, retired electrician, three weeks after left total knee replacement for osteoarthritis. Discharged from hospital on day four, attends outpatient physiotherapy twice weekly in a small private practice with one other physiotherapist. Walks indoors with a single stick. Medical history: hypertension, type 2 diabetes; medications perindopril, metformin and regular paracetamol. Findings: wound clean and dry, no discharge; moderate warm effusion consistent with recovery; knee flexion 80 degrees active, extension lag 8 degrees on straight leg raise with passive extension to 0; pain 4/10 on activity; calf soft; observations normal. He is friendly, has attended every session, and has started bringing food for the staff. He says the treatment has changed his life.

  1. The correct answer is D (Quadriceps inhibition driven by effusion and pain, responding to swelling control and activation work): Full passive extension with an active lag points to a muscle activation problem rather than a mechanical block, and effusion and pain commonly inhibit the quadriceps after surgery. Treating the swelling and retraining activation is the logical response. A fixed contracture or tight hamstrings would limit passive extension too, and tendon rupture would present with a sudden loss of function.
  2. The correct answer is C (Quadriceps setting with biofeedback, short-arc extension work, and effusion management between each session): When the quadriceps cannot yet lift the leg without a lag, activation drills with feedback and small-range work with swelling control restore control safely. Heavy loaded extension and deep squats overload a swollen, recently operated knee. Weighted raises to fatigue will reinforce the lag and increase effusion.
  3. The correct answer is D (Stop treatment, cover the wound and arrange urgent medical review the same day): Fever, spreading redness and wound discharge after joint replacement suggest possible deep infection, which threatens the prosthesis and can become systemic. He needs same-day medical assessment, and further movement or heat-producing modalities are inappropriate. Routing him to a GP appointment within days or a surgical clinic review at the next sitting, or simply rechecking him later, all wait too long for this presentation.
  4. The correct answer is D (Thank him, decline the gift and invitation, offer clinic contact details, and document it): Professional boundaries are protected by declining a significant gift, personal contact and social invitations kindly, while keeping him able to reach the practice for any further care. Recording the exchange shows transparency. Accepting even part of the offer blurs the relationship, and ending treatment does not remove the power difference that boundaries exist to manage.
Case CHB237

Katerina Vlahos, 77, retired teacher, lives with her husband in a regional town. She has non cystic fibrosis bronchiectasis affecting the right lower lobe and lingula on CT, with two infective exacerbations in the past year. She coughs up about two tablespoons of mucopurulent sputum most mornings and finds it hard to clear, especially after a poor night. Medical history: hiatus hernia with reflux, osteoporosis, osteoarthritis in both hands. SpO2 95% on room air, RR 18, HR 80, afebrile, coarse crackles at the right base that partly clear with a cough. She tires after repeated coughing and feels breathless lying head down. At the review, her daughter shows you a web article about a new oscillating vest, based on a small trial of 20 people, and asks whether Katerina should switch.

  1. The correct answer is A (It reduces retained secretions that drive the cycle of infection and airway inflammation): Retained secretions allow bacteria to persist and inflame the airways, which causes further damage, so regular clearance helps break that cycle. Physiotherapy does not reverse the structural dilatation or scarring of bronchiectasis. Clearance may reduce exacerbations but supports antibiotics during a flare up and cannot replace them, and success is judged by effective, comfortable removal of secretions rather than the volume produced.
  2. The correct answer is C (Add breathing control between cycles and use huffs from mid to low lung volume): The active cycle combines breathing control, thoracic expansion and huffing, and breathing control between cycles reduces fatigue and airway collapse. Huffs from mid to low volume move secretions from peripheral to central airways with less effort than repeated forceful coughs. Dropping the cycle's other phases removes recovery, and percussion over ribs in a woman with osteoporosis carries fracture risk.
  3. The correct answer is A (Use left side lying without head down tilt, with active cycle of breathing technique): Head down tilt is poorly tolerated with reflux and hiatus hernia, so a modified or flat position is used and clearance relies on the breathing technique. Left side lying places the right lower lobe uppermost for drainage. Prolonging a tilt she cannot tolerate is unsafe, hypertonic saline needs a prescription, and lying on the affected side makes drainage worse.
  4. The correct answer is B (Appraise its design, then weigh it against guidelines, other evidence and Katerina's preferences): Evidence based practice combines critically appraised research, guideline recommendations, clinical expertise and the patient's values. A small unblinded trial and more sputum weight are weak grounds to change treatment alone. Adopting a device because it is new, rejecting all but huge trials, or ignoring research are all poor appraisal habits.
Case CHB238

Emeka Owusu, 41, a delivery driver, is referred to subacute rehabilitation two weeks after a left Achilles tendon rupture while playing soccer. The orthopaedic surgeon chose non-operative management in a walking boot with heel wedges, with weight-bearing as tolerated. No medical history, non-smoker. Today: mild swelling, skin intact, the ankle rests in plantarflexion in the boot, calf soft and non-tender, resting pain 2 out of 10, HR 70, BP 118/76. He walks with crutches and partial weight-bearing. This is a private claim with no workers compensation scheme. His manager, Rick, has phoned reception asking for Emeka's diagnosis and a return-to-work date. Emeka has not signed any release of information. He lives with his partner and has no other injuries.

  1. The correct answer is D (Avoid dorsiflexion beyond the position set in the boot so the tendon ends are not lengthened): Early healing relies on the tendon ends staying close, so dorsiflexion beyond the boot position must be avoided in this phase. Stretching or passive dorsiflexion beyond neutral risks elongation and a weaker calf later. Complete stillness is outdated, as protected early movement within the permitted range supports healing.
  2. The correct answer is D (Decline to share details, document the call and ask Emeka what information he agrees to release): Health information can be disclosed only with the patient's consent or where the law requires it, and an employer has no automatic entitlement. Even confirming attendance reveals health care use. Documenting the call and then asking Emeka what he wishes to share respects his privacy and lets him control any workplace communication.
  3. The correct answer is C (Stop treatment, keep the boot on and arrange urgent review by the treating orthopaedic team): A sudden pop with loss of push-off and increased dorsiflexion suggests re-rupture, which changes management and needs prompt specialist review. Continuing loading risks further separation of the tendon ends. A negative test after a possible re-rupture would be unreliable, and a delay of a week is too long.
  4. The correct answer is B (Continue calf strengthening and gait work, delay running until strength and endurance criteria are met): Running places high repetitive loads through the Achilles, so it should wait until he can complete single-leg heel raises with adequate height and endurance. Walking quality alone is not enough, and soft ground does not lower tendon load enough. Delaying strengthening for remodelling is outdated, because progressive loading drives recovery.
Case CHB239

Maylene Brown, 71, a lifelong smoker, is on day 3 of an admission with an exacerbation of severe COPD. Echocardiography shows a dilated, hypertrophied right ventricle with raised estimated pulmonary artery pressure, consistent with cor pulmonale and pulmonary hypertension secondary to chronic lung disease. She has a raised jugular venous pressure, bilateral ankle oedema extending to the shins, and chronic hypercapnia. Today: HR 96, BP 128/76 mmHg, RR 24, SpO2 90 percent on 2 L/min via nasal prongs (the medical team has set a target of 88 to 92 percent). She walks with a frame and reports dyspnoea of 5 out of 10 on the Borg scale after 20 metres. The ward manager wants outcome measures documented consistently for a quality improvement project on early mobilisation in respiratory patients, and has asked you to propose a measure.

  1. The correct answer is D (Raised jugular venous pressure with bilateral oedema extending up the legs to the shins): In cor pulmonale the right ventricle fails against high pulmonary pressures, so systemic venous congestion appears as a raised jugular venous pressure and peripheral oedema. Wheeze, hypercapnia, accessory muscle use and exertional desaturation come from the airway and gas exchange problem and are expected in severe COPD without right heart failure.
  2. The correct answer is A (Stop and rest her seated with pursed-lip breathing, and ask nursing to review oxygen titration): A fall below the medical target on exertion means stopping, resting and seeking oxygen adjustment through the ward team and orders, while pursed-lip breathing helps recovery. Raising oxygen without an order risks worsening hypercapnia in a patient who retains carbon dioxide, and removing it is also unsafe. Walking on because desaturation is common ignores the target set for her.
  3. The correct answer is B (Sit her down, check observations, and report the symptoms to the medical team before more exercise): Light-headedness, chest tightness and near-fainting on exertion are warning signs of inadequate right ventricular output in pulmonary hypertension, so the session stops and she is returned to a safe position. A satisfactory saturation does not rule out the problem. Medical review is needed before more exercise, and lowering intensity or retesting leaves her at risk of syncope.
  4. The correct answer is A (A validated walk or mobility test with the same protocol, aids and oxygen recorded each time): A validated functional test given the same way each time, with aids and oxygen recorded, measures the change that physiotherapy aims to influence and can be compared before and after. Different tests chosen by different staff cannot be pooled, spirometry does not capture function or change meaningfully within an admission, and length of stay is influenced by many factors beyond mobilisation.
Case CHB240

Thi Hoa Pham, 31, gravida 2 para 1, 30 weeks pregnant, admitted to the acute ward with right lower lobe community acquired pneumonia. Mild asthma, reliever only. Vietnamese is her first language and she speaks limited English; her husband usually speaks for her. Observations: RR 24, HR 108, BP 108/66, SpO2 94 percent on room air, temperature 37.9. Right basal crackles with reduced air entry, yellow sputum, and pleuritic pain at the right base scored 4 out of 10 after analgesia. Chest X-ray shows right lower lobe consolidation. Antibiotics have started. The obstetric team reports a reactive CTG and no contractions. She lies semi-recumbent and is reluctant to move. Physiotherapy has been asked to assist with airway clearance and early mobilisation.

  1. The correct answer is D (Left side-lying with pillow support, right lung uppermost, avoiding pressure on the vena cava): Left side-lying places the affected right lung uppermost so gravity assists drainage, and it keeps the uterus off the inferior vena cava at 30 weeks. Supine positioning risks aortocaval compression, and head-down tilt increases reflux and breathing effort in late pregnancy. Placing the affected lung down tends to retain secretions.
  2. The correct answer is A (Stop treatment, position her on her left side and inform the midwife and obstetric team immediately): Reduced fetal movement with regular abdominal tightening can signal fetal compromise or preterm labour, so treatment stops and the obstetric team must know immediately. A left lateral position supports uteroplacental blood flow while help arrives. Oxygen and a delayed recheck is a sensible step in isolation but holds back escalation, and continuing treatment or leaving review until rounds delays assessment of a possible emergency.
  3. The correct answer is B (Use the professional interpreter for the consent discussion, and let her husband stay if she agrees): Informed consent needs accurate, impartial interpretation, so a professional interpreter should be used for consent and clinical discussion. Family members may filter or change information and the patient may not feel free to disagree. A nod is not valid consent, and her husband can remain if she wishes.
  4. The correct answer is D (Active cycle of breathing technique with huffing and a supported cough over a folded towel): Active cycle of breathing with huffing and a splinted cough clears secretions without forceful coughing, and the folded towel reduces pleuritic pain. Percussion over a painful inflamed area is poorly tolerated and adds little to active techniques. Forceful repeated coughing raises abdominal pressure and pain, and a cough assist device is not needed in a patient who can clear secretions.
